Here, Fechner outlines the two-part structure of his argument and his intent to bridge the gap between physical science and philosophy.</meta>

<note>The top of the page shows visible bleed-through from the preceding leaf, as noted in the source description.</note>

# PREFACE.

Of the two divisions of this work, distinguished by their own specific titles, the first aims to present atomism <note>original: "Atomistik." In Fechner's time, this referred to the scientific theory that matter is composed of discrete particles, a view then being refined by "exact physics."</note>—the theory of the physical world as shaped by modern exact physics—against the philosophical challenges it faces. It seeks to show that this view is grounded in nature and thus demanded by natural science; furthermore, that it is not only compatible with higher, more general interests, but even serves them. From this vantage point, it also offers some broader reflections on the philosophical directions with which it comes into conflict. The second part aims to show how a philosophical completion of atomism—which its opponents have found lacking until now—is indeed conceivable. This is to be achieved not by abandoning its established essence and its previously determined principles, but rather by anticipating the ultimate goal of the direction it has already taken.

While this work is primarily directed at philosophical opponents, it is by no means intended for them alone. In the main, it addresses a more general public that takes an interest in the broader controversies moving science, and whose judgment on the present matter has, in my view, been unfairly biased by the opposing side. And certainly, the question at hand deserves a wider interest. The atomic question <note>original: "Atomenfrage."</note> is perhaps the point where contemporary philosophy and contemporary natural [science]... <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page.</note>

<note>The following terms appear as a vocabulary list in the source document:</note>
<term>Atomism</term> (Atomistik), <term>physical world</term> (Körperwelt), <term>physics</term> (Physik), <term>natural science</term> (Naturwissenschaft), <term>philosophy</term> (Philosophie), <term>the atomic question</term> (Atomenfrage).

<summary>In this preface, Fechner introduces his two-part defense of atomic theory, arguing that it is both scientifically necessary and philosophically reconcilable with spiritual or "higher" interests. He expresses a desire to correct public bias against atomism caused by contemporary philosophical critics.</summary>
<keywords>atomism, exact physics, natural science, philosophy of nature, reconciliation of science and religion, Gustav Theodor Fechner, atomic question</keywords>

# PREFACE.

natural science clash most severely and, at the same time, diverge most widely. The question of whether atoms exist or not is simultaneously the question of the fundamental configuration—one might almost say the very existence—of both doctrines <note>Fechner refers here to the "doctrines" of physics and philosophy.</note> in their current state.

As for physicists, this writing does not intend to address them specifically or preferentially, insofar as most of them do not require a defense of atomism <term>Atomism: The scientific theory that matter is composed of discrete, individual particles rather than being a continuous medium.</term>, and only a few may care for its philosophical completion. However, for many of them, the compilation of the most important viewpoints—partly empirical <note>Based on observation and experiment.</note>, partly formal—on which atomism can lean, provided in the first part, should nevertheless be of interest. This is because (at least to my knowledge) a compilation of this kind is generally lacking. Furthermore, it easily escapes even those who have not directed special attention to the foundations of the natural sciences how deeply and essentially atomism is interwoven with them on all sides, and how much it is required by the need to link phenomena exactly and clearly. Indeed, one of the main reasons it has not yet found the full and general recognition it deserves, even among natural researchers, might lie in the fact that it has always accomplished its greatest achievements so quietly in the womb of natural science, while conversely being attacked so loudly by philosophers. Many a person breathes its air and thinks it is only air, because he cannot convince himself that the attacks of philosophy are themselves merely airy <note>original: "luftige." Fechner is making a pun: just as one might ignore the air because it is invisible, one might ignore the "atmosphere" of atomic theory, assuming the philosophical attacks against it have substance when they are actually "thin air" or insubstantial.</note>.

Furthermore, the physicist who does not wish to isolate himself one-sidedly within his own science cannot be indifferent to the difference he finds between himself and the prevailing philosophy regarding one of the most important foundations of his field. And since, until now, a discussion on this matter has taken place almost exclusively from the opposing side, it may well touch upon his interest,

V

# PREFACE.

...[wishes] to see the same <note>The "same" refers to atomism, mentioned on the previous page.</note> taken up for once with the means at one's disposal, from the standpoint on which he himself stands. In doing so, it was necessary—because physical atomism <term>Atomism: The theory that all matter consists of tiny, indivisible particles.</term> is not merely, or even primarily, attacked on physical grounds—to look beyond a defense based on physical evidence (which will always remain decisive for the physicist) and consider the more general viewpoints and relationships into which atomism enters. While the first chapters of the first section deal primarily with the purely physical side of the subject, the final chapters go into its more general relationships and return to the very principles upon which the physical arguments themselves are based.

In this regard, I might perhaps claim some of the physicist's attention specifically for a series of reflections in the eighth chapter of the first section, based on the following viewpoint. It seems to me that physicists, and natural researchers in general, when they reach certain boundary considerations <note>original: "Gränzbetrachtungen." This refers to the limits where scientific observation meets philosophical speculation.</note> of their field, are easily disoriented. They believe that behind the world of physical and mental appearance, one must assume a "dark essence" <note>An unknowable, underlying reality.</note> and, in the most thorough investigation, take this into account—a realm for which philosophy either offers or is supposed to offer the key. They believe that everything we see, hear, or feel by touch—indeed, perhaps even what we think—is merely "subjective appearance" <term>Subjective appearance: The idea that our senses provide only a filtered "illusion" of reality rather than reality itself.</term>, drawn like a veil over something that produces this appearance in the first place; an appearance that differs for everyone according to the specific construction of their eyes and brain, which are merely instruments of this illusion. They believe it is always necessary to ask about the truly and objectively "being-in-itself," the Real, which lies behind the whole world of appearance; and that if the nature and relationships of this "Being" cannot be known (as they may well be unknowable), one must at least determine the relationship of the world of appearance to it and, in any case, recognize appearance as such. This, finally, is supposed to be the "true depth." (Kant,

VI

# PREFACE.

<margin>}</margin>(...Herbart, and most natural researchers, when they delve deeply.) I seek to show that it is but the depth of a shadow which one still seeks behind the depth of the entire, full, bright world. Indeed, there are shadows, but only those which things cast upon one another. And seeking out the relationships between them is alone what provides the true and higher light.

Naturally, it cannot be my intention to recapitulate this subject here in the preface; however, I wish to add a little anecdote here for illustration, which occurs to me just as I write this. Prof. Erman the Elder <note>original: "Prof. Erman d. A." referring to Paul Erman (1764–1851), a notable German physicist.</note> told it to me when I visited him in Berlin years ago, and for some reason it has remained in my memory, while so many more remarkable stories have escaped me.

A distinguished Pole or Russian visited a large factory establishment in Berlin that was powered by a steam engine. He was guided through the entire institution, inspected all its parts very closely, followed the interlocking of the machine parts, asked about everything possible, and conversed very intelligently with the foreman about the conditions of the establishment; in short, he seemed to be perfectly oriented regarding the operation and the machinery <note>original: "Getriebe," referring to the internal workings or gears of a system.</note> of the institution. When at last, after everything had been gone through, he said to the foreman’s utter astonishment: "Would you not now also show me the lower rooms where the horses are kept?" — Thus one asks for the horses below, after having seen the whole operation and—if not the builder—at least the workers at the machine, and everything else before one's eyes.

I simply say in that chapter: there are no horses below.

If I were to succeed, with my presentation of philosophy, in dissuading even a single soul who wishes to lose themselves in those dark depths of contemplation—where everything is but weeping and gnashing of teeth <note>A biblical idiom (Matthew 8:12) used here to describe the misery and confusion of certain metaphysical systems.</note> and everyone is against everyone else—then I would already believe I had accomplished something.

<summary>Fechner uses an anecdote about a man looking for "horses" beneath a steam engine to mock philosophers who seek hidden, mystical causes behind physical laws. He argues that there is no "dark essence" beneath reality, only the relationships between observable things.</summary>
<keywords>Johann Friedrich Herbart, Paul Erman, steam engine analogy, metaphysics, materialism vs. idealism, philosophy of science</keywords>

# Preface. VII

I do not consider it useless to point out particularly that, while the second section of this work rests entirely upon the first and would have no foundation without the justifications of the first, the reverse is not the case. This is true even if opponents—from perspectives which it is a primary aim of this work to counter—might perhaps represent it as if the validity of **physical atomism**, as defended in the first section, depends on the validity of a **philosophical atomism** to which this or that person might attempt to elevate it, or in which this or that person (ourselves included) might attempt to conclude it. This would suggest that I could only uphold physical atomism on the basis of the philosophical version in which I attempt to conclude it in the second section. In my opinion, however, this is to turn the matter on its head. Even if one wishes to reject the metaphysical viewpoint (in the sense of the conception of <term>Metaphysics: the branch of philosophy dealing with the fundamental nature of reality</term> on page 127) through which I attempt to achieve a philosophical conclusion, all the arguments that exist for physical atomism according to its current state would nevertheless remain standing.

The situation is that physical atomism, by demanding, asserting, and proving a structure and sub-structure of bodies into discrete parts beyond the apparent <term>Continuum: something that is continuous and has no separate parts</term>—and herein lies its essence—is nonetheless not yet able to say anything definite about the nature of the final members, the <term>Grundatome: fundamental or "prime" atoms</term>. Just as the world is organized into discrete cosmic systems and celestial bodies, so too the celestial body and every other body is organized into "atomic systems" (so-called <term>Molecule: molecules</term>) and atoms, which appear to form a continuous mass only for reasons similar to why stars appear as a continuous mass in a <term>Nebelflecke: nebula or star cluster</term>. That is, in short, what it knows. But how large, how small, how shaped—how, in general, are the final members, the fundamental atoms, to be understood? Physics is unable to tell us anything certain about this. It only knows that—

<summary>The author argues that scientific "physical atomism" is a valid field of study independent of "philosophical atomism," using the analogy of a nebula to explain how discrete atoms appear as continuous matter.</summary>
<keywords>physical atomism, philosophical atomism, metaphysics, molecules, fundamental atoms, continuum, nebulae</keywords>

# Preface.
VIII

...to say them; the organization into discrete parts extends further than the eye and the microscope allow one to follow. The boundary, however, still lies in the indefinite and the dark for them. Now, one can attempt to resolve this indefiniteness—which remains in the final instance on the physical side—out of philosophical interest and with a philosophical foresight; but suppose that interest is not satisfied by the attempt, the vision has deceived, and the philosophical view—which tries to go beyond or behind the physical <term>Connection of facts: the logical web of evidence and observations</term> represented and granted by modern <term>Atomism: the scientific theory that matter is composed of small, discrete units</term>—is erroneous. In that case, this would only demand a new philosophical view, a more adequate conclusion, but would not invalidate that connection of facts, and thus would not invalidate physical <term>Atomism</term>. However, that <term>Atomism</term>—in that cautious limitation in which we maintain it in the first part, and in which every prudent physicist will agree with us (where even much that is physically determinable is left undecided), while the philosophical questions and difficulties regarding the nature of <term>Matter: the physical substance that makes up the world</term> and <term>Force: the active power or energy within nature</term> do not yet even begin and could only be improperly brought into play—that, I say, <term>Atomism</term> in this cautious limitation is truly a view that represents and grants a connection of facts, is precisely what the first part of this work intends to show.

If, however, I do make the attempt in the second part to abandon this limitation and delve into the ultimate <term>Constitution: the fundamental makeup or internal structure</term> of <term>Matter</term> itself, I ask that this attempt be received with indulgence. If one wishes for once to go beyond a <term>Physics: the study of the material world</term> with a <term>Metaphysics: the study of reality beyond physical observation</term>—and I believe it is truly a human need to look ahead toward every goal, even before one stands before it—then I maintain that the view presented there is the most probable one that can be reached, as it reveals itself as the absolute limit of the path that the connection of physical facts...

<summary>The author argues that scientific atomism is a solid framework of facts that remains valid even if philosophical theories about the nature of matter are proven wrong. He justifies his upcoming metaphysical speculations as a natural human desire to explore ultimate truths beyond physical evidence.</summary>

# Preface.
IX

...already forces one to go (which is why physicists before me have also arrived at it), as it recommends itself by carrying with it a pure and clear <term>Nexus: a logical connection or link between elements</term> <note>original: "Nexus"</note> of metaphysical concepts. This nexus is certainly not a <term>Dialectical: a method of philosophical argument that seeks to resolve contradictions</term> one in the sense of modern philosophy, but I far prefer it to that, because it is not merely the product and producer of an ambiguous <term>Formalism: a focus on logical structure or abstract rules rather than the physical content of reality</term>, but rather it summarizes the imaginable connection of world-things directly and compactly into final points and nodes. However, as I myself confess (p. 127) that I do not consider <term>Metaphysics: the branch of philosophy that deals with the fundamental nature of reality, including the relationship between mind and matter</term> to be as exact a science as mathematical physics, I also admit to an element of uncertainty in such considerations as I shall undertake. One may consider this confession of uncertainty regarding one of the most important parts of philosophy to be a heresy against philosophy itself; yet, in any case, the actual state of all metaphysics to date speaks in my favor.

With the foregoing, I believe I have sufficiently indicated the formal point of view according to which I separate physical and philosophical <term>Atomism: the theory that all of reality is composed of small, discrete, and indivisible units</term> in this work; for it would be a misunderstanding to wish to impose upon me a separation in the subject matter itself. In physical atomism, I set forth what can be physically grounded up to now—that is, through a connection of facts; in philosophical atomism, I set forth that which can be philosophically demanded on the basis of what is physically grounded—that is, out of the need for a pure conceptual conclusion. But demands, in general, do not easily possess the certainty of grounds; and it is good to keep the two as sharp and strictly separate as possible. It is for this very reason that two parts have been made out of that which must be subject to a single thing. In proportion as physics progresses, it will now either increasingly satisfy the philosophical pre-demands, or philosophy, for its part, will be able to either correct or more firmly establish its demands according to these advancements; for the more

<summary>Fechner explains the methodological separation between physical atomism, which is grounded in facts, and philosophical atomism, which seeks a conceptual completion of those facts. He admits that while physics provides certainty, metaphysics is currently a less exact science subject to uncertainty.</summary>

# Preface.
X

...the further the progress toward the goal, the more certain the look forward to it. The correct philosophical goal of physics will ultimately be the completion of physics itself.

I would wish that even the opponents of <term>Atomism: the theory that matter is composed of small, discrete units rather than a continuous fluid</term> would keep clearly in mind the separation of the two questions made here: first, does an organization of seemingly continuous masses of bodies exist; and second, is the way in which this or that person—or we ourselves—conceives of the <term>Constitution: the fundamental makeup or structural arrangement</term> of these ultimate members, correct? They should see the "life-or-death" question of physical atomism in that first question alone, because in truth, it lies only there. Only too often—perhaps even usually—is the first question denied simply because it is thrown together, confused, and mingled with the second. Yet, in all other cases, one appropriately separates two questions if they can truly be separated and potentially allow for different answers, which is the case here. What fables and nonsense have not been told about the constitution of the ultimate atoms! How few clear ideas still prevail regarding the essence of matter in general, and these have taken hold in atomistic descriptions no less than in <term>Dynamic: referring here to "Dynamism," a rival 19th-century theory that viewed matter as a manifestation of forces rather than particles</term> ones. However, judging the <term>Validity: the soundness or "Triftigkeit"</term> <note>original: "Triftigkeit"</note> of physical atomism within the designated limits does not depend on this at all, but rather only its further development and deepening. Anyone who, because of such invalid conceptions of the ultimate members and the concepts of matter, force, and so on, wished to consider the <term>Discrete: consisting of distinct, separate parts</term> organization of matter as refuted, would be drawing a conclusion just as invalid as someone who wished to consider the cells in a plant refuted because highly uncertain, wavering, and unclear ideas still exist regarding their original formation, the ultimate constitution of the cell wall and the cell nucleus, and the concept of the cell itself. The cell exists regardless of this. One error of the opponents is, of course, that they believe the existence of the atom is less provable than that of the cell because the former is only [provable] through a connection of experiences,

<summary>The author argues that the existence of atoms (discrete particles) should be judged separately from the philosophical debate over their internal nature, using a comparison to botanical cells to prove his point.</summary>
<keywords>atomism, physical physics vs. philosophy, discrete matter, cell theory, constitution of matter, dynamism</keywords>

# Preface
XI

...this can already be confirmed by a single experience. This is not the place to speak further on this in the preface; the work itself will have to illuminate this point further.

I do not doubt, of course, that my aforementioned wish is made in vain. Philosophical opponents will be just as little inclined to agree to the requested separation of the stated questions as to several other questions upon which I insist no less in the course of this writing; and I do not deny at all that in every <term>a priori: reasoning from theoretical deduction or first principles rather than from observation</term> context, all these questions must also be conceived and treated as a single connected whole. However, one should not forget that my intention in the following pages is not to add a new one to the manifold and conflicting *a priori* investigations into these questions—for which there is certainly no need—but rather to encounter them on a path uninvolved in their conflict. Specifically, I intend to investigate what facts teach and demand in the sense of <term>exact: referring to rigorous, logically mathematical methods</term> (i.e., logically mathematical) connection. But even the philosopher, though he may not wish to walk this path himself, cannot or at least should not reject a <note>The author uses "Control" here in the sense of a scientific check or verification process.</note> verification and testing upon it. All the more do I assume that it may be welcome to others; and as I said, I address myself not to philosophers alone. 

Exact science, however, cannot in principle wish to prove or settle everything from a single anticipated principle—as philosophy has often attempted but never accomplished; and the failure of these attempts thus far makes even the verification by the other path we take here necessary. Therefore, there is a need here for a separation of many questions which philosophy may continue to treat in solidary connection, thereby exposing itself to the danger that with the failure of one part of its structure, its entire structure falls.*)

---

*) A treatise by the <note>Immanuel Hermann Fichte (1796–1879), son of the famous J.G. Fichte, who was a contemporary critic of materialistic atomism.</note> younger Fichte against atomism (in the Journal...

<summary>The author argues that while philosophers prefer to treat all questions of matter as a single theoretical system, a scientific approach requires separating empirical facts from metaphysical speculation to avoid the entire system collapsing if one part is proven wrong.</summary>

# Preface
XII

->**Preface.**<-

Moreover, given the circumstance that—after a dispute with philosophical opponents in the first part—I myself attempt a philosophical conclusion to **atomism** <term>atomism: the theory that all matter is composed of small, indivisible particles</term> in the second part, and that in the course of the first part I even place a main argument for atomism therein, defending it against many physicists (and chemists) on the grounds that it is required by the philosophy of physics, one will not be able to accuse this writing of pursuing an anti-philosophical direction. And as much as the direction of our philosophy, which we are so named—

<note>This section appears to be a lengthy addition or footnote responding to a contemporary review.</note>
The *Journal of Philosophy* (1854, p. 24), which came to my hands after this entire work, including the preface, had already been written, gives me cause to add a few more corroborating words to the above. The separation of both questions demanded above is also missing in this treatise; and what has been said—not incorrectly—by the author <note>Referring to Immanuel Hermann Fichte (1796–1879), a philosopher who often critiqued materialist tendencies.</note> against the attempts of many physicists to either fathom the ultimate **constitution** of atoms, or to conceptually grasp, relate, and interpret the fact of atoms, appears immediately incorrect if it is intended to hold against atomism in general. To the extent that the physicist reaches beyond the limits of the first formulation of the question into the territory of philosophy—and I do not deny the invitation to do so, since I yield to it myself—he also almost necessarily falls prey to the fate that all philosophical attempts to fathom the ultimate and to relate fundamental concepts to one another have had so far: that is, to fall into wavering and contentious territory, where most of it is usually a dispute over words.

But for this very reason, one must separate that which all atomists agree upon and which is clear from a physical point of view, from that which they disagree upon and which is philosophically unclear. By rejecting one along with the other, Fichte <note>original: "schüttet er das Kind mit dem Bade aus"</note> **throws the baby out with the bathwater**, which alone was to be poured away. To this end, we ourselves bring a pot; but we want to save the child.

**Physical atomism**, as it is set forth by me in the 7th Chapter of the first part, can generally neither be founded nor refuted philosophically (from concepts alone); it can, however, itself count among the foundations of a philosophy which wishes to base its concepts on the actual state of affairs. **Philosophical atomism**, as it is set forth by me in the second part, is of course also open to philosophical objections.

A more detailed **reply** against Fichte I have provided in the third issue of the *Philosophical Journal* (1854).

<summary>Fechner defends his work against being called anti-philosophical and critiques philosopher I. H. Fichte for failing to distinguish between empirical physical atomism and speculative philosophical atomism.</summary>
<keywords>atomism, physical atomism, philosophical atomism, I. H. Fichte, scientific method, conceptual vs. empirical, philosophy of physics</keywords>

# Preface
XIII

->**Preface.**<-

not shrink from being opposed to the prevailing view from a certain perspective, the task of seeking the most general, the highest, and the ultimate remains common to both. The difference, in the end, lies only in the path and the manner of that seeking. Now, I endeavor to show that **Atomism** <term>atomism: the theory that all matter is composed of small, indivisible particles</term> is not only perfectly compatible with the search for what **Philosophy** must seek, but that it is itself to be regarded as a discovery in the spirit of this search—a discovery first glimpsed long ago by **Philosophy**, then preserved by **Physics**, and which Physics now offers back to Philosophy (which in the meantime rejected it) in a more perfected form. I seek to show how a **Philosophy** that rejects this discovery abandons itself; yet, through this, **Philosophy** in general will not be lost, for **Philosophy** does not die. Therefore, everything I say in this writing against philosophers and **Philosophy** without qualification should only be understood as being directed against the currently prevailing anti-atomistic direction of **Philosophy**, and not against **Philosophy** in general. It would have been tedious to add this distinction every time; and anyone who does not wish to misunderstand me in this regard cannot misunderstand me.

If, however, one finds the attacks in this writing against the **Schellingian**, **Hegelian**, and **Herbartian** <note>Friedrich Schelling (1775–1854), G.W.F. Hegel (1770–1831), and Johann Friedrich Herbart (1776–1841) were the dominant figures of German Idealism and its offshoots, who often favored organic or continuous views of nature over particulate atomism.</note> ways of philosophizing (as all belong to the anti-atomistic direction and primarily define it today) to be somewhat harsh and blunt, one should not overlook that they are motivated as a defense against equally harsh and—as I attempt to show here—less justified and grounded attacks against the doctrine defended here.

It may well be, however, that in the heat of **defense** or **attack**, the **recognition** recedes too far—recognition which is due in any case to men who, even if they are not the actual possessors of absolute **Wisdom** <note>original: "Sophia"</note>, as they...

<summary>The author argues that atomism is a philosophical discovery refined by physics and clarifies that his criticisms are aimed specifically at anti-atomistic movements (like Hegelianism) rather than philosophy itself.</summary>

# Preface
XIV

->**Preface.**<-

even if they proclaimed it themselves, they were certainly the representatives and preservers of a **Philosophy** <term>Philosophia: the study of the fundamental nature of knowledge, reality, and existence</term> for a long time, and remain so in their successors, who merely confused the lively desire with the possession, and the path with the goal. After all, there is no possession without desire, no goal without a path; and who can ever fully boast of a certain possession, or of a perfectly correct grasp of the goal? Indeed, I confess, my conscience almost pricks me when I remember what I myself owe to those men; how I, who have fallen so far from **Schelling** <note>Friedrich Wilhelm Joseph Schelling (1775–1854), a major figure in German Idealism</note> and only bring this falling away to bear here, yet originally fell with my whole philosophy from his stem; how I plucked the best fruit from a certainly far-deviated branch of **Hegel’s**; how I burned a coal on my own hearth from **Herbart’s** ashes—though I regret and miss the stem and fruit of his system (Zend-Avesta II. 351. 43. 373 <note>A reference to Fechner's own 1851 work, *Zend-Avesta: On the Things of Heaven and the Hereafter*, where he first developed many of these ideas</note>). 

It is not at all intended to be said, by all that has been and had to be said in this writing according to its character as a **Polemic** <term>Streitschrift: a formal written attack on a specific opinion or doctrine</term>, that those against whom it is directed have lived and striven in vain on the whole. For they not only upheld the consciousness that above common sensory observation, the fragmented world-view, and the dead rule, there is still something more universal, higher, more living, and whole—that there are ultimate goals beyond all finite goals—but they also gave this consciousness a form. Even if it was only a wavering and dissolving form, it was nonetheless a form that certainly contains great traits of truth. Nor is it to be said that all who proceeded from them and went beyond them (for who still stands entirely with them?) have only gone into greater errors. On the contrary; that would be to strike our own striving along with theirs. Only in this writing, given this subject matter, there was little occasion to emphasize this, which I now feel I must emphasize here to supplement a judgment that would otherwise rightly appear unfair and half-blind, since

# Preface
XV

->**Preface.**<-

servation <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page: "...above common sensory observation."</note>, the opposition in which their directions stand against the one represented here is, in my opinion, based not on those advantages of a grand, lofty, unified, and living mode of contemplation and formation, but rather on the complete misunderstanding and lack of the conditions for a clear contemplation and a sustainable formation—on the fluidity and groundlessness of their foundations. In this regard, I have nothing to take back from the attacks I have made; or, regarding **Herbart** <note>Johann Friedrich Herbart (1776–1841) was a German philosopher and educator who broke away from Hegelian idealism, moving toward a "realism" that heavily influenced 19th-century psychology.</note>, I take back only as much as I must simultaneously retract concerning those aforementioned "advantages" in his work.

<margin>//</margin>
Perhaps at first glance it may appear striking that this writing contains several particularly sharp remarks specifically against Herbart, since among all the schools of thought mentioned, his might seem the most closely related to the one defended here. Is he not also an **atomist** <term>Atomistiker: a philosopher who believes reality is composed of simple, indivisible units</term>? He is, though in a different sense, such that he directly contradicts our version of atomism. Yet, does this not merely stem from the fact that he descended to a greater depth with his atomism? The **atomistic principle** remains common to both of us. He insists just as strongly as we do on starting purely from the **given** <term>Gegebenes: the raw data of experience or empirical facts as they appear to us</term>—admittedly, only to immediately transform it into a "non-given" <note>Herbart argued that while we start with experience, we must use logic to find the underlying "reals" that are not directly observable.</note>. But do we not share with the atomistic principle both the starting point and the **real ground** <term>Realgrund: the objective foundation or fundamental basis for a system of thought</term> of his philosophical journey? And are our atoms not also a "non-given"?

Thus, one might find the reason for this sharpness in the fact that, where two directions do not entirely coincide, every point of contact is simultaneously a point of divergence that makes the deviation stand out all the more sharply; among relatives, the opportunity for conflict is often greatest. In truth, those points of contact I acknowledge are the very points from which the most essential deviations begin, so that, in the end, one will not find the kinship between our respective **worldviews** <term>Weltanschauungsweisen: comprehensive philosophical frameworks or "ways of looking at the world"</term> to be too great.

<summary>Fechner explains that his harsh critique of the philosopher Herbart arises from their close proximity; while both share an "atomistic" starting point, their fundamental conclusions diverge sharply, creating a "narcissism of small differences."</summary>

->**Preface.**<-

Meanwhile, because what we call our **philosophical atomism** <term>philosophische Atomistik: a theory that reality is composed of indivisible units, which the author likely treats as having both physical and soulful properties</term>—be it in principle or in substance—could all too easily be confused with **Herbart's monadology** <note>Johann Friedrich Herbart's theory that the world is composed of "Reals," simple and unchangeable substances.</note>, especially since it also encounters the latter in the name of "simple beings," I have sought to briefly summarize both those points of contact that it actually has in common with it, as well as the points of deviation from it, in a clear compilation of the main elements of both worldviews (in Chapter V of the second section). In doing so, regarding the more detailed justification of my judgment on Herbartian **metaphysics** (which is the only matter of concern in the questions discussed here), I take the liberty of referring also to a treatise specifically dedicated to this subject in *Fichte's Journal* <note>original: "Fichte's Zeits." refers to the *Zeitschrift für Philosophie und philosophische Kritik*, a leading 19th-century philosophical journal edited by I. H. Fichte.</note>, New Series, Volume XXIII, Issue 1.

However, whether the direction that I represent in this writing in opposition to the aforementioned schools—and which I have attempted to characterize from its formal perspectives in several places throughout this work—is indeed the correct one, is certainly as much a matter for debate as those schools debate among themselves; and this writing itself may now serve to represent that position.

As I have sought in this preface to indicate in advance the direction, character, and content of the entire work across both its sections in the most general manner, it is in the nature of the case that some of this will be revisited in the specific introductions and observations of these sections; may it therefore be excused if, in this regard, some general viewpoints are anticipated here and appear later as repetitions.

# I. Introduction.

The most general fundamental concepts, principles, and methods of mathematics and the natural sciences undoubtedly fall within the territory of philosophy, even if they occupy a different position and carry a different weight there than here; for that which is highest and ultimate for the former is merely something subordinate and derived for the latter. Now, if philosophy disdains a derivation of these foundations of the <term>exact sciences</term> <note>Fechner refers here to disciplines like physics and chemistry that rely on precise measurement and mathematical laws.</note> by a regressive path from the sciences themselves, but rather counts it among its tasks to lay foundations for them from a general point of view "from above," or to control and master the currently prevailing ones, then the exact sciences must be allowed to demand that these foundations be handed over by philosophy—if not already in fruitful application, then at least with the possibility of it—or at the very least be left alone. Furthermore, the methods of the latter to find the "True" must not enter into contradiction, nor remain disconnected from those methods which allow the sciences to find the "Real." Every expansion, improvement, simplification, deepening, or even just a higher linkage and grounding of methods that serve this purpose will be accepted by them with gratitude. Conversely, philosophy may demand that the methods and conclusions of exact science do not conflict with higher ideal and practical interests. Regarding these general principles, there should be no serious dispute in themselves, but only regarding their application.

However, such an application is exactly what is at stake in the dispute between the **dynamic** and **atomistic** views of the fundamental constitution of bodies. The physicist claims to need the atomistic view for his purposes; the philosopher denies him the recognition of this necessity, and claims for his part the impossibility of satisfying higher, more general interests with the atomistic view. As far as the latter is concerned, it is undoubtedly the

<vocab>fundamental concepts, principles, methods, mathematics, natural sciences, philosophy, dynamic, atomistic, fundamental constitution</vocab>

<summary>Fechner introduces the philosophical tensions between the "dynamic" view of continuous matter and the "atomistic" view of discrete particles, arguing that philosophy and science must find a harmonious foundation.</summary>
<keywords>philosophy of science, atomism, dynamism, exact sciences, scientific method, metaphysics of matter</keywords>

...it is not to be held against the physicist if, amidst the conflict of philosophical systems—some of which approach atomism even in the spiritual realm or "behind the curtains of the world" through **monads** <note>In philosophy, monads are indivisible, simple units of existence; Fechner is likely referencing Leibniz's theory.</note> and simple real beings—he does not rely on this latter philosophical claim alone. Instead, seeing the clear necessity of its use, at least in the physical realm, he persists in it until philosophy has actually made such use dispensable. Until then, he is less inclined to see an "empty play of ideas" in a view that guides him better than any other as a link from reality to reality. Otherwise, he might be called as foolish as the dog in the fable who, when faced with the question of whether the ideal shadow or mirror-image in the river or the piece he holds in his mouth was the true meat, refused to hold onto what he was actually holding and what he knew truly nourished him—even if the teeth with which he holds it are merely a "dry, atomistic being," while the river that demands it as a sacrifice surges and flows beautifully, constantly changing its images.

It is the intention of what follows to explain more closely from this perspective what currently binds the physicist to the **atomistic view**, given the present state of his science. Furthermore, setting aside these binding points of view, I wish to show what can make this view acceptable to anyone who does not track the connection and core of things with fixed presuppositions. For contemporary philosophy, of course, the negative view—that no atoms exist—is its firmest presupposition; it is, so to speak, the hollow space around which its otherwise diverging walls still join together harmoniously to form a vault. But who would not admit that contemporary philosophy is in its "evening" and demands a tomorrow and a morning? Now, hope cannot lie in what it already possesses, but in what it rejects. Among many other things to which I would like to attach hope, it rejects atomism—something which, as I intend to show, belongs to the essential material and formal structure of that very science which today, above all others, proves its vitality through its **active power**, its freshness through its progress, and its future through its growing success. And however raw and coarse its atomistic nature may still appear when developed in opposition to the highly potent **unity principles** <note>Refers to the overarching spiritual or rational concepts favored by German Idealism (like Hegel or Schelling) to explain the world as a whole.</note> of the prevailing philosophy, it may be with this as it is with the active...

He suggests that while modern philosophy rejects atoms, this very rejection signals a need for a new philosophical "morning" that embraces scientific reality.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introductory argument, specifically following the comparison between the physicist’s practical approach and philosophical abstractions. It picks up mid-sentence, completing a historical-cultural metaphor regarding the "Germanic tribes" as an analogy for the rise of scientific atomism against the established "empire" of traditional philosophy.</meta>

# I. Introduction.

...[like the] ancient **Germanic tribes**, which, though long held back, finally broke into the aging world-dominion of Rome with their raw hordes, destroying it and rejuvenating it, while at the same time raising themselves to a higher and more universal culture.

To be sure, the current understanding and treatment of **atomism** <note>The theory that all matter is made of indivisible particles; Fechner is defending this scientific view against philosophical critics.</note> by natural science, and its entire development, is in many respects still crude; yet the current rejection of it by philosophy and its entire stance toward nature seems even cruder in every respect. For philosophy descends into nature from above, like a bear into a beehive. He praises the cohesive honey; but that the individual tiny creatures gathered it and have a right to it is far from his mind, and by seizing it with a single grasp, he believes that this is his own way of preparing it. Against the bees, however, he rages fiercely and overturns the entire structure. In contrast, atomistic natural science behaves like the **beekeeper** <note>original: *Zeidler*, a traditional forest beekeeper who harvested honey from wild or semi-wild bees.</note>, who well knows that the entire unified structure is the work of countless individuals under the rule of a quietly guiding law; he tends them, follows their flight, and makes their weaving and their honeycombs the object of his constant and attentive observation. Now then, neither the bear nor the beekeeper will convert the other to his way of treating the swarm and the hive; it is rather a fight to the death. And so, I do not expect to be able to convert the currently dominant philosophy to the cultivation of atomism with the following pages; yet precisely because it is a struggle—because natural science and **natural philosophy** <note>*Naturphilosophie*, a branch of philosophy popular in the 19th century that sought to understand nature through speculative thought rather than just empirical data.</note> can never be indifferent to one another, but must be enemies if they cannot be friends—it might not be without interest even for the philosopher to find the main arguments of the opposing standpoint presented somewhat more precisely than is usually done. For to contest them, or even to judge them, one must first know them. Atomism can no longer be ignored or swept away with scolding. It has become a **factual power**. Now, while the most general reasons of the physicist for atomism are out in the open, the sharpest ones are not. Against the former, generalities are easily countered; the latter, however, demand a sharp engagement, though they may perhaps only encounter the same generalities in response that were already used against the former.

<sig>1*</sig>

**Vocabulary:** **Germanic culture**, **Atomism**, **Natural Science**, **Natural Philosophy**, **Beekeeper**, **Beehive**, **Factual Power**

<meta>This page continues the introduction, shifting from the metaphor of the bear and the beekeeper to the social and intellectual divide between philosophers and physicists regarding atomism. It addresses the public prejudice that equates scientific atomism with atheism.</meta>

# I. Introduction.

Many people also observe the conflict from the outside and would like to form a judgment on the state of the matter, which touches upon the entire context of science because it concerns the fundamental interconnectedness of things. Or rather, they have already formed one; but from where? Likely only from the descriptions of philosophers who treat **atomism** <note>The theory that matter consists of tiny, indivisible particles.</note> as if it were still the atomism of **Leucippus, Democritus, and Epicurus** <note>Ancient Greek philosophers from the 5th to 3rd centuries BCE who first proposed that the world is made of atoms and empty space.</note>, or who even dismiss it without discussing it, condemning it as heresy without knowing or hearing it, as if it were more a matter of <term>anti-philosophy</term> <note>A term used by Fechner to describe views that philosophers reject as being contrary to "true" philosophical reasoning or spiritualism.</note> than philosophy; and indeed it is, though only in contrast to contemporary philosophy. 

In contrast, physicists have always been more concerned with utilizing and refining atomism than with presenting it to the masses or defending it against the attacks of philosophers. These attacks seemed to matter very little to the physicists, since the "philosophically annihilated" atomism continued to bear its physical fruits in the meantime; it seemed self-evident that a tree that bears fruit must also have roots. However, the philosophical condemnations have been so much more accessible to the general public than the practical scientific uses that an almost universal prejudice against atomism has arisen; indeed, to many, being an **atomist** and an **atheist** now seems almost the same thing.

And so, what follows may perhaps contribute something to steering the general judgment of atomism into a more correct path. This will be done by revealing the foundations upon which it stands firm, instead of the foundations from which its opponents present it only to reject it. These are the foundations of nature or—what amounts to the same thing—of a successfully progressing natural science, proven by its achievements. One will not find a detailed presentation of atomism itself here, but rather of the most important points that can and must be decisive for anyone who does not allow the "rule of concepts" to extend further than the facts themselves allow. *)

---

*) A brief summary of what may be considered the main essence of physical atomism according to its most prominent representatives—and what is always meant here when I speak of atomism—is provided in Chapter 7; for now, however, the main goal is simply to focus on its primary and fundamental element: the <term>discontinuity of matter</term> <note>The idea that matter is not a solid, continuous block but is made of separate, distinct particles with space between them.</note>. All other points are strictly and inextricably linked to this one.

<summary>Fechner argues that the public's negative view of atomism stems from philosophical misrepresentations rather than scientific reality. He intends to defend atomism by focusing on its core principle: the discontinuity of matter.</summary>
<keywords>atomism, philosophy vs science, atheism, Leucippus, Democritus, Epicurus, discontinuity of matter, natural science</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introduction's defense of atomism against philosophical skepticism. The previous page established that while philosophers have dismissed atomism as "heresy," physicists have quietly used its "fruit" to advance science. Here, the author proposes to prove that atomism is not only scientifically necessary but also aesthetically and spiritually superior to its rivals.</meta>

<page-num>5</page-num>
# I. Introduction. 5

In short, we will seek to show that **atomism** <note>The scientific theory that matter is composed of discrete, individual particles rather than being a continuous substance.</note> is correct because it is necessary for the science of facts, and only that which is factually correct can be necessary for the science of facts. Furthermore, we will seek to show that the atomistic view of things is also more edifying and beautiful than the **dynamic view** <note>The "dynamic" view, popular in 19th-century German philosophy (Naturphilosophie), argued that matter was composed of continuous forces like attraction and repulsion rather than particles.</note>, if one only stops viewing it in the distorting mirror in which the philosopher shows us its image and stops believing his fabulous descriptions of it. For this, I offer a fable that hits closer to the mark. Atomism, I say, is the poor **Cinderella** <note>original: "Aschenbrödel."</note>, mocked and scolded by her sisters who think themselves more beautiful and wise—and who therefore bicker among themselves—the very sisters who should have cherished and raised her. But after they have long made the world believe the child is only good for rooting around in the ashes, and after she herself has long sought no other place but to work and toil while the others primped before the mirror, it is finally revealed, when it comes to the meeting and the dance, that she is not only the best, but also the most beautiful. What I say here, I intend to prove, not by praising her face and gait, but simply by showing her. For nothing more is needed.

Perhaps one will find that the following presentation is, from the outset, too intent on supporting the view it represents through details, and on refuting the opposite through the same; although one will also find that these details are merely fragments of a unity that remains unbroken, even if it presents itself in pieces; and that after the individual details, the general principle also receives its due. It is not without reason, however, that I place **specific observations** directly in the foreground. **Heeren** <note>Arnold Hermann Ludwig Heeren (1760–1842), a German historian who pioneered the study of ancient economics and trade.</note> once said somewhere: a **pinch of pepper** <note>original: "Messerspitze Pfeffer." Literally a "knife-tip of pepper."</note>, found in the remotest village, is enough to prove trade with **India**, because pepper can only come from there. Indeed, one could argue with general reasons for as long as one liked about whether trade with India had taken place, yet that pinch of pepper would prove more than all general reasons combined. Our opponents will, of course, say that the question must rather be decided from the *concept* of India, of trade, and of pepper. But I still believe the pinch of pepper proves more. And so it was our task to find such pinches of pepper in relation to our question; but in the end, we will find much more than—

<margin>
Bravo!
Scherf. <note>Likely a handwritten signature or approval from a previous reader or the printer, Scherf.</note>

# I. Introduction.

...knife-points <note>original: "Messerspitzen." Likely referring to the tools used to sort the grains in the Cinderella metaphor begun on the previous page.</note>, and along with the grains, find also the connection of the grains, the shrub, and finally all of India; and we do not want to and shall not forget that the connection also belongs to the matter.

<margin><unclear>it is</unclear> <unclear>20?</unclear> <unclear>oli?</unclear></margin>
Fundamentally, moreover, I believe that a general view can only ever be proven or refuted through **details**; for even if it intends and wishes to justify itself as a derivation from an even more general view, this in turn can only be confirmed or refuted through a more extensive examination of the details that fall under it—otherwise, it is established arbitrarily. Instead of rejecting the testing of a general view through details, one must demand it through the **totality** of those details. How is this possible? one asks; the totality of details is infinite. But one can gather infinitely many threads into a few nodes, and infer from one thread infinitely many others like it; and if it does not succeed one way or the other, there remains a gap in the reasoning; there remains uncertainty and arbitrariness in the generalization. **Modern philosophy** <note>The author likely refers to German Idealism (like Hegel or Schelling), which often prioritized abstract logic over empirical data.</note>, of course, does not grasp it this way, or at least does not proceed accordingly; but **physics** does. This is why one philosophical system after another collapses due to the instability and arbitrariness of its foundations, while physics builds itself ever higher on ever firmer foundations. Much could be said about this, but this is not the place and would only be shouting into the wind.

There is something else one might find missing in what follows, and I have so little doubt that everything found missing in this presentation will be framed as a flaw in the matter itself, that I must—at the risk of tiring the reader—lengthen the introduction a bit more to show why what one might find missing here is not to be sought here in the first place.

Only the physicist's reasons for his cause are presented here, while setting aside the general attacks of the opponents, yet without any attempt at a detailed refutation of them. Now, such a refutation will be superfluous in itself if we succeed at all in positively demonstrating the justification of **atomism** <term>Atomism: The scientific theory that matter consists of discrete, individual particles (atoms) rather than being a continuous, undivided mass.</term>; the denials directed against it will then fall away of their own accord. However, a refutation would also be unfeasible here, because a refutation of arguments based on a deeper philosophical context cannot itself happen otherwise than by means of a refutation of entire philosophical systems

# I. Introduction.

...might philosophize <note>Completing the word "philosophi..." from the end of page 6.</note>, which is a different and broader task than we can set for ourselves here, and which is moreover completely spared us through the mutual refutations of the philosophers.

To speak frankly, against which philosophical conception should a defense of **atomism** <term>Atomistik: The theory that nature is composed of discrete, indivisible units (atoms) rather than being a continuous, undivided substance.</term> even turn? For what form of the **philosophy of nature** <term>Naturphilosophie: A movement in German philosophy (led by figures like Schelling) that sought to understand nature as a unified, organic whole through intuition and logic rather than just empirical experiments.</term> or **metaphysics** holds such validity among philosophers themselves today that a compact attack could be directed against it? There is no compact resistance anywhere, except in the assertion of the **continuity of matter**, but not in its justification. Should I turn against **Kant**, against **Schelling**, against **Hegel**, against **Herbart**, or against any of their followers or offshoots? But who today still builds on Kant except to push aside, reject, or trample upon that foundation while moving beyond it? Schelling has himself moved beyond his own philosophy of nature; Hegel’s philosophy of nature has been declared his weakest work by his own followers; of the offshoots of Hegel’s school, none acknowledges the other; and the entire Hegelian and Herbartian schools mutually disregard one another. Where is a solid opponent to be found for the physicist? And should he really find support here? In a shaking building, every pillar calls out: "Hold onto me!"; but he prefers to let them smash each other and walks out under a larger roof.

Indisputably, the philosopher is better off in this regard than the physicist. In atomistic natural science, as it has become consolidated, the philosopher has a definite, solid opponent, and all attacks can concentrate on this one; the physicist can strike one opponent, but what does it help him? He has done as much as nothing. In this respect, natural science is rather like a **sturdy bear**, and the dynamic theories <note>Referring to "Dynamism," a philosophical view that explains matter through forces rather than particles.</note> are like **bees** fluttering in the air, all flying apart and each building a different cell, yet all swarming against the one opponent. His struggle against the individuals is in vain; but their entire honey, I think, he will nonetheless keep.

Allow me one more somewhat strange but fitting image. If one were to put all natural scientists and all philosophers together into a large funnel, they would soon separate through an opposite movement. The natural scientists would gradually gather at the entrance of the narrow tube—

Regarding what can be said from a very general standpoint against the way forces are understood in relation to matter—an understanding that underlies the anti-atomistic views of modern philosophy up to a certain point—this is indeed discussed further on (in Chapter 8). However, this way of thinking takes different turns with different philosophers, where one is always disputing the other.\*) Given the various ways these views are rooted in more general perspectives (ways that can never be brought to actual clarity), and given the impossibility of weighing each individual view without returning to those general perspectives—and finally, the difficulty of actually taking into account all the relevant philosophers—the aforementioned difficulty of a detailed opposition to anti-atomistic philosophy remains. In the end, a purely negative opposition would have yielded so little positive fruit that it was necessary to move away from leading our cause in that manner.

# I. Introduction.

...addressing the very foundations <note>The German text resumes with the end of a phrase describing the "foundations" or "premises" of philosophical arguments.</note> upon which they rest; to do so would be to already half-acknowledge those foundations and thereby give up our cause as half-lost from the start. Instead, the question and the matter stand as follows: rather than showing consideration for views and premises that are still disputed among themselves—as philosophical ones in fact are—is it possible to show that **atomism** <term>Atomism: the theory that all matter is composed of tiny, discrete particles rather than being a continuous substance</term> itself stands firm by looking at a connection of undisputed, established facts? If so, then it is proven that the philosophical foundations which cannot coexist with atomism cannot themselves stand, and all those philosophical modes of contemplation that unanimously condemn it hereby condemn themselves. This is a more general interest that can be linked to these observations: they intend to show by an example—for it is indeed only one—that philosophy, with its way of constructing and mastering things from the conceptual level before having schooled its concepts through the things themselves, is not sufficient for the task.

At the same time, however, it can show that the very reproach raised here against philosophy—that it constructs things according to concepts that are not themselves constructed according to the things—is not "plucked from thin air," as philosophers themselves so like to portray it when they dismiss such charges as vain misunderstanding or slander. Perhaps nothing is better suited than the handling of the **atom question** to bring the true state of affairs in this regard to light.

If, here and in the following, I simply contrast "philosophers" and "physicists" <note>In the mid-19th century, "physicist" (Physiker) often referred broadly to any natural scientist using physical methods, including chemists and physiologists.</note> with one another, and thus simultaneously contrast myself with the former—among whom I might well count myself on another occasion—I do so by referring to their respective main directions. I say this because I am aware of no modern professional philosopher who is not a sworn opponent of **atomism**; on the other hand, I certainly know that many physicists, namely those in the broader sense, also contradict it, and some leave the decision undecided. Yet, as far as I am aware, by far the majority of physicists in the narrower sense adhere to atomism, and in any case, all those who have been deeply involved in investigations where atomism becomes a **vital question** have declared themselves unanimously in its favor.

To prove how much atomism now has the upper hand even among physicists in the broader sense, I will incidentally mention the following. Ludwig in Zurich <note>Carl Ludwig (1816–1895), a highly influential German physiologist.</note> entitles the first section of his—as yet unfinished—Physiology: "Physiology of the Atoms"; and A. Fick <note>Adolf Fick (1829–1901), a physicist and physiologist famous for his laws of diffusion.</note> says in a...

<header>I. Introduction</header>

...review <note>The German text begins with "sprechung," the end of the word "Besprechung" (review/discussion), referring to a critique of this text's section.</note> of this section in the *General Monthly Magazine* <note>original: "Allgemeinen Monatsschrift"</note>, July 1853, p. 603, regarding this matter: "It might appear bold to someone more distant from natural science <note>original: "Naturforschung"</note> that the word **Atom** is used so openly in headings, as in those circles there still haunt **philosophical notions** <term>Philosopheme: traditional philosophical arguments or doctrines, often used here dismissively to mean "armchair philosophy"</term> that look down contemptuously upon **atomism** as a crude, materialistic, and clumsy conception of nature. In the scientific world, thank God, these figments of the imagination are so far dismissed that one does not first need to spend a long time proving that it would be just as absurd to imagine space continuously filled with matter as it would be to claim that two times two equals five." Now, I do believe that atomism, given the current state of its development and considering all the support it has gained so far, actually has to express itself far more modestly; however, one sees in any case that if many philosophers believe they may regard atomism merely as an absurdity, the opposing side does not lack reason to encounter the philosophers' conception in the same way.

<margin>//</margin>
This itself, however, could be held against us from the outset as a primary objection: that the physicists themselves are by no means in total agreement regarding the validity of atomism—there is a "pro and con" here—whereas the philosophers, however much they may disagree on other points, are in perfect agreement regarding the invalidity of atomism; here, only a "con" applies.

<margin>x</margin>
It is true; yet the apparent advantage of the philosophers, which we do not wish to entirely overlook in this regard, might be more than compensated for by the fact that those physicists who adhere to atomism demand it from far more unanimous points of view than the philosophers who reject it. For if we set aside quite general formal points of view regarding the contradiction—wherein I do not deny a certain agreement among the dominant philosophy (excluding, of course, **Herbart** <note>Johann Friedrich Herbart (1776–1841), a philosopher who, unlike many of his contemporaries, developed a theory of "reals" that was more compatible with scientific atomism.</note> and his school, which also wishes to be counted)—and if it becomes a matter of actually engaging with the subject, it can undeniably only be the derivation, position, and relationship of those concepts of **space, force, and matter** upon which the rejection is based. Regarding these, however—as already asserted above—despite all the commonality of their opposition to the physical conception, there is not the slightest agreement among the philosophers themselves. This is perhaps (as at least the atomist can believe) for the very reason that the unanimous rejection of atomism makes an agreement in this regard impossible; for just as paths lead from all sides toward the same center of unification, so too do they lead away from it in every direction.

Moreover, the following considerations might perhaps clarify the reason for the partial discordance among physicists regarding **atomism**—which must indeed be acknowledged—in a way that is not unfavorable to the latter. It must be admitted that **atomism** is nothing that falls directly within experience. Indeed, philosophers and physicists seem to switch roles in this regard: the physicists, who usually prefer to stick to direct observation <note>original: "Augenschein," meaning what is visible or apparent to the senses</note>, here assume something against all appearances; the philosophers, on the other hand, who usually do not cling to appearances, stubbornly defend them and even—which might cause amazement—use them as an argument against the physicist. But undoubtedly, the physicists, precisely because of that tendency to persist in what is apparent and tangible, would not contradict appearances without deeper reasons; that they can also do so successfully in other cases is proven by the <term>Copernican world system</term> <note>The model where the Earth revolves around the Sun, which contradicts the visual appearance of the Sun moving across the sky.</note> and the <term>undulation theory</term> <note>The "wave theory" of light, which suggests light behaves as a wave rather than a simple stream of particles.</note> of light. Meanwhile, that tendency still exists, and the aversion of many physicists to go beyond appearances—which form the basis of all physical investigations—without absolutely compelling motives is great enough to frequently give philosophers weapons of censure against them. These weapons, naturally, should not be used by philosophy in the opposite sense in this case, as happens when it demands that they should see no further with the mental eye than with the physical eye. Now, while the general motives inviting <term>atomism</term> <note>The theory that matter consists of discrete, tiny particles.</note> are open to all physicists, they are unable to break that resistance—so healthy in itself—which is deeply rooted in the physicist's nature. These motives also conflict with the influences of the prevailing philosophy, which the physicist cannot escape—and would like to escape even less if he felt it were more helpful—and with the disrepute into which **atomism** has been brought by the philosophers. <header>I. Introduction</header>

...tive <note>The text begins mid-word, likely concluding "perspective."</note>, certainly not quite so openly; and on this point, the following is to be said in more detail.

I need an illustrative image. A forest appears from a distance as a uniform mass. The collective effect of the trunks and leaves certainly manifests in very obvious phenomena, such as color, waving in the wind, and rustling; but it is fairly indifferent for the interpretation of these phenomena whether one wishes to view the forest as a **continuum** <note>A continuous, unbroken whole without gaps.</note> or not; or rather, since it actually appears as a **continuum**, the view that it is such has an obvious advantage. Even assuming one noticed a hint of the individual trunks in a "streaky" quality, or one saw animals enter the forest and disappear, that would be no different than perceiving the cleavage planes of crystals or seeing bodies disappear in liquids through dissolution; one is therefore not yet forced to assume that the hint of separation becomes a real separation upon closer inspection, where one thing merely penetrates between another. One is thus also not forced to give up the belief in **direct appearance** <note>original: "Augenschein," referring to what is immediately visible to the eyes.</note>, which still lets the forest, like the crystal and the liquid, appear directly as a **continuum**; and above all, the physicist would be wary of doing so. 

But now, the physicist might, through refined means of observation, manage to distinguish the pulses created in the air by the beating of the discontinuous leaves, or the wave patterns formed by the air passing between the discontinuous trunks; he might then be forced, in order to explain these, to actually resolve the seemingly continuous green mass of foliage into individual trembling parts, and the wood mass into individual trunks. This fine investigation could yield a quite conclusive result; yet it would not be everyone's concern, and many who do not occupy themselves with this field of refined investigation—perhaps even unable to follow them—would find it simpler and more natural to stop at the **direct appearance**, which otherwise sufficed so well for explanation. 

<margin>||</margin> That is roughly how it is with **atomism** <term>Atomism: the theory that all matter is composed of tiny, discrete particles rather than being a continuous whole.</term>.

Indeed, up to certain limits, the difference between the atomistic and the opposite view does not assert itself, or at least not decisively, in the treatment of physical problems. The calculation of the attraction of two distant **masses of bodies** to one another by the summation of the effects of their...

# I. Introduction.

...smallest particles gives the same result, whether one wishes to refer the effects to continuous or discontinuous particles; indeed, in the first case, one even spares oneself an intermediate consideration which is necessary in the second case to justify the application of the **method of integration** <note>A mathematical process in calculus used to find the total sum of infinitesimal parts. The author suggests that if matter is already seen as a continuous whole, this step is simpler than if one has to "sum up" individual atoms.</note>. Thus, there can be no talk here of an enticing external advantage in favor of **atomism** for the physicist or mathematician; for he prefers to reach the goal by the shortest path. Also, most problems concerning the propagation of water, air, and light waves can be treated equally well according to both views up to certain limits—though admittedly only up to those limits. In all phenomena in general where particles act in mass—that is, many particles acting in connection—the "mass-like" or coarser nature of the phenomena depends on the connection of forces and the summed effects of the particles in a way that allows for no decision between the two views. But in the finer, more specific determinations of phenomena, not only can a difference make itself felt, but it *must* make itself felt; only here can it be sought, and only from here can a certain—that is, mathematical—decision between the two views be found for the **exact physicist** <note>A scientist who relies on precise measurement and mathematical proofs rather than purely philosophical speculation.</note>. Now, however, there are relatively few physicists who immerse themselves in those fields of both the finest and most difficult investigations—indeed, perhaps not too many who can fully account for the progress of these studies. For these individuals, the most important, if not the only, motives that urge the exact physicist as such toward atomism are missing.

<margin>for the Ph. d.</margin> <note>original: "f. d. Ph. d."; likely an abbreviation for "für den Physiker..." (for the physicist) or "Physikalische Darstellung" (physical presentation).</note>
One might think that the fine phenomena in question here, being "fine," are therefore also ambiguous or subject to ambiguous interpretation. On the contrary, they belong to the clearest phenomena of physics, with which large parts of the science are concerned. The "fine" aspect here is only the **elementary** part from which those phenomena are to be derived. To what extent they are binding for the exact physicist will best be made clear by the following explanation.

One more thing beforehand: The question of whether matter is characterized by **continuity** or **discontinuity** can, of course, only be treated and decided by the physicist on the general foundations of his science. That which is doubtful must rest upon that which is already conceded.

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 13 regarding the physicist's perspective on the structure of matter. Having established that the mathematical choice between continuous and discrete models depends on the specific scale of the phenomena, the author now explores the necessity of a physical medium—the Aether—and how the concept of motion requires a substance to move.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>

# I. Introduction.

For the physicist, this includes the requirement that light and heat are not grasped merely as **pure acts** <note>original: *actus puri*; a philosophical term for an activity or operation that occurs without a physical substance performing it.</note>, but that a **substrate** <term>substrate: an underlying physical substance that serves as the foundation for processes or activities</term> is provided in the **Aether** <note>In 19th-century physics, the Aether was a hypothetical substance thought to fill all space, acting as the medium through which light and heat waves traveled.</note>. The movements of this Aether, by extending into our eyes, bring about the sensation of light; however, one may still doubt whether this medium is itself continuous or discontinuous. Viewing light and heat radiation merely as the actions of **ponderable bodies** <note>Physical matter that has measurable weight or mass.</note> **at a distance** <note>original: *in distans*; the concept of a force acting across a void without a physical medium connecting the two points.</note>—similar to gravity, which is often contrasted with light as its polar opposite—is unacceptable to the physicist. This is not only for other reasons, such as the fact that light (unlike <margin>⌞</margin>gravity) can be stopped or reflected by intervening bodies, but primarily because light progresses gradually through space. This progression can, in any case, only be conceived of as a form of motion. The physicist sees a real motion here and demands, and indeed finds, the substrate for it in the Aether. The existence of such a substrate for light phenomena, and thus a real motion in the physicist's sense, can be disputed by the philosopher. However, if the philosopher does so, he would be forced to acknowledge empty space on a large scale between the various parts of the universe, even though he refuses to recognize empty space on a small scale between the parts of a single body. Thus, he would contradict the idea of space being continuously filled in at least one respect. Regardless of how the philosopher positions himself, since we cannot address every individual viewpoint and no single consensus exists, we are now concerned only with the physicist’s arguments for the **discontinuity of matter**. Within the framework of his science, these arguments must hold true. We readily admit, however, that they cannot hold for someone who questions the entire foundation of physics itself, to which the assumption of a substrate for every motion and activity essentially belongs.

To properly distinguish the viewpoints of this dispute and to focus more precisely on a single point, we should note that **atomism** <note>The theory that matter is not a solid continuous mass, but is made of discrete, individual particles.</note> does not depend on this last assumption alone. Even if the physicist could bring himself to view the propagation and other phenomena of light, heat, and so forth as **pure acts** without a substrate, or merely as the acts of ponderable bodies **at a distance**, the core question of whether atomism is true or not would remain entirely unchanged, and the decision...
