<page-num>15</page-num>

# I. Introduction.

...would remain as before. For it would then no longer be a question of whether the movements (**oscillations**) or the **acts** related to space, with which the physicist finds himself compelled to construct his **theory of light** <note>original: *Lichtlehre*</note>, are performed by discontinuous **aether atoms** or occur in a continuous **aether**; rather, it would be a question of whether they, as substrate-less **acts**, are discontinuous or continuous in space. That is, whether gaps exist in space where none of these acts occur, or whether they extend *continuously* <note>original: *in continuo*</note> through space, since light phenomena must in any case be conceived as occurring in space and the acts must therefore be related to it—that is, they must be localized within it. Thus, regarding **imponderables** <note>Weightless substances or forces, such as light, heat, or electricity, which were once thought to be fluid-like but lacked mass.</note>, it would only be a question of the atomism of the acts or movements instead of the substrate, which leaves the essence of the question unchanged; and regarding **ponderables** <note>Matter that possesses measurable weight or mass.</note>, everything would remain unchanged anyway.

To explain the meaning of this alternative more precisely, one need only think of the discontinuous movements of **celestial bodies** <note>original: *Weltkörper*; referring to planets, stars, and other astronomical objects.</note> relative to one another in space. Even if we were to think of such movements as **pure acts** <note>original: *actus puri*; acts performed without a physical medium or underlying substance.</note>, as successive **developments of activity** in adjacent locations in space without regard for matter, or even if we wanted to make the appearance of the massive celestial bodies themselves dependent on such a development of activity which merely changes its location in space—a view that should not deviate too far from the perspective of many **dynamicists** <term>dynamicists: philosophers or scientists who believe that matter is not made of solid particles, but is instead a manifestation of forces or energy</term>—they would nonetheless still have to be thought of as discontinuous in space. This behavior would have to be distinguished from the **continuity** inherent in the path of motion of each individual celestial body. In any case, one sees that movements or acts in space can be continuous or discontinuous; and it is not a trivial question whether given phenomena depend on acts of one kind or the other. It is a fallacy to believe that it is enough to be clear about the existence or non-existence of a **substrate** <term>substrate: an underlying physical substance that serves as the foundation for processes or activities</term> in space in order to also be clear about the continuity or non-continuity of the acts in space. Both questions are independent of each other.

Furthermore, if it can contribute anything toward finding a common ground in this dispute, we might remind ourselves that even for the physicist, the quality of the **light-substrate** itself is quite immaterial; he does not speak of it, he knows nothing of it; and even the existence of a substrate serves him merely as a support...

...clearer conception of movement as the formal core of a play of phenomena, forces, and laws. For him [the physicist], just as for the **dynamist** <note>A proponent of "Dynamism," a theory that explains matter through forces (like attraction and repulsion) rather than solid atoms.</note>, it is essential to concern himself only with the **acts** <note>original: *Acte*; here referring to the individual instances of movement or force.</note> and the law of those acts in his constructions. He speaks only of this, knows only of this, and links the phenomenon only to this. However, for this very reason, these acts must still maintain the same definite relationship to space for him, if light phenomena are to be linked and related to space with the same definiteness as before—regardless of whether the **substrate** <note>The underlying material or medium.</note>, which is in itself meaningless to him, is thought of or named however one wishes, or even not thought of at all. He may concede this point to the dynamist, even if he himself neither will nor can withdraw this support from his own conceptualization.

What does "substrate" mean anyway? If one wishes to delve into the discussions of a later (the eighth) chapter, one might find that the dispute over whether a substrate exists or not revolves more around a word than a fact. Yet the physicist’s use of this word is reasonably connected to the use of other words with which he is otherwise accustomed to designating facts.

Thus, even if in the following I present the question in the form framed by the physicist—whose reasoning I intend to represent—and accordingly attribute the **acts of movement** of a substrate in space, the **aether**, to light phenomena, without being able to characterize that substrate by anything other than its acts and their laws and the phenomena dependent on them; it may nonetheless be permitted for the philosopher—ignoring this substrate which perhaps seems entirely superfluous to him—to operate instead with **pure acts** <note>original: *reinen Actus*</note> and imagine that light involves only the **continuity** or **discontinuity** of these acts in space. However, the deeper dispute—whether acts are truly conceivable without an **agent** <note>original: *Agirendes*; the thing that acts.</note>, or movements without something being moved; whether a self-moving movement is sufficient for clarity and other requirements; or whether this dispute is actually more about different ways of expressing factual relationships than the relationships themselves—may be set aside for now.

<header>I. Introduction.</header>

I believe, however, that the relationship in the struggle between the **dynamic** and **atomistic** views of nature is the same as when two bulls, after clashing with full foreheads has yielded nothing, now turn one of their sharp-tipped horns against each other; it penetrates deeper; and even if the conflict is only partial, a wound on one side can much more easily become decisive than all the blunt thrusts of the whole body. I mean to say that the conflict between general views will usually be decided less easily by general reasons than by their principal sides measuring themselves against one another specifically. The <term>discreteness of matter</term> <note>The concept that matter is composed of separate, individual particles rather than being a continuous, solid whole.</note> is now the one horn, and the substrate of the <term>imponderables</term> <note>Weightless substances once thought to explain phenomena like heat, light, and electricity.</note> is the other, with which **atomism** confronts the dynamic view of nature; but now, it turns only the former against its opponent.

Unquestionably, the contrast between the atomistic and dynamic views can be grasped from various perspectives, pursued in various directions, and generalized as a contrast between the mechanical and dynamic views. However, the uncertainty that might lie in the juxtaposition of these views does not affect us, according to the explanation we give regarding our use of the words. What we understand here by physical <term>atomism</term> was noted in the footnote on page 4, and will be discussed in even greater detail in the seventh chapter. As a <term>pathognomonic sign</term> <note>A definitive characteristic or symptom.</note> of the dynamist, on the other hand, we consider here specifically only the denial of this atomism—that is, the denial of the subdivision of the apparent <term>continuum</term> of a body (crystal, water, air) into discrete masses or <term>centers of force</term> <note>original: *Kraftcentra*; points from which physical forces are exerted.</note>. It is not that the entire dynamic view, as it is usually understood, consists only of this single point; but a certain circle of other ideas is almost naturally linked to this one point, though it is not so firmly fixed that a completely rigid definition would be possible. The above definition, however, suffices for our purpose of having a simple name for the opponents with whom we must reach an understanding.

Regarding any claims that might go too far or be foreign to the task at hand, I believe I must emphasize that an explanation of the general relationships between the dynamic and atomistic views, such as might belong in a history of philosophy, lies entirely outside the intention of the following considerations. <header>II. First-Order Reasons for Atomism.</header>

...to attempt to decide the question of atoms <note>The "atom question" refers to the 19th-century debate over whether matter is infinitely divisible (dynamism) or composed of indivisible units (atomism).</note> in a way that would lead entirely into the vague and groundless; instead, by discussing the question of atoms from factual perspectives, a contribution shall be provided to the decision between the two views. Now to the matter at hand, that is, the physicist's reasons for his cause.

## II. First-Order Reasons for Atomism. <note>In this context, "first-order reasons" refers to primary, empirical evidence derived from the fundamental laws of physics.</note>

1. The **refraction** of light in physical bodies can be explained equally well by both the dynamic and atomistic views regarding the main phenomenon. Not only does refraction occur, but also, in the case of simple refraction, a constant ratio exists between the sine of incidence and the sine of refraction <note>This is known as Snell's Law.</note>; indeed, even the general phenomena of **double refraction** <note>Also called birefringence, where a ray of light is split into two rays when passing through certain materials like calcite.</note> fit equally well under both views. However, the main phenomenon of refraction carries with it a finer detail: the fact that the refracted white beam spreads out into a narrow fan of colors, because the refractivity of the various colored rays differs slightly from one another. For a long time, the most thorough mathematicians and physicists acknowledged that this **dispersion of colors** was entirely incompatible with the **undulation theory** <note>The Wave Theory of Light, which posits that light travels as waves through a medium.</note> of light; for a long period, this was the sole reason why the **emission theory** <note>The "Corpuscular Theory" championed by Isaac Newton, which suggested light was made of particles (corpuscles). By the time this was written, it had been largely superseded by wave theory.</note>—which is in every other respect so much more improbable and has recently been entirely abandoned for decisive reasons—was preferred over the undulation theory. Now, 
<margin>/</margin> however, the recent investigations of **Cauchy** <note>Augustin-Louis Cauchy (1789–1857), a French mathematician who provided a mathematical basis for the dispersion of light by assuming the aether was composed of discrete particles.</note> have shown that this incompatibility only exists insofar as one assumes that the light wave propagates through the **aether** as through a **continuum** <note>A continuous, unbroken substance with no gaps.</note>; on the contrary, the laws of color dispersion arise as a consequence of the fundamental view of the undulation theory if one posits the particles of the aether as **discrete** <note>Individual, separate points or particles.</note>; indeed, that the color dis-

<header>II. First-Order Reasons for Atomism.</header>
<sig>2*</sig>

...dispersion during refraction is then just as necessarily required as the refraction itself. Thus, the question of whether **atomism** <note>The belief that matter and the "ether" are composed of discrete, individual particles rather than being a continuous, infinitely divisible substance.</note> exists or not is a vital question for the **wave theory**, just as the question of whether the wave theory exists or not is a vital question for physics.

The main result of the calculation is this: that the dispersion of colors is not only explainable but also required, provided that the distance between the **ether particles** is large enough that it cannot be neglected when compared to the <term>wavelength</term> <note>The original German uses "Breite einer Lichtwelle" (width of a light wave).</note> of light (amounting to 167 to 266 ten-millionths of an English inch in air for violet to red, respectively). This also makes it clear that the distance between ether particles by no means extends into the completely unimaginable. Based on this, one could say, for example, that it must be greater than 1/1000th of the width of a light wave (of the dimensions mentioned above); if it were not at least that much, the calculation would no longer show any noticeable influence on the phenomena of refraction. — A somewhat more detailed explanation lies in the following: The *theory shows* that a different refraction of various color rays (that is, rays in which the particles complete a different number of oscillations in the same amount of time) can only depend on a different speed with which their waves propagate through the refracting medium. So long as one views the ether as either continuous, or neglects the distance between its particles compared to the width of a light wave—as was always done previously—this speed necessarily becomes the same for all color waves; however, this is no longer the case when one takes this distance and the mathematical terms introduced by it into account.

2. Another subtle light phenomenon, in which it is no longer merely the total mass-effect but the specific mode of movement of the particles that gains influence, is the **polarization of light**. The connection between the phenomena of polarized light and those of ordinary light can only be represented in the wave theory under the assumption that the ether particles lying in the path of a light ray do not perform longitudinal vibrations, but rather **transverse vibrations**; that is, vibrations whose direction is perpendicular to the direction of the light ray. In an ordinary ray of light, these vibrations occur in all possible directions, though always perpendicular to the ray; in a polarized ray, they occur in purely parallel directions, all perpendicular to the ray. Under this assumption, the subtlest and strangest, most diverse and complex phenomena of polarized light are explained in a completely natural, coherent way. From this point of view, **Fresnel** <note>Augustin-Jean Fresnel (1788–1827), a French physicist whose work on wave optics established that light consists of transverse waves.</note> was the first to correctly grasp the phenomena of polarization in the sense of the wave theory and to pursue them in detail. **Poisson**, one of the most famous and thorough French mathematicians,

<page-num>20</page-num>
<header>II. First-Order Reasons for Atomism.</header>

<note>...vibrations in the same amount of time), Poisson,</note> to whom modern mathematical physics owes a substantial part of its progress, nevertheless entered into a discussion with Fresnel regarding this matter. Starting from the view of the **continuity of matter**, which he had used as the basis for all his previous investigations, Poisson showed\*) that at some distance from the starting point of light, no **transversal vibrations** <note>Vibrations that move perpendicular to the direction the light ray is traveling, like waves on a plucked string.</note> could occur at all; he argued that regardless of their initial direction, they would necessarily turn more and more toward the direction of the ray's propagation itself during the course of travel. Against this, however, Fresnel called his attention to the fact\*\*) that, provided one assumes the **ether particles** are **discrete** <note>Meaning the particles are separate, individual units rather than a single, continuous "smear" of substance.</note>, Poisson’s objection no longer holds. Poisson himself recognized the validity of Fresnel’s argument so fully that he since changed his own fundamental view. All his subsequent investigations (on elastic bodies, **capillarity** <note>The way liquids flow through narrow spaces without the assistance of external forces.</note>, and heat) were conducted in the spirit of the atomistic principle. (He was only prevented by death from treating the theory of light in this same spirit), and he even redesigned the second edition of his *Mechanics* according to this view. One can see from this how much binding evidence—indeed, what a sense of necessity—must exist here for someone who knows how to track the connection between physical causes and effects in an exact manner.

That this was indeed a kind of necessity becomes all the more evident when one considers that Poisson could only achieve this transformation at the cost of the simplicity of the viewpoints upon which the calculations must be based. The application of the **integration method** <note>A mathematical tool in calculus used to find the total sum of infinitely small parts.</note> when summing the effects of the smallest parts now required a special justification\*\*\*), which one can dispense with when assuming the continuity of matter. Added to this is the confession that one now only obtains **approximations** through it, whereas, according to the "dynamic" view <note>The view that matter is a continuous force-field rather than made of atoms.</note>, one would obtain exact results.

Now, an opponent could easily say: but with this, you yourself admit the disadvantage of the atomistic view and the advantage of the dynamic—

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the transition of the physicist Poisson from a "dynamic" (continuous) view of matter to an "atomistic" (discrete) one, following a debate with Fresnel about the nature of light waves.</meta>

<page-num>21</page-num>
<header>II. First-Order Reasons for Atomism.</header>

...dynamic <note>The word fragment "mischen" at the start suggests the completion of "dynamischen" (dynamic) from the previous page.</note> view? The dynamic approach permits a simpler observation and provides a more precise result; what more could one ask for? In response, we recall an analogous case. The motion of a planet around the sun also permits a simpler observation, and its calculation provides a more precise result, if one does not worry about **disturbances** <note>Also known as "perturbations": the way other planets' gravity pulls a planet slightly off its main path.</note>. Without them, the orbit can be found in its pure form; with them, we obtain only approximations, and the entire calculation becomes laborious. Why then does one prefer the imprecise and laborious calculation regarding the disturbances over the precise and simple one without them? Because the disturbance is simply present in nature, and therefore must also be covered by the calculation, regardless of whether our convenience is disturbed by it, or whether a smooth result loses its smoothness. In short, because the simple calculation is not equal to the **complexity of the circumstances**, and its "more precise" results on paper actually reproduce the conditions of reality only imprecisely.

The very same case applies to the complexity introduced into calculations by **atomism** <note>The theory that matter is made of individual, separate particles rather than a continuous substance.</note>. Calculation based on the dynamic view gives more precise results on paper; calculation based on the atomistic view gives more precise results in reality. And if this were not the case, Poisson would certainly not have abandoned the one for the other.

In many questions, of course, the difference in greater accuracy does not become apparent; these questions are then also not to be used for deciding between the two views. But there are questions where the difference becomes so palpable that entire fields of phenomena stand or fall by the calculation, and such cases must above all be the deciding factor.

Let us add the passage itself in which Fresnel (referring to his detailed investigations conducted elsewhere) rejects Poisson's objection. It says the same thing we have said and will say even more often, only in different words: the dynamic view is able to explain phenomena up to certain limits, but not beyond them.

> "I will only repeat to you here what I have already had the honor of telling you several times: it is that the equations of motion for elastic fluids, in which you believe you must find all the types of vibration of which they are capable, are at most..." <note>original: "Je vous répéterai seulement ici ce que j'ai déjà eu l'honneur de vous dire plusieurs fois: c'est que les équations du mouvement des fluides élastiques, dans lesquelles vous croyez devoir trouver tous les genres de vibration dont ils sont susceptibles, ne sont au..."</note>

<page-num>22</page-num>
<header>II. First-Order Reasons for Atomism.</header>

<note>original: "...fond qu’une abstraction mathématique très-éloignée de la réalité; elles supposent ces fluides composés de petits élémens contigus et compressibles proportionnellement à la pression. Cette hypothèse représente bien leurs propriétés statiques, mais non leurs propriétés dynamiques; car, par exemple, on n’en déduirait pas le frottement; ce qui tient à ce qu’on suppose entre les molécules une contiguïté, qui n’existe pas.“</note>
"...at its core nothing but a mathematical abstraction very far removed from reality; they assume these fluids are composed of small elements that are touching and are compressible in proportion to the pressure. This hypothesis represents their static properties well, but not their dynamic properties; for example, one could not derive **friction** from it. This is because one assumes a <term>contiguity|The state of being in direct contact or an uninterrupted mass.</term> between molecules that does not exist." (Annals of Chemistry and Physics, Vol. 23, p. 120.)

3. The phenomena of <term>heat propagation|Wärmefortpflanzung: the movement of heat through space or matter.</term> through bodies and **heat radiation** are of very different types. In the former, heat creeps slowly through the body according to seemingly unique laws; in the latter, it travels with a speed comparable to that of light and according to similar laws. Yet both modes of propagation must necessarily be connected within the general laws of the theory of heat. Fourier <note>Joseph Fourier (1768–1830), a mathematician famous for his work on heat flow.</note> has shown that such a connection exists—that the laws of heat conduction naturally subordinate themselves to those of radiation—provided that one imagines weighable bodies as consisting of **discrete particles** that radiate heat to one another. This is not merely a theoretical exercise on paper; the theory reflects what is actually observed in experience. If one assumes that weighable bodies are <term>continua|Continuous masses without gaps or individual particles.</term>, then every attempt to bring the experimental facts of both phenomena into a scientific connection seems closed off.

<note>The following paragraph is printed in a smaller font size.</note>
As I see from Liebig and Kopp’s *Annual Report* (1851, p. 47), Wilhelmy also—in his work: "Attempt at a Mathematical-Physical Theory of Heat, Heidelberg 1851" (in which the temperature of bodies is set proportional to the <term>living force|lebendige Kraft: an older term for kinetic energy.</term> of the molecules in oscillatory motion)—traces the propagation of heat through conduction back to that through radiation, based on the atomistic view. In doing so, he finds that a body's conductivity depends on the duration of the oscillation of the conducted heat—and thus on the temperature—as has also been experimentally proven by Langberg (Poggendorff's Annals LXVI, 10) and more recently by Wiedemann.

4. Heat radiates most strongly in a direction perpendicular to the surface of a body, whereas in oblique directions, the radiation becomes weaker according to the law of the sine. This is a natural consequence of bodies being composed of layers of atoms; conversely, within the framework of the **continuity of matter**, no viable path for a physical derivation of this fact is available.

I owe the establishment of these four points less to an in-depth study of my own regarding the underlying circumstances—

<page-num>23</page-num>
<header>II. First-Order Reasons for Atomism.</header>

...knowledge <note>The text begins mid-word with "-nisse," likely from "Kenntnisse" (knowledge/insights).</note> as is fitting for a professional physicist—a rank to which I can no longer count myself—as from earlier conversations with my esteemed friend Prof. **Wilhelm Weber** <note>Wilhelm Weber (1804–1891) was a leading German physicist known for his work on electromagnetism and his collaboration with Gauss.</note>, to whom I submitted them once more for review after they were drafted; so that, with his approval, I can present them as "some of the most important pillars which <term>atomism|The theory that all matter is composed of tiny, indivisible particles separated by empty space.</term> has already offered to exact physical research." In the same proportion, however, as exact physical research is able to lean on atomism, atomism is conversely able to lean on the former.

Now I believe I must also add that Prof. Weber, in the letter he sent to me on this occasion, takes full account of the insufficiency—which we too must recognize and have recognized—that atomism presents in its currently still raw state under the influence of the most significant difficulties of mathematical development, should one demand it to be a perfected science already solving all the tasks whose solution it one day promises. Only this remains certain: that it actually already accomplishes more for the physicist and promises more than the opposing view; with which one must be content in a science that does not begin with perfection but strives toward it, and which one must hold as decisive when judging both views, until the opposing view is able to reverse this relationship of achievements and hopes.

In view of everything, one can say: Despite the fact that visual evidence seems to speak against the assumption of atoms, their existence is at least as well-founded as the <term>undulation theory|The wave theory of light, which suggests light travels in waves through a medium.</term> of light and the connection of the phenomena of heat themselves. We do not see the spaces between the atoms, but we do not even see them in an **eggshell**; only the mechanical passage of air proves them here. Just as surely as this passage allows us to conclude the existence of pores in the eggshell, so surely can we conclude from the colors in a **prism** and in a **polarization mirror** that there are even smaller gaps between the particles. This visible world is connected to the invisible by an unbreakable mathematical thread. If one now claims that it would only require a suitable substitution for the atomistic assumptions to explain the phenomena of color, polarization, and so on, then—

...is; this assertion is very easy; but one cannot grant it any weight as long as it has escaped the gravity of proof <note>The author likely plays on words here between "weight" (Gewicht) and the "gravity" or "heaviness" (Schwere) of providing proof.</note>. If this succeeds, the exact physicist, as such, will no longer have any interest in clinging to atomism. For the philosopher is mistaken if he thinks the physicist is "in love" with atoms for their own sake; he loves best that which provides him with the most utility. The philosopher prefers the principle with which he can achieve the most; the physicist does the same, and does so with equal right.

It is true that in physics, one can often substitute one mental model for another and still obtain the same result; however, points always arise where the possibility of such substitution ceases, and those are the decisive ones. Given the current state of affairs, one must believe that in points such as those mentioned above, we have gained such decisive ground regarding the theory of atoms. Now, it is futile to appeal to the vague possibility of further substitutions and to expect the physicists to find them because it is "not the business" of philosophers. The physicists have done their part precisely by bringing the matter to the point where the arbitrariness of substitutions ends and a decision takes hold. And since it is considered solely a matter of their field and expertise to judge how a further substitution should be made—placing the entire burden <note>original: "Onus" (Latin), meaning the burden of proof or responsibility.</note> of it upon them—one should also consider it solely a matter of their field and expertise to judge whether such a thing is possible at all.

Nevertheless, I do not doubt that the opponents' main rejection will lie partly in attacks on terminology—sometimes too narrow, sometimes too broad—regarding physics, philosophy, atomism, or the "dynamic view," as if it were a matter of classification <note>original: "Classification" (Latin/German).</note> or history rather than a specific factual question; and partly in the claim that physicists have perhaps not considered this or that possibility of approaching the matter, and that they ought to adopt an entirely different fundamental concept...

In fact, the <term>Wave Theory</term> has been treated by philosophers in exactly this way until now. But I am speaking now only of the necessity for it that exists for the physicist, which the philosopher must eventually acknowledge as long as he acknowledges physics itself—that is, as long as he must acknowledge its results.

One might easily raise the objection here: but instead of the <term>Wave Theory</term> itself, the <term>Emission Theory|Also known as the corpuscular theory, this was Isaac Newton's idea that light consisted of tiny particles or "corpuscles" shot out from a source.</term> held sway among physicists for a long time. Now the <term>Wave Theory</term> prevails. Where then is the infallibility of the physicist? It does not exist. The physicist claims no absolute knowledge. However, he believes he is learning more of what is certain, and the less certain he imagines himself to be, the more certain he becomes. Previously, the deciding factors between <term>Wave Theory</term> and <term>Emission Theory</term> were lacking; both could be substituted for one another in most respects. Now the decisive points have been found; with the apparent contradictions against the former removed, the apparent superiority of the latter has vanished; on the other hand, insoluble contradictions in the facts have been raised against it. The <term>Wave Theory</term> now satisfies all known phenomena, whereas the <term>Emission Theory</term> fails at the <term>Crucial Experiment|original Latin: experimentum crucis. A definitive test that determines which of two competing scientific theories is correct.</term>; it explains many things only with force, and many things not at all. The <term>Wave Theory</term> has not only all the facts but also all the analogies in nature on its side; why should the physicist give it up for one that offers him nothing in this regard,

it does not even replace the <term>Emission Theory|Also known as the corpuscular theory, this was Isaac Newton’s idea that light consisted of a stream of tiny particles.</term> for him, or rather, it does not yet exist at all. For what could the followers of the dynamic view offer in place of either the <term>Wave Theory|The theory that light travels as waves through a medium, rather than as particles.</term> or even the Emission Theory, by which one might link refraction, reflection, dispersion, polarization, and interference according to number and measure, or predict their appearance under given empirical conditions, or be able to grind a telescope and a microscope? Perhaps Goethe's teaching? <note>Johann Wolfgang von Goethe published a famous "Theory of Colors" (1810) that rejected the mathematical and particle-based approach of Newtonian optics in favor of a more intuitive, human-centered observation.</note> Indeed, we have received from <term>Natural Philosophy|A movement in German philosophy (Naturphilosophie) that sought to understand nature through speculative thought and "vital forces" rather than purely mechanical or mathematical laws.</term> thus far not the means to see things that are invisible to the naked eye, but only the means to look past them.

If one wants to take the hammer away from the blacksmith, one should offer him a better one and not the bellows in its place. The bellows is also good for stoking his fire; but it can do nothing against the iron. Now, however, one see that the Wave Theory and Atomism are themselves connected like the head and the handle of a hammer, with which one strikes reality and works it into shape; and one cannot strike with the head of the hammer without holding it by the handle.

It has been regarded as one of the most beautiful proofs of Newton’s <term>Law of Attraction|Gravity.</term> that, according to calculations carried out in its spirit, Neptune was discovered—a planet which no human eye had seen before. When they searched for it, they found it exactly where the calculation had placed it. The Wave Theory could cite many such striking proofs for itself. I will mention only two of them in passing: the property of double-refracting bodies to split a ray of light into two is well known; never had anything other than this splitting into two rays been observed, which unite into a single one only under certain conditions. Then Hamilton <note>Sir William Rowan Hamilton (1805–1865), an Irish mathematician.</note> found through calculation from the principles of the Wave Theory that a ray entering a double-refracting crystal from the outside in a very specific direction, as well as a ray exiting from the inside in an equally specific direction, can neither split into two nor remain single, but—well, what would one guess? That it must transform into a hollow cone, so-called **conical refraction**. Lloyd and others after him have tested Hamilton’s result and found it perfectly confirmed. The cone of light, caught on paper (cut through by it), forms a bright ring upon it. (Cf. Pouillet-Müller, *Textbook* 1843. Vol. II. p. 306.) If a light ray falls on a single-refracting transparent surface, it is known to divide into a reflected and a refracted ray. Cauchy calculated according to the principles of the Wave Theory that a third ray must also arise here, even if it is only weak compared to the others and vanishes at a noticeable distance from the separation surface; and for this, too, confirmation has been found through fine observations (Liebig and Kopp. *Annual Report* 1847. 1848. p. 165. Karsten *Progress* Year IV. p. 131). One can indeed assert that such dis-

<page-num>28</page-num>

...dle. These are discoveries that stand on exactly the same level as the discovery of Neptune; <note>The planet Neptune was mathematically predicted by Urbain Le Verrier before it was ever seen through a telescope in 1846, serving as a triumph for Newtonian physics.</note> and if there are indeed real-world proofs of a doctrine dealing with the real, they must count as such; although much more than such individual details, the entire coherence of the empirical proves it.

I recall another point where the advantage and benefit of the <term>Wave Theory|Also known as the undulatory theory, which proposes that light travels in waves.</term> is immediately obvious to any impartial observer. Which philosopher, following his own view of light, could ever have hit upon the idea that if a surface or space is illuminated by light, it is enough to let twice as much light fall on the surface or enter the space to make it completely dark (<term>Interference|A phenomenon where two light waves overlap. If the peak of one wave meets the valley of another, they cancel each other out, resulting in darkness.</term>)? It seems downright absurd, yet it is merely a consequence of the Wave Theory. Of course, the experiment only succeeds under special conditions (under ordinary ones, the brightness will instead increase); but what philosophical view of light could foresee it? From the Wave Theory, these results flow naturally in accordance with experience, and the principle of the experiment can easily be made clear. When two water waves meet at the same point, it may be that the crest of one exactly fills the trough of the other; then the wave motion has disappeared. At another time, it may be that the crests of both and the troughs of both meet; then the wave motion is increased. It is the same with light. In the first case, darkness; in the latter, increased brightness. Now, everything depends on the difference in the paths of the waves, calculated from their center, whether crest meets trough or crest meets crest, and so it truly is with light. With an increasing difference in the path, one and the other must alternately occur; and so it truly happens. The smallest details of interference are found to be in *agreement* with this conception.

<margin><term>Sorites|A logical argument consisting of a chain of statements where the conclusion of one becomes the premise of the next.</term></margin>
Let us summarize: A physics that knows how to reach and shape the *real* is a necessary *element* of a *science of things*. The Wave Theory is a necessary *element* of such a physics; *atomism* is a necessary *element* of the Wave Theory; therefore, *atomism* is a necessary *element* of a *science of things*. This conclusion is no less binding for being so simple, and no less rigorous for uniting such a wide field of the strictest investigations into one.

The philosopher is now likely to say: as a *mathematical fiction*, all of this may be very well for deriving the *empirical* <note>Knowledge based on observation and experience rather than pure theory.</note> facts; but higher viewpoints nevertheless forbid us from accepting it as the *real*. But how could it be conceivable that the *real* could be better derived—that is, predicted and shaped—from non-real fundamental conditions than from what is actually real? If empirical conditions can be better derived from *discrete* <note>Consisting of distinct, individual parts (atoms) rather than a continuous whole.</note> rather than non-*discrete* matter, such that they can be produced and reproduced accordingly, this can only be a proof,

He uses a logical chain (Sorites) to demonstrate that if the Wave Theory is necessary for physics, then Atomism is a necessary foundation for understanding the real world.</summary>

<page-num>29</page-num>
<header>## II. First-Order Reasons for Atomism.</header>

...that the former <note>Referring to Atomism.</note> is itself more empirical than the latter <note>Referring to the speculative natural philosophy of the time.</note>. For wherever something cannot be directly **experienced** due to the inadequacy of our senses, its **connection** with what is truly experienceable and the **possibility** of returning to experienceable things through it must decide its **reality**; there is no other sustainable **criterion** for it.

Naturally, to build a castle in the air, one only needs an **air-ax**; but it is strange when one claims that an ax, with which a real house has been built and stands before us, is actually more of an **air-ax** than the former. <note>The author uses the "air-ax" (German: *Luftaxt*) as a metaphor for purely speculative tools used by philosophers to build "castles in the air," contrasting them with the "real axes" of mathematical and physical laws that produce tangible results.</note>

<margin>//</margin>
The invalidity of that philosophical excuse will become even clearer if one remembers that **mathematics** is generally only a **logic** of **concepts of quantity** and **concepts of spatial relations**, a purely **formal science** that cannot, through any trick, extract more from things than lies within them. And even if mathematics has calculated many correct things on false foundations, it is only insofar as these false foundations still possessed an aspect of the truth that was relevant to the conclusion. Therefore, one cannot, of course, consider a general premise to be immediately proven in all its aspects by every single correct mathematical conclusion. But one can do so all the more, the more the totality of mathematical and thus rigorous conclusions proves itself in reality. Philosophers, however, instead of insisting on such a **fulfillment** of the **proof**, completely empty it of meaning by claiming that even when it fully suffices, it only proves the utility of a **fiction**, not the **truth** of the matter. <note>This refers to "instrumentalism," the philosophical view that scientific theories are merely useful tools for calculation rather than literal descriptions of the world.</note>

When **Copernicus** published his new doctrine of the world system, he obtained the **protection** of the **Pope** only because he presented the new view as a **physical hypothesis** intended to facilitate calculations. <note>Nicolaus Copernicus's *De revolutionibus* (1543) included an unsigned preface by Andreas Osiander stating that the heliocentric model was a mathematical convenience, not necessarily a physical reality, to avoid religious conflict.</note> Many of our philosophers are like such Popes. That the foundations from which mathematics has calculated something real themselves bear the character of reality is something they will, after all, never admit, insofar as they consider their system endangered by it; but they allow the **hypothesis** and the **calculation** at most, insofar as it is practically useful; only it must not be **true**. From their own premises, they do not demand at all that they be proven by reality; they are structured such that even the means to attack such a proof are lacking. They can only be proven with words.

<page-num>30</page-num>
<header>### II. First-Order Reasons for Atomism.</header>

...and refute them, because they only spin around in words. But physics cannot be satisfied with this, and nowhere should one be satisfied with it.

What are words, then? They are sounds <note>original: "Schälle." The author uses this in the sense of hollow echoes or empty noise.</note> and remain so as long as they are only derived from words, or have only more words derived from them, until one finally arrives at a word that signifies something demonstrable. There, at last, lies the touchstone of the long series of words. It is even so in the doctrine of the highest and ultimate things: the non-demonstrable must finally prove and verify itself through the demonstrable, and even in the realm of the spiritual, there is a demonstrable and thus imaginable element. This is all the more true in the doctrine of material things, which is our concern here. I will return to this point again.

Philosophers, of course, base their stance on the fact that atoms themselves are not demonstrable. But that is not what matters; how many real things are not directly demonstrable because they are too distant, too hidden, too large, or—as in our case—too small. Now, it is sufficient for something to be imagined in the form of the demonstrable, while simultaneously proving and verifying itself as a necessary starting, middle, or concluding link (if possible, as all three) within the context of demonstrable things, in order to be integrated into science and life as part of that same reality. It is only for this reason that the snow at the North Pole—which no human has yet seen or felt <note>At the time this was written, the North Pole remained an unexplored and unseen territory, serving as a perfect example of a "real" thing that lacked direct witness.</note>—is called real.

If atoms and the undulations <term>undulations: the wave-like motions of light</term> of light are not real because they cannot be directly grasped or seen—with bodies and light instead presenting only a continuous appearance—then the drops in a continuous rainbow and the air vibrations in a uniformly sounding tone are also not real; for these can just as little be "caught" during the phenomena in question or individually distinguished. Rather, like atoms, their existence is demonstrated by what follows from them, what they follow from, what surrounds and attaches to them, what is similar in other cases—in short, from the total context of the phenomena to which they belong. Yet no philosopher has ever disputed that a rainbow can be resolved into individual shining drops, or that physical sound processes can be resolved into individual vibrations. Why? Because these are one step closer to coarse sensory perception than atoms; because ordinary reasoning almost finds its way there on its own; and because the philosopher, though high in words, is not at all in the matter itself at...

<summary>The author argues that scientific entities like atoms are real because they form a necessary part of a logical chain connecting observable phenomena, comparing them to unseen snow at the North Pole or individual drops in a rainbow.</summary>

observation of nature moves beyond common sensory perception and opinion. On the contrary, it is simply the same principle—further pursued and scientifically refined—that allows the physicist to recognize individual, shining drops in a rainbow, or individual vibrations in sound. This same principle allows him to recognize individual atoms within a drop, or individual vibrations within the objective process of light, of which common perception knows nothing.

The points mentioned so far concern, as one can see, the distances between the particles of both the <term>ponderable</term> <note>From the Latin "pondus" (weight); referring to physical matter that has measurable weight.</note> and the <term>imponderable</term> <note>Substances once thought to be weightless, such as the "ether" believed to carry light waves.</note> substrates of physical phenomena. But while the imponderable <term>ether</term> seems to be primarily called upon here, there is, on the other hand, an abundance of other phenomena (which we will discuss in the next chapter) that concern only the relationships of ponderable matter. In these cases, the <term>discreteness</term> <note>The state of being composed of separate, distinct parts rather than a continuous, solid whole.</note> of the parts of ponderable matter proves even more advantageous within the physicist’s system. However, the discreteness of the ponderable and imponderable parts is itself interdependently linked within the physicist's system; one cannot have one without the other. We have placed the previous points first because we believe that they contain the most binding reasons for the atomism of the material world, even if physicists themselves often do not sufficiently appreciate them. And if it seems that such an important decision is based on only a few "fine points" (though others related to them could also be asserted), let us remember that the final decision between the <term>emission theory</term> <note>The older theory that light consists of tiny particles shot out from a source.</note> and the <term>undulation theory</term> <note>The theory that light consists of waves or vibrations; also known as the wave theory of light.</note> also depended on only a few fine points that are not obvious to superficial observation. What may appear as a mere detail is actually a tightly connected "knot" in the total fabric of science, which cannot be destroyed without destroying the coherence of science itself. Indeed, one must not merely count these details, but weigh them according to their influence on the entire structure of science; and one must not just weigh them individually, but consider that their connection to one another gives them a higher significance. That color demands atoms is extremely important; that <term>polarization</term>, <term>heat conduction</term>, and <term>heat radiation</term> demand them is each just as important on its own. But the fact that all of this—from different sides, from completely...

<page-num>32</page-num>
<header>III. Second-Order Reasons for Atomism.</header>

<note>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing from the previous page.</note> different, seemingly unrelated fields of phenomena demand atoms in the same way, and because the explanation of each individual field simultaneously establishes a connection between them, the certainty is increased and the necessity of the atomistic mode of connection is more firmly established. Only by breaking down the <term>substrate</term> <note>The underlying substance or layer in which physical phenomena occur.</note> of phenomena into parts does one gain a way to link the phenomena themselves. The <term>dynamist</term> <note>A proponent of "Dynamism," a 19th-century rival theory to atomism which held that matter is continuous and composed of forces rather than discrete particles.</note>, by attempting to link the substrate into a continuous whole, loses the very connection of all those phenomena that, in reality, depend on the <term>discreteness</term> of that substrate. 

However, as already indicated, besides the supports for atomism already asserted—which can only be fully appreciated by the physicist who delves deeply into the subject—there are other points relating solely or primarily to <term>ponderable</term> <note>Physical matter that has measurable weight, as opposed to "imponderable" substances like light or heat as they were understood at the time.</note> matter. These present themselves even to a more superficial observation in favor of atomism and have, in truth, indisputably contributed much more than the former toward winning adherents to the theory, even if they might not be as decisive when taken alone and individually, and thus have not been universally compelling. We shall come to speak of these in the following section.

### III. Second-Order Reasons for Atomism.

Generally speaking, the reasons to be discussed here lie in the fact that the atomist can link all properties and relations connected with the basic constitution of ponderable bodies under simple, clear, and easily representable points of view. These include: different density, hardness, elasticity, <term>cleavage</term> <note>original: "Blätterdurchgänge"; the tendency of crystals to split along definite structural planes.</note>, expansion through heat, crystal form, <term>states of matter</term> <note>original: "Aggregatzustände"; i.e., solid, liquid, or gas.</note>, chemical proportions, <term>isomerism</term> <note>The phenomenon where two or more compounds have the same formula but different structures.</note>, and so on. The atomist can subordinate these to the same principles of equilibrium and motion upon which he is able to base clarity, precision, and derivations everywhere else, and upon which the physical method as a whole rests. Atomism is, as it were, the key with which the physicist opens the door of a room closed to the senses and reveals its connection with the room directly accessible to him. We can now walk freely from one to the other. The dynamic view keeps the door closed and speaks only in

<summary>The author argues that atomism provides a unified "key" to understanding diverse physical properties like density, elasticity, and chemical proportions, which a continuous "dynamic" view of matter fails to explain coherently.</summary>

What is to be found behind the wall always remains just words in front of the wall. The physicist, in truth, does nothing with the atomistic view except consistently develop the principles that guide him safely in the visible realm into the invisible—that is, into what vanishes and blurs before the eye. The same concepts of mass, distance, arrangement, movement, and laws of motion, which form the fundamental concept of his general theory of bodies, serve him here as they do there, and by this very means make physics a consistent system. Applying these to the basic constitution of bodies and the phenomena depending on them in such a way that the smallest is linked with the largest is precisely what it means to be an atomist, and no other need has granted or maintained the validity of atomism in physics; to transition to an entirely <term>incommensurable</term> <note>Meaning two things that cannot be measured or compared by the same standard.</note> way of treatment is what it means to be a dynamist. There, one deals with directions of cohesion, polarities, potencies, differentiation, indifferention, universality, particularity, individuality, centrality, punctuality, sudden transformation, the <term>sublation</term> <note>The German term is "Aufheben," a central concept in Hegelian philosophy meaning to simultaneously cancel, preserve, and lift up a concept.</note> of concepts into one another, the equating of opposites, and more of the like, through which a physical reality has never yet been found, nor hardly one clearly and unambiguously designated. A rich blessing of sublime words has been spoken by philosophy regarding matter and the facts found by physics; now, we wish to have nothing against this blessing—it should only present itself as something *above* the matter at hand, not as labor *within* the matter, and it should not bind the hands with which one creates something.

In fact, the philosophical treatment of things, as it stands today, is not connected at all with the physical treatment of things; but just as philosophy must insist on a thorough connection and the most consistent development of its highest concepts and general methods within its own domain—and is only philosophy by virtue of this—it must allow physics this same right, and indeed demand it of her, so that she does not merely remain physics, but also becomes philosophical physics. For the philosophy <term>immanent</term> <note>Inherent or living within.</note> to physics, as to every science, does not consist in her dropping her own peculiar general categories and methods from a certain point in her field onward and jumping over to those of philosophy; but rather that she...

consistently develops the principles essential to it to their final conclusion. Quite apart, then, from the material evidence for atomism, the physicist is formally and philosophically compelled toward it. Would that those physicists who renounce atomism for the sake of philosophy would consider this! It may be rejected by the philosophy *outside* <note>By "philosophy outside," the author refers to speculative or metaphysical philosophy that imposes its rules on science from the exterior.</note>, but it is demanded by the philosophy *within*; and indeed, it is rejected by a philosophy that is at odds with itself and with the whole of natural science, but demanded by a philosophy that consists in the harmony of the science itself with which it is concerned.

The philosopher might say: the error lies precisely in the fact that physics wants to subordinate entirely incomparable areas of phenomena, <term>incommensurable</term> <note>Meaning things that cannot be measured or compared by the same standard.</note> in their relationships, to the same principles of observation and explanation. But why should it not want to, if it can? This proves that these areas are not as incomparable and incommensurable as they might appear to the unrefined gaze or as they are portrayed by philosophers. In science, as in life, power prevails. What a science *can* do, it possesses. Indeed, does not the triumph of every science—the proof of the height, strength, and fruitfulness of its principles—lie in the fact that the seemingly most distant and <term>heterogeneous</term> <note>Composed of parts of different kinds; completely different in nature.</note> things can be grasped under them and linked by them? Is this not precisely the aim of philosophy as well? Indeed, in the question at hand, what else does philosophy want but to impose its own principles of unity for linking the heterogeneous aspects of physics in place of those that physics itself employs? But since the physicist can achieve nothing with these [philosophical] principles in either astronomy or chemistry, the linking of both fields in this way can achieve nothing for him. For that, he needs atomism; and if one takes it from him, there is no connection between the two fields for him.

How little one should be deceived by the apparent heterogeneity of two fields when the question is whether they permit common principles of explanation can be shown by a thousand examples. Light shines, sound rings; would the philosopher perhaps also conclude *a priori* <note>original: "a priori"; meaning reasoning from theoretical deduction rather than from observation or experience.</note> that the <term>undulation theory</term> <note>The wave theory, which suggests that light and sound travel as waves through a medium.</note> is not equally applicable to both? What can appear more different than the movements of planets in celestial space and the tones of a musical instrument? And yet, one can describe the revolution of the planets according to no other...

Why should it then be forbidden for physics to draw the phenomena of **crystallization** and **cohesion** <note>The force that holds molecules of the same substance together.</note> into the circle of such common explanatory principles? Indeed, why should it not be allowed to grasp the fine and the small under the same principles as the coarse and the large, simply because in the small, that which spreads out widely in the large flows together before the eye? Atomism is about nothing more than this.

It would seem useless to me to discuss in detail all those relations, which I have presented above in a fleeting overview, regarding our question. It would always only repeat the same thing: that the philosopher substituted a version based on conceptual <term>generalities</term> <note>Abstract ideas that lack specific, physical mechanisms.</note> for the visually clear atomistic version of those relations, which is supported by the whole spirit of the exact sciences. He would never be at a loss to achieve this; and since he places his entire interest not so much in deriving phenomena from other phenomena, but rather in subordinating phenomena to generalities, he will always believe he has fulfilled his task if he has established such general concepts through which phenomena enter into a relationship with his highest generalities. This cannot be difficult for even the most absurd philosophy, but it satisfies no need of the physicist and leaves the subject at the mercy of every change in philosophical opinion. On the other hand, the physicist—to whom the linking of phenomena in their particulars is just as essential as linking them to the general, and must be so—must also demand ways of representation that make the transition possible and prescribe the manner of that transition. But until now, no other way has been found than the atomistic one.

A rapid exposition in regard to the entirety of those relations, by entering into a few examples, may suffice. With the idea of <term>discrete</term> <note>Consisting of distinct, separate parts rather than a continuous mass.</note> body-parts, one gains the possibility of a different proximity and distance between them, of an alternating approach and removal, of a different distance in different directions, of a different grouping, of modified force-relationships depending on arrangement and distance, of dependent stable and non-stable states of **equilibrium**, and the possibility of transitions...

<page-num>36</page-num>
### III. Second-Order Reasons for Atomism.

...from one position of stable equilibrium into another, of continuous movements in relation to one another, are given as one; and we see the totality of these possibilities realized through a circle of phenomena which, though individually they might admit of other interpretations, together appear only as rays of the star of atomism. They find their center and connection only in its fundamental idea. These include the differences, the changes, the transitions of density, of structure, of the **states of matter**, the passage through layers <note>original: "Blätterdurchgänge" — refers to the cleavage planes or internal layers of crystals.</note> and other varying properties of crystals in different directions, **elasticity** and the exceeding of the elastic limit, and continuous organic movements. Closely linked to this connection between the phenomena of **ponderable matter** <term>ponderable matter</term><note>Matter with measurable weight, such as solids and liquids.</note> is the previously discussed connection of the phenomena of **imponderable matter** <term>imponderable matter</term><note>Substances once thought to lack weight, such as heat, light, or the "ether."</note> itself, and with the overall connection of these physical phenomena, the connection of chemical ones as well.

In fact, with the **discreteness** <term>discreteness</term><note>The state of being composed of distinct, separate parts (atoms) rather than a continuous substance.</note> of the particles, the possibility is now also given that the particles of different bodies can penetrate between one another, that they can emerge from between one another unchanged, and that the same components can group themselves in different arrangements. Regardless of what the force relationships between the various types of atoms might be, it can be seen that a state of stable equilibrium could hardly exist in any other way than by an equal balancing of their forces through uniform distribution among one another. Hereby we have the basis for dissolution, combination, and separation, fixed proportions, **isomerism**, including **metamerism** and **polymerism** <note>These are chemical terms for substances that have the same chemical formula but different structures or properties.</note>, within the same simple fundamental concept that linked those physical relations of the ponderable and imponderable into one.

This same fundamental idea, which links physics and chemistry, now also links both to **astronomy**, in which the same relationships recur on a large scale that prevail there on a small scale.

Thus, through atomism, everything from the largest to the smallest and in the most diverse directions merges into one kingdom, and a general clarity pervades this realm.

### III. Second-Order Reasons for Atomism.

...more the atomistic interpretation of all <note>The sentence likely began on the previous page with a phrasing such as "It is no longer merely that..."</note> [phenomena] is connected, but because they are perhaps already individually particularly suitable for making the advantage of the atomistic view felt by the impartial observer; first, the case of *isomerism*.*)

<margin>M</margin>
When a painter mixes the same blue and yellow in the same proportions once and then a second time, the same green is always produced. And why not, as long as both penetrate each other reciprocally. Only in the case of non-uniform mixing could the color appear blotchy. Just as blue and yellow in the painter's pigment (at least apparently) completely permeate one another, so too, in the sense of the *dynamist* <term>dynamist</term><note>A proponent of "Dynamism," a 19th-century theory—often contrasted with atomism—which held that matter is a continuous substance or a manifestation of forces rather than being composed of discrete particles.</note>, do non-uniform *substances* behave in a chemical compound. Why should the identical same product not arise here every time, if the same substances permeate one another in the same proportions? Yet this is not the case. For isomeric bodies prove that substances can instead consist of the same components in the same proportions and yet possess very different chemical and physical properties. In the sense of the *atomist*, this is easily explained. The same *discrete atoms*, when brought together in equal proportions, can nevertheless *arrange* themselves differently one time than another; and the dynamist himself cannot deny that the resulting properties of given systems depend just as much on the *arrangement* as on the *quantity* and *nature* of the constituent elements. However, the dynamist lacks this *explanatory element* and must instead represent it through some abstract general concept that hovers over the matter rather than grasping into it.
<margin>7</margin>

The advantage of the atomistic perspective will become even more evident when we examine the specific cases of isomerism. The dynamic view cannot even allow for the consideration of specific cases of isomerism, since it cannot account for isomerism as a whole; in the atomistic view, on the other hand, the general possibility of isomerism is inherent...

---

*) Here, we define isomerism in its broadest sense—thus including polymerism <term>polymerism</term><note>A relationship where compounds have the same percentage composition but different molecular weights (e.g., C2H4 and C4H8).</note> and metamerism <term>metamerism</term><note>A type of isomerism where substances have the same molecular formula but different distributions of atoms around a central atom.</note>—to encompass all cases where bodies, despite having the same chemical composition according to their ultimate constituents, nevertheless possess different properties. In a narrower sense, as is well known, the term is only understood to refer to cases where they also possess the same atomic weight.

<page-num>38</page-num>
<header>III. Second-Order Reasons for Atomism.</header>

...also the possibility of many special cases is included as a matter of course, for which reality now also provides the evidence.

Indeed, if *isomerism* <term>isomerism</term><note>The phenomenon where different chemical compounds have the same molecular formula (the same number and types of atoms) but different structures and properties.</note> is based on the different arrangement of atoms that are either of the same nature or present in the same quantitative proportions in different bodies, then first of all nothing prevents us from thinking that not just two, but even more such modes of arrangement are possible for given substances at a given quantitative ratio; and in fact, there exists a great number of isomeric modifications for many compounds, specifically for certain *hydrocarbons*.

Nothing prevents us from thinking, furthermore, that in simple bodies—such as carbon, phosphorus, and sulfur—the atoms might group themselves one way at one time and differently at another, and that those substances can assume different properties accordingly; this is indeed confirmed in the so-called *allotropic modifications* <term>allotropic modifications</term><note>Different physical forms in which an element can exist, such as graphite and diamond for carbon, or red and white phosphorus.</note> of the aforementioned substances. On the other hand, in compound bodies such as salts, the ultimate atoms may be distributed one way at one time and differently at another among the heterogeneous *molecules* (composite atoms) of which the body primarily consists. Through this, the bodies can attain an entirely different internal chemical constitution, while the proportions of the constituents as a whole remain the same; the cases of so-called *metamerism* <term>metamerism</term><note>A type of isomerism where substances have the same chemical formula but different structural arrangements, particularly in how atoms are grouped around a central point.</note> provide the evidence for this. Further, one can imagine bodies whose molecules differ merely in their absolute number, but not in the nature or proportion of the atoms that enter into them, so that the atomic weight or the *equivalent number* <term>equivalent number</term><note>Also known as equivalent weight; the mass of a substance which will combine with or displace a fixed amount of another substance.</note> of the bodies is changed simultaneously with the properties; this is confirmed by the cases of *polymerism* <term>polymerism</term><note>Where compounds have the same percentage composition but different molecular weights, effectively being multiples of the same base unit.</note>. And one should note that these names were not created for the sake of atomism to designate its particular imaginings, but rather that they are based on factual relationships which demand distinction by different names, and that atomism simply provides a clear answer to the question of a common viewpoint for them—a viewpoint that one seeks in vain in the *dynamic view* <term>dynamic view</term><note>The 19th-century rival to atomism which proposed that matter is continuous and composed of forces rather than discrete particles.</note>.

The following cases of *isomerism* might offer a special interest in relation to our subject.

Solubility, specific gravity, double refraction, interfacial angles, salt formation—everything is exactly the same for both. For example, if a certain salt of the right-rotating acid deposits in voluminous or needle-shaped, transparent or cloudy crystals with striated or smooth surfaces, the exact same applies to the corresponding salt of the left-rotating acid.

How does the dynamicist <term>dynamicist</term><note>A follower of the "Dynamic" view of nature (often associated with Kant and Schelling), which held that matter is a continuous product of forces like attraction and repulsion, rather than being made of discrete atoms.</note> imagine the reason for this? This tells us merely that two things stand in a certain opposition, and thus categorizes our case with countless other cases that otherwise behave quite differently; beyond that, it achieves nothing. Let us see if the *atomistic conception* offers something more.

*) As noted above on page 19, a polarized ray is one in which all ether particles <term>ether particles</term><note>In 19th-century physics, light was believed to travel through an invisible medium called the "luminiferous ether."</note> perform vibrations parallel to one another (but always perpendicular to the direction of the ray). If one allows such a ray to pass through glass, water, or other such indifferent [inactive] bodies at a perpendicular incidence, the direction of the vibrations remains unchanged. If, on the other hand, one allows it to pass through bodies which, like tartaric acid, exert a rotatory power on light, the direction of the vibrations changes progressively. The angle by which it deviates from the original direction is greater the greater the thickness traversed by the ray and the greater the rotatory power of the substance. The occurrence and magnitude of such a rotation can be established through specific experiments. It should be noted, moreover, that (according to *Fresnel* and *Cauchy*) by "plane of polarization" one does not mean the plane itself in which the vibrations occur, but the plane perpendicular to it, which naturally always rotates by the same angle as the first.

### III. Second-Order Reasons for Atomism.

can consist of a given shape, is also that these molecules—while otherwise possessing an entirely identical nature—behave toward one another in two bodies like right and left gloves or hands; that one body consists, so to speak, of nothing but right hands, and the other of nothing but left hands, which, by turning their homologous <term>homologous</term><note>From the Greek 'homos' (same) and 'logos' (relation); here meaning corresponding parts that face the same way.</note> sides in the same directions, also form systems that are overall oppositely arranged. Under this assumption, everything follows almost by itself; as the success seen in right- and left-rotating tartaric acid shows. The right hands will rotate to the right what the left ones rotate to the left; and the shape of the whole crystal, determined by the arrangement of the parts, will indeed be completely identical in both modifications, yet not congruent <term>congruent</term><note>In geometry, two figures are congruent if they can be perfectly superimposed. Mirror images, like hands, are identical in shape but not congruent.</note>. In other respects, there is no reason why the two should differ in their properties. The circumstance, also proven in other substances, that the right- and left-rotating property persists in solutions and compounds, further supports the previous view. For it proves that the property in question really belongs to the molecules themselves and does not first arise through a specific way the molecules are joined <note>This is a crucial observation: if the property remains even when the crystal is dissolved in water, the "handedness" must exist within the individual molecule, not just in how the molecules are stacked in a solid.</note>. One can detach the hands from their connection and place them here or there; they always remain right and left hands.

A third possibility naturally follows from the previous two. Right and left tartaric acid each behave individually like a system of right hands and a system of left hands; however, as is actually the case in humans, a right and a left hand could each form a pair together, and a system could arise from such pairs; so that in the system, equal numbers of right and left hands enter in an ordered manner. Since in such a system the right hands rotate just as strongly to the right as the left hands do to the left, the total rotating effect would be zero; otherwise, the crystallization and other properties of the system could coincide with neither those of the right- nor left-rotating acid, because the combination of both introduces new relationships. Also such a

---

but rather combinations of atoms, as they enter into the composition of bodies as more immediate elements. Thus, the molecule of tartaric acid is a compound of oxygen, hydrogen, and carbon atoms. The rotating effect of bodies on light, as well as their crystal form, depends directly only on the relationships of the molecules, not of the simple atoms.

<page-num>41</page-num>
### III. Second-Order Reasons for Atomism.

This system is now found realized through **racemic acid** <note>original: "Traubensäure," also known as paratartaric acid</note>, which behaves in every respect like a combination of equal parts of right- and left-rotating **tartaric acid** <note>original: "Weinsäure"</note>; it exerts no rotating effect on light and deviates significantly from them in crystal form and other properties, yet it can be combined from them (with the generation of heat) and also decomposed into them again.

**Camphoric acid** <note>original: "Kamphersäure"</note> behaves quite similarly to tartaric acid in all these relationships, according to recent experiments by Chautard (*Reports of the Academy of Sciences* <note>original: "Compt. rend."</note> Vol. XXXVII. p. 166, or *Erdmann’s Journal* <note>original: "Erdm."</note> Vol. LX. p. 139), and undoubtedly more such examples will be found upon closer investigation.

There is nothing to prevent a substance—besides such <term>isomeric</term><note>Isomerism refers to molecules with the same chemical formula but different arrangements of atoms.</note> modifications whose molecules differ only by a reversed arrangement of the ultimate atoms with respect to right and left—from also presenting those where the ultimate atoms of the molecules are grouped significantly differently. Indeed, besides right and left tartaric acid and their compound, racemic acid, there exists another isomeric modification of tartaric acid (obtained by prolonged heating of racemic acid with cinchonine to 170º C). This modification, like the racemic acid from which it is obtained, shows no effect on light, yet otherwise differs quite significantly from the three aforementioned modifications (*Erdmann's Journal* Vol. LX. p. 136).

One can add one more observation. If right and left tartaric acid combine with a substance such as potash or soda, which exerts no rotating effect on light, then the salts of both acids are, as stated, completely identical except for the character of the <term>hemihedral</term><note>Hemihedral crystals have half the number of faces required by the highest symmetry of their system, often resulting in "handed" shapes.</note> formation and the direction of the rotation exerted on the light. But this equality of both salts ceases entirely if the base with which they combine is itself right- or left-rotating—meaning its molecules also behave like right and left hands (for example, cinchonine). This can again be foreseen from an <term>atomistic</term><note>Atomism refers to the theory that all matter is composed of discrete, tiny particles.</note> perspective. If a right and a left hand grasp the same sphere or the same cube, they will form a system of identical nature with it, aside from the impossible <term>congruence</term><note>The ability to be perfectly superimposed; mirror-image hands cannot be congruent.</note>; but if a right hand and a left hand both grasp another hand that is itself either right or left, they will thereby produce a significantly different system. Thus one sees how all phenomena
