### III. Second-Order Reasons for Atomism.

...well connected through clear and easily followed concepts according to the atomistic interpretation.

I mean, if atomism and the **dynamical view** <note>The "dynamical view" was a 19th-century scientific philosophy, often associated with Kant and Schelling, which argued that matter is a continuous field of forces (attraction and repulsion) rather than being made of discrete particles.</note> otherwise face each other with equally weighted arguments, this single case of **isomerism** <note>Isomerism occurs when molecules have the same types and numbers of atoms but different spatial arrangements, resulting in different chemical or physical properties.</note>—with its various sub-cases—ought to be sufficient, like a "knife-tip full of fragments from the same grain" (in the sense of the example on page 15), to decide in favor of atomism. If one stops at the coarse surface of chemical phenomena, both views have an equal claim; regarding chemical phenomena in general, one can just as easily imagine that substances penetrate each other uniformly as that they interpose themselves between one another with their discrete parts. But a point is reached in the more refined determination of chemical phenomena where this indifferent substitution of one for the other ceases, and a decision is required. Such a case is found in isomerism. The dynamical view only reaches as far as isomerism, just as it only reaches the colors of the prism; however, in the fact that atomism still encompasses both colors and isomerism, the decision for anyone who wishes to decide based on facts must lie with the latter.

Note how the color in the prism and isomerism seem at first glance to have nothing to do with one another, and how there are entirely different researchers and entirely different doctrines concerned with the former and the latter; and yet, for both, the atomistic explanation presents itself with the same necessity.

I may challenge all opponents of atomism to provide an explanation for the phenomena just discussed that is as clear, internally consistent, and acceptable as that provided by atomism. Instead of venturing the attempt, they will likely renounce it from the outset. But does this not mean, in fact, ceding the field to atomism?

Another example: The phenomena of **crystallization** necessitate, in the atomistic sense, assigning specific shapes to the molecules of solid bodies. If we imagine, for the sake of clarity, an extreme case—linearly shaped molecules \*) arranged <term>homologously</term> <note>Meaning the molecules are aligned in a regular, repeating, or corresponding pattern.</note>—then, naturally, the same properties cannot be expected along the longitudinal direction of the molecules as...

---

<note>\*) The molecules of solid bodies are indisputably, as already noted, themselves only combinations or groups of simple atoms, which are essential to the mode of grouping</note>

### III. Second-Order Reasons for Atomism.

...than in the transverse direction. Consequently, a different spacing between the **molecules** and a different behavior of the **ether** <note>In 19th-century physics, "ether" was a hypothetical substance thought to permeate all space and matter, acting as the medium for light and heat.</note> within the body along both directions must naturally result if conditions of equilibrium are to be maintained.

Indeed, all crystals—with the exception of those belonging to the regular <term>cubic system</term> (where a regular shape of the molecules is also to be assumed)—show differences in their properties according to different directions, specifically reaching maximum and minimum values along the axes of the crystal. They exhibit varying degrees of **hardness**, **cleavage** <note>Cleavage refers to the tendency of a crystal to split along definite structural planes.</note>, **elasticity**, different **optical properties**, differing **conductivity** for heat and electricity, varied **expansion** due to heat, and differences in **magnetism** and **dia-magnetism** <note>Diamagnetism is a property of materials that creates a magnetic field in opposition to an externally applied magnetic field, causing a repelling effect.</note>.

The **dynamical view** can, of course, easily find a way to express such differences in properties within the same body across different directions and link these with other expressions through new terminology. This presents no difficulty and can even be done in infinitely many ways, all of which achieve the same result—which is to say, nothing for the physicist. This is because they have nothing to do with the principle of explanation and connection that holds the entire structure of his science together and carries it forward, and with the help of which all achievements in this science have been accomplished. This simple principle is: that to the extent that conditions in nature are the same or not the same, the results will also be the same or not the same. 

Now, however, the proponent of the dynamic view, with all his terminology, cannot make it comprehensible how, for example, the difference in expansion phenomena between various bodies relates to the difference in expansion phenomena along different directions within the same body through that principle. Thus, all those expressions are futile for physics. Instead of continuing to discuss this in generalities, let us examine a specific case and thereby clarify more precisely what we mean.

Crystals are expanded by heat unequally in different directions. For the atomistic view...

<page-num>44</page-num>

### III. Second-Order Reasons for Atomism.

The consideration of this matter is structured as follows: The ratio in which particles move apart due to a given influence of heat is a function of their existing distance, their orientation, and their chemical quality (the possibility that this quality could be further broken down is not considered here). Insofar as different bodies differ from one another in these circumstances, they will also expand differently when subjected to the same influence of heat. These differences, which exist between different bodies as a whole, can now also be found—according to the atomistic view—between different directions within the same body.

The particles of a **crystal** are already at different distances from one another depending on the direction; they are oriented differently toward each other, and the chemically distinct elements within are arranged differently in relation to those various directions. Consequently, expansion also occurs differently in different directions. Both sets of phenomena—those concerning different bodies as a whole, and those concerning the different directions within a single body specifically—are thus linked by the atomistic conception according to the general principle of scientific connection in physics; they are brought into a **real physical connection**.

However, for the **dynamical view** <note>The "dynamical view" was a 19th-century alternative to atomism which held that matter is continuous and that its properties arise from forces (like attraction and repulsion) rather than the arrangement of discrete particles.</note>, this type of connection, which is the primary concern of the physicist, ceases to exist. Those differences that exist for different bodies vanish when considering different directions of the same body. According to that view, the density, quality, and distribution of matter are the same in every direction within any given crystal; one cannot speak at all of the "shape" or "position" of matter within the crystal. Thus, those differences in properties suddenly hang in the air, detached from what they usually rely upon, anchored only to some word—and vast circles of phenomena, which the atomistic view links together in their true sense and spirit, fall apart for the physicist.

Perhaps the proponent of the dynamical view might say: "Why can I not speak of different densities and other fundamental properties of a crystal in different directions just as well as the atomist? And why can I not relate everything to these properties, making them dependent on them, just as the atomist does?" In that case, it would merely be a translation of the atomistic phrasing into the dynamical mode of expression.

But why is it necessary to trace this back to an unequal distance between parts and the change of this distance—things which I cannot see? Could I not just as easily say that a density, which was different from the start in various directions, now increases and decreases in unequal proportions along those directions?

One can certainly say it; however, one can neither imagine nor think anything by it, because a varied density along different directions within one and the same body is not at all imaginable or thinkable without resorting to a different distribution of masses along those different directions. Just try, for instance, to blacken a strip in such a way that it appears black across its width but only gray along its length. That would be the "varied density in different directions" in the sense of the dynamist <note>The "Dynamist" view held that matter was continuous and filled space completely, with properties arising from forces rather than particles.</note>.

The expansion of bodies thus provides a new example of how the dynamical view explains phenomena only up to certain limits but no further; it fails to reach specific determinations. For the expansion of bodies in general and as a whole, it serves as well as the atomistic view; but as soon as one wishes to move from there to expansion in specific directions, the means of transition is missing.

The following consideration may show more closely how much more disconnected everything appears under the dynamical view than under the atomistic one. When a whole body expands through heat, its overall decrease in density and the increase in its total volume are precisely connected, and both the dynamical and atomistic views must recognize this connection as a **reciprocal relationship** <term>Reciprocitätsverhältniss (reciprocal relationship): The mathematical principle where one value increases as another decreases, such as volume and density.</term> between the two. In proportion as the space occupied by the body increases, the density of the body decreases. For the atomistic view, however, the same reciprocal relationship that exists for the body as a whole also exists specifically for the various dimensions of the body. The body becomes less dense in a given direction in the same proportion as its di-

...lies, while also considering that the molecules of solid bodies are themselves generally groups of smaller, partly different atoms, the most varied and complicated relationships—and thus bodies with the most diverse properties—can be represented. From a distance, all the strips used for this purpose will look just as uniformly gray as the dynamist’s <note>In this context, the "Dynamiker" (dynamist) refers to those who believed matter was a continuous force-field rather than discrete particles.</note> uniformly gray strip, and will differ from it only, perhaps, in the intensity and nuance of the gray. Thus, a dispute is certainly possible as to whether the strip is gray in the sense of the atomist or the dynamist. How will it be decided? One will step closer; then, if it is an atomistic gray, it will resolve into its constituent parts <margin><unclear>S</unclear></margin>; if it is a dynamical gray, it will *not*. In physics, this "stepping closer" is represented by a more detailed examination of phenomena. The fact that the dynamical approach does not examine the phenomena closely, but merely perceives them in general from a distance, is what allows it to persist in its illusion.

One can now combine the first example with the current one. The same black points, lines, squares, and the like, in the same quantity and quality, can still be distributed in different arrangements on the same strip. Thus, the case of isomerism <term>Isomerie</term> <note>Isomerism is the phenomenon where molecules have the same chemical formula but different structural arrangements, leading to different properties.</note> has been linked with that of the expansion and other variations in the properties of crystals in different directions; and this does not even require a visual image. The conception <term>Vorstellung</term> <note>The German word *Vorstellung* refers to a mental representation or the way we visualize a concept in the mind.</note> remains just as clear even without any image, whereas under the dynamical view, not even a clear image can be found for it—for the simple reason that the conception itself cannot be found.

But why must the physicist insist on maintaining a foothold in visualization through all this? Because he only knows how to find the path from the conceivable back to the conceivable, and for him, the coherence of the conceptual treatment and the conclusion depends on the coherence of the visualization.

Furthermore, I do not deny, as I have said, that philosophy will also have its formulas—or can find them at will—for isomerism and differences in properties in various directions, just as for all the other aforementioned relationships. These will revolve around the expressions mentioned above or similar ones; philosophy will even be ready and finished with them much faster than atomism with its conception and representation

<header>### III. Second-Order Reasons for Atomism.</header>

...of these same relationships, because it is naturally much easier to subordinate given appearances under general expressions than to cover them with self-consistent, detailed, and accurate mental models. Anyone who cannot appreciate the difference in character and success between these two approaches—and perhaps even believes that the depth of understanding lies in the lack of clarity, indeterminacy, ambiguity, the evaporation into empty abstractions, and the self-contradictions of those philosophical formulas—will, of course, find no argument for atomism over philosophy in the strict way atomism connects and covers all those relationships through a single, clear fundamental idea; for philosophy is never at a loss for words to grasp those relationships.

For the purposes of the physicist, however, the complete fruitfulness <note>Fechner is being ironic here; he means the "complete fruitlessness" or "sterility" of these philosophical systems for practical science.</note> of all previous philosophical interpretations of physical things in the dynamic sense—from Kant to Hegel, Herbart, and the most recent thinkers—is so truly and evidently sterile that even those physicists and chemists who do not adhere to atomism take great care not to seek refuge in such interpretations. At most, if they are somewhat influenced by the prevailing philosophy, they might occasionally borrow an expression from it. Yet there are only a few who do so, and they count for less the more they do it. Anyone who speaks of **polarity** <term>Polarität</term> <note>In the early 19th century, "Naturphilosophie" (Nature Philosophy) used "polarity" as a universal mystical principle. Fechner argues it should only be used as a specific technical term in physics.</note> outside the doctrine of **magnetism** and **galvanism** <term>Galvanismus</term> <note>An early term for electricity produced by chemical action.</note> is a lost man.

What leads some physicists to distance themselves from atomism, aside from the influence of philosophers, has been discussed previously; but nowhere is it more clearly shown than in the field of facts currently being discussed how little reason philosophers have to use this as a weapon against atomism. For here it comes unequivocally to light: it is more often a hostility toward philosophy than a friendship with it that turns those physicists away from atomism. To them, atomism is actually still too *philosophical*; they are generally content to accept those facts, which are linked into one by the atomistic view, as unconnected facts, or to seek laws that apply to specific areas without worrying about a more general connection of these laws themselves. In the spirit of that previously discussed tendency not to leave the empirical behind, they would rather sacrifice the internal and, in our view, truly philosophical connection...

<summary>Fechner argues that while speculative philosophy (like that of Hegel or Kant) uses vague language to explain nature, atomism provides a clear, unifying model that actually works for science. He notes that many scientists reject atomism not because they prefer philosophy, but because they are so wary of "philosophical" theorizing that they prefer to stick to isolated facts.</summary>

<header>### III. Second-Order Reasons for Atomism.</header>

...connection of empirical facts and laws through atomism, preferring instead to take refuge in what seems to them an unprovable hypothesis. But in physics, hypotheses can generally only be proven by connecting facts or laws that satisfy a range of facts; and it would be a strange thing if philosophers truly believed their cause was strengthened by natural scientists who reject a connection between empirical facts and laws simply because that connection itself cannot be demonstrated as an empirical fact in the same way the others can. Yet, strangely enough, even as they <gloss>fight against</gloss> reject it, they simultaneously appeal to it; for indeed, one of the common ways for philosophers to oppose atomism is to say: "The physicists themselves think nothing of it; they are not serious about it; they only use the words, but they do not care for the underlying reality." And it may be that the number of natural scientists of whom they rightly say this is not at all small.

In fact, there are likely many chemists who are quite happy to be able to calculate their chemical proportions using the <term>rule of three</term> <note>A mathematical method to find a fourth value from three known ones, commonly used in basic chemical calculations before more complex atomic theory was standardized.</note> and to see in <term>isomerism</term> <note>A phenomenon where two substances have the same chemical formula but different structures and properties.</note> a curious fact; for both, no atomism is needed; the rule of three and the observed fact are sufficient. There are also quite a few crystallographers who, because they can organize all crystal forms into a perfect system without any need for atomism, declare the theory to be the most superfluous figment of the imagination; after all, it teaches neither how to measure an angle exactly nor how to calculate the relationship between axes and angles. Likewise, there are many physicists who, because there are rules for how bodies expand and contract with heat, or stretch and compress through tension and pressure, consider atomism—which contributes nothing at all to these specific rules—to be an idle hypothesis through which one learns not a hair more than one already knows. Yes, strangely, the more one isolates these individual fields of study, the more superfluous atomism seems—this doctrine of the most minute, individual parts.

But let the chemist, crystallographer, or physicist try for once to bring chemical proportions, isomerism, crystal forms, <term>cleavage planes</term> <note>original: "Blätterdurchgänge"—the tendency of crystals to split along definite structural planes.</note>, expansion, and elasticity relationships, and so on, into a conceivable, rational connection. What then? But he does not try, the pure empiricist; he is merely an empirical, not a philosophical physicist. Indeed, one might say: in that he rejects atomism—

This page picks up mid-sentence, arguing that rejecting the underlying structure of matter actually leads to a more fragmented—and thus more "atomistic" in the negative sense—view of the world.</meta>

<page-num>50</page-num>

<header>### III. Second-Order Reasons for Atomism.</header>

...rejects <note>Continuing from the previous page: "and whoever rejects the atomic connection..."</note> it, he proves himself to be the worst kind of atomist; whereas the true atomist, by defending his theory, proves to be the exact opposite—provided, of course, that one understands "atomism" in the sense used by its opponents, where the word is synonymous with a <term>fragmented view</term> <note>Fechner is playing with the definition of atomism. Critics called it "fragmenting" because it breaks the world into tiny pieces; Fechner argues that without atoms, science remains a collection of "fragmented" facts with no unifying thread.</note> of things.

As we can see, atomism has a two-fold validation: first, by delving into the deepest and finest details of phenomena; and second, by seeking the most general and comprehensive connection between those phenomena. Yet these two paths are linked. For insofar as a great many—indeed, entire fields of—subtle phenomena depend on the fine structure of matter, these phenomena necessarily fall outside the scope of science if science does not incorporate that fine structure into its framework. Without it, they remain merely isolated facts instead of becoming logical conclusions. The coarse, everyday aspect of phenomena requires atomism as little as do individual peculiarities. However, the finest dissection and the most general connection join hands in demanding atomism. Here also lies the answer to the question one might raise: why did the need for atomism only assert itself as natural science progressed, and why has it grown steadily ever since? One always begins with the coarse and the individual, and concludes with the finest elaboration and most complete connection. To someone for whom the fine is too fine and the connection too high, atomism will also remain too fine and too high. And although I generally defend physicists against philosophers, I do not defend the kind of scientific spirit that consists in draining physics of its spirit and stifling its progress. 

But the spirit of atomism goes beyond that. Even those who are unable to recognize the higher merits and advantages of atomism still admit that it is at least the most convenient way to represent things, and that its terminology can certainly be used to make relationships imaginable—even if they want no consequences drawn from it and no reality attributed to this way of thinking. In this way, the spirit of atomism overwhelms them. To me, they appear almost like people who use their natural legs because they are the most convenient means of moving forward, but without claiming in the least that these are actually their *true* legs—which, they imagine, instead remain hidden in the dark and will hopefully one day come to light. The

### III. Second-Order Reasons for Atomism. 51
->4*<-

The fact of making progress <note>original: *Fortkommens*; here meaning practical scientific advancement.</note> is enough for them. They consider atomism as superfluous as the sun, arguing that it is light during the day regardless; they scold the very light by which they see as a "hollow appearance" simply because they cannot grasp it with their hands as they can the objects they see by its light. Yet, they still prefer this to the "philosophical appearance," in which, conversely, everything clear dissolves into obscurity.

It is difficult to say anything general about the prevailing trends in this field without saying something *too* general. I readily admit this regarding the reproaches I level against modern philosophy, even though I hardly know how or at what point I should limit them; a reproach made in general must inevitably become unjust toward individuals or certain perspectives. However, we would also undoubtedly do an injustice to some of the greatest researchers of physical matters if we were to place them in the same category as the aforementioned physicists, despite the fact that they too assign only a precarious value to atomism. Either they demand of a hypothesis that it must already achieve everything it is meant to do before they grant it the full seal of approval; or, even if a hypothesis does achieve everything expected of it, it remains for them nothing more than a hypothesis. There are even those who, for this reason, consider the rotation of the Earth around the sun to be nothing more than the "simplest possible hypothesis."

Unquestionably, the perspective of those who still view atomism with a skeptical eye—despite all they themselves have done for its promotion and cultivation—is quite different from the perspective that determines the dismissive attitude of the others. For the former, it is a demand for the highest degree of <term>certainty</term> <note>The German *Sicherheit* implies reliability or safety in a theory, whereas *Gewissheit* implies a more subjective or absolute philosophical certainty.</note> placed upon atomism, which it neither currently satisfies nor, strictly speaking, ever could satisfy. For the latter, it is a lack of knowledge regarding the security it already offers, a missing overview and appreciation of its roots that already reach so deep and wide, or a lack of need for a more general and higher connection of facts. With the former group, however, it is easy for us to come to an understanding. We ourselves do not wish to present atomism as something absolutely certain—neither in the sense that some philosophers speak of the absolute certainty of their systems, nor in the sense that something directly experienced is, as such, immediately certain. For us, too, there remains a place—

<vocab>Atomistik (Atomism), Thatsache (Fact), Philosophie (Philosophy), Hypothese (Hypothesis), Forscher (Researcher/Scientist), Sicherheit (Certainty/Security), Gewissheit (Certainty/Assurance)</vocab>

<summary>Fechner distinguishes between two types of people who doubt atomism: those who ignore it out of ignorance or lack of need for a unified theory, and serious scientists who maintain a healthy skepticism because the theory cannot yet provide absolute certainty.</summary>

### III. Second-Order Reasons for Atomism.

...nor for the faith that must supplement knowledge in all the highest and final things, in material matters no less than in spiritual ones; in the attempt to link material things, one should simply hold to *atomism* as the most probable and sufficient theory until something more probable and sufficient exists. One should only add that it already possesses so many concordant grounds of probability in its favor—grounds that outweigh contrary ways of connecting facts—that it is quite improbable that anything fundamentally more probable and sufficient will ever be found. Meanwhile, it is certainly to be demanded and hoped that the theory will continue to elevate and establish itself with even greater internal probability and sufficiency. We shall then be quite satisfied if this succeeds to the extent—for we do not even wish to claim that it has already succeeded—that its probability becomes equal to the *hypothesis* of the Earth's rotation around the sun, <note>Fechner frequently compares the "certainty" of atomism to the Heliocentric model, which in the mid-19th century was the gold standard for a proven scientific theory that still technically began as a hypothesis.</note> even if it should eternally be called a hypothesis just like the latter. This name does not matter at all; what matters is the effect attributed to it: the right to determine the course of research, the form and method of representation, the way of connecting to other fields, and the foundation of general views. *All of this*, we say, *atomism* should be permitted to do as the *master builder* <note>original: *Baumeisterin*; a personification of the theory as a female architect or mason.</note> of the physical domain for as long as it can achieve more than any other theory. If another should surpass it, then let atomism step back; but let no theory seek to displace it that has not yet achieved even the smallest thing in physical matters.

However, even if we cannot agree with those researchers who deny atomism its proper appreciation—whether out of ignorance, a lack of overview of its grounds, or a lack of need for a more general and higher connection of facts—we still know how to respect the motive that is often their primary driver. This motive lies in the desire not to mix the certainty of facts with the uncertainty of hypotheses. For, after all is said and done, facts remain for the *physicist* the *first* priority, the *foundation* <note>original: *Grund*; meaning both the physical ground and the logical basis.</note> that must, above all else, be held and preserved purely, strictly, and firmly. Yet philosophy, of all things, can least tolerate that he, instead of standing upon this pure foundation, remains standing purely *at* this foundation; <note>Fechner is making a wordplay: a scientist should use facts as a base to build upon, not as a stopping point where they refuse to look further.</note> and so it remains that everything which the philosophy calling itself by that name has hitherto offered instead of atomism for the—

<summary>Fechner argues that atomism should be treated as the foundational "master builder" of physics, possessing a probability similar to the Earth's orbit around the sun, and critiques researchers who refuse to look beyond raw facts.</summary>
<keywords>atomism, scientific hypothesis, faith vs. knowledge, methodology, master builder, physics vs. philosophy, certainty of facts</keywords>

higher structure, indeed for a higher plan, was factually useless for physics, and is used just as little by those physicists who reject atomism as by those who assert it.

Now the philosopher may still say—and many do not shrink from doing so—that those philosophical ways of viewing physical things, which the physicist cannot use, are also not intended for his use. Well then, let him use what is useful to him, and let one not think that what is truly useful for him leads from reality to reality. <note>Fechner is being slightly sarcastic here, criticizing philosophers who claim their theories are "above" practical utility while failing to provide a better alternative for actual science.</note> — But, let us not return to things already settled! —

And it is bad enough if philosophy itself were to confess that what it says about physical things is not useful for a science of the physical!

# IV. Some Particular Reasons for Atomism.

In the letter that <term>Professor Weber</term> <note>Wilhelm Eduard Weber (1804–1891), a major German physicist known for his work on magnetism and electricity.</note> addressed to me based on the presentation of the main points of this treatise, he recalled some points that might have been cited or further developed in favor of atomism than I have done—though their presentation would have partly led to difficulties—and added the following:

"Finally, the free surface of the atmosphere could also be asserted in favor of the atomistic view. The boundary of the atmosphere, whether at a height of ten or fourteen miles <note>Likely German miles; one German geographical mile is approximately 7.5 kilometers, making this roughly 75 to 105 km, which aligns well with the modern definition of the edge of space, the Kármán line.</note> above the Earth's surface, can be regarded as astronomically proven. This boundary can, of course, be easily explained if one ascribes a limited validity to <term>Mariotte's Law</term> <note>More commonly known in English as Boyle's Law, it describes how the pressure of a gas tends to decrease as the volume of the container increases.</note>. However, there seems to be no reason at all for such a restriction of the validity of Mariotte's Law under decreasing pressure, especially if one imagines air as continuously distributed in space even at the greatest <term>dilution</term> <note>Refers to the thinning of the air as one moves higher into the atmosphere.</note>. For then it is not a matter of changing distance at all, but merely of reducing the masses at equal distances, where it would contradict all analogy to allow a deviation of the forces from <term>proportionality</term> <note>The idea that the force should change in direct relation to the amount of matter (mass) present.</note> with the masses. It is quite another thing if..."

<meta>This page continues the quote from physicist Wilhelm Weber regarding the physical evidence for atomism, specifically focusing on the atmosphere and the definition of volume. It then transitions into Fechner’s own analogy involving the stretching and snapping of rubber to demonstrate the flaws in the "dynamic" (continuous matter) theory.</meta>

> "...the air consists of small masses concentrated in individual points, which move away from each other as the air expands. With the distance of these points, their mutual repulsion must decrease, while the attraction of the Earth (the weight of each point) remains nearly unchanged; consequently, there must be a point of expansion where the weight of the outermost air atoms balances the repulsion of the nearest air atoms lying at some distance below them. And one can maintain that <term>Mariotte's Law</term> <note>Known in the English-speaking world as Boyle's Law, it states that the pressure of a gas is inversely proportional to its volume.</note> does not lose its validity; rather, it is clear that only the concept of the **volume** of a mass of air requires a sharper definition. By this is meant not the volume of the atoms themselves—which is left entirely out of consideration—but rather the spaces in which the atoms are located, the boundaries of which require a certain rule. This rule can be such that an infinite space belongs to the outermost atoms, and that according to this closer definition of the concept of volume, Mariotte's Law itself retains strict validity even in the extreme case."

If I am not mistaken, the following argument is of a similar nature, but perhaps somewhat more accessible for a popular discussion. When one stretches a thread of *gummi elasticum* <note>Natural rubber.</note>, it becomes longer and longer, yet remains a continuous whole. This looks exactly like it would in the sense of the <term>dynamic view</term> <note>The "dynamic view" was a philosophical and physical theory (often associated with Kant and Schelling) that matter is continuous and fills space through forces of attraction and repulsion, rather than being made of discrete particles.</note>; one might say, almost like the view itself. But, if one stretches it too far, it **snaps**. I mean, in the sense of a consistent dynamic view, it should simply keep stretching longer and longer, and in snapping, the theory snaps with it. For according to that view, the matter of the thread remains continuously unbroken until the moment of tearing, and only the density decreases more and more; how can a sudden snap—a suspension of continuity—arise from a mere decrease in density? The "continuity" of the dynamic view is thereby suspended itself. Even from an infinite increase in stretching force, one should only expect an infinite decrease in the density of the thread according to that view, but no snapping. For the atomistic view, things appear differently. According to it, the distance between the particles—which exists from the very beginning—must finally become so great through its growth that it becomes visible, appearing first wherever it first becomes large enough. This aligns with the idea that the attraction, which depends on distance and is noticeable only at imperceptibly small distances—

<summary>Fechner concludes a quote from Wilhelm Weber about the boundary of the atmosphere and then offers his own analogy: a rubber band snapping proves that matter is made of discrete particles rather than being a continuous substance.</summary>

### IV. Some specific reasons for atomism.

...attraction eventually becomes imperceptible as the distance increases. According to the <term>dynamic view</term> <note>A 19th-century theory, influenced by natural philosophy, which held that matter is a continuous distribution of forces rather than discrete particles.</note>, on the other hand, the <term>cohesive force</term> <note>The physical force that holds the molecules of a substance together.</note> grounded in the contact of particles suddenly becomes imperceptible without the contact having previously ceased in any way.

The same case can also be explained as follows: If one could stretch a body of uniform consistency perfectly evenly, then according to the atomistic view, a point would eventually come 
<margin>Dissociation</margin>
where it would fall apart into its atoms or <term>molecules</term> <note>In this context, Fechner uses "molecule" to refer to a small group of atoms acting as a unit.</note>. Only the fact that the atoms are already further apart from each other at some point than at others causes the tear to occur at one location first. But according to the dynamic view, that falling apart into atoms could never happen; the uniform body would have to stretch evenly forever, because after all, no falling apart into atoms is possible, since there are no atoms; because the body, if it were to tear at all, would have to tear in all places at once, which is nonsense. Now, one does not see how—since the tear can occur through no force if all parts are stretched beyond a certain <term>limit</term> <note>original: "Gränze" (Grenze)</note>—it can occur all at once when a single point is stretched beyond a certain limit. The dynamist might say: insofar as force or resistance acts differently at this point than at others; the possibility of a tear at one point depends from the start on a relative weakness of this point compared to the others, and thus cannot be applied from the start to a uniform and uniformly pulled body.

But this answer is erroneous. The possibility of a tear depends, in fact and demonstrably, on the magnitude of the absolute force acting on a point of given resistance, and not at all on the difference in forces or resistances relative to neighboring points. Every point tears when the force acting upon it has become great enough, regardless of how it relates to the difference in neighboring points; it is no easier or harder whether this difference is larger or smaller—it is the same even if no such difference exists at all. Only *which* point tears first, not *that* a point tears at all, depends on the difference in the durability of the points.

<margin>113</margin>
So let us repeat our conclusion once more: a uniform and uniformly stretched body can never tear according to the dynamic view, no matter how great the force becomes; it can only continue to stretch more and more uniformly

He asserts that only the atomistic model, which allows for the separation of discrete particles (dissociation), accounts for the physical reality of a material snapping under tension.</summary>

### IV. Some specific reasons for atomism.

...thin out, because, should it tear at all, it would have to tear everywhere, which is nonsense. Furthermore, an irregular or unevenly stretched body could just as little tear through that same force; it could only continue to thin out more and more, in accordance with the force and the resistance present at every point. But since it actually does tear under the influence of sufficient force, the <term>dynamic view</term> <note>The theory that matter is a continuous field of force rather than made of individual particles.</note> is erroneous and the atomistic view is correct, in that the former contains an <term>antinomy</term> <note>A contradiction between two logical conclusions that both seem true; a paradox.</note> between facts and requirements that does not exist in the latter; for the atomistic view already allows for the tearing of a uniform and uniformly stretched body—namely, into atoms—and the phenomenon of <term>Prince Rupert’s drops</term> <note>original: "geschwänzten Glasthränen". These are glass beads with long tails that, when the tail is nipped, shatter explosively into fine dust. They were a common 19th-century illustration of internal tension.</note> even provides an approximation of such a disintegration into atoms. But if the tearing of all the gaps between the atoms is possible at once, then it is also possible at each individual gap if the corresponding force is applied to it.

Now the proponent of the dynamic view might say: but how can one build something upon premises that do not occur? No perfectly uniform body exists. Therefore, one cannot draw conclusions about what happens in real existence from what would happen in such a body, if it existed.

To this, one must simply say that no view—least of all a philosophical one—is worth anything if it does not suffice and remain consistent when one carries it to its logical conclusion in the mind, even if reality only shows us approximations. Mathematics and philosophy are precisely intended to complete in the mind what reality offers only as approximations. Philosophy normally would not interpret this any other way; but perhaps, when faced with atomism, even weapons such as that particular excuse seem good enough to it.

Furthermore, it is clear even without deeper investigation and deduction that a view according to which bodies are broken apart from the very beginning <note>meaning: they are composed of separate atoms from the start</note> must explain or make more understandable a large-scale fracture better than a view where every body necessarily and essentially remains a <term>continuum</term> <note>A continuous, unbroken mass.</note> until the very moment it tears. For according to the former, it is only a matter of widening a crack that is already there until it becomes visible and permanent; according to the latter, it is a matter of suddenly creating the crack as something entirely new. Every fracture of a body is, in fact, a <term>lethal leap</term> <note>original: "Salto mortale" (Italian for a "deadly jump"). In philosophy and physics, this refers to an abrupt, inexplicable change that violates the principle of continuity.</note> for the dynamic view, while for the atomistic view it is only the <term>leap</term> <note>original: "Saltus" (Latin). This refers to the principle *natura non facit saltus* (nature does not make leaps).</note> which

<summary>The author argues that only atomism can logically explain why materials break, as a continuous substance would theoretically thin out forever rather than snap. He defends the use of idealized scientific models and mocks the dynamic view's reliance on "leaps" of logic to explain physical fractures.</summary>

### IV. Some specific reasons for atomism.

The proverb "Here is Rhodes, here jump!" <note>original: "Hic Rhodus, hic salta." This is a Latin phrase from Aesop’s fables, essentially meaning "Prove it here and now."</note> demands this of it. Show me, it says, that atoms can be discrete. At this, the atomist takes a body and breaks it. Some even burst apart on their own. And indeed, it can be broken at any point, because the crack already passes through the body in every direction.

If (according to the two previous chapters) the atomistic view surpasses the achievements of the dynamic view regarding the most general connection and finest analysis of natural phenomena, one now sees that it also better encompasses the limiting cases of phenomena. Here it is proven once again that the latter [the dynamic view] can only follow the former up to a certain point, but not beyond. Just as the dynamic view only reached as far as the colors in the prism and as far as <term>isomerism</term> <note>The existence of molecules that have the same chemical composition but different structures, leading to different properties.</note>, it also only reaches as far as the fracture of bodies; the atomistic view, on the other hand, extends beyond the fracture. A crack that is present neither visibly nor invisibly cannot become visible even through magnification; a crack that is present invisibly, however, can.

Again, I do not doubt that the dynamic view will also find expressions for the fracture of bodies that combine with its other expressions; but with all these expressions, it cannot establish the continuity of representation and lawfulness which, according to the atomistic view, persists during the fracture of bodies along with the stretching. Instead, to cover the fracture of bodies, the dynamic view must momentarily leap to an atomistic way of thinking and interrupt the continuity of natural law. It does this by allowing the discontinuity—which it otherwise denies everywhere and does not include in the flow and nexus of lawful consequences—to suddenly occur at a specific point of stretching and at a specific point of the body. In doing so, it contradicts not only a natural way of looking at things but also the principles of natural science.

One can easily link considerations of chemical fractures to those of mechanical ones. According to the dynamic view, <term>calcium carbonate</term> <note>original: "kohlensaurer Kalk."</note>, for example, marble, is a continuous body where at every point carbonic acid and lime have identified themselves into something intermediate. Now the expanding principle of heat enters (which may be considered here only in terms of the factual side of its effect, although atomism

<summary>The author argues that only atomism can logically explain why solid objects break or "fracture," whereas the dynamic theory of continuous matter fails to account for the sudden appearance of a gap or crack. He suggests that the dynamic view is forced to temporarily adopt atomistic logic to explain these physical breaks, thereby contradicting its own core principles.</summary>

### IV. Some specific reasons for atomism.

(...able to go deeper than the mere word of an "expansive principle" <note>The "expansive principle" was a concept in dynamic physics suggesting matter is a continuous force pushing outward.</note>), it is certainly understandable how this uniform material can stretch further and further; but how, from a certain point onward, the **carbonic acid** <note>In this context, "carbonic acid" (Kohlensäure) refers to carbon dioxide gas (CO₂).</note> can suddenly escape, is not understandable. For just a moment ago, **lime** <note>Calcium oxide or calcium carbonate components.</note> and **carbonic acid** still formed a single essence, identical within itself, which expanded as a single unit until then. How does the principle that was expanding until that point suddenly become a decomposing one? According to the **atomistic view**, **carbonic acid** and **lime** are already separate within the **marble**; as the **marble** expands, the particles of both move further and further apart, and from the very beginning, the particles of the **carbonic acid** behave differently than those of the **lime**; they are able to do so because they do not form an identical essence with it. Now it is easily understood how a point can finally be reached where the striving for expansion of the **carbonic acid**, increased by the heating, gains the upper hand against the attraction to the **lime**, and causes it to escape from between the parts of the **lime**. That is why the latter remains behind as a more easily crumbled mass; indeed, some bodies literally fall into powder upon a similar escape of a volatile constituent, as the gaps already present become perceptible. Where, according to the **dynamic view**, does that loosening, this powder come from, if only continuity escapes from what is continuous?

The interpretation of other phenomena is directly connected to the previous interpretation. One of the most common ways to transform one **isomeric** or **allotropic modification** <note>Terms describing substances with the same chemical formula but different structures or forms, like diamond and graphite.</note> into another is heating; also, **crystals** that crystallize differently at different temperatures can, even in a solid state, change from one crystal form to another through a change in temperature, as the entire crystal transforms into an aggregate of crystals of the other form. These phenomena are easily explained in connection with the previous observations by the fact that heat does not merely expand bodies as a whole, but exerts effects of a particularly distinct nature on their particles depending on their individual type and position. For the **dynamic view**, every imaginable connection between these facts is missing, and there are leaps in the field of each of these facts themselves.

One might say, perhaps: but the **atomistic view** must also, at the moment of the chemical rupture where the affinity of, for example, the **carbonic acid** to the **lime** through the striving for expansion of the...

<summary>The author argues that atomism better explains chemical decomposition (like marble into lime and CO₂) and crystal changes than the dynamic view, because it accounts for individual particles acting independently.</summary>

### IV. Some specific reasons for atomism.

...is overcome by the **carbonic acid**, one must also acknowledge a leap in the process. To be sure, but only such a leap as when a **balance beam** <note>The horizontal bar of a weighing scale.</note>, which hangs to one side, suddenly flips to the other side due to the continued adding of weights, after it has first risen to a horizontal position; one must indeed acknowledge such leaps (if they deserve that name); but not those where a ten-pound weight leaves the beam in exactly the same place, neither tilting nor bending it, and then one grain more causes it to suddenly flip over. In fact, it is comparable to saying that heat should increase for a long time without the **carbonic acid** <note>Carbon dioxide gas (CO₂).</note> and **lime** <note>Calcium oxide or calcium carbonate.</note> ceasing in the slightest to penetrate one another precisely in the same space; rather, they merely expand as a single unit into a larger volume; then, with a little more heat, the rupture is suddenly there. That is like a bolt from the blue. How does the instrument, which until then was only able to stretch the body as one, suddenly become a knife that also cuts through what it stretches? One does not see how. Of course, if one has grown accustomed to illuminating things with obscure concepts, it is easy to find a remedy. One can say: from the beginning, the heating manifests—along with the actual expansion of the whole body—the *tendency* to also separate its components, but this tendency is initially powerless against the prevailing **chemical affinity** <note>The force that holds different chemical substances together; the original term is "Verwandtschaft."</note>, until it finally becomes great enough to overcome it in turn. "In this way, we have achieved the same thing by the **dynamic** route as you have by the **atomistic** one."

<margin>objection</margin>Undoubtedly, if only a tendency that grows up to a certain point without expressing any detectable effect—a tendency that only refers to what it will one day achieve—were not a completely obscure thing, or rather, let us say, an **absurdity** <note>original: "Unding" — literally a "non-thing" or a conceptual impossibility.</note>.

"How," one says, "do you not think of how the steam in a closed boiler builds up more and more tension; only when its tension has reached a certain limit does the boiler burst and an effect occur; and will you deny that the growing tension, the growing **striving for expansion**, was something more than an absurdity until then?"

How, I ask in return, do you then also believe that when a tethered horse pulls on its lead, stronger and stronger until it snaps, the growing tension, the growing **striving for expansion** of the lead, expressed itself in nothing before the breaking?

<summary>The author critiques the "dynamic" theory of matter for relying on invisible "tendencies" that cause sudden changes, arguing instead that all physical processes involve continuous, observable internal movements as explained by atomism.</summary>

### IV. Some specific reasons for atomism.

...it were a mere **thing of thought** <note>original: "Gedankending"; a philosophical term for something that exists only in the mind and not in reality.</note>, like your tension between chemical substances that grows with heat. Then, surely, a rope would also only break in one's thoughts. I mean, however, that the growing tension—the tendency toward separation—has manifested as an increasing pulling apart of the parts, which finally progressed until it snapped; that is, the particles already began to move further apart as the tension grew (one can prove this with a **compass** <note>A mathematical tool for measuring distance between two points.</note>, by placing its points on any two spots), until the distance even between the two closest particles becomes entirely visible and permanent. 

But your chemical substances are supposed to fill the exact same space, completely unseparated and unchanging despite the growing tendency to part, until suddenly the rupture occurs. Show me where there is ever any growing tension, a growing pressure—in a word, a growing tendency—that finally breaks out into a visible result without having previously manifested a detectable effect in that direction, something more than a mere creature of thought. Even a kettle is stretched across its surface by growing pressure before it bursts; that is, its parts are moved further and further from one another until the distance breaks out somewhere into a visible crack between two neighboring parts. Even the mental tendencies of a human being, before they erupt into external actions, act with palpable power on the human body: the heart can leap from them. What, then, does that tendency to decompose the body—growing with heat—actually mean, if it produces no effect in its own direction? In my view: nothing at all.

If we look closer, we find that most of the levers with which the **dynamic view** <note>The scientific theory that matter is composed of forces rather than solid particles.</note> operates in the realm of the infinitesimal are such "things of thought" (Gedankendinge). They are expected to perform real work without being anything real themselves. And if only they were merely things of thought; but if one gets to the bottom of them, they are not even based on real, or at least not clear, ideas.

Let us add another consideration regarding the organic world. How does the **dynamist** <note>A proponent of dynamism (the theory of forces).</note> conceive of the development of a living creature from a **seed** <note>original: "Keime"; can refer to a germ, embryo, or seed.</note>, and specifically the possibility that from seeds which appear so homogenous and uniform—serving as the origin of organisms—such a great variety of organs, such an infinite diversity of...

<summary>The author argues that physical "tendencies" or "tensions" must involve the actual movement of particles (atoms) rather than being abstract forces, using the analogy of a bursting kettle. He transitions to questioning how the dynamic view could possibly explain the complex development of living organisms from simple seeds.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument for an "atomistic" view of life and matter. On the previous page, the author used the example of a rope or a kettle breaking under pressure to show that physical changes require a real, measurable movement of parts, rather than being a sudden "leap" of invisible forces. Here, he extends this logic to biology, arguing that the complex development of an organism from an egg is only explainable if the egg contains a hidden, physical structure of atoms.</meta>

### IV. Some specific reasons for atomism.

...can arise in creatures. The first beginning of all animals is an egg, which seems to have a very simple and essentially identical construction to any other, and the dynamic view <note>The "dynamic view" (dynamische Ansicht) was a 19th-century philosophical stance that saw matter as a continuous field of forces rather than discrete particles.</note> is unable to provide any concept of how one egg might differ essentially and significantly from another in a way that matters for its later development. 

But the more profound atomistic view allows us to see in every egg—no less than in every crystal—a significant, hidden, and delicate structure <note>original: "Gliederung"</note> that is uniquely organized for every individual egg. This structure does not tend toward permanent states of equilibrium as it does in a crystal, but rather contains within it the entire blueprint <note>original: "Anlage"</note> for future development. The atomistic view can see every later-developing organ as already preconditioned—not by a specific, individual atom, but by a specific arrangement of atoms. 

When one remembers what countless different arrangements even a few points allow, and that with every different position of atoms, different relationships of force are established between them—and that the distance between atoms makes possible the development of the freest movements even in the smallest space—it is then easily understood how, through a small expenditure of atoms originally provided for each egg, infinitely varied outcomes and impulses of living development can be set in motion. This occurs without the quality or quantity of the atoms needing to be different at all. 

Even between the fully developed organisms of the lion and the ox, we cannot rightfully seek the essential difference in either the quality or the proportions of their physical components, but only in the different composition of the organs and tissues formed from them. Why then should we not look for the cause of this already in the germ <note>original: "Keim"; here meaning the embryo or the very earliest stage of a living thing.</note>? But since this cause cannot be found in what is visible, we must pursue it into the realm of the invisible. For the dynamic view, however, everything here dissolves into monotony. It is true that the eggs of different organisms are visibly not entirely identical; yet it is certain that the visible differences in the organisms cannot be intelligibly derived from the visible differences in the eggs. If one moves toward the invisible, however, one arrives precisely at the atomistic concept—if one wishes to arrive at any concept at all that makes the connection between the germ and its development traceable.

To be sure, the dynamic view also has its aids; one...

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of the "dynamic view" of nature, specifically regarding biology. While the previous page argued that physical changes require a structure of moving parts, this page focuses on the "germ" or egg, arguing that the vast differences between species (like a lion versus a fish) cannot be explained by invisible forces acting on a uniform substance, but must instead be rooted in a precise arrangement of atoms within the egg.</meta>

### IV. Some specific reasons for atomism.

One airy notion always leans upon another. Could one not say that the initially undifferentiated unity of the egg divides itself into manifold organs and systems? But why in one egg this way, and in another that way? The reason for this must already exist within the egg itself. The idea that an egg—simply because it is slightly rounder, larger, thicker, or fattier than another (which are roughly the only visible differences)—develops into a lion, a mouse, a fish, or a tapeworm is, in any case, not very easy to grasp. One wants to see a cause that is proportional to such a significantly different result. According to the atomistic view, this is truly the case; for the differentiated structure <note>original: "Gliederung"</note> of organs and parts in the adult organism corresponds to a distinct structure of molecules and atoms within the germ <note>original: "Keim"</note>. The *dynamic view* <note>A 19th-century philosophical stance, often associated with German Idealism, which viewed matter as a continuous field of forces rather than discrete particles.</note> has nothing to substitute for this except obscure concepts.

To the *atomist*, the emergence of an *organic form* is comparable, in a sense, to the emergence of a *curve*, for which just as many points—and only as many—are mapped out from the beginning as are sufficient to determine the curve according to its *mathematical equation* <note>original: "Curvengleichung"</note>. For the *dynamic view*, it is essentially always the same uniform inkblot that differentiates itself into the most varied forms.

Perhaps some proponents of dynamism call upon the "Idea" as a god from the machine <note>original Latin: "deus ex machina," a theatrical device where a god is lowered onto the stage to resolve a plot; used here to describe an illogical or lazy explanation.</note> to explain how different organisms develop from relatively similar germs. But the study of nature <note>original: "Naturforschung"</note> simply cannot reconcile itself with such "Ideas"—which are supposedly capable of developing different things from the same material conditions, or the same thing from different conditions. And a philosophy that sees all of nature merely as the expression, the manifestation, or the activity of an "Idea" can fundamentally tolerate this no better. For why should the germ of such an organism bear the stamp <note>original: "Gepräge"</note> of a distinct "Idea" any less than its later development does? Rather, one should demand that the expression here be at its sharpest, most concise, and most pregnant. After all, in the draft of a drama or the sketch for a painting, all the main features stand out more sharply and starkly against one another than they do in the final execution. Yet the dynamic view substitutes the dullest possible expression for what should be the sharpest possible manifestation of an individual creature's "Idea" at the start of its development.

Enough of this dispute against such philosophical notions—

...interpretations. One would never reach an end with them. Let us remember that our task should not at all be to involve ourselves in the confusion of philosophical perspectives in order to save <term>Atomism</term><note>The theory that all matter is composed of small, discrete, indivisible particles.</note> by fighting them; nothing is to be gained in this way. Instead, we must demonstrate that in order to have a clear, sustainable science of physical things that is true to its principles—and every era will desire such a science—one must adhere to atomism. This, and this alone, is what we have sought to achieve thus far.

## **V. Aesthetic Point of View. Rebuttal of the charge that atomism carries with it or favors a fragmented or materialistic worldview.**

It has always been easier and more common for the philosopher to make atomism distasteful to aesthetic judgment by labeling it as coarse, materialistic, mechanical, soulless, playful, or arbitrary, rather than to refute it through scientific reasons. However, turning this weapon back against them is no less easy. Admittedly, nothing can indisputably be decided in this way; however, since the opponent so enjoys treading this path, feels himself most at an advantage here, and especially loves to strike an "aesthetic terror" into those outside of philosophy regarding atomism, it must surely be permissible to meet him on this path as well with a few words. This shall be done such that, instead of indulging in generalities, we do nothing but place the relationships of both views clearly <note>original: "anschaulich"—referring to something that can be visualized or perceived intuitively</note> in opposition to one another; for intuition <note>original: "Anschauung"</note> is, after all, the ground of aesthetic judgment. And such a judgment cannot be considered entirely indifferent; for "beauty is indeed the sister of truth." We therefore no longer ask: how does atomism validate and prove itself upon the foundations of experience to the examining and sifting intellect, or to combining and judging reason? Instead, we ask simply: how does it appear and appeal to the healthy gaze? How does it speak to the spirit that senses truth within beauty? From this new point of view, let us briefly revisit the relationships already discussed.

## **V. Aesthetic Point of View. Rebuttal of the charge that atomism carries with it or favors a fragmented or materialistic worldview.**

And so, I first ask the <term>Dynamist</term><note>A proponent of 'Dynamism,' a 19th-century philosophical view that matter is composed of continuous forces rather than discrete particles.</note>—who scolds the fragmented nature of the world of atoms by confusing its transparent structure with fragmentation: is it really a more beautiful and edifying view of the world to think of it as filled with a continuous, glued-together mass <note>original: "Klebwerk"</note>, where nothing truly separates, and nothing has room to move out of the way unless it crushes its neighbor? The heavens, in whose order we see the pinnacle of beauty and sublimity, with its individual orbs that keep as far from one another as possible, surely provide us with a different example. Why does the Dynamist not also call the heavens a disconnected, coarse, materialistic, mechanical, dead, or trite arrangement? The heavens are nothing but an atomistic system on a large scale, just as the physicist’s <term>cosmos</term><note>From the Greek 'kosmos,' meaning both 'order' and 'ornament.' The author suggests the micro-world is as beautifully ordered as the stars.</note> is a decorative structure on a small scale.

<margin>9.</margin>
According to the atomistic view, every body is a system, organizing and sub-organizing itself into larger and smaller groups, and finally into particles that, through effective forces, hold themselves in distance, order, and suspension against one another. Everything is individualized down to the smallest detail, yet joined into the most durable whole. Every impulse to a body stimulates each of its particles into a specific movement; but the relationships of force and law in which they all stand link the oscillations of all into a communal and successive dance, to which sound joins itself, playing out on a small scale what is imitated with meaning and feeling on a large scale in the human realm. Every sound that simply rings out into space testifies to nothing more than the unison of the movements of all. The <term>harmony of the spheres</term><note>The ancient philosophical concept that the movements of celestial bodies create a divine form of music.</note>, a myth in the great world, is realized here in the small. The "impact" <note>original: "Stoss"</note> in this world is not a true impact at all in the Dynamist’s sense, upon which he bases the concept of mechanism; he can find that only in his own world. In the world of atoms, every particle gracefully steps aside or retreats, depending on whether there is more space here or there when another approaches, and temporarily yields its place; it is precisely in the dynamic world where neighboring things collide, squeeze, and crush each other. Yet even if the particle gives up its place temporarily, it also demands it back and reoccupies it, so long as the general order of the thing does not break or permanently shift (elasticity and its limits). But they also move together harmoniously, now closer, now further apart, depending on—

<margin>Light.</margin>
How bright it is in all the spaces of atomism! As in a <term>Crystal Palace</term><note>Likely a reference to the famous glass exhibition hall built in London in 1851, symbolizing transparency and modern order.</note>, one sees through nothing but windows. That which we call a window only there possesses true windows; that which we call dark still shines with an inner brightness.

The **Spirit** <note>original: "Geist," which can mean mind, spirit, or intellect.</note> steps forth and asks: "What have I to do with you?" and the atoms say: "We submit our individualities to your unity; the Law is the commander of our hosts, but you are the King in whose service he leads them."

All of this—so organically joined and knotted, structured down to the last detail, individualized to the finest degree, elaborated, ordered, developing an infinite fullness of activity, tangible in its concepts and conceptually determined in its tangibility, offering itself to every clear construction, submitting to the rule of Number, Law, and finally Spirit—flows together into a "lump" for the <term>Dynamist</term>'s intuition and evaporates for his concept into general, ambiguous, and fragmented abstractions. And these are no empty insinuations, but can be demonstrated to be so. And if he calls all of that a mere "trifle" because it is indeed, for his edification and contemplation, still joyful and...

# V. Aesthetic Point of View. Rejection of the Accusation, etc.

it seems delightful that the child might still want to play with the atomistic building blocks; yet there is a deep seriousness in this seemingly childish game, for the most earnest and rigorous considerations have led to it, and the greatest things in nature are mirrored only in the smallest things of nature. How often, however, does one extreme link itself to another. I say that the most earnest and rigorous considerations have led to **that** which the <term>Dynamist</term><note>The "Dynamists" were 19th-century thinkers, often influenced by Romantic "Naturphilosophie," who argued that matter was not made of solid particles but was instead a continuous manifestation of forces.</note> would like to dismiss as a mere trifle, because it corresponds only to the play of things, and not to the play of his own concepts. Or do the Dynamists wish to claim that the research of a Laplace, Poisson, Fresnel, Cauchy, W. Weber, and so many other mathematicians and physicists who have walked this path, are less rigorous than their own—that they walked it merely for the sake of play?

If we look from the world of the smallest things into the larger world—the one we do not need to first construct through deduction and hypothesis, the one in which we dwell and with which we interact vividly through direct observation—the advantage of the atomistic principle reveals itself everywhere and immediately. The atomistic system does nothing more than take **that** which appears so excellent to us on a large scale and develops it down to the smallest scale, pursuing it to its lower limit and, thereby, its ultimate foundation.

<margin><unclear>Sense</unclear></margin> A row of columns with individual pillars is surely more beautiful than a continuous, solid wall; a piano with individual keys and strings is capable of richer harmonies and melodies than a wooden board, a beam, or a rope; a book with individual pages is more insightful and full of content than a lump of papier-mâché! Indeed, when one sees how even the flow of melodies—the unified harmonies of music themselves—do not emerge from something fluid, but rather atomistically from notes set apart from one another, and how the entire scale only progresses through such steps; how the spirit, the most unified being that exists, attaches itself outwardly to a collection of discrete letters and inwardly to a collection of discrete brain fibers; how in humanity, people stand atomistically against one another, and an army, governed by a single spirit, nonetheless consists only of individual persons; how every human step and word again follows the atomistic principle; one should surely learn to value the atomistic principle differently through such <margin>M</margin> examples than the Dynamist does. All beauty, all unity, all power, all spirit of things clearly depends on the atomistic...

# V. Aesthetic Point of View. Rejection of the Accusation, etc.

5*

Principle: The philosopher calls atoms sand, yet they are the letters of things. <note>This is a classical allusion, famously used by Lucretius, comparing atoms to the letters of an alphabet: just as a few letters can be rearranged to form entirely different words and stories, a few types of atoms can form the entire variety of the universe.</note>

Now, indeed, atoms can create neither the beauty, nor the unity, nor the power, nor the spirit of the world through themselves alone; rather, they can merely provide the foundation for all of this; they are nothing but the most submissive substrate; yet it is nonetheless evident that they are the most suitable substrate. The <term>Dynamist</term><note>A "Dynamist" in 19th-century philosophy believed that matter was continuous and composed of forces rather than discrete particles.</note>, however, wants to bind matter directly through itself, and now reproaches the <term>Atomist</term>—who looks for the bond only further up—with splintering the world.

<margin>B</margin>
Indeed, if one listens to the Dynamist, one can certainly easily believe that the dispute between the dynamic and atomistic views is essentially a dispute over the coherence or lack of coherence in the world; indeed, the Dynamist likes to place a primary focus of the dispute here. Now, against some older and especially the oldest conceptions of atomism, the reproach that the world thereby becomes a disconnected aggregate may actually be justified; but anyone who still wanted to fight against this today would only be fighting against crumbling windmills. <note>A reference to Cervantes' *Don Quixote*, implying that critics of atomism are attacking an obsolete, "straw man" version of the theory.</note>

<margin>3</margin>
This reproach does not apply to the modern atomism of physicists; the old grain from which it grew has long since perished. To be sure, it finds no coherence *within* matter, nor does it seek it there, but rather, as already indicated, *above* matter—in a law that governs all matter and its motion, weaving together their connection of forces; is this not coherence enough in its own field? And in doing so, it does not prevent one—as we have just asserted—from still acknowledging the differently or higher-situated viewpoint of the connection of existence in spiritual unity; only that, as a doctrine of the physical, it does not have to concern itself with this. Atoms as such remain only elements of the physical world, so physics has to use them only for constructions of the physical; yet it does not therefore deny spirit and God, and only demands in return that one does not deny atoms for the sake of God and spirit—for they contain no contradiction to them, but rather pave the ground upon which the Highest stands and walks.

In any case, there must be ways to reconcile a strictly atomistic view with the view of the most thoroughgoing teleological <note>Teleology is the study of purpose or design in nature, rather than just mechanical causes.</note>, force-based, and spiritual coherence, since I myself realize this possibility in my views. # V. Aesthetic Point of View. Rejection of the Accusation, etc.

If one contradicts these views, they contradict other views in return.

Now that we already have a bond for the world first in time, then in space, then in motion, then in force and law, then in spirit and God, one would think it were finally enough; and finally, after all these bonds, it would be desirable, even necessary, to assume something that *is* bound. And though many things in the world may bind one another reciprocally, God is nonetheless the one who absolutely encompasses everything in His bond; in contrast to Him, one can now also expect and demand something that, having no bond of its own, is only there to be gathered into the bond, thereby simultaneously granting the bonds of the world firm and ultimate points of attachment. These are the atoms. What does the <term>Dynamist</term><note>In 19th-century natural philosophy, a "Dynamist" argued that matter was continuous and composed of forces, rather than discrete particles.</note> have in their place? Thus, the contrast between the atomistic and the dynamic world is, in truth, not that the former is a fragmented being and the latter a bound being, but rather that the former is a being articulated down to the last detail, while the latter is a blurred being; the former is a being with a firm skeletal structure, the latter is a mollusk.

What, then, distinguishes a living human from a marble or earthenware statue? Primarily, and indisputably, by the fact that the former has spirit, soul, and voluntary movement, while the latter does not. But also by something else: by the fact that the former is organized and articulated down to the innermost and finest details, while the latter shows only a superficially visible articulation and jointing. And who can doubt that the human spirit itself is essentially connected to such organization? In the relationship between the atomistic and dynamic world, however, one finds—conceived only more deeply—exactly the same relationship between the articulation of an organism and a potter’s statue. In the former, articulation extends into the infinitesimal; in the latter, it stops at a boundary visible to the naked eye in rough terms. What use is it now to treat the statue with beautiful words; it remains a dead, un-unlocked <note>Original: *unaufgeschlossenes*. In this context, it suggests a work whose internal potential or structure has not been "opened up" or developed.</note> work, because it lacks internal refinement, articulation, and individual vitality.

The dynamic view recognizes the cell; every organism is a connection of an unspeakably large number of cells. Thus, it is not contrary to the idea of an organism to be linked together from individual parts. Philosophers have always considered the world to be merely a more general organism like our own.
