# V. Aesthetic Point of View. Rejection of the Accusation, etc.

...by means of the **atomistic view**, down to a finer articulation. The **dynamic view** rises just as high, but in its attempt to descend to the same depth, it sinks into the swamp of **continuous matter**.

The philosopher might say against this: however much your atoms appear linked by law and spirit, the concepts of atom, law, and spirit themselves are not linked; they can only be brought into external connection by the atomist, and such a connection can only be demonstrated or justified by him empirically and externally. Only the **dialectical-conceptual connection** is a true, essential connection. But if the atomistic connection can indeed be shown or justified empirically—as we believe we have done in the preceding pages—and the task of **physics** as such goes no further, then it must also be possible to show and justify it conceptually. If **philosophy** does not do this, it is simply not doing its duty; and if it is unable to do so, it thereby demonstrates its own incompetence, proving that it stands in a void and builds from the void into the void. But suppose the dialectical connection in the modern sense—which some of our opponents undoubtedly have in mind as the only significant and essential one—were really to be recognized as such: what obstacle would there be to bringing **atomism** into this connection too, and expressing it in the formulas of this method? The concept of the **discontinuity** of matter is not in the least bit more resistant to the dialectical process than that of **continuity**; a single strike of the <term>dialectical seesaw</term><note>German: *dialektische Wippe*. A satirical metaphor for the way Hegelian-style dialectical philosophy can pivot between opposing concepts to reach a desired conclusion.</note> is enough to turn the one into the other. Everyone has the strikes, the number, and the sequence of them entirely in their own hand. According to experience thus far, the dialectician is generally like God in this respect: his power reaches as far as his will. Indeed, let us ask the dialecticians themselves on their conscience whether they were not already clear about the result regarding the question of atoms even before applying the dialectical process. Thus, it is not the dialectical process that leads them—the whirls of which they seem and believe to follow like <term>table-turners</term><note>German: *Tischrückenden*. A reference to the 19th-century craze for spiritualist séances where participants claimed a table moved by spirit power, though they were often unconsciously pushing it themselves.</note> following the circular movements of the table—while it actually only follows their secret impulse. Whether to the right or to the left, one is as easy as the other.

In fact, now that the scheme and the freedom of the dialectical process are no secret, I do not see the slightest difficulty,

# V. Aesthetic Point of View. Rejection of the Accusation, etc.

...the binding law, the binding spirit, as higher **negations** or **sublations** <note>German: *Aufhebungen*. A central Hegelian term meaning to simultaneously cancel, preserve, and lift up a concept to a higher level.</note> of an inherently discontinuous <term>Hyle</term><note>Greek: *ὕλη*. Primordial matter or "stuff."</note>, and to dialectically derive this matter itself as a negation of the empty spatial continuum in some sort of sequence of stages—just as dialectical derivations are wont to do. Indeed, instead of contradicting dialectical derivation, **atomism** could almost entice one toward it; one can arrive at it so easily through negations that seem to be pulled from the very depths of metaphysical concepts, if one only wishes to do so. To be sure, no other dialectician who has already taken a different path would give any credit to our derivation; but in doing so, they would achieve exactly the same result that all dialectical derivations have achieved against one another thus far—those of the **Master of Dialectic** <note>A reference to Georg Wilhelm Friedrich Hegel (1770–1831).</note> included—and would thereby prove all the more that it is in the spirit of true dialectics.

Thus, one should not interpret as an "incapacity" of atomism what is, in fact, merely an "unwillingness" on our part. This unwillingness stems from the fact that we do not care to use a method with which one can reach not only atomism, but generally *anything* one wants. For the **negation of the concept** is a "night in which not only are all cows black," <note>A famous critique Hegel leveled against Schelling, suggesting that if all distinctions are dissolved into the "Absolute," everything becomes indistinguishable.</note> but into which one can also "blacken in" all the cows, and thus, after the "re-sublation" back into the light of day, find the concept enriched by them; however, it was not the sublation and re-sublation from day to night and night to day that brought the enrichment. Rather, the "cows" had to reproduce in their own way to get there, and then, of course, they easily go wherever they are driven.

In any case, the fact that atomism has not yet been dialectically grounded proves as little against it as it proves against the **undulation theory** <note>Wave theory of light.</note> that the same has not yet happened to it. The undulation theory will nonetheless keep its place, and both are doctrines of an equal and internally coherent order.

But why have all dialecticians unanimously disdained engaging with atomism? Indisputably because the demand for **fixed concepts** which atomism makes—the firm points of connection for observation which it provides—do not suit the "fluidity" of the method; because the atoms lie in its way like stones. One may find other reasons; but they all signify an "incapacity" of atomism just as little as [the previous reasons].

<summary>The author defends atomism against the charge that it lacks a "dialectical" foundation, arguing that the dialectical method is so flexible it can be used to prove anything and mocks Hegel's "night in which all cows are black" metaphor.</summary>

# V. Aesthetic Point of View. Rejection of the Accusation, etc.

...to be proven dialectically, as much as it is simply the impossibility of reconciling the spirit of **atomism** <note>The theory that the universe is composed of small, discrete particles (atoms) and empty space.</note> with the spirit of the **dialectical method**. And in this lies no proof against atomism, but rather only the conflict with atomism. That the atomistic view is no more materialistic than the **dynamic** <note>The "Dynamic" view (often associated with Kant and Schelling) suggests that matter is not made of solid particles but is a product of forces (attraction and repulsion) filling space continuously.</note> one should be self-evident, insofar as according to the atomistic view, matter merely floats finely distributed in space; there is more of the empty than the full (since the *dimensions* of the atoms are assumed to be very small, or even vanishingly so, compared to the distances between them). In contrast, according to the dynamic view, space is entirely filled with matter. In the dynamic world, space itself has no place "before" matter, and the spirit—instead of being able to fly freely through space with ever-new resting points, as in atomism—can only wade through it.

To be sure, between the **celestial bodies**, <term>Weltkörper</term> <note>Literally "world-bodies," referring to planets and stars.</note> most dynamists will also admit a void of ponderable matter and—provided they do not attribute a material nature to the imponderable—a void of matter in general. They assume only that "reaching-across" of forces from one celestial body to another, which the atomist not only admits but *demands* <note>original: "fodert," an archaic spelling of "fordert."</note> between his atoms in exactly the same sense. But it is all the worse for the "look" of the dynamic view if the harsh contrast between completely compact clumps and completely empty space is admitted on a large scale and yet denied on a small scale; that is to say, it keeps the view of the world quite crude; it means hewing the world out of blocks. In the atomistic view, by contrast, the celestial bodies are not compact clumps, but merely tighter systems within wider systems. The dynamist should indeed consider that by rejecting a finer atomism, he falls prey to the crudest kind. Or would it be a finer, more ideal view to assume compact, space-filling atoms as large as celestial bodies, rather than atoms of indeterminable smallness? And that, in the end, is what the entire *material difference* <term>sachliche Unterschied</term> between the *dynamic* and *atomistic* systems comes down to.

To this, the *dynamist* likely says: for us, no harsh contrast exists from the outset between the celestial bodies and the force-filled space between them, because it is the same forces that form matter in space which then also act through space. But the counter-question arises: what then prevents him from applying the same consideration to the world of atoms, which

# V. Aesthetic Point of View. Rejection of the Accusation, etc.

...prompted him to demand a continuous, compact filling of space <term>Raumerfüllung</term> within the realm of the smallest things, such that every point could serve as a center of force <term>Kraftcentrum</term>; yet, in the realm of the large, he is satisfied with space-filling across wide distances through forces that are not tangible, such that the points in the intervening space cannot serve as centers of force. This is the point of contention. If the Dynamist <note>Followers of "Dynamism" (like Kant or Schelling) argued that matter is not made of particles, but is a continuous field of forces—attraction and repulsion—filling all space.</note> also required only a "reaching-across" of forces from one center to another in the world of atoms—just as exists between celestial bodies—then he would be in complete agreement with us, for we also admit this kind of space-filling in the atomic world. However, everyone will agree with us that these two types of space-filling can be distinguished from a factual standpoint and must, therefore, be distinguished.

Now, of course, the Dynamist believes he has an advantage over the Atomist <note>One who believes matter is composed of discrete, individual particles separated by empty space.</note> because he ultimately dissolves matter into forces of a spiritual or conceptual nature. But this does not affect the question of atoms at all, and—if it were otherwise permissible—would be no more hindered by atomism than by astronomy. In fact, since the great "celestial atoms" <note>The author is comparing planets and stars to atoms to show that scale doesn't change the logic of the argument.</note> submit to this dissolution for the Dynamist, the small "body-atoms" could offer no obstacle to this dissolution either. A grain of sand is just as easily—indeed, more easily—dissolved chemically by the same means as a large pebble or rock. The physicist, however, as such, does not concern himself at all with a philosophical dissolution of matter, any more than it would occur to a builder to chemically dissolve his sand and his bricks; that is the business of the chemist, just as the former may be the business of the philosopher.

But does it not involve a strong materialism <note>In this 19th-century context, "materialism" was often a derogatory label for any theory that seemed to deny the primacy of the spirit or soul.</note> when the Dynamist conceives of his forces in such a way that they "cross over" into matter? By seeking to spiritualize matter through the construction of matter out of ideal forces, or by the suspension of ideal forces into matter, he "materializes" the spiritual and the ideal. The forces of the physicist, by contrast, are entirely incommensurable <note>Meaning they cannot be compared or converted into one another; they exist in different "categories."</note> with matter and can never give birth to matter from their womb, nor cross over into it; for him, all forces are ultimately reduced to auxiliary concepts <term>Hülfsbegriffe</term> for the representation of the laws of equilibrium and motion. According to these laws, new positions and movements flow from previous ones, or previous ones are maintained. They are merely relational concepts <term>Relationsbegriffe</term> which, by their nature, presuppose something between which the relation exists. (More on this in the eighth chapter.)

# VI. Objection that Atomism Only Postpones the Difficulty.

I believe I can finally close this section no better than with the following passage from a letter by Prof. W. Weber <note>Wilhelm Eduard Weber (1804–1891), a prominent German physicist whose work on electrodynamics and atomic structure was foundational for 19th-century science.</note> to me, in which he, for his part, rejects the accusation of a "coarse materialism" within atomism—a point to which we shall find cause to refer again in the future.

> "It is a matter," he says, "of isolating such a constant part in the causes of movements that the remainder, while variable, can be thought of as dependent only on measurable relationships of space and time. In this way, one arrives at a concept of **mass** <term>Masse: here used to mean a constant physical property rather than 'stuff'</term>, to which the idea of spatial extension does not necessarily cling at all. Consequently, the size of atoms in the atomistic way of thinking is by no means measured by spatial extension, but rather by their mass—that is, according to the constant ratio for each atom in which the **force** <term>Kraft</term> always stands in relation to **acceleration** <term>Beschleunigung</term>. The concept of mass (as well as that of atoms) is thereafter just as little crude and materialistic as the concept of force; rather, it is to be placed on a completely equal footing with it in terms of refinement and intellectual clarity."

## VI. Objection that Atomism Only Postpones the Difficulty.

One levels the accusation against the atomistic view that it only pushes back a difficulty of observation, which ultimately remains at the heart of the matter. For, after one has arrived at atoms, the question arises again: what do the atoms consist of? Perhaps of smaller atoms with new intervening spaces? And what do these smaller atoms consist of? Finally, one must eventually arrive at continuous masses or at nothing; why not simply stop at the continuous masses that we see?

It will be shown that the alternative does not exist at all in the way it is posed here. Rather, the conclusion of physical atomism into a philosophical one—through a fundamental concept <note>The author likely refers to the "monad" or a similar metaphysical unit.</note> that no philosopher has yet declared invalid—is neglected here. However, for now, we are dealing only with that physical atomism which indeed still lacks such a conclusion, as it exists in today's exact science, which

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of the atomistic viewpoint against philosophical objections, specifically addressing the claim that atomism merely postpones the problem of infinite divisibility. It transitions from the previous page's introduction of physicist Wilhelm Weber's perspective into a broader justification of why physicists rely on discrete particles rather than a continuous "stuff" of matter.</meta>

...one should neither confuse the physical concept with a philosophical one, nor hold it to be contradictory; it is enough if it merely lies on the path toward it.

And so the physicist replies: we do not stop immediately at the continuous masses we see because it does not depend at all on our arbitrary choice where we must stop, and one must not abandon the next necessary step just because it might not yet be the final one. The accusation would only be just if physics, with its atoms as discrete small masses into which coarser ones are organized, intended to have truly established the absolute finality or to have philosophically unlocked the concept of matter. Instead, physics merely intends to establish the physically nearest reality behind the appearing continuum <term>Continuum: a continuous sequence where adjacent parts are not distinct</term> of the coarser masses, leaving it to be decided what is to be done with its atoms in the final philosophical instance. For physics has no means to decide that; however, it certainly does have the means to decide that coarser masses must first be thought of as divided into smaller, discrete parts. These parts resist any further dissolution by earthly forces, even if they might not resist primordial forces <note>original: "Urweltkräfte"</note> or the philosophical concept itself. Physical atomism wants and says nothing more.

But what does the physicist do other than continue, by logical inference, the achievements of the microscope—which allows him to track the structure of matter further than the naked eye—to a limit where that inference begins to lose its basis? In contrast, the philosopher wants the structure limited exactly to the reach of the microscope. By what right? It can be asserted *a priori* <note>from first principles, independent of experience</note> that the boundary cannot be determined through pure reason at any empirical point. And since we cannot reach the upper limit with the telescope—as unresolved, or indeed still unseen, stellar nebulae <term>Sternennebel: distant celestial clouds</term> remain beyond its reach—why should we expect to reach the lower limit with the microscope or even come relatively closer to it? From the microscopic point to the mathematical point <note>a mathematical point has location but no physical size or dimension</note> it is exactly as far as it is in the opposite direction from the telescopic limit to the sphere of the universe; that is, the ratio of the radii is infinite in both cases. What worlds <margin>sq.</margin> of discrete points might still lie within the microscopic sphere of points? Should this vast space be "stuffed" with matter rather than "animated" by it? Indeed, herein lies the brief difference between the dynamic and the atomistic view.

<summary>The author defends the physicist's use of atoms as a logical extension of microscopic observation, arguing that the "continuum" of matter we see is merely an appearance. He posits that there is an infinite space for structure between a physical particle and a mathematical point, mirroring the infinite scale of the cosmos.</summary>

# VI. Objection that atomism only pushes the difficulty back. 75

If there are beings who stand as high above the celestial bodies <term>Celestial bodies: planets, stars, and other large cosmic entities</term> as we stand above atoms, then the philosophers among them might also say: why bother breaking the universe down into celestial bodies at all, since the question only begins anew as to whether these bodies can be further divided? Let us rather stop immediately at the idea of a continuous world. It is evident, however, that their explanation of phenomena—which depend on the factual divisibility of the universe into distinct bodies—could not be as convincing as an explanation that takes this divisibility into account. The realization that bodies first break down into atoms, regardless of what those atoms themselves are made of, is therefore not a mere "pushing back" of the difficulty. Rather, it is progress and a mastery of the difficulty up to the point where the problem can no longer be resolved, at least for the physicist; but also where no interest remains for him to resolve it, insofar as no further influence on phenomena is perceptible.

If one reproaches philosophy because, after speculating so much about light, magnetism, and electricity, it nonetheless failed to recognize the undulations <note>original: "Undulationen"; referring to the wave-theory of light</note> of light, or even remotely sense the factual relationships between electricity and magnetism which modern physics has brought to light—even though philosophy certainly never lacked for *word-relationships* that could not be translated into *facts* <note>original: "Facta"</note>—then the answer is ready. What other excusatory answer would be possible than that it is simply not the business of philosophy to pursue the forms of the empirical <term>Empirical: knowledge gained through observation and experiment rather than theory alone</term> into *special* determinations; it reserves for itself *only* the *General* and the *Ultimate* <note>original: "das Allgemeine und Letzte"</note>. Why not admit the same regarding the structure of bodies beyond the visible continuum <term>Continuum: a continuous sequence where adjacent parts are not distinct</term>? This, too, belongs to the more specific determinations of empirical forms, even if it is no longer directly observable itself, just as it stands on exactly the same level and in the closest connection with the undulations of light and the finer phenomena of heat. The physicist willingly admits that he cannot recognize the "Ultimate" behind the empirical; if only the philosophers would admit that they cannot recognize the *nearest* <note>original: "das Nächste"; meaning the immediate physical structure just beneath the surface of appearance</note> thing behind it!

One should not take offense at the name "atoms," which in any case is not used by everyone. What the physicist requires as an atomist are generally only discrete masses, which for us are ultimately no further divisible, into which bodies, or initially the

He critiques philosophy for failing to predict physical discoveries and suggests that science and philosophy should respect each other's boundaries regarding the "ultimate" versus the "immediate" structure of matter.</summary>

# VI. Objection that atomism only pushes the difficulty back.

...to decompose the **molecule** of the body; whether they are in themselves still divisible is, as said, not his business to judge. It could possibly be the same as with the **celestial bodies** <term>Celestial bodies: planets, stars, and other large cosmic entities</term>, which in relation to one another are true atoms, because there are no forces to transfer something from one to the other; yet they are divisible in themselves. In general, the analogy of atoms with celestial bodies presents itself to the atomist in many ways and from various points of view, and we have already often found occasion to refer to it, although it was neither this analogy that led him to atoms, nor can it currently be properly judged by him how far it extends.

Thus, the physicist cannot even dare to assert that the space between his atoms is absolutely empty, or that a fine, continuous essence does not still extend between them, which simply no longer has any influence on the phenomena he can judge; just as the **ether** extends between celestial bodies, having no noticeable influence on their movements, and which could be denied if it were not recognized through light. Even between the *discrete ether atoms*, which the physicist still needs to represent the phenomena of light, there could therefore still be a finer continuous ether according to all his experiments and calculations. The physicist simply does not speak of such possibilities, which are indifferent to him because they do nothing for his work. If, however, they can do something for the philosopher, it is his business to deal with them. And it would be achievement enough for him if they enabled him to reconcile himself with the exact sciences. The physicist only needs atoms *first*, not atoms *last*. <note>The author is suggesting that atoms are the starting point for physical calculation, but not necessarily the final "truth" of existence.</note> If the philosopher concedes to the physicist his atoms *first*, the latter can gladly concede to him his **fulfillment of space** *last*; the two do not contradict each other. But what, under such a concession from the physicist—which costs him nothing since he gives up nothing by it—does the philosopher still have to oppose to his atoms, since he ultimately denies them only because they conflict with his concepts of space-filling? I find nothing that would not then also compel him to contradict the existence of the celestial bodies, which are discrete in the same sense, yet likewise still have a fine fluid <term>Fluidum: a continuous, flowing substance like a gas or liquid</term> between them. The physicist, for his part, will have all the less reason to contradict a philosophical space-filling in the final instance,

# VI. Objection that atomism only pushes the difficulty back.

...since even the concept of philosophical space-filling lies entirely beyond his concepts. Things that do not encounter one another at all cannot collide.

Now, I do not actually mean that such a "twice-removed" continuous **ether** <term>Ether: In 19th-century physics, a hypothetical substance thought to fill all space, allowing light and heat to travel</term> between the final atoms is the **limiting concept** <note>original: "Gränzvorstellung." An idealized boundary or theoretical endpoint that a theory approaches as it becomes more refined.</note> that one should ultimately arrive at; for in truth, it is no real limiting concept in the sense of the atomistic system. Atomism undoubtedly reaches its philosophical peak not through a compromise with the dynamic view <note>The "dynamic view" refers to the theory that matter is composed of forces rather than solid particles.</note>, but only through a pure sharpening of its own logic. But fundamentally, contemporary philosophy can only turn against such a *philosophical atomism of the future* (which I will discuss in the following part), not against today's *physical atomism*. The latter knows nothing yet of our presumed limit and does not reach toward something absolutely final. Thus, it still leaves contemporary philosophy every room for the **hypostatization** <term>Hypostatization: Treating an abstract quality or force as if it were a real, physical substance</term> of its space-filling forces. Let philosophy simply not fill space with intangible, blurry concepts as long as the science of the tangible can still grasp and hold something within it.

After all, it is not denied—but rather willingly admitted from the beginning and quite often—that the whole of physical atomism is still in a state of great imperfection. A multitude of problems remain unsolved within it, which do not even arise in the philosophical consideration of things because philosophy only views them in a general and superficial way. Insofar as one wishes to use the only secure mathematical path to work backward from appearances to the specific relations of atoms, or to test hypothetical relations through calculations based on experience, one finds the methods of the mathematical and mechanical sciences themselves are not yet nearly advanced enough for this. Therefore, even among physicists themselves, very wavering ideas still prevail regarding the specific relations of atoms. Yet such ideas can quite well exist without the security of the general viewpoint itself being endangered by this uncertainty regarding details. Rather, anyone who does not mistake their own opinion of the matter for the matter itself will humbly admit that regarding shape, size, density, final constitution, the exact...

# VII. Summary of Physical Atomism.

...concerning the law of the forces of atoms, nothing specifically definite can be said as of yet, although enough is generally determined for us to draw conclusions and advantages from it, and to feel bound to the perspectives we previously identified. If atomism does not yet achieve everything one might eventually expect of it, one must remember that being "still a child" is no reproach; rather, the fact that it achieves so much even as a child allows us to expect everything from its future.

### VII. Summary of Physical Atomism.

If we now briefly summarize what we may consider established as certain, or at least with predominant probability, regarding the matter of atomism, it appears to be the following. It is only this core and foundation of atomism—consisting of interconnected propositions, demanded by the connection of the facts themselves, and consistently recognized by the most distinguished representatives of the field—to which the justification in this writing refers. For I say it once more: we have no desire to justify the rashness or absurdities that have been put forward in the name of atomism, just as they are in any other doctrine. If the philosopher wishes to attack those, we abandon them to him—but we do not abandon atomism itself.

<margin>}</margin> **Ponderable matter** <term>Ponderable matter: Matter that has measurable weight, such as solids, liquids, and gases, as opposed to weightless "imponderable" substances like light or the ether.</term> is to be conceived as being spatially divided into **discrete parts**, between which an **imponderable substance***) (Ether) [exists]...

---

<note>*) Substance here means that which reveals a constant existence through the variability and change of phenomena, provided that the phenomena can be related to something constant (mass, in the sense of the physicist; cf. p. 73). This applies to the imponderable just as much as to the ponderable (which is why in every exact mathematical representation of the imponderable, one speaks of the "mass" of the imponderable just as much as that of the ponderable). This does not prevent the philosopher, according to the needs of his own logical context, from conceiving the word substance (and if one wishes, also mass) more broadly, more narrowly, or differently. For us, all words generally only have meaning as designations for factual relationships. In general, physicists will not shy away from calling the imponderable substance "matter" as well; however, since the applicability of the concept of "matter" to the imponderable depends either on how broadly one wishes to define that concept, or on knowledge that we do not yet possess (cf. the following chapter), I consider it more cautious to apply only the general expression "substance" to the imponderable here.</note>

# VII. Summary of Physical Atomism.

is found; although uncertainty still exists in many respects regarding its nature and relationship to **ponderable matter** <term>Ponderable matter: Matter that can be weighed, such as solids, liquids, and gases.</term>, it is nonetheless to be localized in space and thought of as divided into **discrete parts** no less than the former. Between these parts, there is either an absolutely empty space or merely a "Something" which philosophy might assume for the sake of its idea of space-filling, but which has no further influence on physical phenomena and therefore cannot be considered by the physicist. Or, it fills space in a similar way to how one can say of **gravitation** that it fills and permeates space with its effectiveness, while one is nonetheless forced to link it to specific discrete centers between which it must be seen as acting. All the smallest parts (**atoms**), both those belonging to the ponderable and the **imponderable** <note>original: "Wägbaren als Unwägbaren"</note> masses, are related to one another through forces—much like the **celestial bodies**, which can be used to illustrate many of their relationships—and they obey the same most general laws of equilibrium and motion that are established in every exact system of mechanics as being valid for large and small, ponderable and imponderable masses alike. These ultimate atoms are either inherently indestructible, or at least within the realms of physics and chemistry, no means exist to destroy them, and there are no reasons to assume any destruction or liquefaction <note>original: "Verflüssigung"; here referring to the loss of discrete form or dissolving into a continuous state.</note> of them ever occurring.

From these ultimate atoms, in the realm of the ponderable, a greater or lesser number combine into small groups (so-called **molecules** or **composite atoms**), which are further apart from one another than the atoms within each individual group. This is a hierarchical scale that can be built even higher, so that smaller groups combine again into larger ones. (Those groups into which a body is first divisible are often called its **integrating particles** <term>Integrating particles: A historical term for the smallest units of a substance that still retain the chemical identity of that substance, similar to the modern concept of the molecule.</term>). These composite atoms, or molecules, can indeed be **disaggregated**, and their constituent atoms can rearrange themselves into new combinations.

Followed in the opposite direction, one can say that bodies are generally structured and sub-structured into larger and smaller groups of particles, down to the ultimate atoms, of which the former are destructible, but the latter are not.

# VII. Summary of Physical Atomism.

is very large in relation to the dimensions of the atoms in question. Nothing is known of the absolute dimensions of atoms, nor even whether the ultimate atoms have specifiable dimensions. A definite shape can be attributed to **molecules** <term>Molecules: In this 19th-century context, these are "composite atoms" or groups of ultimate atoms.</term> or composite atoms as the outline of the group they comprise, but nothing is known of the shape of the ultimate atoms.

The forces of atoms are partly attractive, partly repulsive in nature; at least, thus far, no one has succeeded in reducing them to purely attractive forces. They act according to functions of the distance between the particles. The exact law of these forces is not known.

According to the assumption established by **Poisson** <note>Siméon Denis Poisson (1781–1840), a French mathematician and physicist.</note> (Journal de l'école polytechn. cah. XX), which has been discussed in more detail by **Wilhelmy** (Essay on a Mathematical-Philosophical Theory of Heat, Heidelberg 1851) and to which most physicists might now subscribe, all **ponderable atoms** <note>original: "wägbare Atome"; atoms of matter that have weight.</note> attract one another, and all **ether atoms** <term>Ether atoms: Hypothesized particles of the 'luminiferous ether,' the medium then thought to carry light and heat.</term> repel one another; attraction occurs between a ponderable atom and ether atoms. Each ponderable atom is surrounded by a condensed sphere of ether, and the repulsion occurring between these ether spheres can, under certain circumstances, outweigh the attraction of the ponderable nuclei, which results in the gaseous state of matter. Regarding attempts to completely eliminate repulsive forces, cf. the second chapter of the second part of this work.

The different temperatures shown by two units of mass of the same substance depend, according to prevailing assumptions, on the fact:
> "that the amplitudes of the oscillations performed by the intermolecular ether atoms are greater in the warmer [mass] than in the colder."

**A. Fick** <note>Adolf Fick (1829–1901), the physiologist and physicist famous for his laws of diffusion.</note> has recently attempted (*Poggendorff's Annalen* <note>The leading German physics journal of the era.</note> Vol. XC, p. 287) to show how the expansion of bodies by heat can be derived from this according to mechanical laws, if one takes the above determinations regarding the forces of ponderable and imponderable substances as a basis. And since expansion is a phenomenon of motion, the atomist is unquestionably right to subordinate it to the general principles of **mechanics** <term>Mechanics: The branch of physics dealing with the motion of objects and the forces acting on them.</term>, instead of being satisfied with the vague expression of an "expansive principle." Admittedly, such attempts can currently only be regarded as trials in a field whose difficulty does not allow for an immediate decision on their success; but in any case, they prove the scope of the atomistic principle.

Thus, one certainly sees—what has already been admitted above—that atomism in its current state still leaves much undetermined; and precisely that which the philosopher would most like to know in order to pin down the physicist: the physicist's views on the shape, size, density, and mass of the ultimate or

<meta>This page continues from page 80, completing the list of what physical atomism currently leaves undecided. It shifts from technical definitions to a defense of the author's "modest" version of atomism against philosophical critics.</meta>

# VII. Summary of Physical Atomism.

Fundamental atoms, the basic relationships of the **ponderable** <note>original: "Wägbaren"; matter that has weight and can be measured.</note> and **imponderable** <note>original: "Unwägbaren"; substances like light, heat, and electricity, which in the 19th century were often thought of as weightless fluids or "ether."</note>—these the theory leaves undecided for now, because it does not yet know how to determine them, though it is not without hope that this will succeed in the future.

This is simply the course of an empirical science that progresses steadily: to conquer the field step by step, so as not to cast uncertainty over what is already secure by striving to possess everything all at once.

Now, perhaps some philosopher will say, dissatisfied that we leave him too little to dispute—indeed, taking away his entire means of attacking **atomism** <term>Atomism: The theory that all matter is composed of tiny, indivisible particles.</term>: "How can you bring yourself to prune atomism so severely? Today's atomism is not nearly as modest as it is presented here; for here and there, in a hundred writings, there is talk of the shape and size, and of other properties of atoms. You are likely representing your own particular view of atomism by reducing it to those few points and adding an uncertainty to every certainty. But just as you are unwilling to take into account the particular views of individual philosophers, addressing yourself instead to philosophers in general, you cannot expect things to go differently for you."

But what I have established and defended here regarding atomism is by no means my own particular view; rather, it is that part of atomism upon which all atomists agree. Exactly that which they do *not* agree on—the matters of this or that particular opinion—is not being defended here, but rather left aside. And I am not alone in holding only this part of atomism as certain. In fact, the most prominent representatives and proponents of atomism likely do not consider much more than this to be certain (at any rate, they do not essentially refer to more in their research). When one wishes to defend or oppose a cause, one should not look at the average person who talks about it, but at the foremost of those who know how to use, handle, and advance it. If, therefore, the philosophers wish to dispute those physicists who prematurely decide on things that cannot yet be decided, then—to repeat—they dispute neither me nor the firm fundamental points of atomism for which I contend; rather, they dispute the very thing that I myself oppose.

<meta>This page continues the defense of "physical atomism" started on the previous page, addressing the philosophical objection that a theory which leaves certain details undecided is "worthless" or "unarguable."</meta>

### VII. Summary of Physical Atomism.

But, says the philosopher, what value does a view still have if it does not decide on the most important points? Indeed, one cannot even argue against such a view if one does not have the points in question clearly before one's eyes.

Well, if today's atomism does not yet seem valuable enough to the philosopher, the physicist for his part would also be glad if the philosopher would gift him with a more valuable one. But should he throw away the **thaler** <note>original: "Thaler"; a silver coin used throughout Europe for centuries. Here used metaphorically to mean something of small but certain value.</note> for an empty bag which, were it full, would certainly be preferable to the thaler? I believe I have sufficiently shown in the preceding pages how much value the physicist—and even the philosophical physicist—must already place on atomism, as deficient as it remains today. In fact, all the discussed advantages of this theory for the **undulation theory** <term>Undulation theory: The theory that light consists of waves rather than particles, which was the dominant scientific view in the 19th century.</term>, the connection of heat phenomena, and the treatment of phenomena related to the **fundamental constitution** of ponderable bodies, are dependent only on the assumption of the **discreteness** <note>original: "Discretion"; in this context, it means that matter is composed of separate, distinct particles rather than being a continuous, solid mass.</note> of the parts of matter and ether and those most general determinations associated with it. They are not dependent on a more precise determination of how the ultimate parts behave in terms of shape, mass, and size, nor how their concept is to be philosophically interpreted in the final instance, nor what fundamental relationship exists between the **ponderable** and **imponderable** <note>"Ponderable" refers to ordinary matter with weight; "imponderable" refers to substances like the "luminiferous ether" thought to carry light waves.</note>. The more precise explanation of the details of phenomena will, however, one day depend on such closer determinations of this view.

But if the philosopher says that one cannot argue against such an indefinite view, why then does he argue against it, if he finds it too indefinite to decide whether it is true or false? Now, however, atomism, as it has been established above, is certainly definite enough that one can make it an object of thorough examination, provided one has the prior knowledge to do so; if not, the indefiniteness lies rather on the side of those who lack that knowledge. Certainly, as it stands, it is a much more definite object of investigation than the opposing view with its dissolving concepts.

## VIII. Some General Discussions. The Concept of Matter and Physical Force.*)

<margin>Very true!</margin>
I do not believe I am mistaken when I say that in almost all disputes between philosophers, as well as those with philosophers, a good portion is a **dispute over words** <note>original: "Wortstreit"; a logomachy or an argument where people use the same words but mean different things.</note>. Incontestably, this could be avoided if, before arguing over what a given concept *is*, everyone first declared what they intend to understand by the word with which they designate the concept. In that case, the dispute would usually be reduced in the main to whether one is applying linguistic usage correctly, or has the right to abandon it. For the rest would mostly turn out to be self-evident or undecidable, and it would show that a dispute over actual facts was never even reached.

What is beautiful, what is good, what is spirit, what is soul, what is matter, what is force, what is life, what is being, appearance, freedom, will, subjective, objective, individuality, personality, plant, animal, cell, cell nucleus, and so on and so forth? Answer me first: what do you want to understand by these? Point them out to me—if not with fingers, then through words—as something that is more than just a word; go back to something where the imagination finds a foothold. If it is something abstract, show me the circle of the concrete from which it is abstracted, in such a way that the abstraction repeats itself automatically; if it is a connecting principle, show me the circle for which it serves as the bond; otherwise, one cannot speak of it at all. Then we shall look closer. You spare yourself the specific demonstration if you always the

This is because they are usually treated in an improper mixture with those points concerning the physical question of atoms itself, and it was therefore not without significance to show that atomism, even if it does not depend on those points, does not need to shy away from a discussion of them—though such a discussion must certainly take on a very different form than it does for the opponents of atomism. In the following section, these discussions would not have found a suitable connection anyway, as the philosophical side of the atomic question is pursued there from an essentially different point of view than here.

### VIII. Some General Discussions. The Concept of Matter, etc.

...keep in view what linguistic usage already points toward, then the term is defined by itself—which, admittedly, it usually is not. But you must point to *something* with the word, and one must know what it is; otherwise, no dispute or investigation into the matter is even possible.

But then, once we know what you intend to mean by those words, we can ask whether it actually exists—outwardly or inwardly—in the way you imagine and represent it. We can ask if it really possesses the specific details, causes, effects, connections, factual relationships, laws, and goals as you conceive them, and if it holds the value you assign to it—provided we first know what you mean by "value." These are the only things actually worth arguing about. Instead, people argue about what is "beautiful," what is "good," what is "spirit," "matter," "life," "being," and so on, with everyone understanding something different and wanting their own definition accepted. Thus, the dispute does not concern beauty, goodness, spirit, matter, life, or being itself, but merely the words themselves, about which people cannot agree.

In a text titled *The Solar System, or a New Theory on the Structure of the Worlds* by S. Sachs (Berlin, 1850)—in which astronomers are accused of all sorts of baseless claims <note>original: "Untriftigkeiten"; refers to arguments that lack cogency or validity.</note> and a new world system is proposed—the following passage appears on page 193:

> "Who can guarantee for us that the star which astronomers take for Uranus is actually Uranus?"

In fact, I can hardly discover any difference between this question and most of the major controversial issues around which philosophy revolves—where different systems clash harshly—if one only goes to the root of the dispute. Now, it is no wonder that if everyone understands a different planet by the same name—if one person places Neptune where another puts the Sun—the entire world system falls into doubt and dispute. One world system constantly displaces another; indeed, many struggle for the same space at once, creating a <term>reciprocal demand</term> <note>original: "Wechselfoderung"; a philosophical term implying that two opposing ideas require each other to be complete.</note> for mutual supplementation. Instead of the difficulty of discovering something new, there is only the difficulty of gaining a validity for the new discovery that extends beyond the discoverer themselves. With each new system, one thinks one is in a...

# VIII. Some General Discussions. The Concept of Matter, etc. 85

...[another] heaven, and the actual result is only that one no longer knows how to find one’s way in the old one or the other. Meanwhile, the astronomer, untouched by this, aims his telescope at the one system that always exists, and seeks to see if he might find a new citizen <note>The author uses "citizen" (Bürger) poetically to refer to a newly discovered celestial body, like a planet or moon.</note> within it.

What is the reason that philosophy stands in such a state? That one considers the foundation of all observation to be dispensable through the observation itself. We always return to this same ground. If concepts are meant to hover above things—pregnant with them before they have even been born or carried to term from them—it is impossible not to confuse words with concepts. This is because words really do, in a certain sense, accomplish what is demanded of concepts: that is, they hover above things and yield everything one wants to draw from them. To be sure, while they hover above things, they are still intended to point toward those things; but things and the relationships between things are more numerous than words. In every new position, a word can mean something else, and often must; otherwise, language would not suffice. Thus, words—and with them the concepts for which they are mistaken—become interchangeable. Finally, if one does not always return from these changeable words to unchangeable—that is, *demonstrable*—things and the relationships of things that can be shown with and within them, asking after them anew in every new context, then the dispute over words can find no end. The fact that it finds no end proves precisely that one does not go back that far. To the seemingly profound dispute over *nothing* at the end, there is added the seemingly mighty progress toward *nothing* at the end; for instead of the flow of things, the flow of the *meanings of words* now becomes the carrier of the ship of philosophy. How swiftly the voyage goes, how quickly the views change! But when the journey is over, the ship—with everything it holds—dissolves into the same stream that carried it, and a new ship passes over it toward the same goal.

Admittedly, there is a realm of thought above things, above everything individual and tangibly demonstrable—a realm of the general and the abstract. And the abstract, too, may communicate within itself; just as scents rise from flowers that stand individually and far from one another, crossing and meeting, and thereby establishing a commerce of flowers above the flowers themselves...

# VIII. Some General Discussions. The Concept of Matter, etc. 86

...found. But if the flower-smoke <note>The author uses the metaphor of "flower-smoke" (the distilled essence or vapor of blossoms) to represent philosophical concepts. If the essence is spread too thin or removed too far from the physical plant, it loses its meaning.</note>, in its greatest expansion, height, and refinement, is no longer connected with its forge <note>original: "Esse". This refers to the hearth or chimney-furnace used by a distiller or chemist.</note>, it is no longer a communion of flowers; it becomes the dead, arbitrary mixture of the distiller and the pharmacist; it becomes the procedure of philosophy with its abstract words and concepts—one no longer knows their source.

Must it be so? But let us consider mathematics in contrast to philosophy. The most abstract concepts and operations of the mathematician, with which he mediates a communion of things themselves far beyond the things themselves, are nonetheless all explainable in the final instance by what is demonstrable—the 1, the 2, the adding, the multiplying, even infinity itself, as a going further, always further, and never being finished; something negatively demonstrable insofar as an end simply cannot be shown, cannot even be imagined; therefore, an end is not to be imagined in the infinite either. For this reason, a **word-strife** <note>original: "Wortstreit". A central theme for the author, referring to empty arguments over terminology rather than reality.</note> is hardly possible in mathematics, and thus almost no dispute remains within it; it is merely a matter of new discoveries and new methods. And so I also believe that little dispute would remain in philosophy if one had overcome **word-strife**; however, little would then remain of the whole of philosophy as it has existed until now, and the actual philosophy of things would only then begin.

The philosophers argue, and things go their way; philosophy is interwoven into this course, intervenes in this course itself, helps to promote it; but that is the **One Philosophy** that passes silently through all philosophies and works through them, and would always be and remain only **One**, like natural science, if **word-strife** did not make many out of it. Now, this unity and power are not entirely lost in the strife of words, yet their clarity is thereby darkened, their strength thereby broken, their very possibility called into question. And what clarifies and promotes the world is ultimately not the feuding philosophy above the sciences, which exhausts and splits itself apart; it is the **all-unifying** philosophy within the sciences.

How much has been written about what is **necessary** for philosophy, especially in our times, when it is in such great **distress** <note>The author plays on the German words "noth thut" (is necessary) and "Noth" (distress/crisis).</note>; where has one not sought its primary defect; how many proposals for mediation and improvement has one not made; and the fundamental—

# VIII. Some General Discussions. The Concept of Matter, etc. 87

The fundamental ill upon which everything depends—or at least with which every ill in philosophy is connected, and without whose removal things can only change but never improve—is one that people hardly dare touch, for fear of exposing their own deep wounds: it is the practice of using words without a clear and definite explanation, or the ability to be explained through what is demonstrable <term>Aufzeigbares; things that can be physically pointed to or shown</term> and imaginable. People admit to the existence of this nuisance of unclear understanding and **word-strife** <note>original: "Wortstreit"; a recurring theme for the author, referring to empty arguments over terminology rather than reality.</note> as if it only mattered here and there, failing to see that the entirety of philosophy, in all its depth, is swallowed up by this very problem. They admit it as if it were a tiny splinter <note>A reference to the biblical parable from Matthew 7:3 regarding the "splinter" in another's eye and the "beam" in one's own.</note>, while they themselves contribute a massive beam to the structure; for it is from such beams that the house of philosophy is built; they are its foundation and its peak.

Philosophical systems ultimately hang upon transcendent <term>transcendent; concepts that go beyond human experience or the physical world</term> concepts such as the **thing-in-itself**, the **I**, the **Absolute**, **Being**, or **simple quality**; yet these nails are all hammered from air into air. For what do these words actually mean? Are they perhaps abstractions from the world of appearances? If so, they might mean something self-evident, but something that leads no further than the self-evident itself. However, in the transcendent sense—the sense in which they are truly meant to be understood—they mean something that either cannot be understood at all, or can be understood in infinitely different ways, thus leading into the incomprehensible or into that which is understood differently by everyone.

Has philosophy been done an injustice by all that has been said here? There must, after all, be a reason why, among the most diverse schools of philosophy, none has led to anything that commands the kind of universal understanding and recognition that mathematics and natural science enjoy. The reason cannot lie in what is contradictory between the different philosophies; it must lie in something they all share. And who can find any other reason than that their conflict itself revolves more around the relationships between words than between things? For if one looked to the things themselves, the contradictions would partly vanish in the face of the facts, and partly reveal what is still indeterminable based on those facts.

One can concede that even in the dispute between the **atomistic** <note>The view that matter consists of discrete, indivisible particles (atoms).</note> and the **dynamic** <note>The view that matter is a product of forces, such as attraction and repulsion, rather than solid particles.</note> views, as it is mostly conducted, a portion is merely **word-strife**, depending on the fact that the dynamic view uses and understands the words **force**, **matter**, and **the filling of space** <note>original: "Raumerfüllung"</note> differently than the atomistic view. Indeed, it would be a strange...

He suggests that even the debate between atomistic and dynamic views of matter is largely a misunderstanding of terminology.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that philosophical concepts often dissolve into "word-strife." # VIII. Some General Discussions. The Concept of Matter, etc. 88

[...it would be a] rare exception if, in discussions within or with philosophy on this subject, most things did not ultimately amount to a **strife over words** <note>original: "Wortstreit"; a central theme for the author, referring to empty debates about definitions rather than physical reality.</note>. We, for our part, have sought in what has preceded to reduce this strife over words to a strife over facts; we have done this by explicitly focusing not on the question of what one *wishes* to understand by those words, but rather on the question of whether that which the physicist understands by them actually exists according to the facts, in the way he understands it. 

What the physicist understands by the words he uses is subject to no ambiguity, because he always refers them to—and explains them through—that which is **demonstrable** <term>Aufzeigbares; things that can be physically pointed to, shown, or measured</term>, adhering to common usage or providing precise explanations where that usage is exceeded. To him, words signify only what can be shown in such a way or explained through such means. The atoms themselves, although not directly demonstrable to the eye as individuals (that is, in isolation), are nonetheless as easily and firmly grasped with the "mental hands" of the imagination as entire material lumps are with the physical ones. This is because they are imagined simply as small lumps following the pattern of large ones, and because the path itself is clearly imaginable by which one moves from the larger to the smaller: through continuous division, until one can go no further with the means and forces available to the physicist and chemist.

The question is, then: do these small lumps exist in **discreteness** <term>Discretion; the state of being separate or distinct, as opposed to a continuous, unbroken mass</term>, as the physicist imagines, also in reality? He proves this through a connection of facts from reality, in the same sense that he otherwise infers and discovers realities from facts. Now, the philosopher may be free to go back behind the matter and forces of the physicist into the unimaginable with his concepts of *matter*, *force*, and the *filling of space*. In the way he [the philosopher] grasps these concepts, he may be quite right that space is continuously full; the physicist cannot even judge this, because he is unable to bring any clarity to the philosophical formulation of these concepts; therefore, for his part, he cannot contest such a "filling of space" at all. Thus, he allows the philosophical "filling of space" to stand; he only demands, in return, that philosophy should not contest the **discreteness** of matter in his sense—that is, it should not contest that, as surely and in the same sense as the celestial bodies are discrete masses, there are also...

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of philosophical abstractions. In the previous page, the author argued that physicists use words based on "demonstrable" physical facts, whereas philosophers often engage in "word-strife." Here, he specifically addresses how different philosophical schools (Herbart and Hegel) view the reality of atoms and matter.</meta>

...smaller [parts] in the bodies that appear to the physical eye as continuous wholes <term>continua; singular "continuum," referring to a solid, unbroken substance</term>, and that the reason why they appear to it as continuous wholes is nothing other than the inadequacy of the senses, which also makes the distant star-nebula appear as a continuum. If the philosopher were satisfied with this, the dispute would be over; however, he is not.

<margin>//</margin>At the same time, we are quite ready to admit, in Herbart’s <note>Johann Friedrich Herbart (1776–1841), a German philosopher and psychologist who influenced education and early psychology; he believed true reality consisted of simple, unchanging "reals."</note> sense, that atoms are really only a **contradictory appearance** <note>original: "widerspruchsvoller Schein"</note>, far removed from all true reality. For since he declares everything that one calls "being" <note>original: "seiend"</note> in ordinary speech to be contradictory appearance according to that Sachsian principle <note>likely a reference to the dialectical or logical principles used by contemporary thinkers like Sachs to critique sensory experience</note>—and regards beyond-actual, inherently contradictory concepts as true reality—we explicitly wish for atoms to be counted as part of that "contradictory appearance," not as "reality" in Herbart’s sense. They should only be "real" in the sense that everyone *except* Herbart understands reality, while Herbart understands it as "unreality."

Nor may they exist in the Hegelian sense; since in that sense, only that which emerges dialectically from the self-development of the **concept of Being** <note>original: "Seinsbegriff"; a core pillar of Hegel's logic</note> truly exists. So far, however, the concept of Being has not unfolded into atoms—neither in the minds of the professional dialecticians nor in our own. Although, as already stated, there is no doubt that it would be perfectly capable of doing so if the inclination for this direction of self-movement happened to strike it <note>The author is being satirical about the perceived fluidity and arbitrariness of Hegel's logic.</note>. Until then, they may belong among those appearances that are inadequate to the Concept, those "decayed sham-existences" <note>original: "in sich zerfallenen Scheinexistenzen"</note> of which nature itself is so full according to Hegel; indeed, to be full of such things belongs to the very concept of Nature according to Hegel.

<margin><image-desc>A small pencil scribble or flourish in the right margin.</image-desc></margin>But, says the philosopher, you speak of atoms as small parts of matter, and yet you do not even know how to say what matter itself is; thus your atomistic ideas ultimately float in the uncertain and the empty.

On the contrary, matter is the most tangible thing there is for the physicist, because it is precisely the "tangible" that constitutes the physicist's matter. It is only the philosopher who evaporates its concept into the unimaginable, the uncertain, and the empty, pulling the rug out from under its feet and sending it headlong into a fall.

# VIII. Some General Discussions. The Concept of Matter, etc.

...[it is the task of both] the philosopher and the physicist to treat the fact of matter conceptually as well, let us briefly show how the physicist stands in this regard; and if one does not find the following explanation explicitly <note>original: "explicite"</note> in any physics textbook, it is nonetheless implicitly <note>original: "implicite"</note> contained in all of them, as it is nothing but the simple presentation of the way the physicist actually perceives and treats matter.

<margin>// [hand-drawn bracket]</margin>
Above all, the physicist understands—in complete agreement with the most common linguistic usage, with which he remains in constant connection through this very agreement—by matter that which makes itself noticeable to the sense of touch; that is, precisely the tangible <note>original: "das Handgreifliche," literally "that which can be grasped by the hand."</note>. And even if a concept itself is not, cannot, and should not be something tangible, nevertheless, since the tangible exists in the world, it wants to be recognized as a fact <note>original: "Factum"</note> and, considering its wide prevalence, brought under a general concept. Initially, therefore, for him, the tangible is and is called matter; he assumes matter exists wherever something can be grasped, regardless of what one might search for behind that tangibility—behind the touching or feeling itself—or from what higher reasons one might derive the existence of a tangible thing. Tangibility is something demonstrable, graspable through experience, and further traceable; and that suffices to give the concept the firm foundation required for his purposes. Yet the reference to tangibility provides only the basis of the concept; the physicist stops there just as little as the philosopher would wish to (though he goes beyond it in a different direction), and thereby distinguishes himself from the savage and the peasant <note>The author uses these figures as a rhetorical device to represent a "primitive" or unscientific reliance on immediate sensory perception alone.</note>, for whom matter remains nothing but the tangible. He finds through experience—for he knows no other way to find it—that along with the property of being able to be grasped with the hands, or more generally felt through touch, other demonstrable properties show themselves in a solidary connection, concerning phenomena of equilibrium and motion, which, however, can be tracked even more easily through sight than through touch, and then includes these along with their experimentally discovered laws (which include the concept of forces as an auxiliary concept) among the definitions of matter (hence the explanation one often finds, that it is "that which is movable in space"), and now infers the existence of matter from such phenomena, even when he cannot place himself in the circumstan- <note>The text cuts off here, likely intending to say "circumstances" or "relationships" (Verhältnisse).</note>

# VIII. Some General Discussions. The Concept of Matter, etc.

circumstances in which he can truly grasp matter itself directly by touch, whether because it is too distant, or hidden, or perhaps too diluted and refined. Finally, he finds all sensory perceptions—including hearing, smelling, and tasting—to be in such a solidary relationship with the phenomena of touch or sight and those types of conditions which are characteristic of the existence of matter, that he ultimately assumes matter is essentially involved in all of them. With sound, there is a visibly and tangibly vibrating string or bell; the scent of a flower can be condensed into a tangible liquid; that which is tasted is itself visible and tangible. And even where nothing immediately visible or tangible is present to reveal the existence of matter, the phenomena of hearing, smelling, and tasting are nonetheless causally connected to it. Thus, matter becomes the most general foundation of natural phenomena.

Now, within those phenomena and conditions that are generally characteristic of the existence of matter, there are nonetheless important differences in detail, so that one is prompted to distinguish between various types of matter, so-called substrates <term>Substrate: From the Latin 'substratum,' meaning an underlying layer or substance that supports properties or phenomena; the "stuff" things are made of.</term>, to represent them. One may, however, be doubtful whether the so-called imponderable <term>Imponderable: Literally "un-weighable." In 19th-century physics, this referred to substances like light, heat, or electricity that were thought to have no measurable weight.</term> substrates or substances (or their common element, the ether <note>original: "Aether"</note>), to which a certain circle of phenomena has been specifically attributed, still deserve the name "matter," insofar as they can never be brought into a state where they can be touched, yet they still obey the same general laws of equilibrium and motion as tangible things. 

The dispute over this is either a semantic one—asking whether one wishes to call substances "matter" if they share only certain very general properties with the tangible but not tangibility itself (after all, the use of every word originally involves a degree of arbitrariness)—or a dispute over whether those substances would indeed show the property of being tangible if they could be sufficiently condensed. Indeed, one might ask whether "ponderable" <note>measurable by weight</note> substances are not actually just condensations or tighter clusters of the imponderable ones (just as some suggest that entire celestial bodies are formed from the ether), while the imponderable ones are merely the atomic dust <note>original: "Atomenstaub"</note> remaining loosely between them. This is a question that cannot yet be regarded as decided, and therefore we shall not [dwell] on it...

# VIII. Some General Discussions. The Concept of Matter, etc.

to hesitate to designate the imponderables <term>Imponderables: In 19th-century science, "imponderable" substances were those like light, heat, and electricity, which were believed to be physical fluids or ethers but lacked measurable weight.</term> as matter. Regardless of whether they are called matter or not, for the reasons mentioned earlier, their substance must be imagined as just as localized and as discrete <term>Discrete: In this context, consisting of distinct, individual parts (like atoms or particles) rather than being a continuous, unbroken whole.</term> as that of ponderable substance <note>meaning: "ordinary" matter that has weight</note> if the phenomena are to be represented. The ether, whether or not it can be reduced to something tangible, has its place between the celestial bodies just as surely as if it were a tangible fluid; and its waves are to be conceived as progressing through space just as much as if they were waves of the sea, and finally, as shown, can only produce colors in the prism and polarization insofar as the vibrations in these waves arise through forces acting between discrete centers, regardless of how one chooses to conceive the nature of these centers themselves in the background.

The philosopher might now say something like: you have reduced matter to that which can be felt; but what is that which is felt itself—the object of feeling behind the feeling? — Nothing that concerns the physicist; he knows only what he feels and what is associated with that feeling by other perceptions and phenomena, and what can, according to laws, possibly be associated with it under other circumstances; and what can be abstracted from the totality of these and inferred from that totality. Physics refers to nothing further; its task is and remains enclosed within this circle; within this, it seeks to be so oriented, and to provide such orientation, that every given phenomenon can be classified within the totality of actual and possible phenomena according to perspectives of kinship, connection, and succession, so that when certain phenomena are given, others can be predicted accordingly.

To this end, physics can also go beyond the immediately apparent and proceed to limiting considerations <term>Limiting considerations (Gränzbetrachtungen): Theoretical investigations into the boundaries of what is observable, often involving mathematical models of "ideal" states that cannot be directly perceived.</term> of the apparent, yet always while adhering to representations in the form of the apparent and with the intention of returning to the truly apparent. All conceptualizations and positions, all constructions, methods, and hypotheses of physics and the natural sciences in general have only such a sense and purpose. To attempt to impose others with a different sense and purpose upon them is to misinterpret and confuse their task. And even if other [perspectives] might find a place elsewhere, they will [not contribute] to that which, in this respect, [provides] clarity in the natural sciences and life...

creates—yet life itself owes the clearest orientation in phenomena <term>Phenomena: Things as they appear to our senses, as opposed to how they might exist independently of us.</term> to the natural sciences—one must not contradict them without confusing science and life itself, even if one considers it possible from the outset that a doctrine which penetrates deeper than natural science might grasp, justify, explain, and generalize its conceptualizations, positions, constructions, and methods even more deeply.

But now also, if we, in order to gain the deepest version of the concept of matter, pose the question quite generally: what is the object of feeling, behind the feeling, apart from the feeling, the pure "in-itself" <note>original: "Ansich"; a reference to the philosophical idea of the "thing-in-itself," or reality as it exists independently of human perception.</note> of matter? If we direct this question not to the physicist—who has answered it sufficiently by saying, "I know nothing of such an object and it is none of my business"—but to the philosopher, or to human beings in general, they will know not a hair more or anything different to answer than the physicist, provided their words are intended to be more than just words. In fact, behind the appearance provided by feeling, there is as little to be found for the philosopher as for the physicist; there is nothing <margin>2</margin> knowable, statable, or discussable for humans at all. But in connection with it and above it, there is much to be found: an unspeakable circle of other appearances, which has its consequences in a new circle of appearances and includes the possibility of such in advance, laying the foundation for abstractions and viewpoints of a higher kind; and herein lies the only object of all meaningful, the only foundation of all higher consideration. 

Humans must now follow the viewpoints and threads of both the simultaneous and sequential connections of all physical and spiritual <note>original: "körperlichen und geistigen"; this refers to the 19th-century view of the world as composed of both material/body and mental/soul phenomena.</note> phenomena according to facts. They must specifically keep in view and name the identical, the constant, and the repeating elements therein that are possible according to laws. They must analyze the individual details in a downward path through ever sharper observation, and link them in an upward path through ever more general concepts and laws, until reaching the most general and the ultimate, under which everything factual is united, and as far as the finest analysis penetrates. Finally, they must supplement the inadequacy of immediate observation and the limitations of the individual's standpoint through conclusions following rules and laws, which are as far-reaching and as general...

How much there is to do here, how high, how deep, how general these reflections can go; how much unknown and unimagined is to be found on this path; God and immortality themselves do not elude it. Yet all of this leads not back *behind* feeling, but only *beyond* feeling into a more general circle of appearances <term>Appearances: German "Erscheinungen," referring to things as they are perceived or manifest to a consciousness, whether human or divine.</term> to which it belongs—to beings alongside and above us, to whom things may appear differently and more extensively than to us, and to perspectives under which their and our appearances intertwine. These are obtainable from the realm of appearances themselves with our own spirit manifesting to itself, but from nothing behind them and with nothing behind them.

This is, in the final instance, the position that the concept of matter holds for the physicist and, according to a clear understanding, in general. The physicist and every clear thinker starts from something demonstrable <note>original: "Aufzeigbarem"; something that can be pointed to or empirically verified.</note>, and thus quite definite; and if he goes beyond the demonstrable, the relationship to it, the connection with it, and the explanation through it always remain, clearly traceable within the imagination. The philosopher, however, in believing he can trace the concept of matter back *behind* what appears, traces it into the void.

If one asks at all what the world ultimately consists of, it is appearance (self-appearance in spirit and God, objective appearance in nature), laws of appearance, determinations, connections, and relationships of appearances, which include the possibility of future and new appearances. Otherwise, there is nothing, and behind it, there is nothing. Of a world that appeared neither to itself nor to others, one could not speak at all; there would be no reason to assume its existence. Everything that is in the world can therefore only be defined with regard to the way it appears to itself or to others, or how it might possibly appear under other circumstances in another context, or be understood as a relationship, connection, determining factor, or possibility of appearances—for otherwise, it is nothing. One can name this "nothing" in many ways; it remains a—
