Matter exists only in that, or insofar as, it appears objectively through sight or touch, or at least can appear under suitable circumstances. And what we call the "objective" in sensory appearances, as opposed to the "subjective" of perception, is never anything that exists *outside* of the appearance; rather, both—the objective and the subjective—are merely the same appearance grasped in a different context, or, if one prefers, a different relationship of that same appearance. 

The very same appearance of a lightning bolt—which has the character of an objective natural phenomenon or natural process insofar as I consider it in connection and relation to the appearance of thunder, rain, etc.; in short, as a member and determining moment of the world of collective sensory appearances that can be experienced by me and everyone else—becomes at once a subjective perception, an intuition <term>Intuition: German "Anschauung," meaning the direct mental "viewing" or internal representation of an object.</term> of the lightning, insofar as I consider it as a determining moment of the unity of consciousness that spans and links all appearances within me. In this way, I view it as intervening in the higher world that is linked within me by this unity above the world of sensory appearances; as a starting point for memories, as a foundation for concepts, as the provider of material for thoughts. All of this has no direct relationship to an "objective" lightning bolt, but only to the *perception* of it, because in such a context the appearance is called simply "perception," not something objective. The objective is the side of the sensory appearance turned toward its neighbor; the subjective is the side turned upward into the spirit. Yet both are merely two sides, not two things. Thus, the same stone in the base of a pyramid turns one side toward the other stones of the same base, and another toward the peak, yet it remains the same stone.

What can indeed easily provide an occasion to seek something more than sensory appearance *behind* the appearance is the circumstance that the possibility of other types of sensory appearances, and sensory appearances for others, is linked in solidarity to the given sensory appearance. The orange that I see, I can also touch, smell, and taste; I can produce a sound from it by striking it. I can do this not only now, but repeatedly; and not I alone can do it, but countless others can do it too; and this whole...

# VIII. Some More General Discussions. The Concept of Matter, etc.

<meta>The catchword from the previous page "by" completes here as "by a complex..."</meta>

An interconnected complex of countless possible appearances, limited from a certain point of view, represents an **objective thing** to us; this thing, therefore, certainly consists of more than a momentary individual appearance or any finite sum of such appearances. Rather, behind all the individual appearances of a thing, there always remains a "Something" that can yield countless further appearances; and this is easily **hypostatized** <term>Hypostatize: To treat a conceptual or abstract idea as if it were a distinct, physical substance or a real "thing" existing on its own.</term> as an unknowable "thing-behind-it." Yet this dark "Something" is nothing other than the unexplained, interconnected possibility of these appearances themselves, which can be linked to the current one—appearances of the same kind as those one has here or elsewhere. One does not explain the interconnected possibility of all these appearances by assuming a "thing" behind them; rather, one explains the "thing behind it" of which one speaks by tracing it back to this possibility—a possibility given in connection through a general **causality** <term>Causality: The principle that everything that happens has a cause; here, the laws that govern how one appearance follows another.</term> of appearing—and by entering into the factual connection and the laws of the sequence of appearing. In this way, one learns to foresee what will be found when one penetrates into the body that one now sees and touches on the surface, or when one walks around it, how it will appear in a different light, or what will happen if one treats it with chemical agents. The assumption of a "thing behind it" explains and teaches nothing of all this; but what, then, *does* it explain or teach?

One should no more search for a dark "thing-in-itself" <term>Thing-in-itself: Based on the Kantian term "Ding an sich," referring to the hypothetical reality of an object as it exists independently of human observation.</term> behind my soul than behind physical bodies to hold their manifold and changing appearances together in unity. Instead, what holds its appearances together is something **immanent** <term>Immanent: Inherent or remaining within; something that is part of the internal structure of an experience rather than something external to it.</term> to these appearances themselves, and at the same time the clearest thing there is: it is the **consciousness** of the appearances, whose unity appears in and with them. It is truly strange to seek yet another ground for this absolute, bright, luminous, clear, living unity of the soul in a rigid, dark, simple, or transcendent being behind it, and to believe that one thereby makes the unity of consciousness clearer or more explainable.

One must not reproach this way of viewing things by claiming that—because it declares the appearance to be the essential content of all real existence and makes it the foundation of all consideration, behind which one never goes—it sinks us hopelessly into the flow, whirlpool, change, and dispersal of appearances. On the con- <note>original: "Im Ge-" (likely "Im Gegentheil," meaning "On the contrary").</note>

# VIII. Some More General Discussions. The Concept of Matter, etc.

7

On the contrary, every deeper reflection should lead precisely to this: to find the universal and firm points of unification, anchors, and goals within the realm of appearance. These points reside in the laws of appearance, in the conservation of matter and **active force** <note>German: *wirkungsfähigen Kraft*—likely referring to the early physical concept of "living force" (vis viva) or energy conservation.</note>, in the connection and meeting of spirits through consistent perceptions of time and space, in the universal categories of thought, in moral ideas, and above all, in the unification of consciousness which finds its required peak in God. Now, one might certainly see the true essence of the world in these highest, final, and most universal things, and where several such higher viewpoints present themselves, one might even seek the final chain linking them together; what is certain is that all of this can only be grasped as the core, peak, goal, abstract, immanent moment, bond, or totality of an internal and external world of appearance. It is something higher and more universal *within* the appearance, not something unknowable or incomprehensible *behind* the appearance.

<margin>to link it with an all-encompassing consciousness and to call this totality God.</margin>
Since every appearance only exists through a consciousness into which it falls, and since the most diverse appearances have this—and only this—in common, and since consciousness itself exists only as the linking of a **manifold** <term>Manifold: A collection of many different elements or parts; in philosophy, the "raw data" of the senses before the mind organizes them.</term> and a change of appearances, nothing, I say, prevents us from placing the totality of appearing—and with it the **real ground** <note>German: *Realgrund*—the actual, fundamental basis for why something exists or happens.</note> of all things and all events—into a single, eternal, all-encompassing consciousness itself. This consciousness gives birth to all temporal appearing from within itself and takes it back into itself; its unity is the final anchor, core, and knot of all things, so that all unified points of reference ultimately depend upon it—points through which appearances are linked to so-called "things outside us" and to "thoughts within us." The fact that our consciousness feels externally determined during outer perception would simply depend on the fact that the universal consciousness, by reaching beyond our own, acts determinatively upon ours with that which it possesses beyond our own—just as within our own consciousness, every individual moment is determined by the totality of the rest. And if, even in our relatively lower individual consciousness, a sensory appearance seems objective or subjective depending on whether it is viewed in relation to appearances of the same level or to the unity of consciousness above it, then this would only become generalized for the higher, divine consciousness that is superior to our own—

Regardless of whether one wants to accept them or not, the previous view—that nothing exists as real except appearance and that which binds and transcends it in the sense described, while any **thing-in-itself** <term>Thing-in-itself: *Ding an sich*; a term made famous by Immanuel Kant referring to reality as it exists independently of human observation. It is not some arbitrary claim, a hypothesis that could be true or false, but an **identical proposition** <note>original: *identischer Satz*; in logic, a statement that is true by definition, similar to a tautology.</note>. Above all, one must ask what one wants to call "something," "real," or "being," and what one wants to call "nothing" or a "non-entity." Now, some might say that this is self-evident, and according to common usage it might indeed seem so; but certainly not according to philosophical usage. Indeed, how could one still say, after Hegel and Herbart <note>Georg Wilhelm Friedrich Hegel (1770–1831) and Johann Friedrich Herbart (1776–1841) were influential German philosophers who redefined basic ontological terms like "being" and "nothing" in complex ways.</note>, that it is self-evident what "something," "real," "being," and what "nothing" are? On the contrary, it has become the most incomprehensible thing; instead of distinguishing the two, one is supposed to identify them or even confuse their common meanings. Therefore, it is indeed necessary to say what one intends to mean by these terms before using them, if one is to...

...be understood.*) So I now say clearly and distinctly what I call *Nothing* in contrast to *Something*. I call *Nothing* that which does not appear, nor can appear, nor whose existence can be inferred from appearances according to rules that can be proven in the world of appearances of the spirit and nature (in which worlds we are enclosed), the assumption of which can contribute just as little to grounding the existence of this world of appearances as a whole or anything individual within it according to rules of experience or through self-understanding. And I believe that this use of the word *Nothing*—by which everything Kantian and Herbartian and any other **thing-in-itself** <term>Thing-in-itself: *Ding an sich*; a philosophical concept, most famously used by Immanuel Kant, referring to reality as it exists independently of human observation and sensory perception. The author argues that if we cannot experience it, it is functionally non-existent.</term> behind the appearance becomes a *Nothing*—is in the spirit of living language itself, which everywhere calls *Nothing* that which is *Nothing* in that sense. Through this connection to common usage, the philosophical use justifies itself. All the preceding is basically merely an exposition of the given definition; I show in detail and more closely what I understand negatively by *Nothing* and positively by *Something*; I explain the connection of this usage with other word usages; of a factual

*) I just read in a review of Wuttke’s *History of Heathenism* (*Gersdorf’s Repertory* 1854, p. 28): “The church of the Buddhists, if one may use this expression here, has *Nothing* to teach; it only has to provide an impulse, namely to lead humans to the correct standpoint that the All is the Nothing and the Nothing is the All, and then one immediately sees everything by itself.” Here, in the use of the word *Nothing* in a religion that probably counts the most followers on earth, compared with the use commonly accepted among us, one sees how far one can go with the various uses of the word *Nothing*, and how necessary it is, therefore, to explain what one intends to understand by it. For the Buddhist religion understands by *Nothing* that which is otherwise grasped as the absolute opposite of *Nothing*. Naturally, depending on whether one understands *Nothing* as the *All* or the opposite of the *All*, a totally different-sounding doctrine will emerge; and in one doctrine, where one throws together two concepts that are separated in the other, one will be somewhat less clear than in the other, and will "fish for different fish in muddy waters than in clear ones." <note>original: "im Trüben andere Fische fischen als im Klaren." A German idiom suggesting that lack of clarity allows for different, perhaps less valid, conclusions to be drawn.</note> But in itself, the different word usage of *Nothing* underlying this should not be able to change anything in the matter. Furthermore, based on the passage above, one could confuse the Buddhist use of the word *Nothing* with the Hegelian one; for one could also say literally of the Hegelian doctrine, or at least it would not hesitate to do so itself: it has led humans to the correct standpoint that the All (the absolute Being, which **dialectically** <term>Dialectical: A method of argument or thought that involves the resolution of opposites; in Hegel's philosophy, it describes how a concept (Being) moves into its opposite (Nothing) to reach a higher truth (Becoming).</term> includes everything within itself and gives birth to everything from itself) is the Nothing and the Nothing is the All, and thus one sees everything by itself.

There is no talk of gains or losses in all of this. It may look like a new philosophical view, and it is as good a one as the most profound ones we have; but the most profound philosophical views fundamentally revolve around nothing but wordings, without being as clear about it as we are, and without attributing such a clear—that is, demonstrable—meaning to the words themselves. For our part, with the previous explanations, we only satisfy the rules we gave ourselves at the beginning.

Now, one cannot argue against an identical sentence <note>The author is referring to a "tautology"—a statement that is true by its own definition. He suggests his definitions are logically self-evident.</note>. And yet people will argue against it; and in doing so, they will do nothing more than use the words *Nothing* and *Something* differently than we do, without gaining anything new or different in the matter itself.

To turn back from this general consideration to the concept of matter in particular, one might easily find oneself misled by the following observation. According to the above, the tangible, the palpable (and *as a secondary aid* <note>original: "in subsidium"</note> the visible) should form the basis for the concept of matter, which can be neither further traced back nor analyzed; but to find something tangible, my entire organized hand must be present, specific conditions must exist under which it comes into contact with the stone or the wood it feels; all this, it is said, lies hidden in the concept of the tangible; therefore, it is factually the most complex concept which, instead of forming the ultimate foundation in an analysis of matter, requires the most extensive backward analysis, and if one engages in it, one finds that matter, which is supposed to be traced back to the tangible, is itself required to provide the hand with which one grasps; thus, one enters a complete circle of reasoning.

But this is just as if someone said: the simple substances of chemistry are not truly simple, and one should not want to go back to them as an ultimate in chemistry; for in order to produce them, the most complicated operations with very complex crucibles, retorts, filters, and so on, are necessary, in which these substances themselves are already contained. Rather, the very fact that these tools also cannot be analyzed into anything further than that which is obtained by means of them and other bodies is proof that the whole world of bodies consists of nothing more than these substances, that they are the ultimate thing one can reach. Thus it is also true that

### VIII. Some More General Discussions. The Concept of Matter, etc.

the hand, the eye, by means of which I obtain sensations of touch and sight, can themselves only be perceived as "hand" and "eye" through sensations of touch and sight; but this very fact proves that everywhere in the physical world, one eventually arrives at sensations of touch and sight as the ultimate elements into which the corporeal can be qualitatively dissolved. Thus, one may also investigate what the simplest activities of the mind are; and one will find they are the same ones that the mind requires for this very investigation.

One should generally beware of believing that the simplicity of a concept or a thing is jeopardized by the complexity of the conditions under which it can be thought or produced. For a simple sensation of touch to occur, it is fundamentally not just the hand and a stone that are necessary, but the entire interconnection of the world <note>The author uses "Weltzusammenhang" to describe the idea that nothing exists in isolation; every small event requires the history and physics of the entire universe to function.</note>, without which hand and stone could not exist. Nevertheless, the feeling of touch remains a simple thing, something that cannot be further traced back or analyzed; as such, it can be isolated from the interconnection of the world, examined individually, and followed through its variations and combinations. It then becomes clear that what is "touchable" can enter into such relationships with other touchable things that new sensations of touch arise from them—just as a human being, who has been conceived, can conceive new human beings with another person in the same way. Fundamentally, everything that exists is only possible within the total context of everything else that exists; nevertheless, it is necessary to recognize and seek out the individual and the simple within this context.

The fact that in the world of appearance <term>Erscheinung</term> <note>In 19th-century philosophy, "appearance" refers to the world as we perceive it through our senses, as opposed to how things might be "in themselves."</note> one thing can only exist with and through another can easily lead one to assume that behind everything appearing, there is something "existing in itself"—an independently existing Being that casts all those dependent shadows. For, it is said, if one thing always refers to another as the ground of its existence, then ultimately a ground for all existence is missing. If A says, "I can only exist as long as B exists," and B in turn says, "I can only exist as long as A exists," then both have ultimately referred to nothing. Herbart <note>Johann Friedrich Herbart (1776–1841) was a German philosopher who argued that "reals"—simple, unchangeable entities—must exist behind the changing world of appearances to explain them.</note> allowed himself to be led by this consideration into an entire metaphysics that seems to me, I must say, absurd. <margin>*) Compare the further elaboration of this viewpoint in my treatise directed against Herbartian metaphysics <note>Johann Friedrich Herbart (1776–1841) was a philosopher who argued that reality consists of simple, unchangeable "reals" (Realen) hidden behind the world we perceive. give the ground and core to their appearance, they must seek it in the totality of which they are both members; the whole is the support and core of the whole and of everything contained within it. The root, stem, leaf, and blossom of a plant cannot seek to ground their existence reciprocally upon one another; they exist as parts of the entire plant, and the entire plant—just as it appears to us through our senses—exists simultaneously with our senses as a part of and in subordination to the whole world and its laws which encompass the whole*). In the whole, one has every reason to seek the ground of the individual, not in something even more individual and different; yet one can investigate how and according to what rules the individual fits into the whole and what the ultimate elements are. In the light that shines through the whole, one must seek the clarity of the whole, not in something dark behind the world and the light; yet one can illuminate the light itself with a higher light by fathoming the relationships of the phenomenon of light within itself and to that which it illuminates.

Therefore, one should not be led astray by the fact that what is visible and tangible, and the acts of seeing and feeling themselves, are only possible to the extent that other visible and tangible things—and even things beyond the visible and tangible—are possible. One should not assume that nothing truly exists of all this, or that something must be sought "behind its back" <note>A metaphorical way of saying "behind the scenes" or "underlying the surface."</note> which exists through itself. That which alone exists through itself is not something outside of all this appearance; rather, it is the whole, which includes all this appearance itself and conducts and proves its existence precisely only within the manifestation. As parts, moments, and sides of this reality which exists in itself, even the most individual appearance then has its relative reality, and it is only of such reality that we can speak in relation to the whole. While the whole remains eternal and evolves eternally, the individual disappears and passes away, provided it does not—like the human spirit—belong to the eternal moments of evolution of the whole itself.

One could, although it is fundamentally idle to do so, think away the senses of touch and feeling from the total interconnection of appearance; after all, there are blind people, and at least on individual patches of skin...

Inasmuch as a real thing is characterized not merely by the appearances that actually exist, but also by those that can possibly exist under other circumstances (which themselves, in the final instance, are always only characterizable through a connection and relationship of appearances), the complete characterization of a thing requires knowing how it will present itself under every conceivable modification of circumstances, up to the boundary cases of such modification. The more exhaustive and thorough the observation is intended to be, the more one will find oneself driven to include the ultimate limits of conceivable modification in the consideration—even if the modification could not actually proceed that far—insofar as that which *can* happen is connected in a continuum <note>The author argues that there is no sharp break between what we can see and what is theoretically possible.</note> within the observation and for the conclusion.

...touching and seeing on the surface, could themselves lead to that assumption of matter in the depths.

Just as we believe we have only fully recognized the body of the Earth when we know what would appear to us if we were to penetrate into its greatest depths, or the heavens when we know what would appear if we could increase the power of our telescopes indefinitely or grant our eyes an infinite range; so it also belongs to the complete and exhaustive characterization of bodily things in general that we consider one of the most important boundary cases <note>original: "Gränzfälle." In philosophy and science, a boundary case is an extreme example used to test the limits of a definition.</note>, asking ourselves: how would bodies appear if we could refine and sharpen our senses—which we can already refine to certain limits with the microscope—to an indefinite degree? And to this we have given the answer: then the particles that now appear continuous would appear separate, as atoms. Even though our senses will always remain too coarse to see the atoms as such in their separation, the atoms still retain the character of reality in light of the way all real things are characterized (not merely according to how they *actually* appear, but also how they *would* appear under circumstances that are imaginably connected with existing ones). And although we only arrived at the assumption of atoms through complex deductions from what is evident to everyone's sight and touch, once we have arrived there, the observation of what is evident to everyone's eye and touch is itself simplified and clarified; through this, we gain "bridges of connection" for our imagination, as shown previously.

Furthermore, nothing prevents us from giving everything that lies beyond all possibility of actual appearance—even if it is a boundary representation of what is actually apparent—the name of a <term>metaphysically real</term> <note>Something that exists as a fundamental reality or first principle, but cannot be directly experienced by the senses.</note> thing instead of a <term>physically real</term> <note>Something that can be perceived through the senses or measured by scientific instruments.</note> one (which is ultimately a matter of definition; cf. the philosophical section of this work). Thus, if such a great sharpening of the senses were absolutely impossible, such that we could never see or feel atoms as what they are—that is, in their separateness <note>original: "Discretion," used here in the mathematical or physical sense of being composed of distinct, individual parts rather than a continuous whole.</note>—we would assign to them only a metaphysical rather than a physical reality. In any case, the search for such metaphysical realities is rewarded by the fruitful and clear conclusions...

<header>VIII. Some More General Discussions. The Concept of Matter, etc.</header>

...for physical reality itself, which we know how to base upon it, and it justifies itself insofar as this is the case. In this regard, there is, from a certain perspective, an approach toward traditional philosophy, whose metaphysical reality also does not coincide with common sensory experience, and in which philosophy seeks the foundation and conclusion of that common experience. The only difference is that our approach is not intended to be something behind, before, or above the common experienced reality in a way that would reduce or evaporate that reality into an empty illusion. Rather, it is the conceptual boundary <note>original: "gedankenmässige Gränze." The author suggests that atoms are not "hidden" things, but rather the logical limit of our observations.</note> of experienced reality itself—a boundary from which and toward which a flow of imagination and deduction moves through experienced reality.

Ultimately, the entire dispute over whether atoms are something real or not leads back to the reef of verbal disputes if one does not reach an agreement on what one wishes to call "real"; and, as always, the word itself matters for nothing, but rather only the thing itself <note>original: "die Sache." In German thought, this refers to the actual subject matter or essence as opposed to mere terminology.</note>. Despite any assertion that discrete atoms are real, we cannot make it so that we actually see and feel them as such; despite any assertion that they are not real, opponents cannot take away the fact that through the mental representation of them, we orient ourselves better within the visible and tangible than if we did not imagine them. They cannot take away the fact that our representation of them follows the same principles of deduction from the conditions of apparent reality by which we infer that reality itself, and that, conversely, derivations of what falls within apparent reality can be founded upon this representation of them. 

This connection—in which they stand with apparent reality through representation and deduction—and these functions <note>original: "Leistungen," meaning the "work" or "services" the concept of atoms performs for science.</note> they perform for apparent reality are in any case real. They are not in the least diminished or withered if one chooses not to call the atoms themselves "real"; furthermore, in this regard they surpass the concepts that philosophy would like to put in their place for orientation among phenomena, and that is the decisive reason for preferring them over those philosophical concepts. Here is the essence of the matter; the word "real" adds nothing and takes nothing away. Yet we remain within the context of common linguistic usage when we call atoms "real" given such a character and such performance. They produce phenomena, they condition phenomena; that is enough. Even if they do not appear themselves, linguistic usage does not require it. In this sense

...we have insisted above and everywhere and will continue to insist that the atoms are real things.

In a certain sense, an analogous case arises with this question as with the question of whether one should call <term>imponderables</term> <note>In 19th-century physics, these were "weightless" substances like light, heat, or electricity that were thought to occupy space but had no mass.</note> matter, since they do not demonstrably possess the property with which the concept of matter was originally associated—tangibility—even though they share all general relationships of equilibrium and motion with tangible things, according to which we otherwise judge the existence of matter (cf. p. 91). Nevertheless, the treatment of imponderables from the common viewpoints that exist for them along with <term>ponderables</term> <note>Ordinary matter that has weight and can be measured on a scale.</note> remains useful and necessary. Thus, we might doubt whether we should still call discrete atoms real things, since they no longer demonstrably possess <term>perceptibility</term> <note>original: "Erscheinlichkeit." This refers to the quality of being able to appear as a phenomenon to our senses.</note>, the basis of all reality; but since they possess everything else by which we otherwise judge the real, they are also to be treated from the common viewpoints they share with real things.

I think enough has finally been said about this.

If the <term>Dynamist</term> <note>A follower of "Dynamism," a theory (popularized by thinkers like Kant or Schelling) suggesting that matter is not composed of solid particles but is a manifestation of fundamental forces like attraction and repulsion.</note> intends to deepen the concept of matter by constructing it out of forces, then, as already conceded, the atomistic physicist can have no objection to it, so long as he himself is unable to bring the philosophical concept of forces to full clarity, other than simply stating that he is unable to do so. Just as the word "force" is understood differently by the philosopher than by the physicist, it can naturally also be used differently; and with an arbitrary or fluid definition, it must also be possible to make it useful for any desired application, and therefore also for the construction of matter. But this much is certain: the physical concept of force, in its sharpest definition, greatest clarification, and depth, does not allow matter to be constructed from it; indeed, I maintain that only an unclear and crude understanding of it could lead to using it for the construction of matter.

According to the most common and crudest understanding, force is regarded as something that resides in matter or adheres to it like an external property, yet also extends beyond it into the distance and thus makes the existence of matter known. Instead of getting to the bottom of this crude, unclear idea by asking for the factual basis that underlies it, and thereby translating it into a clear, true one, the phi—

In the meantime, a celestial body is nonetheless distinct from the gravitation that extends beyond it, and every piece of matter is distinct from the force acting beyond it. Physics requires <term>centers</term> <note>Fixed points or nuclei from which forces are seen to originate.</note> for the force, which cannot themselves be grasped as force, and there is a need to trace this difference between matter and force back to a clear meaning instead of blurring it. Thus far, the <term>dynamic view</term> <note>A 19th-century philosophical school (including thinkers like Kant and Schelling) that argued matter is not composed of solid particles, but is a product of attractive and repulsive forces.</note> has not been able to do this in a way that physics could make use of, or so that agreement among the philosophers themselves and a connection with living language could be achieved. According to most Dynamists, a "conflict of opposing forces" is supposed to be what turns force into a body; yet neither this conflict nor the forces preceding the bodies themselves were ever able to be brought to clarity; rather, they fall into that transcendent <term>ether</term> <note>A reference to the "luminiferous ether," a hypothetical medium once thought to fill all space, used here to criticize vague, metaphysical speculation.</note> in which words sail around in vain to find a firm anchorage.

<margin>_</margin>
For physics, the matter factually presents itself thus: the Sun and the Earth are given to it through experience as something visible and tangible, localized in space and changing their position relative to one another. Likewise, laws are given to it by experience, according to which the change of position depends on the existing position and the <term>mass-magnitudes</term> <note>The quantitative measure of matter, which in 19th-century physics was often defined by its resistance to acceleration.</note>; these magnitudes themselves are only inferred from the motion-relations of the visible and tangible, and for physics they can mean nothing more than precisely the factual evidence from which they are inferred and which depends on them. Nothing further is given here besides the visible and tangible, motions, and the laws of motions. Where then is there any talk of force? It is, in fact, to be found neither in that crude sense nor in that philosophical sense. Force, for physics in general, is nothing more than an <term>auxiliary expression</term> <note>original: "Hülfsausdruck." A conceptual or mathematical tool used to describe phenomena without necessarily claiming to represent the ultimate underlying reality.</note> for the representation of the laws of equilibrium and motion, and every clear conception of physical force leads back to this. <header>VIII. Some More General Discussions. The Concept of Matter, etc.</header>

...of force; yet if we look closer, they are only laws of **equilibrium** and **motion** that apply when matter confronts matter. To say that the Sun and Earth exert a **force of attraction** on one another means nothing more than this: Sun and Earth move toward each other according to a law when they interact; the physicist knows nothing of "force" other than the **law** itself; they have no other way to characterize it. One might say: but there must be a *reason* why the Sun and the Earth move toward each other. This reason is simply nothing other than the law; the law exists such that, when these conditions of the coexistence of bodies are given, these new results follow. One may search for a thousand words to describe this "reason," but in fact, one will find nothing but the law to hold onto, and nothing else upon which to base a clear conception of the reason for motion or force. 

Thus, instead of physical force residing specifically within bodies and acting out from one onto another; instead of it acting in places where it is not; instead of it being "latent" in one body only to become active upon the arrival of another; instead of it "constituting" matter or "dialectically" collapsing into it—notions where each is more obscure and self-contradictory than the last—everything that can be said about force actually and conceptually reduces to an **omnipresent law** and the compliance with it. Before this law, neither distance nor proximity exists as an obstacle; rather, the law makes any *change* in distance or proximity dependent on the *existing* conditions of distance and proximity, thereby linking the distant and the near, the future and the past themselves. 

If force "resides" anywhere, it resides only in the law; the law possesses, at the same time, the <term>force of law</term> <note>The author uses "Gesetzeskraft," playing on the double meaning of "force": the physical power and the binding authority of a rule.</note>, meaning that what it dictates is carried out. What we specifically attribute to an individual body as its "force" is merely the **share** with which it contributes—according to its individuality and its position relative to other bodies—to the fulfillment of the law. This law, insofar as it is universal, refers to all conditions of matter and therefore prescribes to each body what it must perform and experience in its arrangement with others. This share, with which every body (and even every element of a body) specifically contributes to the realization of the law, can certainly be conceptually distinguished from the universal law that spans across all matter, all time, and all space...

<summary>This page argues that "force" should not be thought of as a mysterious substance hidden inside matter, but rather as the observable operation of universal laws. The author suggests that what we call physical force is simply the "share" an individual object contributes to the fulfillment of these omnipresent laws of motion and equilibrium.</summary>

### VIII. Some More General Discussions. The Concept of Matter, etc.

...and specifically referred to the body, or rather the element of the body, as a <term>force<note>original: *Kraft*</note></term> inherent to it—or, if you will, "seated" within it. However, this signifies merely a specific relationship of dependency: the way physical things behave according to the general law. It is nothing that belongs to it constantly or for its own sake, nor is it even what the body consists of. How a tower stands, or how a celestial body moves, is its own business only insofar as it is the business of the general law; all towers stand, all celestial bodies move, simultaneously under its protection. The forces through which they stand or move represent and testify to nothing other than the power of the law over them all, within them all, and their subjection to this power.

Everywhere, force is only a matter of the **coexistence** of matter; in and of itself, no body has a force, unless it is the force resulting from the <term>coexistence<note>original: *Zusammensein*</note></term> of its own parts.*) Quantity (mass) and (chemical) quality influence the force of matter, but both can only be measured by the way matter—the visible, the tangible—behaves in coexistence with other matter. Ultimately, these terms again signify nothing more than constant, lawful relationships: how a given piece of matter behaves when it is together with other matter. It is possible, moreover, that this dependence of force on the variation of matter can be eliminated, so that a pure dependence on the **arrangement** would remain (cf. the philosophical section). In any case, force is so much a function of the coexistence of matter that it changes with every modification of that coexistence. If two bodies in outer space come twice as close to each other, their force quadruples. The force of gravity even transitions into cohesive force when it [pulls] the material parts from a percept...

*) If one wished—which is certainly possible from a certain perspective (cf. the philosophical section of this writing)—to expand the usual concept of force (which requires the coexistence of at least two parts) to include <term>inertia<note>original: *Beharrungsvermögen*</note></term>, which applies even to a single particle, it would still only be a <term>limiting concept<note>original: *Gränzbegriff*</note></term> of force. This is because, among the various cases of a particle’s coexistence with any other particles, the "lowest limit" is the case where it is not with another, but only with itself. Thus, it would still be the category of **coexistence** that defines the concept of force, rather than an "inner essence" of matter. Indeed, it would be difficult to explain how the same matter, under the influence of inertia alone, could behave in such diverse ways regarding rest, motion, direction, and speed. Nevertheless, the standard physical view generally separates the **law of inertia** from the laws of force. Ultimately, this is merely a matter of definition, and the relationship between force and coexistence remains unaffected in either case.

<summary>This page argues that "force" is not an inherent property of an individual body but rather a manifestation of universal laws that occur when matter interacts or "coexists."</summary>

### VIII. Some More General Discussions. The Concept of Matter, etc.

...[spat]ial distances into imperceptible ones. Through such a transition, the <term>celestial bodies<note>original: *Weltkörper*, referring to planets, stars, and other large cosmic masses</note></term> have clumped together into coherent masses. The same materials, which in the external world merely obeyed inorganic forces, fall under the influence of organic forces as soon as they enter into an organic composition. The same material particle can thus gradually experience and manifest the most varied forces. How could all this be possible if <term>force<note>original: *Kraft*</note></term> signified the very essence of the body parts themselves, or adhered to them constantly as something fundamental? On the contrary, this is the natural consequence of the dependence of the concept of force upon the <term>concept of law<note>original: *Gesetzesbegriff*</note></term>. Every different material arrangement has, according to the most general law, a different arrangement as a consequence; it always and everywhere has the same consequence whenever and wherever it recurs. This relationship can be expressed for every different arrangement in a different proposition—that is, a Law—and can thereby characterize a different force. Accordingly, in every specifically constituted arrangement, a specifically constituted force also prevails; there are as many kinds of forces as there are ways of arranging matter. One can, however, bring many specific ways of arrangement under general concepts, and accordingly the associated laws, and accordingly the associated forces. In this way, one can specialize or generalize the laws and forces as much as one wishes. **Gravitational force**, **elasticity**, **chemical force**, and **organic force** are all forces that each relate to certain ways of arrangement in a certain generality, and they have specific forces below them and more general forces above them.

<margin>merely?</margin>
<margin>Take Note</margin>
Force always seizes the bodies or body parts—through whose <term>coexistence<note>original: *Zusammensein*</note></term> it is awakened—in their interconnection, and causes changes in their system which are linked in mutual dependency according to the principle of the equality of **action and reaction**. Insofar as one considers this interconnection of changes which a system of physical parts experiences as a result of their coexistence, one can perceive them as the effect of an **internal force** of the system; however, insofar as one specifically considers the changes that each part of the system experiences through its coexistence with others—or conversely, which other parts of the system experience through coexistence with them—one can speak of **external forces** that the part of the system experiences or manifests. Both, the internal force of the system and the external forces of its parts, are always inextricably linked.

It has indeed been thought that organisms differ fundamentally from non-organic bodies in that the former are set in motion by internal forces, and the latter only by external forces, and that there is a fundamental difference between these forces themselves. The organism drives its blood and stirs its limbs through forces prevailing internally within it; the planet, the falling stone, the cannonball is set in motion by the external force of the sun, the earth, or the exploding powder. Yet this distinction is futile. Every external force that a body experiences and manifests is simultaneously a "moment" or element of an internal force—namely, the internal force of the larger system to which the body belongs. Every body belongs to a larger system in which an internal force prevails; conversely, every internal force of a system can be decomposed into external forces—specifically, the external forces which its parts exert upon one another. Thus, the external force with which the planet strives toward the sun is a moment of an...

<header>VIII. Some More General Discussions. Concept of Matter, etc. 111</header>

...[gravitational] internal force of the planetary system, which brings about the motion of the sun and all planets in connection; the external force with which the stone is pulled toward the earth is a <term>moment<note>In 19th-century physics and philosophy, a "moment" (Moment) refers to a constituent element or a specific aspect of a complex process, rather than a point in time.</note></term> of the internal force that dwells within the system of the stone and the earth, and through which the earth is pulled toward the stone at the same moment just as the stone is pulled toward the earth; the external force that drives the cannonball forward is a moment of the internal force that acts within the system of the cannon, the powder, and the ball, and through which the cannon is simultaneously driven backward while the ball goes forward. And conversely, the internal force of the organism can be decomposed into external forces that the parts of the organism exert upon one another, whereby the equality of <term>action and reaction<note>A reference to Newton’s Third Law of Motion: for every action, there is an equal and opposite reaction.</note></term> exists just as well as in the inorganic external world. The blood is driven forward by the force of the heart acting upon it from the outside—essentially the force of the entire remaining organism; the arm is moved by an external impulse from the brain, to which the whole rest of the organism contributes; but just as one part of the organism experiences effects from the other parts, it simultaneously exerts effects on the other parts; both effects appear in connection as internal effects of the organic system and can be attributed to an internal force of the same. Elasticity is an internal force; yet nothing prevents it from also being decomposed into external forces of the body parts upon one another: thus every part of the elastic steam in the boiler is pressed by the others; thus every part of a string is driven to vibration by the action of the others.

Many imagine the various forces, such as the force of gravity, elastic, chemical, organic force, and so on, so to speak as independent **mythological beings**, which intervene in the corporeal world from an ideal realm, and each—according to a specific, constant peculiarity—alters the conditions in this or that region particularly subject to them and calls forth new arrangements therein, regardless of the existing ones. They do not consider that all these forces depend no less upon the existing arrangement than the subsequent arrangement depends upon them; that they signify precisely the realization of the <term>lawful relationship<note>original: *gesetzlichen Bezug*; the author argues that "force" is simply the name we give to the predictable way one state of matter follows another according to a law.</note></term> between successive arrangements and change their own concept just as the concept of the arrangement changes in which they are said to act. That the force of gravity, the chemical, and the organic force produce such extraordinarily different effects is no wonder, because the arrangements in which they act, to which they are related according to their concepts, are already so extraordinarily different; as soon as these arrangements approach equality or become equal, the effects of the forces governing them immediately do so as well.

It is important not to confuse or mix the concept of **physical force**, which is what all this has been about—the force through which the motions of the planets, the phenomena of electricity and elasticity come about—with the **psychic feeling of force** or the exertion of force as man knows it within himself. To be sure, the feeling of exertion that we have when we move a load or strike a blow is itself connected to a corporeal or physical force in the sense used so far; the arm could not stretch without the will setting up physical conditions in the brain that trigger the stretching according to the laws of our physical concept of force, and one can now, when entering into general...

He specifically critiques the "mythological" view of forces as external agents and distinguishes between objective physical force and the subjective feeling of exertion.</summary>

<header>VIII. Some More General Discussions. Concept of Matter, etc. 111</header>

...[general] considerations might ask whether and how the governing of physical forces (respectively the laws of equilibrium and motion) is connected with a governing and willing of the spirit in nature or above nature in general; however, these are questions that <term>natural science<note>The original German term is *Naturforschung*, referring to the empirical investigation of the physical world.</note></term>—which as such does not pursue the relationships of the physical to the spiritual, but rather the physical within itself—neither touches upon, nor can change anything in the **concept of force** in physics, regardless of how they might be answered in <term>natural philosophy<note>In the 19th century, *Naturphilosophie* was a branch of philosophy that sought to understand the organic and spiritual unity of nature, often contrasted with the more mechanical "natural science."</note></term> or a general doctrine of the relationships between the spiritual and the physical. It would be entirely groundless and merely confusing, in any case, to want to impose on natural science an explanation of the concept of force through categories that only have meaning in a connected consideration of the spiritual.

To be sure, no one could be more inclined than myself to enter into questions of that kind, as even a three-volume book of mine is primarily occupied with them. <note>Fechner is likely referring to his work *Zend-Avesta: Or on the Things of Heaven and the Hereafter* (1851), which explores the soul-life of plants, the earth, and the stars.</note> But however much I recognize a thorough connection of the physical and spiritual in a fundamental unity in the final instance, and have sought to bring this to light myself, there is nevertheless a point of view regarding the distinction between the two that one must never lose sight of without confusing the humanities and natural sciences, along with spirit and nature. This is simply not the place to elaborate on this further. We stand and remain with all our questions here, in any case, purely in the domain of nature.

In the preceding, we have done nothing more than expound upon the **concept of force**, as previously the **concept of matter**, in the sense in which it has always been factually understood and used in physics, even if not expounded in words; and accordingly, we believe we are right in saying: the physicist's concept of force is not suitable for constructing matter from it—at least not for constructing the physicist's matter from it. It is a <term>relational concept<note>A term indicating that the concept (like 'force') does not describe a thing on its own, but rather the relationship between two or more objects.</note></term>, which only has meaning for the coexistence of matter; one cannot make the coexisting matter itself out of that. It would be just as if someone wanted to construct tones out of musical intervals, which only signify a relation between tones. If, therefore, the philosopher wants to construct his matter out of forces, then both, or at least one, can only be entities of a peculiar kind that have nothing in common with the matter and forces of the physicist. But since the philosopher nonetheless wants to illuminate physics with his concepts of matter and force, he misses his purpose from the start by using words that have a fixed material meaning in physics in a different sense, and it is impossible for him to be understood by it.

But would the physicist do well to perhaps give up his concept of force—that is, his factual meaning of the word *force*—to adopt that of the philosopher instead? In itself and in the matter...

...he could gain nothing by changing the meaning of a word; yet it would be advisable to agree to it, if the philosophical meaning were not only clear in itself, but also integrated into a more general context of meanings that spread a greater and higher clarity over the physicist’s field than he himself has achieved with his own system of word usage until now; in short, if the clarity he has had so far were not diminished, but rather increased by it. <margin><unclear>with [unclear] [unclear] [unclear] [unclear]</unclear></margin>
Looked at more closely, the conception of the relationship between matter and force in philosophy and physics is exactly reversed. According to philosophy, matter is grounded in a relationship or conflict of forces (attractive force, repulsive force); according to physics, force is grounded in a (lawful) relationship of matters. The physicist’s matter is immediately demonstrable as something tangible; his relationship of force is immediately explicable as a relationship of law through demonstrable conditions; here, everything is clear. The <term>a priori<note>original: "aprioristischen" — Latin for "from the former," referring to knowledge that comes from theoretical deduction or innate reasoning rather than observation.</note></term> forces of the philosopher are not immediately demonstrable and are only explicable through those same <term>a posteriori<note>original: "aposteriorischen" — Latin for "from the latter," referring to knowledge derived from experience or empirical evidence.</note></term> forces that the physicist has before his eyes; the conflict of forces can only lean on vague analogies. Consequently, the entire construction of matter out of a conflict of forces prior to experience falls into obscurity, and the attempt to explain it through the very thing that is supposed to be explained is in vain.

It is now, however, certainly understandable that if one constructs matter from forces that already blur and dissolve in their concept—and furthermore holds fast to the inherently correct <term>insight<note>original: "Aperçu" — a French term for a quick, intuitive grasp or summary observation.</note></term> that force, like the law on which force depends, is something omnipresent, all-pervading in space—the matter constructed from these forces must become something dissolved, blurry, and all-pervading in space, and <term>atomism<note>original: "Atomistik" — the theory that matter is composed of discrete, indivisible units called atoms.</note></term> can find no place. However, there is nowhere a proof that a thing cannot stand simply because it does not stand when one removes its solid ground and turns it upside down.

For natural science, nature is generally only a world of external sensory appearance, and therefore it is not surprising if the substance of nature, matter, is also conceived and treated by it only as a subject of such appearance, characterized only by appearance and its relationships, and if the force of nature is only [defined] from...

...abstracted from such relationships. It lies in its concept of nature. Likewise, it is of course not surprising if philosophy—insofar as it understands "nature" to be a <term>transcendent<note>Existing beyond the limits of ordinary experience or the physical world; something that cannot be directly observed.</note></term> being behind the nature described by the scientist—also understands the matter and force of nature to be transcendent beings behind the matter and force of the scientist. It is no wonder, then, if philosophy cannot consider the concepts of matter and force to be exhausted by tangibility, visibility, or the relationships and abstractions derived from them, or even consider their true essence to have been touched at all. This lies similarly in philosophy’s own concept of nature. And finally, it is not surprising if what the philosopher says about the relationship between matter and force fits what the physicist says as poorly as if one were speaking of the back of a blade and the other of its edge. It is only surprising when the philosopher wishes to substitute the back for the edge. The physical formulation of the relationship between matter and force resembles one face of a <term>Janus-head<note>original: "Januskopf" — In Roman mythology, Janus was the god of beginnings and transitions, usually depicted with two faces looking in opposite directions.</note></term>, and the philosophical formulation the other. Both are joined together at the back and show the same form in opposite directions. One looks forward into the bright fields of <term>appearance<note>original: "Erscheinung" — In a philosophical context, this refers to the world as we perceive it through our senses, rather than how it might exist in its "true" or hidden essence.</note></term>, the other backward into the transcendent darkness. Now, the second disturbs the clear insights of the first with the dreams that awaken in this darkness, imagining that he sees into the mysterious depths of things while the other looks into a blinding nothingness. And yet, everything he dreams has flowed to him, secretly and confusedly, only through the connection he shares with the other, coming from the other's world of observation.

Undoubtedly, we cannot claim that what has been said here in a certain general sense against the <term>dynamic<note>A view that explains the properties of matter through the action of forces (like attraction and repulsion) rather than as solid, inert substances.</note></term> conception of the relationship between matter and force applies to every dynamic view in the same way. We have already stated: it is generally impossible to strike one view precisely while also hitting another. Every view ultimately understands the relationship between matter and force somewhat differently; although, given our decision not to go into more specific detail, the previous points will apply to more than one. Only a few different turns of phrase would be needed to make them fit others. However, it was explicitly not our intention to meet the ever-new twists of the dynamic view with ever-new counter-arguments.

# VIII. Some More General Discussions. The Concept of Matter, etc.
**8***

Since this section has led us away from our main question into reflections that concern the general position of this question within the entire system of observing things—rather than its direct resolution, which does not rely on such underlying generalities—we wish to finally conclude it with some most general and summarizing considerations. If, in both the <term>natural and human sciences<note>original: "Natur- und Geisteswissenschaft" — The 'Geisteswissenschaften' (literally "sciences of the spirit") refer to the humanities, such as history, philosophy, and social sciences, which study human culture and the mind.</note></term>, we refuse everywhere to go back behind what can be demonstrated, the <term>phenomenon<note>original: "Erscheinung" — In philosophy, this refers to the world as it is perceived by our senses, as opposed to a "thing-in-itself" which might exist independently of observation.</note></term>, and what is representable within its context; if we do not wish to grant even philosophy such a right because it lacks the power to do so, only establishing abstractions, generalizations, connections, laws, and limits that lead from the demonstrable and the representable beyond themselves without ever being able to detach from their reference to it; then the question arises: what ultimately remains for the need of a deeper consideration, for a more ideal interest, for a longing not merely satisfied with what is given? For one will never lock the human spirit and the human soul so tightly into the phenomenon that it would find itself content in this imprisonment, no longer longing for a deeper <term>essence<note>original: "Wesen" — The fundamental nature of a thing, often contrasted with its outer appearance.</note></term>, no longer striving for something <term>supersensible<note>original: "Uebersinnliches" — That which exists beyond the reach of the physical senses.</note></term>, and no longer truly holding onto the unseen.

But this entire need, interest, and longing, by being directed into the proper limits of its possible satisfaction, is satisfied to a fuller extent than by the pretending to offer glimpses into the impossible and the empty. For while this mode of consideration refuses to go back behind the phenomenon and what is thereby representable, it does not refuse to analyze the representable to its ultimate degree—thus obtaining the elements and methods of construction for the smallest as well as the largest things that can enter into appearance. While it knows nothing of a "quality" or an "essence" positioned behind the phenomenon and finds only empty words therein, it does not renounce the recognition of the essential nature of all phenomena—that is, the constant, the lawful, the general, and the eternally remaining elements in all appearance—and, in relation to those, weighing every individual phenomenon. While it does not know how to begin with something "Highest" or "Infinite," it looks toward it as a goal; while it confesses to having no inkling of something that can never enter into the phenomenon nor be gained from it, and even considers an "Idea" to be nothing if it does not appear in a mind, it nevertheless strives for...

# VIII. Some More General Discussions. The Concept of Matter, etc.

...all the more so, from that which appears today, here, and to me and you and as many as possible, to investigate what can appear elsewhere and at another time and under any other conditions and to other beings, and to research the existence of other beings themselves. The Beyond, God, and souls—regarding whether and how they are, that is, how they appear to themselves—thus enter the circle of consideration based on the generalization, expansion, enhancement, and culmination of what appears to us, and how we appear to ourselves. Whoever speaks of another kind of the supersensible, or speaks of the supersensible in a way other than what is contained within the circle of the <term>phenomenal<note>original: "Erscheinlichen" — things as they appear to our senses or consciousness.</note></term> listed here and what can be gained from it, does not know what he says, does not know what he seeks.

These remain the basic principles of all sustainable knowledge; in matters of faith, many things may be positioned differently. Above all, these principles are: to firmly establish what is given, to clarify our ideas of it, and to collect from it everything that is possible to collect; to find the non-given from the given, and to find other imaginable things from what is already imaginable. One must never do the reverse: never try to find the given from the non-given, nor the imaginable from the unimaginable. One must support the highest upon the lowest, and the most general upon the most specific; meaning should be assigned to the most abstract ideas only in relation to their <term>concretes<note>original: "Concretis" (Latin) — specific, tangible examples or objects that give an abstract concept its meaning.</note></term>. We must construct our concepts according to things, and only then understand things according to concepts. No word should be used without relating it—either on its own or in context—to something in reality (or a representation of reality) that can be pointed out or clearly explained. Yet, while respecting all of this, one should seek in everything the most general, highest, ultimate, One, and Eternal; no individual, low, or finite thing should be permitted to exist without being connected to and finding its completion in that higher unity.

By placing the requirement to support the highest upon the lowest and the most general upon the most specific, I am not asserting that philosophy, as the science of sciences, should be nothing more than a mere compilation of the individual, the low, and the finite, upon whose foundation it must first ascend. Rather, the following image has always seemed apt to me: one must build a tower from the broad base, not from the tip, and the higher the tower is intended to be, the broader the base must be. However, once one has reached the tip—indeed, at every stage toward the tip—one can look much further across the land than from all the lower levels, which nevertheless...

<summary>The author defines his philosophical method: higher spiritual concepts like God and the soul must be derived from a broad base of observable facts and logical generalizations. He uses the metaphor of a tower to show that while the "tip" of philosophy offers the broadest view, it remains entirely dependent on its solid, empirical foundation.</summary>

# VIII. Some More General Discussions. The Concept of Matter, etc.

...which first had to lead to the peak. Look around further, I say, and thereby grasp the general conditions more correctly; although he who walks in the land itself will always grasp the individual more accurately than he who looks at it from above on the tower. One may also direct one's thoughts toward the top of the tower in advance, before one has actually built the tower to that height; yet one should consider this more as a prospect of higher and wider views into the land, rather than holding such views as already established. Above all, in dreaming of ideal vistas, one must not lose sight of the real ones.

There is, however, certainly a way to build the tower on its peak—indeed, to let it rise quickly to a dizzying height—so that it nonetheless seems to stand as firmly as if it were rooted in the ground, and all its brackets seem as durable as if they were made of steel and iron. In reality, one creates this illusion by observing the tower in a **concave mirror** <note>original: "Hohlspiegel"</note>; in philosophy, one does this by observing the *world* in the mirror of the *Schellingian*, *Hegelian*, or *Herbartian* systems. <note>Friedrich Schelling (1775–1854), G.W.F. Hegel (1770–1831), and Johann Friedrich Herbart (1776–1841) were towering figures of 19th-century German philosophy. The author is critiquing their tendency to prioritize abstract systems over empirical observation.</note> These systems differ mainly in that the first two <note>Schelling and Hegel</note> mistake the upside-down position of the tower for the true one, while the latter <note>Herbart</note> treats the illusion as the tower and the tower as the illusion; the peak of the former floats entirely in the air, while that of the latter touches the solid ground only slightly.

In what way, then, is the real tower preferable to the illusory image? One can actually step upon its stairs; its bells actually ring out into the land; from its summit, one can actually see new things. The illusory image is only there to be looked at.

Certainly, great insights have emerged from *Schelling* and *Hegel*. But everything dissolves into futility or first requires a firm shape and foundation, because their gaze, instead of being directed sharply at *things*, wants to conjure up the *things themselves*. Nonetheless, I prefer the boldly out-looking, far-reaching gaze of *Schelling* and *Hegel* far above the hair-splitting <note>original: "spintisirende"</note>, narrowness-constricting gaze of *Herbart*. I prefer their world full of powerful, interlocking, holding, and supporting **mist-images** <note>original: "Nebelbilder" — grand but vague intellectual constructs</note> to the individual **mist-bubbles** into which the world disintegrates [in Herbart's system]. I prefer the hand that reaches into the blue to encompass the *world* with a single grasp, to the hand that prepares to rub it into dust. Both are equally futile efforts; yet the former striving is at least directed toward that which is: the high, the eternal, the infinite, the whole.

He favors the "grand" vision of absolute idealism over the "fragmented" realism of Herbart, even while finding both flawed for lacking empirical grounding.</summary>

...and the human hand is soon confused now with God's hand, and the grasping for the entire contents of the world with the content itself; meanwhile, the other <note>Referring to the "other" philosophical system, likely Hegel's or Schelling's as contrasted with Herbart's below.</note> shatters the work of God's hand and throws God himself as a speck of dust among the ruins. And so it is certainly no wonder if many a person, to escape drowning, would rather be cast upon the sand, even if he can expect only starvation there instead of drowning.

## **IX. Relation of Atomism to the most general, highest, and ultimate things.**

In conclusion, let us return with some more general considerations to the objection raised by opponents at the beginning: that atomism is incompatible with our more general and higher interests. Many of our previous observations have already paved the way for this reflection, so that after everything, no one might say: "For what shall it profit me, if I should gain the whole world with atomism, and yet suffer the loss of my soul?" <note>A paraphrase of Matthew 16:26. The author is addressing the fear that a scientific, materialist view of atoms "kills" the spirit.</note>

It is easily shown that the entire accusation rests only on the fact that the opposing philosophy simply takes the incompatibility of atomism with the *way* in which it itself seeks to satisfy the most general and highest interests as an incompatibility with those interests themselves. And how far apart these two things actually are! Rather, atomism would try in vain to achieve more in terms of this satisfaction than today's philosophy, were it to try following its paths.

Let us examine in more detail the points to be considered here. Does the <term>dynamic view</term> <note>The "dynamic view" (dynamische Ansicht) was a popular 19th-century alternative to atomism, suggesting that matter is not made of particles but of continuous forces or "powers."</note> open any glimpse into the depths of things, such that one might perceive something new, different, finer, or more hidden through it, where atomism could not follow? Does nature, spirit, force, life, or organization appear there—

<summary>The author defends atomism against the charge that it is spiritually hollow, arguing that traditional philosophies confuse their own abstract methods with the actual higher interests of humanity. He begins to contrast the "dynamic" view of nature with the "atomistic" one.</summary>

<keywords>atomism, Johann Friedrich Herbart, soul, philosophy of nature, dynamic view, materialism vs idealism, 19th-century philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s defense of the atomistic worldview against metaphysical and theological objections. The author now begins this page mid-sentence, concluding his rebuttal of the idea that alternative philosophical systems are somehow more "sublime" or "spiritual."</meta>

...more beautiful, graceful, rich, ideal, praiseworthy, or sublime? I believe I have shown the opposite of all that. In what, then, does the advantage of the <term>dynamic view<note>The "dynamic view" (dynamische Ansicht) was a popular 19th-century philosophical alternative to atomism, suggesting that matter is not made of particles but is instead a continuous field of forces filling space.</note></term> and the "crime" of the <term>atomistic view<note>Atomism (Atomistik) is the scientific theory that matter consists of discrete, indivisible particles.</note></term> ultimately consist? It is simply that the latter refuses to tolerate the concept of a "space-filling force" within physics; it positions matter, force, and space conceptually and materially differently than the dynamic view does. But what has actually been gained for humanity by that dynamic concept of "space-filling"? What has this conceptual arrangement contributed to our orientation among phenomena, to the clear visualization of their connection, to their predictability, or to the possibility of deducing one from the other? — It has offered only words, whereas atomism can point to deeds.

Now, there is still much—indeed, higher things—that lies beyond mere intellectual interest altogether, and to which the question of atoms is actually quite remote. Yet, because everything—knowledge, faith, and action—is ultimately connected, and because each must align with the others according to the content and spirit of the whole, one can certainly ask: how does the question of atoms, which initially only concerns the structure of the physical world, relate to questions regarding the highest and ultimate things? As far apart as God and the atom may lie, a chain must still reach from one to the other; and where no such chain is found, neither one nor the other can truly exist. The same spirit that moves through atomism must still be recognizable as a breath of that same spirit that moves through all the heavens, if atomism is to coexist with God, and God with it. 

Is this the case? But is it necessary to remind ourselves once more that the world of atoms—lying deep beneath our eyes at the "lower end" of the world—is at the same time the reflection and completion of that same world whose structure we admire in the heavens high above us? And if we perceive in the heavens the testimony of the greatness and fullness of divine power and an ordering spirit, why should we see less in the atoms? If astronomy is compatible with God, why then should atomism be any less so? It is merely the complement to astronomy, showing that nature—the foundation of God—is not only built with order across the greatest distances, but also in depths that our physical eyes are unable to penetrate.

Perhaps also regarding the question of whether the body and soul, which exist and walk so firmly united here below, perish together in that same union, there might be a difference—

<summary>The author argues that atomism is not a "godless" science but rather the microscopic counterpart to astronomy; both reveal a divinely ordered universe, one in the vast heavens and the other in the infinitesimal depths of matter.</summary>

### IX. Relationships of Atomism to the Most General, Highest [Themes], etc.

...tion whether the body is a being that even now, without a physical bond, holds together only through force and spirit, and, constantly dispersing into its individual parts, reconstitutes itself anew from them; or whether that which is corporeal is in itself something continuous, a fluid, a whole. I believe, however, that the more severe the splitting and dispersal of matter we see the spirit already overcoming and outlasting in the present, the less we have to fear from such things in death. It appears that the splitting and dispersal of matter is precisely what provides the spirit with the occasion for binding and ever-renewing that bond.

Let us recall, in the case of the rupture of life, the rupture of physical bodies (p. 54). In the sense of <term>atomism<note>The theory that all matter is made of discrete, tiny particles.</note></term>, the already existing distance between atoms merely expands rapidly until it becomes visible; that is the only novelty. In the sense of the <term>dynamic view<note>The philosophical rival to atomism, which argued matter was a continuous "fluid" or field of force with no gaps.</note></term>, continuity itself snaps. Likewise, in the sense of the atomistic view, death is only a rapid increase of that same separation and dispersal of our body's atoms that already exists and occurs daily; that is the only novelty. In the sense of the dynamic view, the previous continuity snaps. Thus, the atomistic view is like a spinner who, in death, only suddenly draws the thread of life out long; the dynamic view is like a <term>Fate<note>original: "Parze." In mythology, the Fates (Parcae) were deities who spun, measured, and ultimately cut the thread of human life.</note></term> who cuts it through.

One may also hold the question of atoms up to the questions of morality and freedom. I see nothing—and no one has yet shown anything—that could put us in a worse position regarding those questions if we choose to think of the material world as consisting of individual parts linked by force rather than as a fluid substance. On the contrary, by developing the principle of individuality in the physical world down to its most fundamental level, and by allowing each individual part to follow its own motion while still conforming to general law and association, atomism provides, even in the world of the smallest things, the most beautiful illustrative images for a social organization and structure. In such a structure, each individual has their right, duty, and activity only according to their position within the whole. A human being cannot achieve anything higher in morality than an atom does in its association, except that the human achieves it through freedom. It goes without saying that more freedom of movement occurs when each particle has space around it than when it sticks to its neighbor; and spiritual freedom is also
