# IX. Relationships of Atomism to the Most General, Highest [Themes], etc. 121

...of a different nature than this physical one, it still finds its most perfect instrument within it.

These brief hints—it would seem easy, yet useless, to develop them further—should suffice to show that <term>atomism<note>The theory that all matter is made of discrete, tiny particles.</note></term> is not only compatible with all the demands we might make regarding the highest and final things, but actually meets them in a pleasing and beneficial way, extending far beyond the requirements of physics.

In this regard, it must be remembered again that modern atomism is no longer the ancient atomism, to which some reproaches may have applied that can no longer affect the modern version.

To be sure, there are still atheists and those who deny immortality among the atomists today; however, there are no fewer among their opponents. Indisputably, atomism—like <term>natural science<note>original: "Naturforschung"; the empirical study of the physical world.</note></term> in general, of which it is a branch—carries a danger of losing God and what is connected to God. This happens to one who forgets that atomism only concerns the ultimate structure of the material world, not the ideal unity, the pinnacle, and the essence of things, and perhaps loses sight of one over the other or thinks one is removed or excluded by the other. But what aspect of truth and reality is not subject to the danger of leading to error if it is given sole weight, or if the totality is sought within it or built upon it? 

On the other hand, atomism—which has nothing to do with God because that lies entirely outside its task—is much less in danger of doing away with God, or reducing Him to such an empty concept, or letting Him be absorbed into such anthropomorphism, as are those philosophical trends that attribute a <term>cloven hoof<note>original: "Pferdefuss"; a German idiom referring to a hidden devilish or malicious quality.</note></term> to atomism. Thus, the danger lies on each side, just in a different place: there in a negation, here in certain positions; which is worse? Ultimately, however, it is the fault of neither atomism nor philosophy, but of the atomist or the philosopher who loses God or allows His idea to be turned into illusion and madness.

In general, one can say that faith in those highest ideas takes science in hand much more as a staff than relying on it as a foot to stand on. That atomism is at least as useful as a staff of faith—should one seek such a thing—as the <term>dynamic view<note>The rival theory to atomism, which viewed matter as a continuous force or fluid without gaps.</note></term>, I believe I have shown in any case with the preceding hints;

The dynamic view of things resembles a mist that covers the landscape in a continuous appearance, substituting its billowing, drifting, and fleeting movement for the things it hides. This continuous mist must dissolve into discontinuous drops of rain and dew; from this comes fertility and clarity, and things appear in their brilliance.

## ON
# THE PHILOSOPHICAL THEORY OF ATOMS. <note>original: "Die philosophische Atomenlehre." This title refers to the 19th-century philosophical movement, led by thinkers like Gustav Fechner, that sought to reconcile the physical existence of atoms with a spiritual or metaphysical understanding of the universe.</note>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of the first chapter of the treatise. Following the title page, the author shifts from the general assertion that atomism is divinely architectural to a rigorous defense of why atoms must be justified both physically and philosophically.</meta>

## I. Introduction.

The physicist has challenged the philosopher: if you want to take atoms away from us, provide us with a replacement; do not merely claim *that* you can, but show us *how* we might achieve the same results—if not more—without atoms, and how we can establish, maintain, and further develop our real and formal coherence <note>original: "Real- und Formalzusammenhang." This refers to the objective physical connections between things (real) and the logical or mathematical structures that describe them (formal).</note> without them. In a word: show us how we can do without them and yet still retain a physics worthy of the name.

Indisputably, the philosopher can now hold something equivalent against the physicist: if you want to force atoms upon us, do not merely say *that* they exist, but show us how a philosophical system is possible with them, and how a philosophical view of nature <note>original: "Naturansicht." A holistic perspective or "vision" of nature common in 19th-century German science.</note> can be constructed with them. Quite apart from the dispute over whether such a view should be constructed <term>dialectically</term> <note>a method of argument that resolves contradictions through logic</note> or not, it is not enough to simply arrive at a physical limit where the conclusions drawn from experience fail—as happens in physical atomism. Rather, it is necessary to specify a true limit, one set by the concept itself. Despite all the conflict between philosophical systems, they will demand this unanimously, because it lies within the very concept of philosophy itself. The physicist may entrench himself behind his inability to go beyond what stands in traceable relation to experience; for the philosopher, however, that is precisely the vocation. To what, then, are these small masses finally supposed to lead, at which the physicist stops halfway? They are not yet the ultimate point at which one can remain. Even if a physical step forward is gained through them, philosophically they remain merely a problem that has been pushed back, and the goal lies as far beyond them as before. But a path is only justified if it has a goal at all, and even when one is only halfway there, one should look toward that goal; otherwise, one fumbles with open eyes worse than in the dark. And if no sustainable goal can be found, one should leave the path, no matter how much temptation lies along the way.

<summary>This page introduces a fundamental debate between physicists, who require atoms to explain natural phenomena, and philosophers, who demand a logical and conceptual justification for their existence beyond mere empirical observation.</summary>
<keywords>atomism, philosophy of nature, physics, limits of experience, dialectic, scientific method, material coherence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introduction from the previous page, where the author established the tension between physics and philosophy. Here, the author proposes a new "Metaphysics" that is built upon the "exact sciences" rather than existing independently of them.</meta>

## I. Introduction.

Well then, let us not deny philosophy the right to press for a finality in the analysis of the material world and to go where the science of matter itself is currently unable to reach. Let us allow philosophy to remind science that it cannot remain entirely indifferent to such ultimate questions. In this regard, let us also acknowledge a certain claim <note>original: "Anmuthung." Here meaning an expectation or demand placed upon the author's work.</note> made of us. To be sure, no one will expect us to build a philosophical system entirely from scratch here; no one would have the patience to listen to it. However, we may well meet the demand to establish a philosophical conclusion to physical atomism—one where the mind can find rest in a concept while simultaneously gaining a connection to more general principles. 

One must only refrain from demanding that this conclusion and connection fit perfectly into the specific system of this or that particular philosopher. What suits one would surely not suit another. It is enough that—aside from the physical necessity encountered on the way—such a conclusion and connection satisfy the general philosophical requirement on which all philosophers agree: the very concept of philosophy itself. Even if this approach truly aims at a new philosophical system, it is simply the fate of philosophy to progress through new systems. And if a new system requires development on an old foundation—well then, the entirety of the **exact sciences** belongs to us as that foundation. The novelty of the **metaphysics** we have in mind—for it is metaphysics we are dealing with here, after all—lies in the fact that, after so many attempted foundations for metaphysics, we are finally making the science of the physical (though not without the science of the mental) its basis. In doing so, we elevate the name ***metaphysics*** to an ***act***; that is, we make it something truly *after* physics, instead of something *independent of experience* <note>original: "a priori"</note> or *behind* physics.

True, what metaphysician would not claim that he, too, makes the consideration of the physical one of his lower foundations? Yet it remains peculiar, and almost difficult to interpret, that many prefer raw, direct observation of the physical as such a foundation over the actual science of physics. It is as if they were still standing at the starting point of <term>Naturphilosophie</term> <note>A 19th-century German movement that sought a speculative, unified understanding of nature, often criticized by experimental scientists for being too abstract.</note>, where the science of the physical with its raw...

# I. Introduction.

...consideration <note>original: "trachtung," the conclusion of "Betrachtung" (consideration/observation) from the previous page.</note> still almost immediately coincided, and therefore, of course, could not be preferred to it. Now science is here and has become highly developed, yet people remain stuck at that old standpoint and cheer for Goethe as he lays his laurel upon the old stone. <note>A reference to Johann Wolfgang von Goethe, who often favored intuitive, holistic observation over the analytical, mathematical approach of modern physics.</note> We, however, want to build the entire developed science of the physical underneath **metaphysics**; let the latter recognize only the final peaks of this science and weave them together with other peaks into one ultimate summit. **Atomism** lies on this path and culminates within this summit. 

Right now, however, such a metaphysics might be required as a counterpoint, after Herbart’s <note>Johann Friedrich Herbart (1776–1841) was a philosopher whose "metaphysics of reals" the author finds incompatible with the "exact" methods of physical science.</note> metaphysics has based itself only upon the total ruin of everything on which **exact science** builds—the complete destruction of its fundamental concepts—and has usurped the name of the kingdom it destroyed. Now, in the "Hades of Being" which they have created, the **monads** <term>monads: fundamental, indivisible units of reality; here the author compares different philosophical definitions of these "atoms of the soul."</term> wander as intangible ghosts; with us, however, they walk in the full realm of light—indeed, they generate that light itself through the beating of their wings.

Following all this, I see the task of **metaphysics** as finding the most general and the **limit concepts** <term>limit concepts (Gränzebegriffe): ideas that represent the absolute boundaries of what can be known or measured, where physics ends and pure thought begins.</term> of the "Given" <note>original: "des Gegebenen." In philosophy, the "Given" refers to the raw data of experience before it is processed by the mind.</note> and to research, follow, and present them in their most general relationships and connections. The method for this consists of a generalization and continuation of what has been found, proven, and verified through experience, empirical inference, and calculation. This extension goes beyond what can be empirically and mathematically tracked and proven, reaching toward the boundary that thought demands. Thus, the condition of attaining a most general and ultimate point determines the *form*, while the origin from experience determines the *content* of the concepts found in this way. Or in short: it is a grasping of the most general and the limit concepts of the Given by proceeding and inferring based on the Given itself toward the most general and the ultimate.

This path—since it admittedly leads beyond what can be proven by experience and calculation and finds its goal half through a **rational inference** <note>original: "Vernunftschluss."</note> and half through a **rational demand** <note>original: "Vernunftfoderung." A "rational demand" is a concept the mind requires to make sense of the world, even if it cannot be strictly proven.</note>—cannot, of course, possess the same certainty that the exact methods themselves have, nor that which the dialectical method claims for itself. What the exact sciences leave uncertain, metaphysics cannot make "exactly" certain; otherwise, it would belong to the exact sciences themselves. It cannot compel the reason as an exact inference does, but only a grea...

And thus, what we present as a **metaphysical idea** will, from a physical perspective, always remain merely a **hypothesis**—one that perhaps may one day be exactly proven or, for that matter, refuted, when physics has finally caught up with metaphysics at its ultimate goal. For now, however, it can only serve to open up a vista, rather than a complete insight, for science. One should therefore not demand any physical proof for it other than that it forms the conceptual conclusion of what has been physically proven. But that itself is no longer physics.

In the meantime, another proof will naturally emerge to meet this one. It will be shown how the same conclusion—to which one feels driven while seeking to satisfy a requirement placed upon physics by philosophy—places such pure concepts at the head of the observation of natural things. These concepts, in their opposition, mutual supplementation, and unified connection, seem to provide the most favorable starting and connection point for a more general consideration of natural relations. The view we present in the following is based on the meeting and connection of these two viewpoints, each of which on its own might seem insufficient to provide a firm footing:

1) One arrives at this view by conceptually following to its end the path that exact science treads with certainty.
2) With this end, the most favorable conceptual situation opens up for **philosophy**, under which the real conditions of nature can be grasped in the most general way.

## II. Basic Viewpoints.

Given the preceding, I now say: instead of stopping halfway, as **atomism** is accused of doing, or finally coming to nothing—and thus being forced to either remain purely physical or become nihilistic—a third option remains as the philosophical conclusion of physical atomism. This is the arrival at **simple beings**, which possess only a location <note>The author is referring to "mathematical points" that occupy a coordinate in space but have no physical volume or "extension."</note> but no longer have extension, while through their distance from one another, they allow the systems composed of them to still possess such extension.

## II. Basic Viewpoints.

The **simple** is not yet **nothingness**; one should be careful not to confuse the two. They are very different categories; why else would there be two different words for them, and why would they present such different characters to us? At any rate, no philosophical offense can lie in the assumption of "simple real beings," since people have tolerated **Herbart’s** <note>Johann Friedrich Herbart (1776–1841) was a German philosopher who proposed that the world is composed of "reals"—indestructible, simple substances.</note> simple real beings—or at least did not reject them purely for their simplicity—and have so often claimed a simple nature for the **soul** itself. The only difference is that both of these only feign simplicity, insofar as Herbart’s simple real beings contain the entire multiplicity of real occurrences, and the soul contains the entire multiplicity of ideal occurrences. Now, it can hardly be a reproach if simple real beings are, for once, actually and "really" simple. As such, however, our beings will prove themselves in every respect, since no "happening" <note>original: *Geschehen*, referring to events, processes, or changes over time</note> enters into them; rather, they alone enter into the happening; they are the rigid dice with which it plays.

Indisputably, there is something simple in the soul: the abstract unity of **consciousness**, whereas every concrete consciousness links a multiplicity within itself. Opposite this abstract simplicity, in which the multiplicity of the spirit finds its peak, stands the **concretely simple**, into which the world of bodies is individualized at its base. However, it seems to me a "topsy-turvy world" to treat simplicity and separateness as the substance of the soul, while treating the binding and the fluid as the substance of the body. In every respect, the spiritual behaves as something essentially linking, while matter behaves only as something subject to external linking. After all, our body itself falls apart without a soul, and our soul exists here on earth only as the bond of the body and of bodily occurrence and change (which is why it is not always the same body); it must be thought of as existing in solidarity with other spirits within the universal divine bond.

Insofar as one cannot philosophically stop at **atoms** as small extended masses as a final point, two possibilities generally remain open: either to fill the entire extent of space with matter, or to reduce the extent of matter to nothing. The **dynamic view** took the first path, and from first principles <note>original: *a priori*</note> it could be just as right as our **monadology** <term>Monadology: A theory, most famously associated with Leibniz, that the universe is made of fundamental, point-like units called "monads."</term>. But now, not only does the sum of the observations in the previous section from experience <note>original: *a posteriori*</note> compel us; rather, it forces us onto the second path

<summary>The author distinguishes his "simple beings" (atoms as points) from the complex "simples" of Herbart or the soul, arguing that matter is essentially something that is acted upon and linked, while the spirit is the active force that binds.</summary>

## II. Fundamental Perspectives.

...to enter into; rather, everything that follows will also demonstrate that, within the realm of general philosophical connections, we fare better with this conception than with the dynamic one. To support the claim that physics itself offers no objection to the assumption of simple atoms, one can first point out that even before our time, esteemed physicists and mathematicians such as Ampère, Cauchy, Séguin, and Moigno have asserted the existence of simple atoms (for which one may compare the compilation in the fourth chapter). Furthermore, W. Weber, in his letter to me, established their possibility—indeed, he based a spiritual conception of <term>atomism</term><note>Atomism: The philosophical and scientific theory that the universe is composed of tiny, indivisible units.</note> upon them, as seen on page 73. Aside from these authorities, however, the claim that seeking the location of <term>matter</term> in extensionless, discrete points contains nothing contradictory to the spirit and methods of physics can be justified. After all, physics reduces the mass of entire extended bodies—for instance, the Sun and the Earth—to points when calculating the magnitude of their mutual attraction, or considers it concentrated in points (the centers of gravity), and uses the distance between these points for its calculations.

<margin>z</margin> To be sure, this is only in a figurative sense—a mere fiction intended to simplify the representation of phenomena in composite bodies without losing clarity; however, it is all the more consistent with the spirit of physics to accept this same conception as true regarding the fundamental elements of bodies, thereby providing this fiction with a real foundation in the metaphysical realm.

One might add the observation that the attraction of bodies, as a function of their distance, cannot be understood in any way other than as an attraction from point to point. This is because a definite distance only exists between two points; consequently, the function of the distance only becomes definite in this way. One might even ask whether a function that, by its very concept and essence, refers to points does not also inherently presuppose the discreteness of these points. This is because a point cannot be a continuum in itself, nor can it create one when combined with other points, nor can it result from the analysis of a continuum (cf. several subsequent discussions on this topic). In the meantime, we do not wish to base any proof on such considerations, which always retain some degree of obscurity, though others have certainly done so.*)

*) Indeed, Moigno (in *Cosmos* II, p. 378) believes he has found a decisive argument for the simplicity of atoms in their essentially point-like nature...

## II. Fundamental Perspectives.

From another side, the following consideration presents itself. Under the assumption of discrete, simple <term>atoms</term><note>Atoms: In this context, the author refers to indivisible, extensionless points of matter.</note>, the attraction of two whole finite bodies to one another is calculated simply and cleanly, without remainder, through the <term>summation</term><note>Summation: The mathematical process of adding together the individual forces of distinct points to find a total force.</note> of the attractions of a finite number of specific points at specific distances. Should the ultimate atoms still possess a small degree of extension (volume), then the attraction of two whole finite bodies would have to be calculated half through such finite summation and half through infinitesimal <term>integration</term><note>Integration: A mathematical method used to calculate totals for continuous masses; the author argues this is less "pure" than simple addition when applied to discrete particles.</note> (on account of the continuum that each atom would still enclose). Finally, should the <term>dynamic view</term><note>Dynamic view: The theory that matter is a continuous force or field rather than composed of distinct particles.</note> be correct, one would—as it at least initially seems—only require integration to calculate the attraction of two bodily masses. Unquestionably, from a formal point of view, the first of these three principles (pure finite summation) can contend with the last (integration); indeed, at first glance and from a certain perspective, integration might even seem to have more in its favor than finite summation. However, it would certainly not be a formally favorable relationship if (in the sense of the second principle) the calculation had to be based half on finite summation and half on infinitesimal integration, as would be the case if one attributed an extension to discrete atoms. In contrast, we gain in the idea of absolutely simple atoms a pure principle of calculation that can be carried out consistently throughout the entire material world. Everything within the realm of the attraction of finite bodies is now reduced to a cleanly resolved finite summation of the effects of the smallest parts. But if integration in the sense of the dynamic view seems to offer the same advantage of a consistently applicable principle—and to that extent seems even more preferable, since in the execution of attraction-calculations one is always prompted to take refuge in integration—the following must be taken into account, quite regardless of the factual reasons developed in the previous part, which simply do not allow us to submit to dynamic assumptions:

The integration used in the calculation of the attraction of two bodies is, in general, strictly and properly applicable only to perfectly <term>homogeneous</term><note>Homogeneous: Bodies that are uniform in density and composition throughout.</note> bodies, or to those in which the density changes continuously in imperceptible transitions from point to point according to a specifiable law—a case that does not occur in reality at all. In any case, it is quite impossible to carry out integration consistently through the whole material world, where so many heterogeneous bodies set themselves apart from one another. Even if one wished to calculate the attraction of the Moon and the Earth against each other in the sense of the dynamic assumption—imagining both Earth and Moon were continuously filled with uniform matter and integrating accordingly—the possibility of doing so ceases as soon as one wants to calculate the attraction of the Moon and the Earth together upon a third celestial body. Here, one can only sum the forces, and this is what is done everywhere. Thus, with the dynamic view, one necessarily falls back into the second principle, according to which finite summation is mixed with infinitesimal integration. Finite summation cannot be generally replaced by integration even for the sake of approximations; on the other hand, every integration can be reduced, in an indefinite approximation, to finite summation—

...to find the centers of attraction. I confess, however, that I did not find his argumentation to be entirely clear.

# II. Fundamental Perspectives.

can be traced back; indeed, in practice, it almost always *must* be traced back to it. One must consider that an integration sign is not yet an actual integration; and integration can usually only be achieved through <term>quadratures</term><note>Quadratures: An older mathematical term for finding the area under a curve, essentially a method for calculating an integral through numerical summation.</note> or the summation of a finite number of terms of an infinite series. And even where integration is purely executable, its results cannot be distinguished in experience from finite <term>summation</term><note>Summation: The process of adding up individual, distinct parts to reach a total, which the author argues is the true nature of physical reality.</note>. Thus, the choice remains as to which is more correct in principle.

According to the <term>dynamic view</term><note>Dynamic view: The theory that matter is continuous and composed of forces or fields rather than discrete particles.</note>, one does not gain a purely practicable principle for calculating gravitational effects, neither in theory nor in practice. Conversely, according to the <term>atomistic view</term><note>Atomistic view: The theory that matter consists of indivisible, discrete units.</note>, one certainly gains such a principle—at least in theory—since summation is regarded as the truly correct method, while integration is seen only as an <term>approximation</term><note>Approximation: A value that is nearly but not exactly correct; the author suggests that calculus is just a "close enough" tool for a reality made of separate points.</note> of reality. In certain cases, summation can be substituted for integration without any error noticeable in experience. 

Viewing it this way is not contradictory, as we are forced to rely on approximations everywhere in calculations relating to the realm of nature. Even if the dynamic view were correct, calculating the attraction of the Earth through pure integration would still have to be seen as an approximation. The dynamic view can no more account for the Earth's composition from heterogeneous masses (whose effects cannot be combined under a single integral) than it can for the irregularities of its surface, which are just as impossible to capture that way. One could therefore only say here that integration provides an approximation that does not deviate noticeably from the truth in experience.

<term>Calculus</term><note>Infinitesimalrechnung: Literally "infinitesimal calculus," the branch of mathematics dealing with continuously changing quantities.</note> presents itself quite differently in relation to space and time than it does in relation to matter. Space and time are essentially uniform and continuous, and cannot be conceived otherwise. Thus, there is no impropriety in believing that the calculation of surfaces, lines, and volumes is subject to different perspectives than the magnitudes of attraction and other relationships of matter.

One may call these simple beings material points, centers of force, punctual intensities, substantial units, simple reals, or <term>monads</term><note>Monads: A term popularized by the philosopher Leibniz to describe the fundamental, indivisible units of reality.</note>; the name is indifferent. Their nature, meaning, concept, use, and value are determined solely by the fact that they emerge as the limit of the decomposition of objectively (sensory and external) graspable real spatial content, endowed with observable properties. They are to be defined only in such relation to what is given through experience. Accordingly, they are to be imagined as points—not behind or outside time and space, but *within* time and space—with the caution that however small one wishes to imagine these points, it is still not small enough. Mathematics has already accustomed us to such things; we remain always within its view, if not led by its hand. Furthermore, nothing prevents further speculation on the nature of these points; indeed, with a derivation from above...

# II. Fundamental Perspectives.

...to meet the investigation from below, if one has confidence in it; but for us, they remain only a <term>limiting concept</term><note>Limiting concept (original: *Gränzvorstellung*): A conceptual boundary that we may never reach in physical experience but must assume exists to make sense of our observations.</note> necessary for the construction of what is given—the final **building blocks** of the given, from which it is built because into them it can be decomposed.

Now, of course, the construction of every house requires, besides the stones, also space, time, measure, rule, plan, and for all that outwardness, **Someone** for whom the whole house is built, who builds it, and who inhabits it. The building blocks do not do it alone, nor through themselves; neither the connecting nor the decomposing is their deed; they are merely that which has been decomposed; and the most general, highest, and best that belongs to the construction was lost during the decomposition of the structure and is no longer to be found in the stones; yet there is also no construction without the building blocks, and in the case of great structures, one will have to ask from what and how they built themselves or were built, until the concept itself sets the limit of the question. This limit is finally to be found in the **simple**, and only to be found in it.

One can quite well concede that the concept of absolutely simple, <term>point-like</term><note>Point-like (original: *punctueller*): Having the characteristics of a mathematical point—possessing position but no extension or physical dimensions.</note>, and in the strictest sense **infinitely small beings** remains burdened with the same difficulty as the concept of an infinitely large world, insofar as the imagination can never exhaustively keep up with it. We can, however, no more do without the concept of the **infinitely small** than that of the **infinitely large** in mathematics and the contemplation of the world; and instead of banishing it, the task is only to introduce it in the place where it bears fruit, and where one achieves less without it than with it. All hesitation must vanish if we allow the matter to be understood thus: the results derived from the assumption of atoms regarding observable reality will be found more accurately, without limit, the smaller one conceives the ultimate atoms to be. We express this briefly by saying: they are points. If one wishes, one can paraphrase all that follows in the sense of such a conception; but this would only increase the wordiness of the presentation and decrease the clarity of the focus.

With the concept of the absolute simplicity of our atoms, the concept of their absolute **discontinuity** stands in immediate connection; for insofar as they are without a multiplicity of parts and sides, they can neither be a <term>continuum</term><note>Continuum: Something that is continuous and unbroken, without separate parts.</note> in themselves, nor share parts or sides with something else, but each can only coincide entirely with itself. Conversely, they are as...

# II. Fundamental Perspectives.

...it is necessary to think of absolutely discontinuous beings as absolutely simple. Our real beings are therefore absolutely simple and absolutely discontinuous all at once. It is the case with these and other properties of simple beings—which we will discuss further—just as it is with the properties of a circle; without being conceptually the same thing, they nevertheless conceptually and factually entail and require one another, because they are connected in something identical. Let us now note that all discontinuity we may find in the world of space and time does not actually enter through space and time themselves, but through something that we must think of as being *in* time and space. Discontinuity is a matter of the space- and time-content, not of space and time themselves; and though the discontinuity we encounter between different bodies and movements may initially be called merely apparent (insofar as even the discontinuous <term>celestial bodies</term><note>Celestial bodies: Planets, stars, and other physical objects in space.</note> are still connected by the <term>ether</term><note>Ether (original: *Aether*): In 19th-century physics, a theorized medium that permeated all space, thought to allow the transmission of light and gravity.</note>), we now see the ground of relative or apparent discontinuity in our simple beings traced back to an absolute. Space and time are the absolutely continuous; matter is the absolutely discontinuous; and if one goes to the root of the matter, even apparently continuous matter is truly discontinuous. Just as the concept of continuity <term>hypostatizes</term><note>Hypostatizes (original: *hypostasirt*): To treat an abstract concept as if it were a distinct, physical reality or substance.</note> itself purely in space and time, so does the concept of discontinuity in matter.

While absolute continuity belongs to time and space for themselves, and absolute discontinuity belongs to the simple elements of matter for themselves, in the world of bodies—which includes both matter and space at once—relations of continuity and discontinuity come into play. Generally, as long as one does not go to the ultimate level, it is not a question of absolute, but only of relative continuity and discontinuity in the physical world. Through relative continuity, every body hangs together within itself; through relative discontinuity, every body closes itself off from its surroundings and gains divisions, an internal structure.

If a mass of air rests over a mass of water, each of the two—despite the fact that their atoms, taken absolutely, are discontinuous toward each other—is nevertheless, taken relatively, perfectly continuous in itself, insofar as the distance and the arrangement of the atoms or molecules remains continuously the same throughout the extent of each mass; they are, on the other hand, relatively discontinuous and thus demarcated from one another insofar as a "jump" occurs in the distance or arrangement ratios of the atoms (or molecules) in the transition from one body to the other.

In the meantime, the preservation of absolutely equal distances and equal arrangement through the extent of a body itself is merely the absolute <term>limit-</term>

...concept for the continuity of a body, which indisputably never strictly corresponds to reality, and one can further speak of relative continuity or discontinuity depending on the closer approximation to or greater deviation from this <term>limiting case</term><note>Limiting case (original: *Gränzfall*): An ideal or extreme theoretical scenario used as a point of comparison for real-world observations.</note>. This approximation can, however, take place in a twofold sense: either the successive distances vary very little, or they vary continuously by equal <term>differences</term><note>Differences (original: *Differenzen*): Mathematical increments or the specific amount by which values change in a sequence.</note>.*) This latter case, too, is again only a limiting case, to which only approximations can occur. Finally, one can say that every slow variation of a lower or higher order (by differences of lower or higher order) in distances and arrangements still allows the concept of relative continuity to persist; every jump in variation, or more generally every locally rapid variation, provides a boundary. Because slow and rapid are relative terms that merge into one another through all possible gradations, nothing absolute can be established regarding where continuity should still be assumed and where a demarcation between bodies should be placed; and while one decides easily in extreme cases, in other cases it may depend on a comparison of "more or less" whether one wishes to speak of continuity or a boundary. Thus, the human body is demarcated from the outside world, very decidedly discontinuous with it, while its internal divisions have less sharp boundaries against one another and, in relation to the body's demarcation from the outside world, allow its internal continuity to persist.

A body that consists not uniformly of ultimate atoms but of <term>molecules</term><note>Molecules (original: *Molecule*): In 19th-century physics, this referred to "atom groups" or clusters of fundamental particles, rather than the specific chemical structures we identify today.</note>, i.e., groups of atoms whose particles are incomparably closer to one another than the molecules are to themselves, is by no means to be called continuous if one refers to the distance and arrangement ratios of the ultimate atoms—since jumps in the distances between atoms occur between the molecules—but it can be called continuous or discontinuous if one refers to the distance and arrangement ratios of the molecules (i.e., their centers of gravity), depending on whether they vary only slowly or in leaps. Insofar as there are also molecules of a higher <term>compositional level</term><note>Compositional level (original: *Zusammensetzungsstufe*): The hierarchical organization of matter, from simple atoms to complex clusters and beyond.</note>, one can also distinguish higher levels of continuity in bodies.

In motions as well, where time enters along with space and matter, the concept of relative continuity and discontinuity between bodies comes into consideration; and from this side, too, a contribution can be made to the discontinuity between physical things, insofar as their atoms or molecules are in differing states of motion.

Perhaps one disputes the essential belonging together of the concepts of absolute simplicity and discontinuity by arguing that simple <term>spatial points</term><note>Spatial points (original: *Raumpunkte*): Zero-dimensional locations in space.</note> nonetheless hang together continuously with others. Thus, it is argued, the simple could also be continuous. But it is rather mathematically recognized that space cannot be represented as a continuum of points, but only as—

## II. Fundamental Perspectives.

...a continuum of continua, which is to be thought of as being just as divisible without limit as it is expandable without limit.

This does not prevent one from imagining a point at any arbitrary location in space that one may wish to focus upon; but regardless of how many points at how many locations one may imagine, one cannot generate a spatial continuum with them. One cannot only not exhaust space with them, but cannot even produce a finite spatial magnitude. Every contact between points is a coincidence <note>Original: *Zusammenfallen*. Literally a "falling together," meaning the points occupy the exact same location and become indistinguishable.</note> of the same. The point can indeed only be placed *within* continuous space, but continuous space cannot be *composed* of points. If one finds a contradiction in the fact that one can imagine points everywhere in continuous space and yet cannot fill continuous space with points, one forgets that "everywhere" is not even feasible according to the very concept of the point and the continuum itself; for no matter how closely one wishes to imagine points, as long as they remain points, an infinite number of other points can still be imagined between them, and so on indefinitely. The apparent contradiction arises only from the assumption, established from the outset, which contradicts the concept of a point: to imagine a <term>point</term> <note>Original: *Pnnkt*. A typo in the source German text for *Punkt*.</note> at any arbitrary location does not yet mean imagining it everywhere; the former is possible, the latter is not. The concepts of the point and the continuum are simply incommensurable <note>Incommensurable: Having no common standard of measurement. In this context, it means that no number of zero-dimensional points can ever add up to a three-dimensional space.</note>, and one achieves no more with however many points than with a single one toward the exhaustion of the continuum; the required "everywhere" always collapses into a summed-up nothingness as soon as one attempts to carry it out with points.

Insofar as, according to our conception, matter is contained merely in points, it follows that if one wanted to compress all the matter in the world until it touched, one would compress it into a single point. The appearance of its extension depends on its dispersion. However, there are forces present that maintain it in this dispersion; that is, rules according to which points can only move in such a way that they never come into actual contact.

Even in the vastness of cosmic space, a meeting of two celestial bodies can hardly take place; and even if a meteorite falls to Earth, the meeting is only apparent; it only goes as far as the distance between atoms. In the atom-world itself, a meeting is undeniably impossible.

One asks, perhaps: how does it happen that the concept of the discontinuous <term>hypostatizes</term> <note>Hypostatize: To treat a conceptual or abstract idea as if it were a distinct, physical reality. Here, the author wonders why we treat "matter" as a real thing while "space" and "time" are treated differently.</note> itself only in one way as matter, while that of the continuous does so in a twofold way as time and space? That does not seem at all in the spirit of a pure opposition of concepts; it does not seem so symmetrical, not as self-evident as one might expect and demand in the realm of ultimate basic concepts and highest opposites, but rather like a disharmony grown out of a false framing or positioning of them. If we, on the other hand, set matter itself as continuous...

He posits that matter consists of discontinuous points held apart by forces, making true contact between atoms or celestial bodies impossible.</summary>

## II. Fundamental Perspectives.

...as space and time, then three continuities would interpenetrate to form the full existence of the material world; indeed, the number three often appears as the standard for all world classification.

Even if we grant this last point—though I believe there is little to be gained from it—closer inspection reveals it to be merely an obstructive knot for the dynamic view <note>The "dynamic view" usually refers to the philosophical tradition (associated with Kant and Schelling) that views matter as a product of forces rather than discrete particles.</note>, while serving as a new point of connection for our own. For matter would only possess the continuity one might attribute to it as a spatial continuity, derived only from space (or, if one prefers to reverse it, space would derive it from matter); the continuity of both would be one and the same. In contrast, space by no means borrows its continuity from time, nor vice versa; both possess their continuity inherently. Thus, even according to the dynamic view, one would have only two fundamentally essential continua instead of the beloved three; the concept of a continuum simply does not belong to the concept of matter itself.

For us, however, *matter, time, and space are linked into a metaphysical triad through their subordination under the three primary and limiting concepts of quantity: Nothingness, Unity, and Infinity.* For in our simple beings <note>Referring to fundamental particles or monads that constitute matter.</note> we have something that includes, or is, absolutely no continuum; in time, something that is strictly one and only one continuum—that is, the single direction in which time flows; in space, something that is a continuum according to infinite relations and directions, an infinity of continua, a continuum of continua.

The three dimensions of space designate only three primary directions; in reality, however, one can follow infinitely many directions within it.

The center, radius, and periphery of a sphere illustrate, to some extent, a unified whole of the simplicity and directionlessness of the material point, time stretching in one direction, and space expanded in infinite directions. One should not over-exploit this model, however, by attempting to derive further relationships between matter, space, and time beyond what is appropriate. A model cannot prove, only illustrate; and it should not be expected from the outset that a model falling entirely within the realm of the spatial could sufficiently represent the relationship of space to that which is not space in every respect.

The fact that it is inherently impossible to imagine space and time as anything other than continuous is also proven by the fact that we assign no boundary to space and time, and that we [cannot imagine] a beginning and—

## II. Fundamental Perspectives.

...we are quite unable to conceive of an end to them [space and time], as continuity would break at that boundary. On the other hand, even assuming that matter were continuous, it is perfectly possible to imagine it as discontinuous. We do this everywhere with celestial bodies on a large scale, and we can also easily imagine ourselves standing at a material boundary of the world, looking out into the unlimited empty space. This proves that continuity, and with it unboundedness, cannot belong as essentially to the concept of matter as they do to the concepts of time and space. Furthermore, all limitation in space and time arises only through matter.

} 3/1
</margin>

This leads us to a new contrast, or a new way of perceiving the contrast, between simple beings <note>Referring to fundamental, indivisible particles or monads.</note> and space and time. A simple atom, despite the fact that its extension is *nothing*, is not itself "Nothing"; rather, it hypostatizes <note>To "hypostatize" is to treat an abstract concept as if it were a distinct, concrete reality.</note> the ultimate boundary of being from a quantitative perspective; it is an *infinitesimal* <term>unendlich Kleines: an infinitely small quantity</term> in the strictest sense. In contrast, time and space are something *infinitely large and absolutely unbounded*, respectively in one direction [time] and in infinitely many directions [space].

The infinitely small line, surface, or body elements with which higher geometry <note>The author is referring to calculus or differential geometry, which uses "infinitesimals" to calculate curves and volumes.</note> deals are not something absolutely infinitely small, but only relatively so. While they are indeed infinitely small compared to all finite spatial magnitudes (lines, surfaces, bodies)—just as these are compared to infinite space—they themselves stand in an infinite relationship to spatialities of a higher order of smallness, continuing thus into the indefinite and maintaining finite relations of size among themselves. The point alone, which for this very reason forms the boundary of spatial magnitudes rather than being a spatial magnitude itself, stands in the relationship of the infinitesimal even to all infinitely small spatialities of any order. It is the only "small" thing that has nothing smaller beneath it or within it—an infinitesimal of an infinite order—and no longer allows for any finite comparison of size. But as noted, it cannot be placed *through* space, but only *into* space; its concept is no longer enclosed within the concept of space (which is defined by continuity and things being outside of one another), but rather forms a boundary of the spatial concept where something other than space begins—and this "Other" is precisely matter.

Just as the concept of unboundedness relates to one side, the concept of indefinite divisibility relates on the other side to that of absolute continuity. If one were to imagine a part of a continuum that could not itself be grasped as a continuum of parts, then continuity would, so to speak, break off "downward" in the same way it would break off "upward" if one were to imagine the continuum as bounded. ## II. Fundamental Perspectives.

...absolute continuums are therefore also absolutely divisible into infinity; on the other hand, simple beings <note>The author uses "simple beings" to refer to fundamental, indivisible particles, often called monads in philosophical traditions.</note>, as absolutely discontinuous, are also absolutely indivisible; they are, so to speak, absolutely hard entities. One can only cut *between* the atoms, not *into* the atoms. In contrast, space is the softest thing there is, and is everywhere cut without resistance by the diamond point of the atom.

Perhaps, in seeking to appropriately contrast the metaphysical concepts with one another, one is more inclined to contrast the continuity of time and space with the discontinuity of *number* rather than *matter*, and to consider the concept of discontinuity as purely <term>hypostatized: treating an abstract concept as if it were a real, physical thing</term> in the former rather than the latter. After all, mathematics divides into one part dealing with continuous space <note>This refers to geometry.</note> and another dealing with discontinuous number <note>This refers to arithmetic or discrete mathematics.</note>; this seems to prove that space and number, rather than space and matter, represent the pure opposition of continuity and discontinuity.

But even this objection is easily resolved upon closer inspection and only leads to a new confirmation and enrichment of our metaphysical complex of concepts. Undeniably, the contrast between discontinuity and continuity exists between number on one hand, and space and time on the other; only, it is not merely "conceivable abstract number" that confronts space and time as a complement to reality in nature; one might write as many conceivable numbers as one wants in space and time, and yet one has nothing; rather, instead of abstract number, we must consider the *actually countable*. But what is that? In the heavens, it is the stars; with these, the concept of the countable is inscribed most prominently and realistically into space and time; but what are stars other than material globes? Thus we are back at matter, and find in it the representative of the countable. Yet the stars are not the final word; what they appear to be from a distance—purely countable points—is finally realized only in the simple atoms; from them, all real numbers are finally constructed, the hosts of stars themselves, along with what lies between them. Yet it remains beautiful how these stars, shining above our heads in the night, mirror what is real but unrecognizable to the eye in a "deeper night of being" <note>A poetic metaphor suggesting that the invisible world of atoms follows the same logical laws as the visible stars.</note>. One knows, of course, that even through a telescope, a distant star remains a point, which allows for no measurement, only counting.

According to the preceding, we thus find the concept of the countable purely and realistically hypostatized in our atoms, while at the same time the concept of the measurable is found purely in space and time...

## II. Fundamental Perspectives.

...hypostatized <note>original: "hypostasirt"</note>. What is measurable is so only on the side of that which is continuous within it; what is countable is so only on the side of that which is discontinuous within it. Space and time represent that which is absolutely measurable, while our simple beings <note>The author uses "simple beings" to refer to fundamental, indivisible particles, often called monads or atoms in philosophical traditions.</note> represent that which is absolutely countable. However, just as the totality of space and time is ultimately immeasurable because it encompasses everything measurable, so too is the totality of our simple beings uncountable because it encompasses everything that is countable. The dynamic view of how space is filled possesses a pure <term>hypostasis: the realization or embodiment of an abstract concept</term> in the world of the *Real* only for the concept of the measurable, not the concept of the countable; yet even the pips on a die, in their simple play, roughly represent the *simple*, *discrete* <term>discrete: separate and distinct, not continuous</term> nature that underlies all countability.

The uncountability of simple atoms seems to be required as a corollary to the immensity of space; furthermore, no reason is conceivable that would have restricted the existence of atoms to a specific number. Thus, however far one might advance into the immensity of space, one will always encounter atoms anew. This does not mean, however, that these atoms are everywhere organized into worlds inhabited by rational beings; rather, one might imagine that this provides only an inexhaustible *matter* for an eternally progressing development and formation of worlds. The ordered world itself would, at any given time, form merely an ever-expanding compact *core*, or an increasing number of cores, amidst the scattered matter stretching through infinity. Admittedly, the concept of a material world extending into infinity presents a certain difficulty from another perspective. The motion of every atom is, strictly speaking, determined by the co-existence of every other atom that exists. How can this motion itself become something definite if the number of atoms is indeterminately large? Perhaps the following answer can be given: as the influence of distant matter becomes increasingly weaker, the assumption that matter extends through infinity does not lead to an indeterminacy of the effect on each atom, but rather to a *boundary determination*. Indeed, it seems to me that it does not contradict the spirit of mathematics to assume this; for many infinite series with decreasing terms yield finite *limiting values* through *summation* <note>The author is referencing calculus and the mathematical concept of a limit, where an infinite sum can result in a finite number.</note>. Yet, I do not claim that every difficulty is resolved by this view.

If one accepts this, then one can dispense with the need for an <term>original impulse: the "first push" often theorized in physics to explain why celestial bodies move in orbits rather than falling straight into each other</term> for the motion of the universe, without having to imagine matter collapsing into the center of gravity of the world or moving in straight lines toward it—for in a material world extending to infinity, there is no center of gravity. The original impulse, which deflected the Earth from a direct path toward the sun, can then be replaced by the *boundary effect* of the forces that all other masses besides the sun have exerted from eternity upon the Earth (or respectively, upon the matter from which it coalesced). This effect is such that the nearest masses had the most decisive influence, while the distant ones could be practically ignored for the sake of calculation. In this way, generally

## II. Fundamental Perspectives.

...one can explain, based on the different original positions that the material particles held relative to their neighboring particles, how each apparently received a different <note>The author is addressing the "First Mover" problem: if all matter is the same, why does it move in different ways? He suggests that position alone accounts for the variety of motion.</note> **original impulse**. In the meantime, one can observe—without regard for such a consideration—that it is generally no more difficult to conceive of matter as being in motion from the beginning, that is, from eternity, than as being at rest; thus, such a consideration is not strictly necessary to remove the difficulty of the so-called original impulse. One simply must not think of it as a "push" delivered at a specific, finite point in time.

A system composed of many simple beings can again be viewed as a countable unit in contrast to other such systems. Every celestial body is such a unit; every human body is a smaller one. But the **absolute One**, which can be grasped strictly only as such and not also as a multiplicity, is only the *simple*. The dynamic view has no absolute One. The All is certainly also a unity; yet it is not a countable unit compared to others; that is the nature of the "One."

Our human number system has 10 digits, which suffice for all human calculations. Nature’s number system has only one digit: the **atom**, and with it, it suffices for the calculations of the universe. Our number system itself is arbitrary, formed only according to our ten fingers; one could manage with nine, with eight, or with two digits; <note>Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz (1646–1716), a philosopher and mathematician who developed the binary system and the theory of "monads."</note> **Leibniz** occupied himself a great deal with the dyadic <note>original: "dyadischen" — referring to base-2 or binary.</note> number system. But one can go even further; one can manage with a single digit; thus one arrives at the **monadic number system**, where the number of digits is in every case as large as the sum it is meant to express; that is the number system of nature, the simplest possible. Here we again gain an absolute boundary-concept.

Why does man not make use of this simplest possible system? Because the numbers would become too long for him and the overview too difficult. He does not stand within the Absolute; he stands in the middle of things. In Nature, however, there is no lack of space; space is an infinite calculation-table, and a difficulty in overseeing the numbers does not exist for the Spirit, because the result <note>original: "Facit" — the sum total or "bottom line" of a calculation.</note> of these numbers is drawn within it automatically—because, in a certain respect, the Spirit is itself the inwardly appearing result of the outwardly appearing atomistic system. However, this is not the place to justify this more closely or to pursue it further.

<meta>This page transitions from the mathematical nature of atoms to the philosophical framework of space and time. It explores how these "forms of intuition" allow individual, unconnected atoms to join together into the complex structures of the material world.</meta>

## II. Fundamental Perspectives.

Time and space themselves are only the most general **ways of connection** in the realm of the real; their continuum is an absolute connection in and of itself. Whatever is to be thought of as connected in reality must, above all, be conceived as connected through space and time; and only upon this foundation are specific ways of connection applied, which already includes the entry of content into space and time.

The concept of the "way of connection" can be replaced by the essentially identical concept of **form**. Space and time are the most general forms in which that which exists appears: space is the form of the *side-by-side* <note>The author uses "Nebeneinander" to describe things existing simultaneously in different locations.</note>, and time is the form of the *one-after-another* <note>The author uses "Nacheinander" to describe things happening in sequence.</note>. These are, moreover, merely other words for space and time, since conversely, one can say "spatiality" and "temporality" for being side-by-side and one-after-another. Furthermore, every specific form exists only as a particular determination of space and time.

Space and time have been called **forms of intuition** <note>original: "Anschauungsformen." This is a term famously used by Immanuel Kant to describe the "filters" through which the human mind perceives the world.</note>; there is nothing to prevent this, considering that the whole of nature is essentially only a matter of intuition or sensory appearance. Accordingly, the most general forms in which nature appears can only be forms of intuition. These forms of intuition take on an **objective** character insofar as one considers spatial forms in relation to other spatial forms, or temporal forms in relation to other temporal forms. They take on a **subjective** character insofar as one grasps all intuitions as determinations of a unified consciousness linking them together; for in the case of natural phenomena, nothing else can be shown for the distinction between the objective and the subjective (cf. p. 95).

In contrast to space and time, **simple beings** <note>As established on previous pages, "simple beings" refers to atoms.</note> are, in themselves, something absolutely unconnected. They are connected to nothing in themselves, and nothing is connected within them; however, they submit to every way of connection by means of time and space. Thus, they correspond to the purest concept of **matter** <term>Stoff: raw, formless material</term>—material that is inherently formless yet available for any form, i.e., any way of connection. Here, our theory of atoms <note>original: "Atomistik"</note> simply meets the instinct of common language, which tends to use the terms "matter" and "material" <note>original: "Materie und Stoff"</note> with the same meaning.

It is true that the clay from which a statue is modeled is usually thought of as something coherent. But in that respect, it is not a *pure* material; rather, it already brings something of a "form"—that is, a way of being held together—into the hands of the artist. Thus, it facilitates the shaping from a certain side, while simultaneously restricting it from another. Consider what countless shapes a pennant fluttering in the wind can take—

## II. Fundamental Perspectives.

...men; but he cannot accept them all, only as many as are needed to ensure that coherence is maintained. Clay already grants more freedom in this regard; but it cannot grant full freedom. One only possesses full freedom with a substance where every particle is, in itself, completely unconnected to the others. The **dynamic view** <note>The "dynamic view" refers to theories (common in 19th-century German philosophy) that viewed matter as a continuous force field rather than discrete particles.</note> makes the world out of clay, for it recognizes a certain inherent connection of matter from the very beginning and considers it essential to the substance, even though the divisibility of bodies—as we have seen (p. 54)—contradicts this. Our **atomism** <term>Atomistik: the philosophical and scientific theory that the universe is composed of small, indivisible particles.</term> alone adopts a substance according to the pure concept of matter.

While time and space are absolute forms in themselves, when combined with matter, they produce forms of **relative significance**. This is because the existence of material points determines the infinite possibility of spatial and temporal connections according to one specific relationship or another, thereby marking it off from other possibilities.

The outer form of a body is given by the spatial way its surface parts are connected. This arises because the matter of the body joins together through **relative continuity**, while simultaneously distinguishing itself from its surroundings through **relative discontinuity** (p. 134). However, discontinuous matter itself contributes nothing to the "continuous stroke" of the surface; rather, it merely provides **determining points** for it. Consequently, forms in nature receive a far more *ideal* <note>In this context, "ideal" means existing as a mental concept or mathematical abstraction rather than a solid, physical "stuff."</note> meaning than in the dynamic view. The latter has compact, massive, leaden forms; our view has only "thought" forms, in that our imagination draws lines and surfaces between the **boundary atoms** of the body. Here again, our atomism simply aligns with common usage, which—just like the words "substantial" and "material"—gladly interchanges "formal" and "ideal." And just as the heavens otherwise reflect atomism in many ways, one can also see on our star charts how the outlines of the constellations represent the reality of how bodily forms are drawn.

The "stroke" of a figure through the stars could, of course, be drawn differently. In general, any arrangement of points can represent an unlimited possibility of the most varied forms. The task of drawing such forms through them is, in itself, undetermined. Yet it is not *absolutely* undetermined; it merely requires a "completion to determination," just as it offers such a completion. Every rule for how points in general are to be determined...

## II. Fundamental Perspectives.

...should serve a figure, such an addition provides what is needed; for it is enough to provide one in order to henceforth have a different specific figure or class of figures with every other specific arrangement of points. The rule alone would not do it; the points alone would not do it; the rule combined with the points does. And as one changes the rule, all figures become different, yet each changes in a specific way. Thus, **atomism** <term>Atomistik: the theory that the world is composed of discrete, individual particles rather than continuous matter.</term> offers the most general foundation imaginable for a **general theory of forms** <note>original: "allgemeine Formenlehre" — a field of study, often associated with Goethe, concerned with the laws governing the shapes of living and non-living things.</note>, whereas the dynamic view <note>Referring to the "Dynamists" who believed matter was a continuous force field.</note> offers only individual examples of it. Moreover, the mind will generally always have its reasons and purposes for choosing one mode of determination over others; and where no special reason or purpose exists, it will prefer the simplest and easiest as the most natural.

The simplest rule and the simplest way to firmly determine a figure through a given arrangement of points consists in using, in every case, the **relative minimum** of space to connect the points—since the points themselves, as points, already occupy the **absolute minimum** (cf. p. 138)—that is, to connect them by straight lines and planes. These also have the property of forming the strict middle-case or boundary-case between all possible symmetrically equal lines or surfaces that can be placed around a line or on both sides of a surface. Both factors—that the mind reaches its goal via the shortest path and that, following the **principle of sufficient reason** <note>A philosophical principle stating that everything must have a reason or cause; here, the mind has no "reason" to veer left or right, so it stays straight.</note>, it finds no cause to deviate to one side over the other—may work together so that the mind prefers this mode of determination as the most natural wherever no special reason for another mode exists. That is to say: the mind is inclined to connect given points in the imagination by straight lines and planes rather than by curved lines or surfaces. Consequently, where no other determining factor is present, one can regard this mode of determination as absolutely valid and generally fundamental; **crystals** provide the models in nature for this. Furthermore, curvilinear and curved-surface forms can be regarded as **higher boundary forms** <note>original: "Gränzformen"</note> of rectilinear and flat-surface forms, insofar as they arise when the number of straight sides or flat surfaces becomes infinitely large and their size becomes infinitely small; however, one cannot conversely regard the straight as the upper limit of the curved if one requires definiteness in the lower limit, because the straight line can be the boundary form of indefinitely many different curved forms.

In the meantime, generally speaking, the mind always retains the possibility and freedom to draw any figures through given points at a finite distance, according to any motive or any self-made rule. Where these points are not used to create corners according to the simplest rule, the most obvious approach is to use them to designate **maxima** and **minima** <note>original: Latin terms for the highest and lowest points of a curve.</note> of curvature, **inflection points** <note>original: "Wendepunkte"</note>, or **singular points**; and this happens not infrequently.

## II. Fundamental Perspectives.
10

The fact that we prefer to connect points in our imagination via straight lines and planes, rather than through curved lines and curved surfaces, can be proven through sufficient **induction** <note>The logical process of reaching a general conclusion by looking at specific examples.</note>. If only two points (for example, two stars in the sky) are given, one undoubtedly imagines their connecting line as a straight one; for us, three points always determine a plane, despite the fact that one could just as easily imagine those two points connected by a curved line, or the three by a curved surface. Wherever any plurality of points, grasped by the eye at once, can be connected by a straight line or plane, the mind will surely do so, instead of laying a wavy line or wavy surface through them. We see this, for instance, when the cleavage planes <note>original: "Blätterdurchgänge" — the planes along which a crystal naturally splits.</note> of crystals are represented atomistically. Any three randomly arranged points are inherently just as suitable for defining a circle as a triangle; yet one will always perceive a triangle through them, just as one perceives a quadrilateral through four points in a plane. The eight corners of a cube could also define the surface of a sphere; but everyone, seeing their arrangement—even if the cube is not solid and the outline is not fully drawn—thinks of a cube. If, however, many points placed in a circle can indeed represent a circle quite well to us, this is related to the previously noted circumstance that **polygons** <term>Polygon: a geometric shape with at least three straight sides and angles.</term> with a very large number of very small sides visibly coincide with curved figures.

Admittedly, one might think that in this case, as in others, another principle comes into conflict with—and perhaps outweighs—the principle of minimizing the effort of imagination by shortening the connecting lines as much as possible. This competing principle suggests that "living force" <note>original: "lebendige Kraft" — a historical term for kinetic energy. The author is asking if the "energy" required to make a sharp turn at a corner makes curves more "efficient" for the brain.</note> is consumed with every sharp change of direction, so that with frequent changes of direction, one might find it easier to draw a longer curved line than a shorter, multi-sided straight line. But first of all, as the sides of a figure become smaller and more numerous, the sharpness of their change in direction also decreases; and secondly, I do not find that one is in any way less inclined to perceive three or four points as a triangle or quadrilateral, nor any more inclined to connect them in the imagination with a curved line, when they are placed very close together than when they are far apart. This is true despite the fact that here, the expenditure of living force due to the sharp turn at the corners would have to become relatively greater compared to the length of the path—which proves that the principle of energy expenditure does not even come into consideration here.

One might find this striking; but it is undeniably rooted in the fact—and proves against the views of some (which can certainly be proven in more than one other way)—that we do not receive the **visual impression** <term>Gesichtseindruck: the immediate sensory data received through the eyes.</term> of figures that strike the eye all at once through a successive following of them, but rather through a simultaneous interaction of their parts. Otherwise, the eye or the **imagination** <term>Einbildungskraft: the mental faculty of forming images or concepts not present to the senses.</term>, when presented with, for example, three or four points, would surely shun the sharp turn at the corners and prefer to move through the points in a gentle curved path. The movement of the eyes is only necessary to grasp in detail a figure that was first captured indistinctly as a whole. After all, even with the swiftest back-and-forth movements of the eye, a merely successive following of points in the visual field could produce at most the impression of a very winding line, not a surface. Therefore, despite the fact that the successive perception of a figure is certainly also at the eye's disposal, the first perception just as certainly happens simultaneously. And what for the...

### II. Fundamental Perspectives.

What applies to direct sensory perception applies equally to the mental image or imagination that mimics it. Rather than saying the mind, the imagination, or the creative faculty draws lines between given points, it would be more accurate to say that the impression or idea of a connecting line arises simultaneously through the gathering of these points. Accordingly, one must translate the shorter expression into the truer one throughout.

One can observe how our perspective creates a connection between external form and internal structure. While the external form is determined by the lines and surfaces (specifically straight lines and planes) that one draws through the boundary points of the body, one can also draw lines and surfaces through the points of the interior; the lines and surfaces drawn in the former sense form a single, unified system with those drawn in the latter. Indeed, in mathematics, the connecting straight lines and planes drawn through the perimeter and the interior of a system of points are treated under shared perspectives and formulas. For the dynamic view <note>The "dynamic view" refers to a 19th-century philosophical perspective, often associated with Kant or Schelling, which saw matter as a product of forces (attraction and repulsion) filling space, rather than a collection of discrete particles.</note>, by contrast, the external surface and the internal structure always retain something incommensurable <note>original: "Incommensurables" — meaning they lack a common basis for comparison or measurement.</note>.

The framework that, according to our view, permeates the interior of every body and links itself to the surface through the boundary points, can be regarded as the expression of a third dimension of the bodily form. The dynamic view, by filling space throughout its depth with physical substance, must instead stop at the surface when defining the bodily form. In our conception, all the matter of the body is immediately absorbed into its form, since it constitutes the very points that define it; in the dynamic view, matter is contained within the form only as if in an external sack.

Insofar as one can draw a straight line from every atom—on the surface as well as in the interior of a body—to every other atom (not just the nearest, but also the furthest), and a plane through any two others, the structure of every body presents itself as a highly intricate meshwork <note>original: "Maschenwerk"</note> according to the most general determination of form through straight lines and planes. However, since the arrangement of atoms is thought of as arbitrary, one might ask how it should be determined so that, for a given total volume of the body, the smallest possible expenditure of spatiality is required for the connecting straight lines and planes. It is the regular arrangement, in which not only do many otherwise diverging connection lines and planes coincide, but the total sum of the connecting lines and planes becomes the smallest possible. (To take a very simple case: in a square, which is the most regular figure that can be determined by four points in a plane, the sum of the sides and diagonals—and indeed the sides on their own—is smaller than in any rectangle or ir-

<summary>This page argues that a body's internal structure and external form are a single geometric system defined by the arrangement of atoms, contrasting this with the "dynamic view" which treats form as a mere container for substance. The author suggests that regular geometric shapes are nature's way of minimizing the "expenditure of spatiality" between points.</summary>
