->## II. Fundamental Perspectives.<-

...regular square of the same area.) This is also the limiting case <note>original: "Gränzfall" — a boundary or extreme scenario in a mathematical series.</note> among all equally possible displacements of atoms in different directions by equal amounts (for example, the limiting case between such a displacement of four atoms that results in a vertical rectangle once and a horizontal one the next time). Through regular arrangement, the network of the structure is simplified; every approach to regularity is simultaneously an approach to its simplicity. Thus, despite the infinitely manifold possibilities of determining form through points, one can always obtain boundary determinations from this or that perspective. These are of fundamental importance, partly due to the approximation nature shows toward them, and partly due to the tendency of the mind to adhere to them.

Another such boundary determination is the following. One might ask how, given any arrangement of points, one can establish the most complete connection possible of all points with the smallest expenditure of space in the connecting lines and planes, without actually drawing a line or plane from every single point to every other. According to this, in the atomistic representation of the parallels to side faces or the cleavage planes <note>original: "Blätterdurchgängen"</note> of a cube or rectangle, only the connecting lines and planes parallel to the side faces will be significant, not the diagonals. This mode of connection, being the most natural, will always suggest itself most easily to the imagination. Thus, with four points defining a square, no one thinks to include the diagonals, because the sides of the square already achieve the complete connection of all points via the shortest possible path. One might therefore consider the <term>normal structure</term> <note>The "normal structure" refers to the idealized or standard arrangement of internal components in a body.</note> of bodies to be determined by this principle. For the most complete connection, it is necessary that the connecting lines intersect as much as possible at the defining points.

For curved-surface structures, which are themselves a limiting form (p. 144), fundamental boundary determinations can also be found. The spherical surface represents the simplest and limiting form of all curved surfaces from more than one perspective: partly due to the equal relationship of all its parts to a center point, partly due to its uniform curvature everywhere, and partly because it encloses the greatest volume <note>original: "Inhalt"</note> within the relatively smallest surface area. In this regard, it aligns with the plane, except that the plane requires the absolute smallest area to connect given points (or their intermediate lines), whereas the spherical surface requires the relatively smallest area, insofar as the goal is to enclose the largest possible volume through a connecting surface between points. If, therefore, a connection of points is to be achieved not with the absolute smallest, but with the relatively smallest expenditure of surface area in this sense, then spherical surfaces must be drawn through the points instead of planes. This is the principle of organic cell construction, whose most general schema is a sphere that fills itself with new spheres—just as, conversely, the first sphere itself originally belonged to the filling of a larger sphere. The large yolk sphere at the beginning of embryonic formation, filling itself with small yolk spheres through the cleavage process <note>original: "Furchungsprocess" — the early stage of an embryo where cells divide rapidly.</note>, shows the first stage of development of this schema; the developed organism, with its endogenous cell formation, corresponds to a far more advanced stage.

->## II. Fundamental Perspectives.<-

The points determining the position of the spherical surfaces are given by their mutual points of contact. These represent the intersection points of the planes of the previous normal form <note>The author is comparing a structure made of flat planes to one made of spheres.</note>. Note that in our schema of spherical structure, both internal and external points of contact of the spherical surfaces come into play. The fact that only contacts, and not intersections, occur at these determining points is based on the principle that intersections would violate the condition of using the smallest possible surface area to enclose a given volume <note>original: "Inhalt" — here referring to the spatial volume contained within a cell.</note>. If intersections occurred, a portion of the volume already contained within one cell wall would also be contained by another cell wall of the same rank; whereas with mere contact, each cell instead gains its own distinct volume.

From this perspective, it is not the centers of the spheres or cells, but rather their points of contact that should be considered the primary points of organic cell structure. They also form, as it were, the nodes—the connection points of the organic tissue—through which (ideally speaking) metabolism <term>Stoffwechsel: the exchange of matter and energy within a living organism</term> between cells occurs even in developed cell structures. This exchange, of course, finds a broader path than just through points after the cells have flattened against one another. These nodes also mark the prior cleavage sites <note>original: "Furchungsstellen" — refers to the points where an egg or cell splits during early development.</note> in the furrowed sphere where the spheres last remained connected. However, the centers of the spheres can also be considered primary points insofar as they are what designate new cleavage sites, whereby the centers of the spheres eventually become external points of contact.

Indisputably, these considerations could be developed more precisely and pursued further, but that is not the task here, nor is this the place for it. It is likely that the so-called Principle of Least Action <note>original: "Princip kleinster Wirkung" — a physical principle stating that nature acts in the most economical way possible.</note> is somehow involved in organic forms shaping themselves according to the indicated schema.

Just as a coherent form arises when we mentally connect the positions occupied by different points simultaneously, so too does one arise when we connect the positions that the same point occupies successively; that is, one can also derive forms through motion. Here it is evident that the form is something purely conceptual; for only to the extent that one connects the past with the present in thought does form arise during motion. The stars also present these kinds of forms to us in the simplest way, namely, in their celestial orbits.

The dynamicist <note>A follower of "Dynamism," a school of natural philosophy that viewed matter as a manifestation of forces rather than inert substance.</note> might certainly say that if matter in motion continuously occupies different positions one after another, it is only fitting that it should also occupy them simultaneously in its filling of space. But if it truly already occupied all the different positions at once through its filling of space—those positions which it occupies successively in motion—then it would

it would neither require movement to occupy them one after another, nor would movement be possible in a space that is already occupied. Rather, space requires movement to fulfill its void, and movement requires empty space for its path. Space is not the ape <note>In this context, "ape" implies a mere imitator or a parody.</note> of time, but the wife of time; similar to it in many respects, yet complementing it in others.

The forms created through the movement of simple atoms are merely linear, whereas the forms created through the spatial arrangement of atoms (insofar as one includes structure among them) pass through the depth of the three dimensions. This is undeniably related to the fact that time has only one dimension, while space has three. Furthermore, forms created through movement are inherently determined, whereas spatial forms are inherently indeterminate and only relatively determinable.

One can observe that the twofold way in which forms are to be thought objectively in the realm of nature corresponds to the twofold form of <term>apperception: the process by which the mind coordinates new perceptions with existing ideas to make them conscious</term> with our bodily organs. By means of an arrangement of discrete nerve fibers in both the visual and tactile organs, we can grasp a form all at once; through the movement of our fingertips or the fixed point of the eye's axis, we can grasp it successively. As previously noted (p. 154) <note>This page reference to "p. 154" within page 149 suggests the author is referring to a later section or a different volume of the work.</note>, however, in the eye, successive perception is subordinate to simultaneous perception; in the tactile organ, it is the reverse—a circumstance that deserves attention as a complementary relationship between both senses. The eye first grasps a figure as a whole and then examines the individual parts with the help of movement; the finger grasps a figure as a whole with the help of movement and examines the individual parts through stationary touch. This difference depends partly on the fact that the field of vision, which the eye surveys all at once, is large in proportion to the expansion and modification of it that can arise through the movement of the eye; conversely, in the tactile organ, the tactile field covered by the movement of the finger is large compared to what it can cover all at once. This is also partly due to the fact that the eye can perform much finer movements than the finger, whereas the finger, resting in the same spot, can alter the sensory perception by modifying the pressure in a way that the eye cannot.

How the imagination is able to build continuous bridges over what is materially discrete is shown in a quite striking way at the blind (so-called <note>Named after Edme Mariotte, the physicist who discovered it in 1660.</note> Mariotte’s) spot in the eye. This spot—where one might expect the greatest sensitivity, as it is the entry point of our optic nerve into the eye—is actually completely insensitive to light. It is so large that, in terms of its width, it occupies about eleven full-moon widths in the sky when viewing it; yet one sees no gap in the sky, because the imagination covers the gap with the neighboring blue of the sky. One might therefore doubt the existence of the gap in the visual field; yet if one places a colored wafer on

white paper and let its image fall on the gap in the visual field <note>The "gap in the visual field" refers to the "blind spot" where the optic nerve exits the eye, lacks photoreceptors, and thus cannot detect light.</note>, one does not see the wafer <note>original: "Oblate" — in 19th-century scientific experiments, these were small colored paper discs or sealing wax wafers used as visual targets.</note>, but rather its place is likewise covered by the whiteness of the surroundings, which is transferred onto it by the imagination. If one looks at a white line or a white square or a white cross on black paper in such a way that the center of the figure falls on the gap in the visual field, no gap appears in the figure, nor does one see the figure—the line, the square, the cross—shortened or shrunken; they appear completely full, in full extension and size, because once again the neighboring white covers the gap, even if a colored wafer lay in the center; nothing of it appears; its place is covered over by the white.*) In principle, it is no different with the continuous sensation of the discrete tactile nerve fibers. Thus, an atomistically disposed sensory organ would undoubtedly fill the gap between its simple atoms with the same sensation to which it is stimulated by the neighboring atoms of the gap.

To matter and to form we can further demand a third, a forming principle, which orders the atoms and guides them in their paths. Such a principle is not lacking; it lies in the forces, and we know that forces are representatives of laws, and all laws ultimately stand under a highest law. Thus, the essence of form is a thought, and the ground of form is a law. That a plant builds and rebuilds itself in such a way depends on the fact that atoms order and move themselves according to this law. Even freedom can only be formative on the basis of law, or by being simultaneously law-giving and form-giving.

With what, then, does the dynamic view bind its clay? Again through clay, for the forces which hold matter together and move it, according to that view itself, transform into matter.

Furthermore, connected with the foregoing, the concepts of absolute <term>extension: the property of occupying space</term> and <term>intension: the internal quality or depth of a thing, as opposed to its outward spread</term> are found hypostatized <note>To "hypostatize" is to treat an abstract concept or a quality as if it were a concrete, distinct physical reality.</note>. Space and time are a pure "being-outside-one-another" <note>This refers to the philosophical idea that space and time consist of parts that are distinct and external to each other.</note>, whereas simple real beings are a pure "being-within-themselves." To be sure, various simple beings also stand in a relationship of relative being-outside-one-another; but the existence of none of these is tied to this being-outside-one-another, while the existence of space as well as time essentially lies in the being-outside-one-another itself; also, that being-outside-one-another of simple beings is only mediated through space.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical inquiry into the nature of matter and perception. <page-num>151</page-num>
<header>II. Fundamental Perspectives.</header>

The dynamic perspective offers only a pure <term>hypostasis<note>In philosophy, a "hypostasis" refers to the underlying fundamental reality or substance that supports qualities or attributes.</note></term> for the concept of extension. The spiritual realm itself cannot be considered something purely <term>intensive<note>An "intensive" property refers to qualities like temperature or brightness that don't take up space, whereas "extensive" properties relate to physical size or duration.</note></term>, because it requires time; by its very nature, it is something extended in time. In a single instant, nothing can be felt and nothing can be thought.

As purely extensive frameworks, space and time cannot represent or provide "content" on their own; one cannot say that space and time exist *within* something. On the contrary, everything that exists must be conceived of as existing *in* space, *in* time, or in both. Conversely, <term>simple beings<note>The author likely refers to "monads" or fundamental atoms—indivisible units that make up the fabric of reality.</note></term> can only provide content, but they do not possess extension themselves. Thus, space and time are in themselves absolutely empty, while simple beings are that which bring "fullness" into this void.

While simple beings fill space, they do not "occupy" it in a physical sense. If they truly occupied it, space would not contain them as "content" (as the logic of their relationship requires), but rather they would "cover" or overlap the space. Just as anything that covers the circumference of a circle is not *inside* it, points can exist within a circle in any number. Instead of a circle's circumference, consider space itself. Furthermore, the "fullness" of reality—which consists of a multitude of distinct, individual things—would vanish if space were absolutely "filled" or saturated. The infinite richness that simple beings bring into space is only possible because space itself remains "unoccupied."

Instead of saying that space is filled by matter, one could say from a certain (albeit limited) point of view that space remains as empty with the existence of matter as it would be without it. This is because all simple beings, taken together as points, ultimately converge into a single point that represents no physical extension.

Time is no more "filled" by the existence of matter than space is; for "being in time" is no more to be confused with "filling time" than "being in space" is with "filling space." However, a combined filling of both space and time occurs, if one wishes to phrase it so, through **motion**. Should a simple being ever come to a total rest (though absolute rest does not exist), it would, during that duration, fill time with nothing. It is not the simple being itself that fills space and time during motion, but rather the *motion* of the simple being that fills them, insofar as motion is a product of both.

Further observations regarding motion are contained in the third and sixth chapters.

As we have seen in the preceding sections, a series of concepts that hold the greatest significance in the real world—though they are partly entangled in manifold relations and partly diverge widely within it—converge in the simple atom as if in an identical center. In this center, they find a common, absolute <term>hypostasis<note>A fundamental reality or substance that supports other properties. The author argues that the atom is the "anchor" for these abstract concepts.</note></term>. These are the concepts of the simple, the discontinuous, the infinitely small, the indivisible, the countable, the material, the <term>intensive<note>Refers to qualities that do not depend on size or amount, such as pressure or temperature, rather than "extensive" properties like volume.</note></term>, and the filling. Opposite these, a series of corresponding but contrary concepts are linked or identified within the Absolute: one series traceable in one direction through time, the other traceable in infinite directions through space. In the union of this fundamental trinity—<insert>space, time, and matter</insert>—lies the knot of nature.

Without the simple atom, this entire <term>nexus<note>A connection or link between different elements of a system.</note></term> and its self-completing opposition would be lost. If matter simply filled space <insert>without being composed of atoms</insert>, we would have time stretching in one direction, space stretching in infinite directions, and matter likewise stretching in infinite directions. In such a case, matter—instead of acting as a counter-pole to infinity through its own infinite smallness and thereby providing the relations of finitude—would become a <term>tautology<note>A logical redundancy; saying the same thing in different words. The author suggests that without atoms, matter and space would be indistinguishable.</note></term> of space from one perspective, while from another, it would displace space and obstruct it for the sake of movement.

If previous attempts to trace <term>atomism<note>The philosophical and scientific theory that the universe is composed of small, indivisible particles.</note></term> back to its origins have generally led only into darkness and confusion, the reason lay solely in the fact that they were not traced back far enough. Rather, thinkers have shied away from the final step, which leads at once out of the darkness and confusion into clear light. As long as the "ultimate" atoms remain finite <insert>meaning they still have a measurable size</insert>, one has not yet reached the end; one remains compelled to relocate the very thing needing explanation back into the means of explanation. Wanting to build the world in the last instance out of little spheres—which some have regarded as the pinnacle of atomistic wisdom—is like wanting to build a house out of little houses instead of stones. To then use <term>tetrahedrons<note>A triangular pyramid; one of the five Platonic solids often used in historical theories of matter.</note></term> and cubes for this purpose is to use a child's toy box for the construction of the world.

Only when the ultimate atoms become "simple" <note>In this context, "simple" means "not composed of parts" and occupying no space themselves—essentially mathematical points.</note> does the grandest and simultaneously simplest, the most sublime and simultaneously finest method of world-construction confront us. All the burden that those "small masses" still imposed upon the creative spirit is...

Nothing has vanished; every obstacle that their rigid impenetrability placed in the way has been transformed into force, with which the simple beings, guided by law, join together into that ornate structure: the Cosmos.

### III. On the Motion of Simple Atoms; Their Relation to Corporality and the Question of Their Quality.

It is not sufficient, in determining the behavior of a simple being, to say that it exists only at a single <term>point in space<note>Original: "Raumpunkt." In this philosophical context, it refers to a mathematical coordinate that has no extension or size.</note></term>; it must also be added that it exists at that <term>point in space</term> for only a single moment. <term>Matter</term> is essentially something in motion; it is well known that all rest is only relative. What does not move against another, moves with it. Every simple point of matter occupies its simple <term>point in space</term> essentially for only a single simple point in time, and is in a different place at every other moment. However, just as time progresses continuously, the atom also progresses through space only in a continuous manner.

In the path of the simple <term>atom</term>, the general concept of a "path" is <term>hypostatized<note>From the Greek "hypostasis." To hypostatize is to treat an abstract concept (like a mathematical line) as if it were a real, physical substance.</note></term> in its purest form. The motion of any whole body is broken down into the forward motion of its center of gravity—which provides the path—and the motion of its parts in relation to that center of gravity (in the case of a solid body, this is the rotation around an <term>axis</term> passing through the center of gravity). The first type of motion is represented by the movement of a simple <term>atom</term> on its own. The latter, because it presupposes a plurality of parts, interactions, or a mutual holding of one another, cannot be found within the simple <term>atom</term> itself. Instead, it only appears as a new quality within <term>combinations</term> of atoms; for in the simple <term>atom</term>, one cannot find everything that exists in a body, because a body is essentially a combination of a plurality of atoms—and that plurality has its own significance and its own result.

Although a simple being exists at a simple <term>point in space</term> for only a single moment, it appears, according to the different velocities that simple beings can possess,

However, we do not consider such contradictions—in neither Herbart’s nor Hegel’s sense—to be valid means of progress, but rather as invalid conceptualizations to be removed. <note>Johann Friedrich Herbart (1776–1841) and G.W.F. Hegel (1770–1831) were titans of German philosophy. Hegel famously used contradictions (dialectics) as a tool for reaching higher truths, whereas the author here insists that a contradiction in a theory is simply an error that needs fixing.</note> Therefore, we believe that the concept of elementary velocity itself should never be referred to the dwelling of matter at a specific point in time and space—where, in fact, no such "dwelling" exists—but only to a time- and space-element. Only Herbart, not mathematics, knows how to construct the continuous out of points <margin><unclear>incorrectly understood</unclear></margin> without the presence of continuity.

The space- and time-elements themselves, with which the <term>exact theory of motion<note>Original: "exacte Bewegungslehre." This refers to kinematics or the mathematical study of how objects move.</note></term> deals (and which requires the sharpest conceptual clarity), do not present themselves as indivisible points. Instead, they are themselves still infinitely divisible and quantitatively comparable, and they thereby provide room for higher <term>differentials<note>A reference to calculus, where "differentials" represent infinitely small changes in a quantity.</note></term>. Now, however, there is no contradiction in the fact that one simple being requires a different small amount of time than another to traverse a continuous space, however small that space may be. Nothing prevents us from imagining all possible quantitative relationships between these small spaces and times.

Beyond mere motion, a complete determination of the behavior of the simple <term>reals<note>Original: "Realen." In the philosophy of Herbart, "Reals" are the ultimate, simple, and unchangeable building blocks of reality, similar to atoms but more abstract.</note></term> still requires the law of motion and its changes. This, as shown earlier, simultaneously encompasses the concept of their forces; for everything attributed to the "working of forces" in the physical world can be reduced to the law of the change or preservation of states of motion or equilibrium, as bodies or their parts interact with one another.

From space, time, simple beings, their motions, the relationships between them, and the laws governing them, everything can presumably be constructed that is at all constructible in the material or objective realm of nature. These constructions will always appear in the form of the visible, the tactile, and the relationships between them, because the simple beings themselves—acting as <term>limiting concepts<note>Original: "Gränzvorstellungen." A philosophical term for an idea that marks the boundary of what we can perceive or understand; we can think of the atom, but we cannot see it directly.</note></term> of what we can see and touch—are not

...of what we can hear, smell, or taste, and their relations are found to be limit-relations <note>The author uses "limit-relations" (Gränzverhältnisse) in a mathematical sense, similar to a limit in calculus—the point toward which a value tends.</note> abstracted from the relations of the visible and the tangible. They appear, and in our imagination are meant to appear, only as the smallest visible and tangible points, albeit with the admission that they are nonetheless even smaller than the smallest thing we can actually see, touch, and thus imagine with our eyes and hands—whereby they shift from being purely physical to being metaphysical. Yet, presumably, a single atom in relation to the atomic system of our nerves is sufficient—through the vibration or resistance it creates there—to establish an element or <term>differential of sensation<note>Original: "Differenzial der Empfindung." In 19th-century psychology and physics, a "differential" of sensation refers to a stimulus so tiny it is below the threshold of conscious awareness, but which contributes to a full sensation when combined with others.</note>. This only requires a summation <note>Meaning a mathematical integration or adding up of these tiny pulses.</note> to produce the final sensation that we require empirically.

One adheres to those forms in the constructions of the physical realm of nature because they allow not only for counting, measurement, clear determination, and description; but more significantly, because nature appears and holds true as a real physical world—that is, as the very object of our analysis—only under the forms of the visible and the tangible. This includes what is spatially extended, moved, and what offers resistance to itself and to us (none of which falls directly into senses other than sight and touch).

When one considers the relations of our simple atoms in contrast to the bodies composed of them, one finds that the atoms lack many properties belonging to the latter, since these properties only arise through the combination of atoms. Insofar as the concept of a "body" has been formed only with regard to these properties, nothing prevents us from saying that atoms are **incorporeal**, and that bodies are therefore composed of incorporeal beings. This contains no greater contradiction than saying a society is formed of individuals who are not themselves a society, or that a tree is formed of cells to which the concept of a "tree" is still foreign. From another perspective, however, nothing prevents us from calling atoms "physical" [or corporeal] as the most essential elements of the physical world, without one needing to seek the full properties of a body within them. To argue over this designation—whether physical or non-physical—would be a mere dispute over words; they are one or the other depending on the relationship in which the words are understood, or the assem-

<meta>catchword: assem- [Zusam-]</meta>

<summary>The author argues that while atoms are too small to be seen or touched, they are the metaphysical foundation of physical sensation. He also dismisses the debate over whether atoms are "corporeal" or "incorporeal" as a mere semantic argument, noting that complex properties emerge only when atoms combine.</summary>

<header>III. On the Motion of Simple Atoms; Their Relationship, etc.</header>

...context in which one uses them. However, it may be useful to further explain the relationship between atoms and bodies regarding their properties and to address some questions that arise in the process.

The fact that our simple beings <note>The author uses "simple beings" (einfachen Wesen) to refer to fundamental, indivisible atoms or monads.</note> have no extension or shape does not prevent the bodies composed of them from having extension and shape; after all, one determines the extent and outline of a forest not by the extent and outline of the individual trunks it consists of, but by the space they occupy in their entirety. Simple beings may have no density, yet this does not prevent the bodies composed of them from having density; one measures the density of a population not by the density of individual humans, but by the number of them existing within a given space. They may be, in themselves, without quality or of an indifferent quality, yet this does not prevent the bodies formed from them from having various qualities depending on the different arrangement and motion of the simple beings; even humans, animals, and plants consist of the same materials; only their distinct arrangement and motion give them different qualities.

<margin>?</margin>
<margin>(</margin>
They may be, for themselves, mindless beings, yet this does not prevent the mind <note>The German "Geist" translates to mind, spirit, or intellect depending on context; here it refers to conscious awareness or soul.</note> from attaching itself to their combinations; in humans, too, the mind depends on the combination, not on the individual pieces.

But whether one should really consider simple beings to be quantitatively and qualitatively identical or indifferent <note>Meaning they lack distinct characteristics and are interchangeable.</note> may still appear doubtful. If one, as I do with <term>Herbart<note>Johann Friedrich Herbart (1776–1841), a German philosopher and contemporary of the author, who proposed that reality is composed of "reals"—simple, unchanging elements.</note></term>, though in a different sense, takes the "given" as the starting point and anchor, one must follow the demands of the "given," yet as long as these demands are not decided, one may always keep the simplest possible view in mind. And those are simple beings that contain no point of comparison within themselves. Any inequality would allow for at least one further decomposition based on a coincidental view in <term>Herbart's</term> sense, which we can perhaps do without. And so I say, experience does not—at least not yet—compel us to reject this simplest conception as impossible. For every molecule of different size, density, shape, chemical or dynamic nature, which the <term>physicist</term>, <term>chemist</term>, or <term>crystallographer</term> now uses to explain phenomena...

### III. On the Motion of Simple Atoms; Their Relationship, etc.

<margin>]</margin>supposed for the sake of [this idea], one can always imagine a group of varying extension, density, arrangement, and relative motion of our simple beings <note>The author refers to atoms as "simple beings" (einfache Wesen), implying they are the indivisible, fundamental units of reality.</note> being substituted; and when one remembers, on the one hand, that the phenomena of chemistry already necessitate the assumption of composite molecules, and on the other, that such striking and manifold qualitative differences—such as those between individual colors or individual tones—have already been successfully reduced to differences in vibration ratios <note>original: "Schwingungsverhältnisse"</note>, which depend only on a different arrangement (determining the tension) without regard for any different fundamental quality of the vibrating matter; indeed, that the polar opposition of magnetic forces <note>original: "Magnetismen"</note> can be reduced to a pure opposition of motion; then, given our ignorance of the ultimate fundamental laws of the molecular, the possibility still remains, generally speaking, that all secondary qualities <note>In philosophy, "secondary qualities" are properties like color, smell, or taste that exist in the perceiver's mind, whereas "primary qualities" like extension and motion exist in the objects themselves.</note> presented to us by the physical world are derivable from different arrangements and the motions dependent upon them of simple beings of an inherently indifferent quality, according to laws existing for that purpose. But the hopelessness of achieving this with our current knowledge must be acknowledged, and this is not at all a vital question for the survival of the atomistic view, but only for its simplest possible configuration.

Attempts can, however, be made in this regard; and even if these have no particular physical significance so far, provided they are based on or lead to things physically unprovable, they still have a philosophical significance insofar as they can open general prospects and remove general objections; and in this regard, something more may be mentioned here concerning some relevant viewpoints.

Repeatedly, it has been considered to make all phenomena dependent on the best-defined force, gravitation, and to eliminate the qualitative difference in matter—which seems required by the fact that some matter repels and other attracts—through suitable ways of consideration. For this, the following viewpoint first presents itself.

Apparent effects of repulsion can come about in a twofold way under the influence of purely attractive forces: first, in such a way that a body is attracted more strongly in one direction than in the opposite direction, and consequently the more weakly attr—

### III. On the Motion of Simple Atoms; Their Relationship, etc.

...seems to flee the [approaching] body; secondly, in such a way that by combining attraction with the effects of inertia <term>inertia: the author uses the term "Beharrung" (persistence) to refer to the law of inertia, where an object remains in its state of motion unless acted upon</term>, as a result of a given lateral impulse, the body describes a curved path that leads it away from the attracting body temporarily or perhaps indefinitely, as is the case with celestial motions. At present, it is difficult to calculate how many of the apparent repulsive effects occurring in nature are to be attributed to such causes.

In any case, the second cause alone is sufficient to allow movement away <note>original: "Entfernungsbewegung"</note> in the vast space of the universe to play exactly as large a role as movement toward <note>original: "Näherungsbewegung"</note>. In fact, both perfectly compensate for one another in celestial motions. Upon superficial consideration—and indeed how the matter is actually understood by most natural philosophers—one could just as well assume an attractive and repulsive fundamental force (gravity and centrifugal force) in space that balance each other out, just as one assumes two opposite magnetic and electrical fundamental forces that generally compensate for each other. However, since in the vast space of the universe this apparently polar dual force, taking inertia into account, can be reduced to a simple force of attraction—indeed, must be reduced to allow for an accurate and clear analysis of phenomena and the application of calculations—it would be questionable whether the same is not the case with the dual force that the phenomena of the smallest scale seem to demand.

I myself previously made an attempt from this point of view (Biot’s *Textbook of Physics* <note>Jean-Baptiste Biot, "Lehrbuch der Physik", 2nd edition 1828, Vol. I, p. 408</note>) to eliminate repulsive forces from the world of the smallest scale by enlisting the motions of the smallest scale, and to make the effects of the ponderable <term>ponderable: matter that has weight or measurable mass</term> and the imponderable <term>imponderable: substances once thought to be weightless fluids, such as light, heat, or electricity</term> dependent on a common force of attraction (gravitational force). Another attempt aimed in this direction, which in certain respects coincides with mine, has recently been made known by Séguin (see *Cosmos* by Moigno <note>Abbé Moigno's scientific journal "Cosmos", Volumes I & II; Marc Seguin was a French engineer and physicist</note>, Vol. I & II). To my own attempt, I can now, of course, no longer attach any significance, and it seems very questionable to me whether Séguin's will appear more sufficient. However, other possibilities generally present themselves for grasping the matter.

Just as the law of inertia [regards] every atom for itself, or insofar as it is grasped only in its relationship to infinite space,

<page-num>159</page-num>

### III. On the Motion of Simple Atoms; Their Relationship, etc.

...merely compels [the atom] to persist in its once-assumed direction and speed, though these can be—and undoubtedly are—extremely varied for different atoms from the very beginning. Similarly, perhaps the most general law of force <term>force law: the mathematical rule governing how objects pull toward or push away from each other, like gravity</term> governing the behavior of atoms toward one another only necessitates that the increase in speed which any two atoms gain relative to each other remains the same for those specific atoms at the same distance; and that this speed increases or decreases in the same proportion whenever the distance is proportionally changed. However, both the original direction of the relative speed and its magnitude could be fundamentally different for any two different atoms. In other words, different atoms could behave toward one another partly through attraction and partly through repulsion. The same atom might even act attractively toward one and repulsively toward another (as is the case with the two types of electricity), and they could possess the most varied strengths of absolute force toward one another (as seems to be suggested by chemical affinities <note>original: "chemischen Verwandtschaftsverhältnisse"; the historical term for the forces that cause specific chemical elements to react with or bind to one another</note>). This would always occur in such a way that they maintain their once-assumed behavior in this regard just as firmly as each atom persists in its once-assumed speed and direction through inertia.

Now, nothing would prevent us from actually attributing a fundamentally different primary quality and primary quantity to different atoms. However, such a "quality" would not mean a special constitution of the atoms in and of themselves—as in Herbart’s <note>Johann Friedrich Herbart (1776–1841) was a German philosopher who proposed that the world is made of "reals," simple elements that each have their own unique, internal quality.</note> theory—but would instead reveal itself only in their relationships and express itself through changes in those relationships. This is similar to how the two types of electricity appear to have the same nature when considered alone, yet reveal a different quality only in relation to each other. This quality consists of nothing other than the fact that their relationship to one another changes differently under the same circumstances of position and distance. They thereby prove that, besides the circumstances of position and distance, there is another irreducible factor at play upon which the phenomena depend.

One sees in any case from the preceding that while the drive to find the simplest and most unified fundamental concepts can easily lead to the thought that all forces of atoms must ultimately be reduced to a single attractive primary force, there is also another way of thinking—

<summary>The author argues that atoms may have inherent, diverse qualities of attraction and repulsion (similar to electric charges or chemical affinities) that are persistent and irreducible to a single law of gravity.</summary>

<page-num>160</page-num>

### III. On the Motion of Simple Atoms; Their Relationship, etc.

...appears in as many ways as possible. Undeniably, the root of the great diversity of natural phenomena and natural events is to be sought in their boundary and fundamental relationships. It is highly questionable whether the different original distribution <term>original distribution: the specific arrangement and placement of atoms at the very beginning of the universe</term> and motion of atoms—which do not differ at all in their forces, that is, in their lawful behavior—is sufficient to determine this diversity. Furthermore, one sees no *a priori* <note>original: "a priori"; a Latin term meaning "from the earlier," referring to knowledge that comes from theoretical deduction or logic rather than observation</note> reason why, given that it is equally conceivable that two atoms might move away from each other along the line of their connection as it is that they might approach each other, one relationship should have been preferred over the other. On the other hand, it would be just as invalid to conclude that because both are equally conceivable <term>conceivability: the logical possibility of something being imagined or thought of without contradiction</term>, both must be equally real. 

A wheel can be imagined rolling forward just as easily as backward, yet the development of the world always proceeds, on the whole, only forward. Thus, it would also be possible that, although the fundamental tendency of things could be imagined as a tendency to flee one another just as well as to unite, in reality only the latter occurs, going hand in hand with that principle of progress. This could be expressed as follows: alongside the fundamental principle of progress, there exists a fundamental principle of love, but not of hatred, in the world. Where hatred appears, it arises from the conflict of different directions of love. Undeniably, however, nothing at all can be decided about this subject based on such considerations, which can be turned this way or that.

If I am not mistaken, the way of grasping the matter that I will develop in the seventh chapter might have the most to recommend it, as it combines universality with a precision and simplicity of viewpoint, and promises to meet the extensive needs of physics. Yet the subject remains hypothetical for the time being.

How beautiful it would be if, amid the uncertainty in which we still hover regarding these fundamental relationships on the part of exact science, we could enjoy a certain decision from philosophy. But the easier it may be for philosophy to provide such an answer, the lighter, unfortunately, that answer will weigh.

<page-num>161</page-num>

# IV. Historical Perspectives on the View of Simple Fundamental Atoms.

The view that the fundamental atoms of the physical world are simple developed in my mind long ago. As I believe, it was largely independent of external suggestions—and certainly not prompted by Herbart’s <note>Johann Friedrich Herbart (1776–1841), a German philosopher who proposed that reality consists of "reals," or simple, unextended beings.</note> "simple beings"—but rather grew from the perspective of bringing physical atomism <term>atomism: the theory that the universe is composed of very small, indivisible particles</term> to a philosophical conclusion. In the meantime, when I began to occupy myself with Herbartian metaphysics, I encountered so many points of connection, and even more points of opposition, between our "simple beings" and those of Herbart. This led me in 1852 to write a treatise intended for Fichte’s <note>Immanuel Hermann Fichte (1796–1879), a philosopher who edited a major journal on philosophy and criticism.</note> journal, though only a kind of introduction appeared there (1853), in which I referred ahead to our simple beings.

Regardless, I can claim no priority <note>original: "Priorität"; meaning the credit for being the first to conceive the idea</note> for the thought of simple beings; given the same motives that prompted it in me, it must have easily suggested itself to others who occupied themselves deeply with atomism before me. To be sure, many have only expressed the idea in order to reject it without thorough examination. For instance, Liebig <note>Justus von Liebig (1803–1873), a pioneering German chemist.</note> says in his *Chemical Letters* (1844, p. 57):
> "It is entirely impossible for the intellect to conceive of small particles of matter that are absolutely indivisible; in a mathematical sense, they cannot be infinitely small, without any extension, because they possess weight."

Against which it must be said that extension has nothing at all to do with weight, and weight depends only on a relationship, not on the absolute nature of the material parts.

On the other hand, in Moigno’s <note>Abbé François-Napoléon-Marie Moigno (1804–1884), a French physicist and priest who popularized scientific developments.</note> *Cosmos* (1853, Vol. II, p. 374), I find Ampère and Cauchy explicitly named as representatives of this view (on the occasion of the presentation of Séguin’s views)*);

---

<margin>]</margin>*) Faraday is also mentioned by Moigno in their company, though only from the perspective that he also reduces matter to centers of force. But these centers of force are, according to him, continuous (*Philosophical Magazine* 1844, February), and in this regard, he stands rather in opposition to the atomistic view, which assumes a discrete <term>discretion: the quality of being made of separate, distinct units rather than a continuous, unbroken mass</term> nature of the centers of force.

<header>IV. Historical Perspectives on the View of Simple Fundamental Atoms.</header>

"...that matter essentially consists of simple elements without extension, or monads," <note>original: "que la matière se compose essentiellement d’éléments simples et sans étendue ou monades"</note> and the following elaboration according to Cauchy is attached\*:

> "According to Newton," said Mr. Cauchy <note>Augustin-Louis Cauchy (1789–1857), a brilliant French mathematician and physicist.</note> in one of his lessons on sublime physics in Turin, "the constituent molecules of bodies would be solid, hard, and invariable, such that they could not change dimensions or shapes. But this opinion cannot be reconciled with a phenomenon recently observed by Mr. Mitscherlich. <note>Eilhard Mitscherlich (1794–1863), a German chemist who discovered that crystals of different substances can have the same form.</note> By subjecting crystals to the action of heat, this skilled physicist recognized that they undergo unequal expansions in different directions, and that the inclinations of their faces vary; now, to explain this phenomenon, it is necessary to suppose that by the addition of caloric <term>caloric: a historical scientific theory that heat was a weightless fluid that flowed from hot to cold bodies</term> the constituent molecules not only move away from one another but actually change shape. . . Ampère <note>André-Marie Ampère (1775–1836), the French physicist for whom the unit of electric current is named.</note> has shown, for his part, that to account for several phenomena relating to the combinations of gases, it was enough to consider the molecules of different bodies as each composed of several atoms, whose dimensions are infinitely small relative to the distances that separate them. . . . If, therefore, it were given to us to perceive the constituent molecules of the various bodies subjected to our experiments, they would present to our gaze a kind of constellations; and in passing from the infinitely large to the infinitely small, we would find in the final particles of matter—as in the immensity of the heavens—centers of action without extension placed in the presence of one another. . . . In the opinion of Mr. Ampère, the dimensions of the atoms, in which the molecular centers of action reside, should not be considered merely as very small relative to the distances that separate them, but as rigorously zero. In other words, these atoms, which are the true simple beings of which matter is composed, have no extension. . . . It seems, at first glance, that to deprive a particle of matter of extension is to annihilate it completely; but upon reflection, it is easy to conceive how matter, even composed of simple atoms, nevertheless continues to enjoy the properties that manifest its presence: extension . . , impenetrability <term>impenetrability: the principle that two pieces of matter cannot occupy the same space at the same time</term> . . , tangibility, etc."

<header>V. Relationship of the Physical to Herbartian Monadology.</header>

...etc. In the mathematical theory of light, we consider the sensation of light as being produced by the propagation of motion in an ether composed of atoms, which would have no extension and which act upon one another at very small distances." <note>original: "Dans la théorie mathématique de la lumière nous considérons la sensation lumineuse comme produite par la propagation du mouvement dans un éther composé d’atomes, qui n’auraient point d’étendue..."</note>

> "It follows from what precedes, that if it pleased the Author of Nature <note>A common 19th-century phrasing for God as the architect of physical laws.</note> to modify only the laws according to which atoms attract or repel one another, we could see at that very moment the hardest bodies penetrate each other, the smallest particles of matter occupy immeasurable spaces, or the most considerable masses reduce themselves to the smallest volumes, and the universe concentrate itself, so to speak, into a single point." <note>original: "Il résulte de ce qui précède... l’univers se concentrer, pour ainsi dire, en un seul point."</note>

The assumption of simple atoms further underlies the views of Séguin, <note>Marc Séguin (1786–1875), a French engineer and physicist who made significant contributions to the development of the steam engine and bridges.</note> which were discussed on page 158, and is defended by Moigno <note>Abbé Moigno (1804–1884), a French priest and physicist who worked to reconcile science and faith.</note> with his usual vivacity. Séguin links his view on this to preceding general discussions on cohesion with the following words (Compt. rend. T. XXXVII. p. 705):

> "By this very fact, that there are no possible limits to the smallness that can be assigned to the molecules of bodies, is it not simpler, more natural, more elegant, and even more in harmony with the idea that we have of the works of the Creator—who has set before us everywhere this insurmountable barrier of the infinite or the infinitely small, against which our mind is forced to constantly break itself—to consider the ultimate molecules of bodies as devoid of dimensions, as Messrs. Ampère and Cauchy have admitted, or better to reduce them to simple centers of action, <term>centers of action: the idea that an atom is not a "thing" with size, but merely a point from which physical forces (like gravity or electricity) emanate</term> as Mr. Faraday <note>Michael Faraday (1791–1867), the English scientist who pioneered the study of electromagnetism.</note> has done?"

Finally, I can also refer back to the passage previously shared on page 73 in the letter from W. Weber <note>Wilhelm Weber (1804–1891), a German physicist known for his work on magnetism and electricity.</note> to me, in which he for his part acknowledges the possibility of conceiving of atoms as being without extension.

## V. Relationship of the Physical to Herbartian Monadology.

Let us now consider in a few words the relationship—partly of kinship, partly of difference—in which our "simple beings" stand to those of Herbart, <note>Johann Friedrich Herbart (1776–1841), a philosopher who proposed that the world is made of "reals"—unchanging, simple entities similar to Leibniz’s monads.</note> and the various worldviews associated with them.

For us, as for Herbart, the substances to which we ultimately return present themselves as absolutely simple, discontinuous, non-extended, unchangeable beings, which are not empirically graspable in themselves, yet whose assumption is based entirely on the observation of the empirical, and whose relationships can only be derived from it. In our view, as in Herbart’s, all events, all causality, all the forces of nature rest on the shifting relationships of their "togetherness" <note>term: "Zusammen" — Herbart’s technical term for the state of multiple "reals" or monads existing in relation to one another.</note>; these simple beings are the ultimate cores of all relationships in nature.

But Herbart’s simple beings emerge more as a residue of destruction, whereas ours emerge as a residue of the analysis of what is given; the former as phantoms <note>original: "Schemens"</note> behind the given, the latter as the ultimate elements of the given; the former to be thought of as outside time and space—only improperly and as a mental image, even if necessarily—as points; the latter to be thought of sincerely and explicitly as points in time and space; the former as the creators of space and time, the latter as siblings of space and time; the former possessing various fundamental qualities in themselves, the latter indifferent in themselves without any quality; the former in relationship only through accidental and <note>original text says "uud" instead of "und" (and)</note> accidentally changing views, the latter in relationship through eternal laws that unchangeably govern eternal change. Herbart’s "happening" arises from the togetherness of the simple beings, yet it falls within them themselves, and is conceivable only as an affection of them; our happening consists only in changes of their togetherness, and they enter into it unaffected. Herbart’s simple beings are *called* unchangeable; ours *are* unchangeable. Herbart’s simple beings hover between physical and spiritual nature; ours lie entirely on the physical side. <margin>1</margin> According to Herbart, the soul is a single simple being in the midst of the body, distinguished from the other simple beings of the body only by its most favorable central position and in external interaction with them; according to us, the soul is a unified being incomparable to the simple beings <margin>Analogy</margin> of the body; rather than being a single one in their midst, it is instead the general bond between them and their interactions and changes. According to Herbart, the life of the soul is only a multiplicity from the outside, excited in the truly existing, inherently independent, eternally rigid simple being as "self-preservation" <note>term: "Selbsterhaltung" — In Herbart’s psychology, the soul’s reaction to maintain its integrity against external disturbances.</note> or resistance against threatened disturbance of a changing appearance, linked to unity through the simplicity of the being; according to us, the life of the

<header>V. Relationship of the Physical to Herbartian Monadology.</header>

The soul is the unified self-appearance <note>term: "Selbsterscheinung" — A central concept in Fechner's philosophy, where the inner mental experience and outer physical appearance are two sides of the same reality.</note> of that which truly and essentially exists, unfolding and organizing itself into a manifold and an exchange of determinations; it is what it is only in this living, unified self-appearance and maintains itself as that which it is, so that one does not need to look for a "thing-in-itself" <note>original: "Ansich" — A reference to the Kantian concept of a reality that exists independently of our perceptions.</note> behind it. These simple beings, then, are merely the ultimate elements of the external appearance of that which simultaneously appears to itself as a whole. 

According to Herbart, God is the simple being into which most of the rest of the world shines, and which shines most into the rest of the world—a single being among many individuals. According to us, God is the unified being through which the entire world appears to itself as one, and in whom we all mutually appear to one another. 

According to Herbart, the soul is immortal because its simple, fundamental essence remains unchangeable behind the shifting, non-essential appearance of life, and thus outlasts life's changes. According to us, the soul is immortal because its fundamental essence is the self-appearing unity—or the unified self-appearance—of the very change and transformation of existence itself, and every change eternally generates new changes. 

According to Herbart, the separation of individuals consists in an absolute "discretion" or isolation of their fundamental essences behind the appearance; their connection, however, lies in the fact that through their "togetherness" <note>term: "Zusammen" — Herbart’s technical term for the state of multiple "reals" or monads existing in relation to one another.</note> in their simple qualities, an opposition arises (according to a contingent view) that threatens them with mutual disturbance, against which they maintain themselves. 

According to us, the spiritual separation of individuals consists simply in self-distinction—meaning that each knows immediately only about itself, not about the other. Their connection, however, consists in the fact that a Higher Being distinguishes both within itself as particularities of its own higher self-appearance, and knows of both, reaching across both with its cognitive relations. However, how the bodily separation and connection (to which that spiritual one is linked) is to be understood cannot be explained here without a more precise investigation into the functional relationship between body and soul.

The development of this entire fundamental view, which has been contrasted here in its main aspects with Herbart’s, can be found in my work *Zend-Avesta* <note>A major work by Gustav Theodor Fechner published in 1851, subtitled "On the Things of Heaven and the Hereafter."</note>.

It cannot be denied that Herbart’s simple beings, as well as Hegel’s concept of "being" as the foundation of all reality...

## V. Relationship of physical to Herbartian Monadology.

and the ground of all spirit, are a philosophical ultimate in a completely different sense than our simple beings. These simple beings hide nothing of space, time, law, or spirit within themselves, nor are they able to produce such things from themselves; rather, they are only able to provide the substance of the world—and thereby have their own existence—within a compact connection to and relationship with these things. In general, they only have an existence for the outward appearance, which itself only exists insofar as it falls within a self-appearance <note>term: "Selbsterscheinung" — In Fechner's thought, things have an outer side (physical) and an inner side (mental/soul). Self-appearance is the inner experience of a being.</note>. Also, our atoms <note>The author uses "atoms" not as hard little balls of matter, but as the "simple beings" or "monads" that serve as the most basic units of reality.</note> are not meant to be a philosophical ultimate in that sense; the "given" should not be so locked away within them that the task then consists of first returning to the "given" from them, as is the case with Herbart <note>Johann Friedrich Herbart (1776–1841) proposed a world made of "reals"—simple, unchanging entities that are hidden behind the world we perceive.</note>, nor should it be evaporated into the abstract so that the concrete lies far below, as is the case with Hegel <note>Georg Wilhelm Friedrich Hegel (1770–1831) developed a system of absolute idealism where physical reality is seen as a manifestation of the "Idea" or "Spirit."</note>; instead, the concretely given elements of the corporeal world should only be opened up to their ultimate depth so that one can move from any one thing to any other through their intermediary, and be able to provide something new oneself. This is only possible if we go to the ultimate elements of the concretely given and loosen them so that they are available for every exchange and combination.

Now, however, according to our view, nothing prevents us from demanding and establishing a Unifying and Ultimate principle over all those concepts that present themselves to us in a solidary link and relationship, and assigning to it the name of the Absolute, of Being, of God, or whatever else one wishes. Its concept is determined either by being and containing everything in One that can be spoken of, or by being the highest and most universal of everything that can be spoken of—for it can be grasped in both ways. In the former case, those concepts appear alongside others belonging to the metaphysical organism as abstractions of that **most real being** <note>original: "Ens realissimum" — A traditional philosophical term for God as the being who possesses all possible perfections or reality.</note>; in the latter case, they appear as the concrete manifestations of that **most abstract being** <note>original: "Ens abstractissimum" — A term for the most general possible concept of being, stripped of all specific qualities.</note>. They appear not one after another or derivable from one another, but simultaneously, hanging together in different directions—even intersecting with different modes of connection—which are to be pursued in metaphysics (of which only a fragment is present here); not according to an abstract, monotonous schema, but on the basis of the observation of real being.

# APPENDIX.

The following two chapters may be regarded as an appendix to the philosophical division of this work, insofar as their content is not exactly essential to the general nexus <note>term: "Nexus" — the interconnected series of ideas or the logical framework of a system.</note> of philosophical atomism, but serves to expand and supplement it in several respects.

## VI. On the Mutual Conceptual Positioning of Motion, Space, Time, Number, Force.

Instead of starting from time and space to arrive at the concept of motion, and considering the latter as a compound function of the former (as we did in the second and third chapters of the second division of this work), some have proceeded in reverse. They have declared motion to be the simple original concept <note>original: "Urbegriff"</note> from which to start, in order to consider time and space as functions of it. Space, they claim, is only graspable as an external product or external appearance, and time only as an internal measure of motion.

Now, undeniably, space and time can indeed be regarded as derivatives of motion, insofar as they can be abstracted from it, but not the other way around; however, it is for this very reason that metaphysics must place them first as the simpler elements. The *abstract things* <note>original Latin: "Abstracta"</note> here precede the *concrete things* <note>original Latin: "Concretis"</note>. And it is not to be admitted that they are only graspable at all as abstractions from motion. Rather, in this respect, time and space are like oxygen and hydrogen, which can certainly be obtained from water, but do not need to be obtained from it;

<meta>This page continues the discussion on whether space and time are derived from motion or vice versa. The author is arguing against the view that motion is the primary "original concept," using physiological examples to show that we can perceive space without active movement.</meta>

...while conversely, water can only be produced from oxygen and hydrogen.

To the open eye, the flatly placed hand, or the warm body, spatial "being-apart-from-one-another" <note>original: "Auseinander" — a term used to describe the basic quality of spatial extension where things exist outside of one another.</note> presents itself without the help or any lingering taste of motion. When both have been denied, it could only be because one has, on the one hand, confused the difficulty of entirely excluding motion and the representation of motion <note>original: "Bewegungsvorstellung"</note> from external and internal spatial intuition <note>original: "Raumanschauung" — the way the mind directly perceives or "looks at" space.</note> with a necessity of the former for the realization of the latter; or, on the other hand, because one has interpreted the actual dependence of many conditions of spatial perception on motion—or on the conditions of motion—as a dependence of spatial perception itself upon motion.

Rather than the movement of the eye or the tactile hand being necessary to give the form of spatial extension to intuition or tactile sensation, one can claim that the more one avoids movement of the sensory organ, the broader and more extended the spatiality appears all at once. Meanwhile, during the movement of the organ, the attention tends to concentrate much more on the point of sharpest sensation; thus, the combination of successive sensations that certainly occurs gives the impression of a line instead of a surface, relatively more so than the combination of simultaneous sensations. I say "only relatively," because even during the movement of the eye or the tactile hand, something extended is grasped at every moment; otherwise, as previously noted, the movement would simply give the impression of a mathematical line. Instead, we perceive a band. The fact that it is possible to survey an extension during an electric spark, which lasts only about a millionth of a second; and that one can have extended afterimages <note>term: "Nachbilder" — the visual impression that remains after a light stimulus has ceased.</note> in the eye which move *with* the eyes, and thus cannot arise *through* the movement of the eye—all of this points in the same direction. It remains true, of course, that without internal motion—vibrations of our nervous system—we would be able to have a consciousness neither of space nor of anything at all; and one can ask generally how our consciousness relates to this, a question which concerns the connection between body and soul. But this is not the question at issue when we ask whether, in the context of our life of intuition and representation, our spatial intuition or spatial representation is essentially dependent on a movement—

### VI. On the mutual conceptual relationship of motion, space, etc.

...intuition or the representation of motion <note>original: "Bewegungsvorstellung" — the mental image or idea of moving</note> and whether the concept of space accordingly depends on the concept of motion; for the question here is only whether the external movement of the eye or the hand might involuntarily trigger representations of movement which, in turn, pull spatial intuition along with them. This is precisely what we must reject. Even if we wish to trace how spatial intuition is linked to the movements of our nervous system within the study of the relationship between body and soul, we cannot perceive these movements as anything other than something ordered and occurring *within* a space, rather than being able to grasp space itself as an external appearance or representation of movement.

Even without a trace of an actual representation of movement or a feeling of motion at all, the intuition—the mental representation of extended spatiality—exists in the cases previously mentioned. We are indeed aware, or can become aware at any moment: "here is room for movement"; but we distinguish very clearly between the case where movement actually takes place and where none takes place. What would this distinction mean if space itself were merely the external representation of movement? One might then speak of unconscious representations or feelings of movement in all of this; however, the concepts or words "space," "time," and "movement" do not refer to a dark, unconscious state, but to something clearly conscious or something that can be made conscious.

Regarding time, music—just like boredom—allows us to feel temporal succession <term>Nacheinander: the "one-after-another" quality of time</term> without regard for movement. The beat of music is something purely temporal, where (provided we are not thinking of the performer) we do not have a feeling of movement-relationships, but rather of the relationships of a pure succession of sensation. And while there is music to whose beat one likes to walk or dance, there is also music to whose beat one falls asleep. In cases of boredom, time is felt to be longer the more movement is lacking; conversely, time passes most quickly for us when we lead an active life <note>original: "bewegtes Leben" — literally a "moved life," meaning a life full of activity and change</note>. We have a need for time to be filled with movement, but we often feel that it is *not* filled; this is what we call "empty time."

One can, therefore, have space and time for themselves without movement, insofar as one thing can ever be had without the other; for...

### VI. On the mutual conceptual relationship of motion, space, etc.

Ultimately, everything in the world is, of course, interconnected. But if a bird flies past the eye, or if one moves one’s fingers feeling across a surface, then one has that which was previously separate now joined together in movement; and one can then, of course, separate it again from the combination through abstraction. This complexity of the concept of motion <note>original: "Bewegungsbegriffes"</note> compared to the relative simplicity of space and time remains true as long as one understands the concepts—or rather the words—motion, space, and time as they are understood in science and daily life. To understand them such that motion appears as something simple and fundamental compared to space and time, one would have to give the concept of motion a position or meaning that is justified neither by facts nor by linguistic usage, thereby sacrificing its usefulness for both science and life.

<margin>~</margin>One might be tempted to view the concept of motion as a prerequisite for the concept of time because the measurement of time cannot be obtained except by means of motion. But the concept, the essence, the quality, the internal relation of time is one thing; the measurement, the quantitative comparison, and the external relation of one time to another is another. A yardstick <note>original: "Die Elle" — an ell, a historical unit of length typically based on the reach of an arm</note> is always thicker than the fabric it is meant to measure; likewise, motion, acting as the measure of time, contains even more than just time. A bridge between two banks connects both banks, yet it contains more than either bank; similarly, for the external relationship between two times, more is needed than mere time—it requires a means of transmission between the two. Should one say that space essentially includes the concept of matter, or essentially depends on it, because space can only be measured with a physical ruler? Every measurement of the three fundamental entities <note>referring to the triad of space, time, and matter</note> must rely on the other two, but that does not mean their underlying concepts depend on them. Movement is just as necessary for the measurement of space as it is for the measurement of time, because to bring two spaces—or rather their material endpoints—into alignment, one must perform a movement; yet this no more makes the concept of space dependent on the concept of motion than it does the concept of time. Thus, the measurement of matter—mass—also presupposes the other concepts of time and space and their function, motion, without the concept of matter itself depending on them.

The measurement of time is based on the following considerations:

### VI. On the mutual conceptual relationship of motion, space, etc.

Equal times are defined as those in which equal spaces are traversed by means of equal—that is, equally generated or, with regard to the **law of inertia** <note>original: "Beharrungsgesetz"; Newton’s first law, which states that an object remains in its state of rest or uniform motion unless acted upon by an external force</note>, equally continuing—motion. For the **measurement of time** <note>original: "Zeitmass"</note>, a clock is therefore required. Double and triple time is obtained through the summation of two or three equal times. The judgment of the equality of times is not possible at all without regard to equal motion; thus, the concept of equal times cannot be established in any other way. If one takes motion *B* as the product of space *s* and time *t*, thus $B = \frac{s}{t}$ <note>The author uses "product" (Produkt) here in the sense of a resultant relationship, though the formula expresses speed as a quotient of space divided by time</note>—which, of course, is only to be understood in a certain sense (see below)—then the dependence of the equality of times upon the equality of spaces and motions follows of its own accord. The equal generation or maintenance of a motion must be recognized, respectively, by the repetition of the generating circumstances or the absence of altering circumstances. And this, in turn, presupposes the validity of the most general law of all occurrence (p. 43), without which no **natural science** <note>original: "Naturwissenschaft"</note> would be possible at all: that to the extent that conditions are equal or unequal, the results will also be equal or unequal. The conditioning circumstances, however, ultimately reside in the spatio-temporal relationships of **matter** <note>original: "Materie"</note> to one another. If one does not know how to produce or maintain these identically, no measurement of time is possible.

While the concept of time is something very simple and can be easily explained by referring to the flow of consciousness <note>original: "Zeitfluss des Bewusstseins"</note>, the measurement of time is something that rests upon complicated prerequisites. In this regard, the relationship between concept and measurement in the case of time is the same as with all other things; it is no different in practice than in theory. In general, it is simple to recognize where an object belongs according to its concept; to measure it precisely is difficult. Indeed, the precise methods of measurement and the manufacturing of accurate measuring instruments and standards—scales, weights, clocks—belong to the most difficult tasks of **physics** and **mechanics**. The spiritual or mental realm <note>original: "Das Geistige"</note> has even defied all measurement up to this point. He who wishes to ground the concept upon the measurement loses the spirit.

Since weights, measuring rods, and clocks are transitory and never absolutely in agreement, and thus are not exactly reproducible; since the dimensions of the earth, and with them the **rotation time** of the earth, are affected by its progressive cooling,

<summary>The author explains that while time is intuitively understood through consciousness, its measurement requires the observation of equal motion and the presupposition of the law of inertia.
