### VI. On the mutual conceptual relationship of motion, space, etc.

If their mass is increased by meteorites, the question arises whether it is even possible to obtain absolutely fixed units of measurement for matter, space, and time; indeed, whether it lies in the nature of things that such units can be established. I believe that this is indeed the case in principle; however, I can only develop the details of my view on this in the following chapter.

I am inclined to explain the concept of **motion** as follows: **Time**, **space**, and **matter** can only be grasped directly in the world of phenomena as *that* which they are—the *forms* and *substance* of the world of phenomena—and any definition can ultimately do nothing more than point toward this fact. However, insofar as one incorporates what has been thus grasped into a concept, one can discover **similarities** and **opposites** between them. Through these relations, one can partly **link** them together and partly **characterize them in opposition to one another**, as was done in the second chapter.

What applies to time, space, and matter can be transferred to their **functions**. In the final instance, these too will only be graspable in the actual world of phenomena as *that* which they are; but by taking them into a concept, one may also seek to discover **similarities** and **differences** in them. Thus, one might test whether motion, as a function of time and space, has something in common with other functions and can, accordingly, be subordinated with them under a common concept. This concept would serve as the **genus** <note>original: "genus"; the broad category or "kind" in logic</note> which, combined with the **specific difference** <note>original: "differentia specifica"; the identifying characteristic that distinguishes a species from others in the same category</note> of the constituent factors, can form the definition of motion. Indeed, from this point of view, the often-used expression that motion is a **product** of space and time (here always to be understood conceptually) could be justified in a certain sense, even if only in a limited one.

Specifically, it is a completely analogous mental operation to the one I perform when I think of "5 six times" as when I think of a spatial **coexistence** <note>original: "Nebeneinander"; literally "side-by-side," the fundamental characteristic of space</note> occurring **successively** <note>original: "nach einander"; literally "one after another," the fundamental characteristic of time</note>. With this, however, I fulfill the concept of motion; and just as the **product** of 5 times 6 is equal to the **product** of 6 times 5, a "succession" thought of as "coexistence" gives the same concept of motion as a "coexistence" thought of as a "succession." — The **product** 5 $\times$ 6 can be thought of as generated by a fivefold repeated placement and addition of 6, and likewise the concept of motion can be thought of as generated by a **successively** repeated placement and addition of the **coexistence**. In both cases, however, the concept of the **product** is much—

<summary>The author explores the philosophical definition of motion, proposing that it can be understood conceptually as a "product" of space and time, similar to a mathematical multiplication of spatial coexistence and temporal succession.</summary>

### VI. On the mutual conceptual relationship of motion, space, etc.

...circumscribed more than eliminated. If I divide a line into two parts and call one "Front" and the other "Back," then the positional relationship of Front to Back is the opposite of that from Back to Front\*); and if I divide a stretch of time into two parts, the relationship of the earlier to the later part is the opposite of that of the later to the earlier\*\*). One can designate this opposition in both cases, as with numbers, through an opposition of the sign <note>The author refers to positive (+) and negative (-) signs in mathematics</note>. With regard to this, the analogy between the **numerical product** and the **time-space product** <note>The author is treating "Motion" as the result (product) of multiplying "Space" and "Time"</note> can be pursued even further. Specifically, if one changes the signs of both factors in a product of two numbers, the product remains unchanged; if, however, one changes the sign of only one factor, the sign of the product changes. Thus, the motion "earlier right, later left" remains unchanged if I transform all terms into their opposites; for I then obtain "later left, earlier right," whereas reversing the terms for either time alone or space alone yields the opposite motion.

As long as I intend to express nothing else by the name **Product** than that the thing so designated is everywhere the result of an equally immediate **reciprocal determination** of two concepts by one another, the expression will be beyond reproach. In this sense, I can also call the **Position** <note>original: "Position." In German philosophy, this often refers to a "positing" or a positive assertion</note> which arises from the **negation of the negation** <note>A classic Hegelian dialectical concept where a first negation is itself negated to reach a higher level of certainty or "position"</note> a "product" of both negations, just as this product remains unchanged if I reverse the signs of both factors, but flips into its opposite if I change the sign of only one. But one would be mistaken if one wished to derive more in common from the shared name than that from which it was derived; rather, one must examine the properties of products specifically—

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the conceptual definition of "Motion" as a "Product" of "Space" and "Time." Having previously used the mathematical rules of signs to explain directionality, the author now warns against overextending this numerical analogy to the qualitative nature of physical reality.</meta>

# VI. On the mutual conceptual relationship of motion, space, etc.

...seek, and not immediately try to transfer the **algorithm** <term>Algorithm: here, the specific rules of calculation used in arithmetic</term> that applies to **numerical products** to other types of products.

Indisputably, the nature of a product depends not only on the nature of the **function** <term>Function: the mathematical relationship or operation connecting parts</term> through which its **factors** <term>Factors: the individual components being multiplied together</term> are linked, but also on the nature of the factors themselves. In a numerical product, one is dealing with factors that are homogeneous, qualitatively identical, and quantitatively comparable; in **motion**, as a product of "Juxtaposition" <note>side-by-side/Space</note> and "Succession" <note>one-after-another/Time</note>, one deals with factors that are non-homogeneous, qualitatively unequal, and quantitatively incomparable. Now, it by no means follows automatically that two products, which behave so differently regarding their factors, will show the same properties and the same applicability in all relationships.

How cautious one must be not to base hasty applications on the name "Product" is shown by the following example. To double a numerical product, one must add two identical numerical products; to double a **motion**, one must add two identical motions, e.g., two identical steps; but in the case of a numerical product, this results in the doubling of only *one* of the two factors, whichever one chooses for that purpose; in the case of motion, *both* factors are doubled; the double step contains double the **space** and double the **time**. This naturally depends on the fact that the doubling of a motion is not aimed at doubling the *quality* of the juxtaposition or succession—which provide the conceptual product of motion—but rather at their *quantity*; meanwhile, in the numerical product, the doubling is indeed aimed at the quantitative factors themselves which produce the product; thus, there is nothing comparable here.

One could seek another difference between motion (as a product of space and time) and a numerical product in the idea that numbers are themselves **abstracts** <term>Abstracta: things that exist only as concepts in the mind</term>, valid only in thought, whereas time and space are concrete forms of external reality. But this difference is only apparent. The "six" on a die is a concrete number just as much as the face of the die on which it appears is a concrete space; the concept of "Juxtaposition," on the other hand, is just as much an **abstract space** as the concept of a number is an **abstract number**. Furthermore, I can represent a numerical product like 5 $\times$ 6 just as easily as motion: once abstractly in thought, and another time concretely in observable reality.

The apparent products of space and time with which **mathematical mechanics** <term>Mathematical Mechanics: the study of motion using purely mathematical formulas, often associated with Newtonian physics</term> operates are instead products of the numbers by which time and space are measured, rather than products of time and space themselves in the sense used so far, and one must not confuse the two. The same applies to the **quotients** of...

<summary>The author cautions against treating the "product" of space and time exactly like a mathematical multiplication, noting that while numbers are homogeneous, space and time are qualitatively different factors. He clarifies that doubling a physical motion (like a step) doubles both space and time, unlike a numerical product where only one factor needs to change.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on whether physical concepts like "Motion" can be described as a mathematical "Product." The author is currently exploring the limits of mathematical terminology when applied to qualitative physical realities like Space and Time.</meta>

# VI. On the mutual conceptual relationship of motion, space, etc.

->175<-

**Space** and **Time**, with which physics and mechanics are concerned. Mathematics, in general, has nothing to do with the products or quotients of qualitative factors; it does not recognize such things, and the expansion of the concept of a "Product"—which we establish here as possible in a certain sense—does not affect mathematics, nor can it contradict it, as long as we never forget that caution in using the name "Product" by which conflict with math is excluded. Ultimately, it is only a matter of definition, or of a broader or narrower use of a concept (or word, respectively), whether we wish to consider the name "Product" applicable beyond the realm of mathematics. What is certain is that the mental operation through which a product arises in mathematics extends beyond mathematics itself; on the other hand, however, it must also be acknowledged that extending the use of the word "Product" beyond math remains dubious insofar as it can easily lead one to transfer everything that applies to mathematical products onto other types of products.

Insofar as Space and Time are quantitatively incomparable in themselves, the question arises: how does one arrive at the concept of **velocity**? Is velocity not a quantitative ratio of the space traversed to the time used to traverse it?

A direct ratio of this kind certainly does not exist; rather, both Space and Time can each be quantitatively compared directly only with a unit of their own kind <note>i.e., you can only compare a length to another length, or a duration to another duration</note>. However, one can consider any two parts of space and time belonging to a specific motion as "associated units" of space and time. By then comparing the space of any other motion with that spatial unit, and the time with that unit of time, one obtains specific **numerical measures** <term>Masszahlen: the specific numbers resulting from measurement</term> for both, the ratio of which gives the velocity.

If, therefore, the concept of velocity is to be sought in a comparative relationship between space and the time required for traversal, it can only be insofar as one has an indirect comparison in mind. This, however, becomes possible because Space and Time—although different as **Juxtaposition** <note>original: "Nebeneinander"; referring to the side-by-side nature of Space</note> and **Succession** <note>original: "Nacheinander"; referring to the one-after-another nature of Time</note>—nevertheless agree in that they are both equally **homogeneous continua** <term>Homogeneous Continua: smooth, unbroken expanses where every part is of the same nature as the whole</term>, and that the motion in which they meet creates a link between them.

Just as little as time is to be made dependent on motion, so little can it, in my opinion, as is likewise practiced by some...

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the quantitative nature of physical concepts. Having previously discussed how "velocity" is often treated as a mathematical product of space and time, the author now turns to the relationship between the concept of "Number" and "Time," arguing that numbers exist independently of the temporal act of counting.</meta>

### 176 VI. On the mutual conceptual relationship of motion, space, etc.

It is not the number itself that should be made dependent on time, but only the act of *counting*; the number, however, is not essentially bound to a counting process (at least not to counting in the narrower sense, which is always a temporal act). When one looks into the sky, one immediately perceives a large number—a plurality—of stars without first having to count them; granted, one does not know exactly *how many* there are. However, if one includes "determinacy" in the concept of a number (though this actually only belongs to the concept of a *specific* number), one also immediately sees two eyes in every person's face or three points in every triangle, without first counting them. The claim that a "rapid counting" occurs here is identical to the view considered earlier: that spatial **juxtaposition** <note>original: "Nebeneinander"</note> can only be achieved through a temporal "one-after-another." In reality, however, counting is only necessary to gradually increase a collection that one already has "together" on a small scale from the very beginning. Ultimately, everything counted must always be gathered back into a simultaneous sum if one is to have a "number" at all; otherwise, one is drawing water with the **sieve of the Danaides** <note>In Greek mythology, the Danaides were condemned to fill a leaky vessel for eternity; here, the author suggests that without a mental "total," individual units would leak out of the mind as soon as they are gathered.</note>, and the units picked up one by one would fall apart just as they are collected. Counting is, in fact, no more necessary for the generation of a number than the movement of the eye or finger is for the generation of space. The number we already possess without counting is merely enlarged by it.

Indisputably, there are numbers that are essentially dependent on the concept of time: these are the **ordinal numbers**, such as *First*, *Second*, and *Third*. But if one wishes to make the cardinal numbers 1, 2, 3 dependent on the concept of time as well, what then remains to distinguish them from the ordinal numbers?

As discussed previously (p. 134), I consider numbers to be absolutely <term>hypostatized</term> <note>To "hypostatize" is to treat an abstract concept—like a number—as if it were a real, physical substance or entity.</note> by the discrete elements of matter in nature; these can be gathered into sums in space or followed in any order in time, thus representing both types of numbers. This will soon gain further significance in what follows.

**Motion** can indeed be thought of abstractly as the temporal sequence of a spatial juxtaposition without (at least explicitly) thinking of an object being moved; likewise, **matter** can be thought of as something discrete without thinking of motion. However, insofar as we grasp motion and matter concretely—as they occur in nature and truly underlie our abstractions—motion does not occur without matter that moves, and matter does not occur without motion, in which...

<summary>The author argues that "Number" is a spatial or conceptual plurality independent of the temporal act of counting, distinguishing between cardinal and ordinal numbers. He suggests that matter itself "hypostatizes" or gives physical reality to numbers, and concludes that matter and motion are concretely inseparable.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 176 regarding the relationship between mathematical concepts and physical reality. ### VI. On the mutual conceptual relationship of motion, space, etc. 177

...it is conceived (p. 153). Nothing then prevents us from saying that moved matter, or **concrete motion**, is a product of all three fundamental factors of nature: **space, time, and matter**.

To be sure, conceptual difficulties may arise here. If matter is inherently discontinuous <term>Discontinuous: composed of separate, distinct units rather than a single, unbroken whole</term>, how can it yield a common product with factors like space and time, which are inherently continuous? To think of the discontinuous as continuous—and even as continuous in two senses at once—seems like a contradiction. At least, so it appears. One can only answer: look at how it behaves in reality; reality resolves this apparent contradiction through motion. In truth, however, the contradiction is merely an illusion; and as for the supposed wisdom one is meant to find in contradictions, there is nothing to it here, just as there is nothing to it elsewhere. For the atom remains just as discontinuous from other atoms while in motion as it does when conceived without motion; space and time remain just as continuous in themselves as they would be without atoms. However, a **mutual determination** <note>original: "Wechselbestimmtheit"</note> of all three arises, which manifests in nature as moved matter or concrete motion. The concept of moved matter must be formed based on this observation. A true contradiction would only occur if the discontinuous atom were declared to be the same thing as continuous time or continuous space; but we are only speaking of a mutual determination. So it is with all products. The factors do not become identical; rather, they determine one another to form something new; one must simply observe what that new thing is.

This must also be kept in mind when considering what the products of space and matter, or time and matter, might be, as opposed to the product of space and time on its own. One must not demand that what is inherently discontinuous become continuous, and thus conclude that the product of space and matter results in **filling space** <note>original: "Raumerfüllung"</note>. This would be positing a true contradiction, because the inherently discontinuous would not be determined by the continuous, but rather identified with it. Rather, one must observe how it looks in reality when the continuous is directly determined by the discontinuous, and the discontinuous by the continuous.

Accordingly, one can [view] **corporeality** <note>original: "Körperlichkeit"</note>, in which discrete atoms are connected by spatial continuity; conversely, within the cohesive space, a discretion <note>The quality of being composed of distinct, separate parts (discrete).</note> [is introduced] by the physical atoms...

### VI. On the mutual conceptual relationship of motion, space, etc.

...is viewed as that in which space and matter are **mutually determined** <note>original: "wechselbestimmt"</note>. All properties of matter depend on the relations of this mutual determination. The same atoms, however, which, when spatially connected, produce physicality as a sum in which all must be thought of as related to one another simultaneously (thereby representing a **cardinal number** <note>original: "Summenzahl"; a number denoting quantity</note>), can also be related to one another temporally in succession as first, second, and third atoms. In this way, they represent an **ordinal number** <note>original: "Ordnungszahl"; a number denoting position in a sequence</note> and thus provide the sequence or order in which the elements or combinations of matter can be tracked through time. This becomes necessary when considering the effects of one atom upon another, the propagation of motion from one atom to another, and so on. Indeed, in our **observation of nature** <note>original: "Naturbetrachtung"</note>, we cannot manage if we view matter solely as spatially combined and not also as something that can be tracked in temporal order.

One might object that the spatial connection of atoms is real, while the temporal order in which I choose to think of them is merely possible. However, in truth, both are entirely equivalent. Taken absolutely, I can say that a bridge exists from every atom to every other atom in spatial extension, which allows for the possibility of transition for the perceiving mind, though it considers now only this connection and now only that one. Likewise, taken absolutely, a bridge exists from every atom to every other atom through temporal succession, which allows for the possibility of transition for the thinking mind, though it chooses now only this mode of transition and now only that one.

Into the triple product of moved matter, as it presents itself in **concrete natural reality**, all three binary products now enter collectively, each two contributing a common factor, and can be specifically abstracted from it. Concrete motion requires motion in its abstract formulation as a **time-space product**. It also requires a spatial union of several atoms; for **concrete motion** arises only through the **interaction** <term>Interaction: the reciprocal action or influence of atoms upon one another (Wechselwirkung)</term> of atoms, and concrete motion exists only in the altered relation from atom to atom. Finally, the union of atoms essentially includes the possibility of tracking the relations that occur during motion, starting with any one as the first and proceeding in any order to every other.

Or, if one wishes to exclude the latter and thereby define the concept of force more narrowly—as is common—by restricting it to external forces, it is the lawful dependence in which the modification or preservation of the state of motion or rest of matter stands in regard to its existing spatio-temporal relations to other matter\*). ->### VI. On the mutual conceptual relationship of motion, space, etc.<-

...the latter two certainly involved in the case of electrical forces). The measure of **force**, however, lies in taking the positive or negative modification (acceleration, deceleration, creation, or cancellation) of the **velocity** of a given **mass of matter** as a unit and comparing others against it.

When we speak of **gravitational force**, **cohesive force**, **electrical force**, and **magnetic force** in relation to one another, we are referring to the different laws according to which the modifications occur in given combinations of matter—thus, to the **concept** or the **quality** of the force. However, when we speak of a double or triple force, we refer to the different proportions of magnitude in which they stand—thus, to the **quantitative** side of the force. **Physics** and **mechanics**, when speaking of forces in general, deal sometimes with the concept (the quality) and sometimes with the measure (the quantity) of the force; one must not confuse the one with the other. In the discussions on page 107 and following, we were concerned only with the concept of force.

How it happens that a new arrangement of matter lawfully emerges from a given **arrangement** <note>original: "Zusammensein"; a term often used in 19th-century German philosophy to describe how elements coexist or interact within a system</note>, why acceleration is exactly this value and not perhaps double or triple everywhere—and thus why the **absolute magnitude of force** in the world is different from how it is found—why **persistence** <note>original: "Beharrung"; refers to inertia or the tendency of a body to remain in its state of rest or uniform motion</note> combines with acceleration, or why one effect of force combines with another at all (and specifically according to the simplest rule), all of this will ultimately not be explained, but only accepted as a **fundamental fact** <note>original: "Grundthatsache"</note>. However, nothing prevents us from placing these facts in relation to one another, or to other fundamental facts of existence, and seeking higher connecting perspectives. In such cases, for example, it will be shown that the **principle of greatest simplicity** and the **principle of sufficient reason** <note>original: "Princip zureichenden Grundes"; the philosophical principle that nothing happens without a reason why it should be so rather than otherwise</note> truly do apply wherever they can be considered.

As long as we remain within the physical realm, we have nothing more to do regarding forces than to observe the **fundamental facts** presented here as precisely as possible in all their particulars, to designate them as clearly as possible, to reduce them to the most general determinations possible, and to develop them into the greatest possible wealth of conclusions through the most extensive, contradiction-free combination of those facts.

VII. Hypothesis on the General Law of Force in Nature.

Regarding **metaphysics**, according to our conception of the field (p. 127), its task concerning forces can only be this: to seek a conclusion in something most universal and ultimate, building upon the foundation of the most general and final principles that **physics**—as an empirical science guided by mathematics—has been able to reach thus far, and continuing in the direction that has safely led us there. However, a metaphysical consideration of forces in this sense is still entirely lacking. Unfortunately, it must be admitted—and has been admitted previously—that the physical foundation for this is still relatively weak and narrow, and the direction for moving beyond it is not yet clearly mapped out. In the meantime, the following chapter may be viewed as a tentative fragment of such a consideration. Once the metaphysical determination of physical **force** is achieved, one is free—and it may even be considered a higher task of metaphysics—to seek its relationship to other generalities and boundary concepts of existence.

## VII. Hypothesis on the General Law of Force in Nature.

After all the discussions held in the third chapter, it is not very probable that the phenomena of nature can be constructed solely with the help of **gravitation** <note>original: "Gravitation"; the force of attraction between masses</note> and **inertia** <note>original: "Beharrung"; the tendency of an object to remain in its state of rest or uniform motion</note>. However, before one decides to permit **fundamental forces** that are related to a different basic quality of **matter**, another path may still be attempted.

It is conceivable that gravitation, without being the most general force governing the events in nature, represents only a special case of a most universal force. Or, to put it another way, the **law of gravitation** might be just a special case of a most universal law under which all events in nature fall. Specifically, it would be the case that applies to noticeable distances between material particles, whereas the general law would allow effects to become noticeable at distances as small as those involved in **molecular phenomena**—effects which would then perceptibly vanish at any larger distance. And thus...

<note>The author is suggesting a "Unified Field Theory" of sorts, proposing that the forces we see at a large scale (like gravity) and the forces at a tiny scale (molecular) are actually the same law acting differently depending on distance.</note>

According to this, the law of gravitation would actually only be an **approximation law** <note>original: "Annäherungsgesetz"; a rule that is nearly correct for practical purposes but lacks absolute precision at all scales</note>, being all the more accurate the greater the distance between the particles, yet noticeably precise at any perceptible distance between them. But just as the law of gravitation does not refer to a different fundamental quality of matter, the same could hold true for the most general law under which it is subordinated.

Indeed, several attempts have already been made to express the general force through an infinite series of terms, which increase according to **powers of the distance** <note>mathematical exponents, such as $r^2$ or $r^3$</note> between the particles, in that they are inversely proportional (reciprocal) to these powers. The first term, reciprocal to the square of the distance, was intended to represent **gravitation**, against which the following terms would vanish at any perceptible distance between the particles; conversely, at **molecular distances**, the following terms would take on a predominant magnitude. By alternating the plus and minus signs, repulsive forces could be combined with attractive forces in the same series, where one or the other might predominate depending on the distance ratios. However, until now, neither has a rational principle been found for establishing such a series, nor, as far as I am aware, has the thought been seriously pursued or any success achieved with it.

The following contains a new attempt of this kind, which however is based on different viewpoints than the attempts made so far. Should it succeed, it would automatically fulfill the possibility—which was still left quite indefinite in the third chapter—of making all diversity in the effects of force dependent merely on the diversity of the **arrangement** (and respectively the modification of the arrangement ratios) of **simple atoms**—which are in themselves neutral—without regard for any quantitative or qualitative difference between them. And this is certainly the most favorable outcome for an **atomism** <note>the theory that all reality is composed of indivisible, elementary particles</note> that seeks to define itself in a simple and pure manner.

At first glance, one might almost regret that such a simple law as the law of gravitation is not meant to be the most general one at the same time; however, it will be shown that its simplicity is in fact merely a deficiency, and that we can gain a law which possesses an equally simple but higher

->### VII. Hypothesis on the General Law of Force in Nature.<-

...and places it within a more general viewpoint, which is capable of unfolding into an unspeakably greater wealth of specific instances of laws than the law of gravitation—which itself represents only the second of the cases of this law known thus far. — The following consideration leads to this.

Usually, inertia <note>original: "Beharrung"; the tendency of an object to remain at rest or in motion unless acted upon by an external force</note> and force are perceived as something fundamentally different. Nevertheless, the outcome of inertia can be combined with the outcomes of force exactly in the same way and according to the same rules (for example, in projectile motion <note>original: "Wurfbewegung"</note>, through which the parabolic path is created) as the outcomes of forces are combined among themselves. Furthermore, a conceptual relationship between inertia and force—keeping in mind for now the most clearly defined force, the force of gravitation—can be found through the viewpoint that the law of inertia determines the behavior of *a single* particle on its own (existing *only* with itself) without regard for its coexistence with others, whereas the law for force determines the behavior of *each* particle in coexistence with *one* other, but without regard for its coexistence with several more, and without regard for the previous law. Since the law of force determines the behavior of each particle toward the other reciprocally and jointly <note>original: "solidarisch"; implying a shared, mutual effect</note>, nothing prevents us from also saying: the law of inertia determines the behavior of *each individual* particle for itself without regard for its coexistence with others; the law of force determines the behavior of *every two* particles in connection, without regard for their coexistence with several more, just as without regard for the first law. The outcomes of both laws then combine at each particle.

We have here the first two stages of a series of laws; can this series not be continued further?

If there is a law that determines the behavior of *each single* particle for itself, and another law to be separated from the former that determines the behavior of *every two* particles in connection (the outcomes of which combine with those of the former), why not furthermore do the same for *every 3* particles specifically, for *every 4* particles specifically, and so on—laws that are to be separated from the previous ones, but whose outcomes combine with the outcomes of the previous ones?

Until now, *that* which happens in a combination of, for example, three particles, has been derived purely from the combination of the outcomes

->### VII. Hypothesis on the General Law of Force in Nature.<-

...derived, which are determined by the laws valid for each single particle and each pair of particles. It is certain that this suffices for all calculations of celestial phenomena; but does it also suffice for molecular phenomena? Could results not become noticeable here that depend on forces which are determined collectively <note>original: "solidarisch"; meaning the particles act together as a single unit or "jointly" rather than as a simple sum of individual parts</note> by the coexistence of more than two particles?

Let us follow this thought by seeking to generalize the relationship that already exists between the first and second law, progressing toward further laws.

The first law <note>The law of inertia, describing a single particle's behavior</note> determines the behavior of a particle on its own; the second law <note>The law of gravitation, describing the interaction of two particles</note> determines the behavior of that same particle according to the conditions of its coexistence with each other particle. It assigns it a corresponding velocity and direction which does not generally coincide with that determined by the first law, but rather combines with it. Likewise, the various directions and velocities assigned to the particle by the second law—depending on whether it is grouped with this or that other particle—combine with one another.

The third law would then collectively determine the behavior of the particle according to the conditions of its coexistence with each pair of other particles (for which we will attempt to determine the rules more closely below). It would assign the particle a corresponding velocity and direction which does not generally coincide with those determined by the two previous laws, but combines with them. Just as the various directions and velocities assigned to the particle by the third law—depending on whether it is grouped with these or those two other particles—will combine with one another, and so on for the forces determined by the coexistence of groups of four particles, five particles, and so forth. Generally, the principle is this: that the outcome of the higher laws, instead of being graspable as a mere composition of the outcomes of the lower ones, always combines itself *with* the outcomes of the lower laws themselves.

One would only still need proof or the assumption that the forces determined by the higher combinations (i.e., those consisting of more particles) decrease more rapidly with distance the higher the combination, so that all forces exceeding gravitation, for the movement of such distant...

### VII. Hypothesis on the General Law of Force in Nature. 185

<note>This page continues a sentence from the previous page regarding higher-order forces.</note> ...celestial bodies can be disregarded, while in the molecular realm they could play a large and even greater role than gravitation.

Even within the molecular realm itself, depending on the distance relationships of the particles or <term>molecules</term><note>The author uses this term in a historical sense, referring to the smallest units of matter before modern atomic theory was fully standardized</note>, lower forces might vanish in the face of higher ones, or higher forces against lower ones. In this way, relatively isolated combinations could be thought of as being left merely to their own internal forces regarding the behavior of their particles toward one another—even though, strictly speaking, every combination, as a member of the universal system <note>original: "Weltcombination"</note>, is subject even to the highest universal forces.

Before we attempt to define the principle thus established in general terms and develop it into its logical conclusions, let us allow some general considerations to speak in its favor.

From the outset, there is no *a priori* <note>Latin: "from the earlier"; meaning knowledge that proceeds from theoretical deduction rather than observation</note> necessity to think of the behavior of a particle as being legally determined solely by its own existence and its coexistence with one other particle, making everything dependent merely on a combination of those two determinations. For every particle is just as much "together" and capable of being grouped with two, three, or more other particles as it is with one. Indeed, it may seem unlikely from the start that nature should have contented itself with the first two steps on a path that stands open to infinity.

This general consideration is supported by the fact that the higher forces to which we are led show themselves to be truly suited to satisfying the actual needs of **physics**, as will become clear upon a closer examination of the consequences of our principle. The question is not, in fact, whether we want to have forces other than gravitation, but how we should think of them in relation and connection to gravitation and how to determine them more precisely. In this regard, our principle should offer the most favorable conditions possible.

Let us mention here, quite preliminarily, a few points where our principle opens promising prospects.

Nothing seems to support the assumption of forces which depend collectively <note>original: "solidarisch"; meaning the particles act as a joint unit rather than a simple sum of individual parts</note> on the conditions of the coexistence of all parts of a system—or, what is the same, on laws which...

### VII. Hypothesis on the General Law of Force in Nature.

...collectively determine the behavior of all its parts, is nowhere more welcome than for the interpretation of the phenomena presented by **organisms**. Indeed, it seems hardly conceivable that one could make the play of these phenomena dependent merely on a combination of forces acting from one particle to another. On the contrary, in the sense of our hypothesis, there exists for the totality of an organism's parts a force that governs their relationships as a whole, along with many subordinate forces for the specific subordinate systems included within the general arrangement.

Likewise, the interpretation of the various fundamental qualities of **chemically simple substances** <note>The author is referring to chemical elements, which at the time were being investigated to see if they were truly "simple" or composed of even smaller identical building blocks.</note> is easily brought into relationship with our principle. Chemists have already repeatedly arrived at the thought that the substances we distinguish as qualitatively different simple substances differ only because they are based on <term>molecules</term><note>In this context, the author uses "molecules" to describe clusters of atoms that form the basis of what we call elements.</note> made of a different number of identical atoms; and the proven <term>commensurability</term><note>The property of being measurable by a common standard. Here, it refers to the observation that atomic weights often appear as whole-number multiples of a base unit, like hydrogen.</note> between the atomic weights of many simple substances greatly supports this idea. Unquestionably, this commensurability points to a general principle, and nothing really stands in the way of the possibility of generalization if one sufficiently increases the so-called atomic weights—more correctly, *molecular weights*—of simple substances by multiplying them by a common factor; this would be necessary anyway if the molecules were composed of a plurality of simple atoms. Our principle aligns with this idea insofar as it allows the various fundamental qualities of so-called chemically simple substances to be related to the difference in the law that prevails within their underlying molecules, depending on the number of atoms involved. Not only would a different internal state of the molecule (different number and arrangement of atoms) be established by this, but also a different behavior toward the molecules of other simple substances. The so-called simple substances would therefore be merely the simplest combinations of the simplest substance.

Thus, our principle promises from the outset to be just as effective for the representation of nature’s most complex arrangements, i.e., organic ones, as for the simplest arrangements, i.e., the molecules of the...

### VII. Hypothesis on the General Law of Force in Nature.

...so-called simple chemical substances; these are, in a sense, the borderline cases of the field to be covered by it.

Our hypothesis further benefits from the fact that, according to W. Weber's <note>Wilhelm Eduard Weber (1804–1891), a German physicist who formulated a law of electrical force.</note> investigations in the field of electricity, the necessity has already been asserted to assume forces that are determined not merely by the coexistence of any two particles, but also by the presence of others (compare on this the treatise of the Jablonowski Society <note>The Jablonowskische Gesellschaft der Wissenschaften, a scientific society in Leipzig.</note> 1846, p. 376, or my work *Zend-Avesta* II, p. 287, where the passage from Weber is quoted).

However, to gain a more definite basis for assessing the scope and capabilities of our principle, it will be necessary first to define it more closely. That is, we must establish the dependency of the forces it encompasses upon the circumstances of the particles' coexistence in a generally corresponding way, just as specifically occurs for gravitation. To this end, the best, if not the only, path would be for us to seek this through a generalized formulation of the law of gravitation itself. The common expression of the law of gravitation, which applies to every two particles, certainly permits no generalization for more than two particles, and this has undoubtedly contributed to pushing back the thought of a general law like ours; but it is easy to translate the common expression into another, which satisfies the factual reality just as well and achieves what is required.

According to the usual formulation of the law of gravitation, the force of each particle is directed along the line connecting the particles; that is, it strives to move in the direction of this line toward the other particle. But since in a combination of three particles, each particle is connected with two others, the <term>force of the third order</term><note>A force that emerges from the relationship between three particles simultaneously, rather than just the sum of interactions between pairs.</note>, which is supposed to be collectively determined by the coexistence of all three, can be directed neither toward one connecting line nor the other, as naturally neither one has any advantage over the other. What will its direction be? Since the common expression of the law of gravitation allows for no generalization in this respect, we shall translate it into another, which captures the facts just as well as the first, but now permits the transfer to the combination of three or more particles. ...say no longer that the force of each particle is directed toward the other particle, but rather it is directed toward the common <term>center of gravity<note>Also known as the center of mass; the point where the total mass of a system may be considered to be concentrated.</note></term> of both particles, as if this were the center attracting them both together. In practice, this actually amounts to the same thing, but it can now be applied to any number of particles.

Even with a combination of three particles, four particles, and so on, the force determined collectively by the particles being together will be directed everywhere toward the center of gravity of the combination—or, insofar as repulsive forces will naturally arise later among the series of higher forces, it will be directed away from it—as if the entire attractive or repulsive force of the system originated from there. Since, moreover, the combination of gravitational effects in groups of three or more particles also drives each particle toward the center of gravity, no actually new direction is introduced by the <term>higher force<note>A term the author uses to describe forces that emerge from the interaction of multiple particles, going beyond simple two-body Newtonian gravity.</note></term>; rather, its simple effect is merely added to or (in the case of repulsive forces) subtracted from the composite effect of gravitation, without, however, being able to be identified with it.

<margin><unclear>blue pencil flourish</unclear></margin>
The notion that the direction of the force of each particle in a combination is determined in relation to the common center of gravity of all the particles in the combination, rather than in relation to another particle, may pose a difficulty for anyone who views force as something residing specifically within the particles and acting across to other particles. This difficulty naturally disappears if one abandons this inherently unclear notion in order to make force dependent on the <term>concept of law<note>In German "Gesetzesbegriff." This refers to the philosophical shift from seeing force as a physical "fluid" or "entity" to seeing it as a mathematical description of how objects must behave under certain conditions.</note></term>, as we have already done previously. We say: bodies exert a force on one another when they move away from or toward each other according to a law relating to the circumstances of their coexistence. Since, according to this, the force itself only arises and is determined through the coexistence of the particles—insofar as the law states a given force effect only for a given coexistence—it also appears quite appropriate that the direction of a particle is not determined one-sidedly in relation to the other, but in relation to a goal established by their collective existence; just as the particles [seek] the common goal, the common—

### VII. Hypothesis on the General Law of Force in Nature.

...common center of gravity, would actually be reached if they were left to the sole effect of attractive forces.

In any case, it is certain that the **factual** nature of the force-effects known thus far permits the one expression just as well as the other, so that no **objection** to our way of thinking is possible from this standpoint. However, views cannot be refuted by mere opinions.

Indisputably, nothing prevents us from imagining our <term>higher forces<note>The author uses this term to describe complex forces that emerge when three or more particles interact, as opposed to the simple two-body attraction of standard gravity.</note></term>—which we conceive as being directed simply toward the center of gravity of the combination—as also being <term>decomposed<note>In physics, "decomposing" a force means breaking a single force acting in one direction into several components acting in other directions, usually for the sake of easier calculation.</note></term> along the lines connecting the particles. However, such a conception could not serve as the starting point for deriving the mode of action of these higher forces, insofar as they are intended to be determined uniformly by the coexistence of several particles; nor would the general consideration of the phenomena be simplified by it—even if, as is not contested, such a decomposition may be necessary for the practical needs of calculation.

The dependence of force on the distance between particles is usually expressed for gravitation as follows: the force is inversely proportional, or reciprocal, to the square of the distance. However, since even with three particles there are three distances instead of one, this expression cannot be directly transferred to the higher forces. But we can translate it into the following: instead of saying that the magnitude of the force acting in a combination of two particles *a* and *b* is reciprocal to the square of their distance, I can just as well say it is reciprocal to the product of the distance from *a* to *b* and the distance from *b* to *a*—in general, the product of the distances to be taken in any way from particle to particle. According to this, the force in a combination of, for example, 3 particles will be reciprocal to a product of six simple distances or three distance-squares—that is, in the case of equal distances, to the sixth power of the distance; in a combination of 4 particles, to a product of 12 simple distances or 6 distance-squares, and so on.

This result is very satisfying in that it naturally accounts for the great weakening of <term>molecular forces<note>Short-range forces that hold molecules together; unlike gravity, which reaches across solar systems, these forces drop off almost instantly as particles move apart.</note></term> with distance, which experience **demands**.

At first glance, one might find it strange that in the case of higher forces beyond gravitation, the magnitude of the force is determined by the product of the lines connecting the particles...

And why should the distances of the **particles** from one another not have just as much influence on the force as their distances from the **center of gravity**? In any case, a **generalization of the law of gravitation** can only be achieved in the manner assumed here.

The number of simple **distance factors** which enter into the **reciprocal products** of the **force** is determined for the successively higher combinations by an easily understood and easily derived rule. It amounts to 0 = 0 . 1 for 1 particle (**the case of inertia**); 2 = 1 . 2 for two particles (**the case of gravitation**); 6 = 2 . 3 for three particles, and so on; generally following the series:

->0 . 1 = 0 for 1 particle.<-
->1 . 2 = 2<-
->2 . 3 = 6<-
->3 . 4 = 12<-
->4 . 5 = 20<-
->5 . 6 = 30<-

and so forth. If one moves to the **squares**, one naturally has to take half of these numbers, which gives the **series**:

->0, 1, 3, 6, 10, 15, 21 and so forth.<-

in which two **odd** and two **even** numbers follow each other, which is important for a conclusion to be drawn later.

If one imagined three **atoms** arranged in a straight line at equal distances from one another, and the initially large distances were reduced until the particles were urged <note>The author uses the term "sollicitirt" (solicited/urged), a common 19th-century term for a force acting upon a body.</note> by the **force of the third level** just as strongly as by the **composition** of the two **forces of the second level** acting on each, then the distance at which the particles are found would provide a fixed **spatial magnitude**. This could serve as an absolute **unit of measurement for space**, unless it were perhaps even more practical to use the side of a **cube** to be mentioned later. In any case, it is a magnitude to which the primary relationship between the lowest **molecular force** and the **gravitational force** is linked from a quantitative perspective, and which can be called the **constant** of this ratio, or of the lowest **molecular force** itself. Based on the series on the previous page, while gravity (2 particles) relies on the 2nd power of distance, the force for 3 particles relies on the 6th power. Thus, 2 to the power of 6 equals 64.</note> would drop to 1/64; however, if the distance were halved, both would increase in inverse proportion. Such constants would also need to be found for the ratios of the remaining forces, whether in relation to the immediately preceding force or to the entire sum of the preceding forces, as well as the law according to which they progress.

Regarding the dependence of force on **mass**, if one wished to postulate a difference in the masses of the simple **atoms**—which, however, is intended to be eliminated by our principle—the (moving) force acting on each particle in a **combination** would have to be set proportional to the simple **product** of the masses; for example, with three atoms of masses *m*, *m'*, *m''*, the product would be *mm'm''*. One might indeed think that the masses and distances should be considered in connection such that each mass, divided by the distance it has from another, formed a factor of the product. In that case, in a combination of 3 atoms, each mass would enter the product twice, and in one of 4 atoms, three times. However, the distances enter the product twice only because each distance actually exists doubly as both positive and negative, which does not apply to the masses.

Insofar as we are inclined to assume that all atoms in all combinations are of equal value, the force by which the simple atoms in any combination are acted upon becomes generally independent of the masses of the atoms, provided the mass of each atom can be set = 1; the product of even a great many masses then also remains 1.

In **gravitation**, every two particles of equal value are driven toward one another or toward their common **center of gravity** with equal **acceleration** by the force acting in their combination, so that the position of this center of gravity remains unmoved. If, in combinations of more than two particles under the influence of the higher forces prevailing therein, the position of the center of gravity is likewise to remain unmoved, then the acceleration can no longer be equal for all, but must stand in proportion to the distance from the center of gravity, so that the particles move toward it from rest with velocities that are proportional to these distances. # VII. Hypothesis on the General Law of Force in Nature.

If both conditions—equal acceleration of the same particles and the preservation of the position of their <term>center of gravity<gloss>German: *Schwerpunkt*; the point at which the total mass of a system is balanced.</gloss></term>—coincide, then one must decide which of the two is to be maintained. Undoubtedly the latter, because we know of no system in which the center of gravity could be displaced by the effect of its own <term>molecular forces<gloss>Forces acting between individual particles or molecules within a substance.</gloss></term>. The equal acceleration of uniform particles in the case of **gravitation** would then only be seen as a special case, which depends on their equal distance from the center of gravity. Since the direction of force must be referenced to the center of gravity, it is to be expected anyway that the relative distance of the particles from it will not be without influence on the relative speed with which the particles strive toward it; and if the greater distance of the particles from one another weakens the acceleration for all particles collectively, this is no obstacle to the relation of their acceleration being governed by the ratio of the distance from the center of gravity.

The preceding has not yet led to the distinction between **attractive** and **repulsive forces**. However, one easily arrives at such a distinction in the following way.

The direction of the force, whether attractive or repulsive, can be held fixed by the respective negative (meaning a reduction of distance) or positive (meaning an enlargement of distance) sign of the **distance product** (p. 189). This sign is always the same for every two successive stages of force and changes in the transition to the next two stages of force, as is easily shown as follows:

If one takes the direction from atom *a* to *b* as positive for two **atoms**, then the direction from *b* to *a* is negative; the **distance product** is thus negative, and consequently, gravitation is attractive. All squares of the distances are negative for the same reason <note>The author is using a specific algebraic convention where the directional interaction between a pair of atoms results in a negative product to represent attraction. In his model, a "square" represents the dual interaction between two atoms (a to b and b to a), resulting in a negative value.</note>, and since, according to p. 189, three squares enter into the distance product for three atoms, the distance product for three atoms is also negative; therefore, the <term>ternary force<gloss>A higher-order force involving three particles simultaneously, rather than just two.</gloss></term> is likewise attractive. In the distance product for four atoms, on the other hand, six squares enter; in that for five atoms, ten squares enter; thus, the distance products here become positive, and the relevant forces are repulsive in nature. The law cited above (p. 190) regarding how the distance products progress carries with it the fact that the change, as one ascends the series of force levels, always continues in the previous manner.

<summary>The author argues that the conservation of the center of gravity is a fundamental law of internal forces and explains how forces alternate between attraction and repulsion based on the number of atoms involved in a interaction.</summary>
<keywords>center of gravity, molecular forces, gravitation, attractive forces, repulsive forces, distance product, atoms, ternary force</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s mathematical and physical derivation of forces within particle systems. Having previously discussed the preservation of the center of gravity, the author now defines how forces are layered within "combinations" (groups) of particles, where each particle is simultaneously influenced by the group as a whole and by various sub-groups.</meta>

# VII. Hypothesis on the General Law of Force in Nature.

Every higher force in a <term>combination<gloss>German: *Combination*; a grouping of particles or atoms that acts as a single system.</gloss></term> necessarily includes the co-existence of all lower forces within that same combination; for naturally, a connection of, for example, 5 atoms also includes combinations of 4, 3, and 2 particles, down to 1; whereas the reverse is not the case. The highest force in each combination always occurs only once, insofar as it is determined by the togetherness of all particles in the combination—although, of course, every particle is affected by it according to the specified rules. The lower forces, however, occur multiple times, because within every higher combination several lower combinations of the same level can be found; their effects combine with one another and with the primary force at each particle.

Thus, for example, in a combination of three particles, each particle is subject to:
1) a single force of the third degree; 
2) a composition of two forces of the second degree (because it belongs to two combinations of the second degree at the same time), and 
3) a single force of the first degree or <term>inertia<gloss>German: *Beharrung*; literally "persistence," referring to the tendency of a body to remain in its state of rest or motion.</gloss></term>. 
(These three forces, of which No. 2 is itself composed, finally combine into a common <term>resultant<gloss>The single force that represents the vector sum of two or more forces acting on an object.</gloss></term>).

In a combination of four atoms, each particle is likewise subject to:
1) a single force of the fourth degree; 
2) a composition of three forces of the third degree; 
3) a composition of three forces of the second degree; 
4) a single force of the first degree, all of which in turn combine, and so on.

It is not unimportant to note that the composition of all lower forces (with the exception of inertia based on a possible <term>primal motion<gloss>German: *Urbewegung*; an original or fundamental movement inherent to the particles.</gloss></term>) can no more shift the <term>center of gravity<gloss>German: *Schwerpunkt*; the point at which the total mass of a system is balanced.</gloss></term> of the entire combination than it can be shifted by the highest force itself. This follows from the fact that every lower force, on its own, leaves the center of gravity of the partial combination in which it acts unchanged; therefore, the combination of these forces, acting in different partial combinations, can contribute nothing to the displacement of the resulting center of gravity of the entire combination.

Let us briefly summarize the essentials of the previous definitions:

In every combination of any number of particles, a force prevails which, according to its magnitude and direction, is determined by the conditions of the togetherness of all particles at once

# VII. Hypothesis on the General Law of Force in Nature.

[...is generated,] and has the significance that the velocity of all particles increases or decreases in proportion to its magnitude, acting simultaneously in the direction in which they are driven by the force. In terms of magnitude, this force is reciprocal to the product of the squares of all distances that can be measured from each particle to every other. In terms of direction, it drives the particles as an attractive force toward the common <term>center of gravity<gloss>The point at which the entire mass of a system may be considered to be concentrated; the balance point.</gloss></term> or as a repulsive force away from the common center of gravity, depending on whether that product turns out to be negative or positive (assuming each square itself is set as negative). The distribution of this force’s effect on individual particles—that is, the motion of the individual particles by virtue of this force—occurs such that the principle of the <term>conservation of the center of gravity<gloss>A fundamental law of physics stating that the center of mass of a system of particles will not change its state of motion unless acted upon by an external force.</gloss></term> is maintained. According to this, calculating from a state of rest, they move toward or away from the center of gravity with velocities that are directly proportional to their distance from that center. Insofar as every higher <term>combination<gloss>German: *Combination*; here referring to a system composed of multiple particles acting as a unit.</gloss></term> (i.e., one consisting of more particles) includes all lower stages of combination (with fewer particles), every particle generally belongs to several lower and higher combinations and to one highest combination simultaneously. All the movements that a particle would adopt by virtue of its inclusion in each of these combinations must be determined individually; these are then composed with one another and with the motion it possesses through <term>inertia<gloss>German: *Beharrung*; the property of matter by which it continues in its existing state of rest or uniform motion in a straight line.</gloss></term>, according to the rule of the <term>parallelogram of forces<gloss>A geometric method used to find the resultant force when two or more forces act on a single point.</gloss></term>.

If we compare the series of forces we have established with the one mentioned at the beginning of this chapter—which had been considered previously—ours is based on a different, higher, and more general point of view. In our series, the higher force members are not simply made dependent, like the lower ones, on the relationship between every two particles and directed along their line of connection. Instead, they refer successively to combinations of more and more particles and, accordingly, acquire ever-new directions—specifically, toward the center of gravity each time. Furthermore, ours is based on a completely rational principle, according to which the distance products (which are reciprocal to the forces) increase as rapidly as experience requires, and attractive and repulsive forces alternate; whereas in previous formulations of the series, a principle for this was lacking.

It is evident, moreover, that the entire principle of our law... <meta>The page ends with the partial word 'Ge-', likely 'Gesetzes' (law).</meta>

# VII. Hypothesis on the General Law of Force in Nature.

[...law only] suits <term>atomism<gloss>The theory that all matter is composed of tiny, discrete, indivisible units.</gloss></term>, because in a system of, for example, three particles where two are in contact, the force relating collectively to all three would become infinite. This happens because zero enters into the product of the distances, and the force is the reciprocal <note>original: "reciproken Distanzproduct"; in mathematics, if a distance is zero, its reciprocal (1/0) is undefined or infinite.</note> of that product. This force would necessarily overpower the gravitational force acting within the system, even if the third particle were at any large, finite distance, which does not align with our observations of experience.

One might even add that this entire perspective is only compatible with simple atoms. This is because between the infinitely many different points of two atoms of even the smallest physical extension, the entire infinite sum of higher-level forces would act simultaneously with gravity. Consequently, it would not even be possible to distinguish these forces from one another. Should our hypothesis be confirmed by experience, it would simultaneously decide the case in favor of the assumption of simple <note>By "simple," the author means point-like atoms that have no physical size or internal parts.</note> atoms.

As vast as the generalization is that the law of gravity undergoes with the preceding explanation, I do not claim that this is the greatest generalization it allows. On the contrary, at the conclusion (when discussing <term>imponderables<gloss>A historical scientific term for substances that were thought to have no weight, such as light, heat, or electricity.</gloss></term>), I will point to factors that seem to demand further generalization. Next, however, let us consider some of the main consequences of the law as it has been presented so far. These must, for the time being, consist more of suggestions to pave the way for closer examination rather than truly rigorous developments.

**Elasticity.** From the fact that as the proximity of particles increases—meaning the compression of the body—ever higher forces become perceptible and eventually outweigh the lower forces (which grow more slowly as particles approach), and since these forces change their sign <note>Changing "sign" refers to switching between attraction (+) and repulsion (-).</note> every two stages as they ascend, it follows that as particles get closer, an attractive force and then a repulsive force alternately become greater than all others. Likewise, the sum of the attractive and repulsive forces (since it is not just the strongest force that matters) predominates alternately. Under the influence of this imbalance, the particles will continue to move closer or further away until equilibrium—specifically a state of stable equilibrium—is established between the two. This is a point where, upon further approach, the sum of the repulsive forces, and at greater distance, the sum of the attrac-

# VII. Hypothesis on the General Law of Force in Nature.

Meanwhile, it will suffice to continue the forceful approximation of the particles from such a point onwards, further and further, in order to reach a second point (a point of <term>labile equilibrium<gloss>A state where a slight push will move an object away from its current position, unlike stable equilibrium where it would return.</gloss></term>), where the relationship reverses; from this point onwards, the attraction grows more strongly with proximity than the repulsion. As the particles follow this predominant attraction upon crossing such a point, until the repulsion once again becomes powerful enough to halt the further approach, a new state of stable equilibrium must be achieved at a higher <note>original: "Verdichtung"</note> compression. Something analogous can occur during the forceful expansion of a body, where the body then remains permanently expanded.

The second position of stable equilibrium, which the particles assume in this process—and therefore the density of the body—is necessarily separated from the first by a certain distance. It is clear from this how one and the same substance can present itself under very different levels of density, which must be connected to other differences in properties, because different force ratios determine the state of equilibrium. This point of view might explain the <term>allotropic modifications<gloss>The property of some chemical elements to exist in two or more different forms, such as carbon existing as both graphite and diamond.</gloss></term> in which many substances appear. If bodies seem to present themselves in transitional stages between both levels, this is undoubtedly based only on a mixture of body parts found at one or the other level, and will not occur in properly crystallized bodies.

**Crystallization.** In order for the particles of a body left to its own forces to be in stable equilibrium, it is not necessarily required that they are all equidistant from one another. They could, for example, be so close in one direction that equilibrium occurs under the influence of very high attractive and repulsive forces, while in the perpendicular direction, at a greater distance, they would be in equilibrium under the influence of lower forces. Then the body will be more easily split along the latter direction than the former, because the lower forces offer less resistance to the removal of the particles from the equilibrium position than the higher ones, since they change less rapidly with distance. This provides the general perspective for the **structure** or the <term>cleavage planes<gloss>The tendency of crystals to split along definite crystallographic structural planes.</gloss></term> <note>original: "Blätterdurchgänge"</note> of bodies. # VII. Hypothesis on the General Law of Force in Nature.

Since particles <note>The text refers back to the surface particles mentioned at the end of the previous page.</note> find themselves in different conditions than in the interior, the arrangement of the particles here must fulfill special conditions if they are to be in stable equilibrium. Generally speaking—even if a more precise proof is yet to be provided—it can be seen that certain symmetry conditions of the positioning are essential for this; this provides the viewpoint for **crystal forms**.

Undeniably, in all crystals, forces of a higher order are active, or at least more active than those which cause the <term>adhesion<gloss>The force of attraction between the surfaces of two different bodies.</gloss></term> of bodies to one another, which is likely primarily the force of the third order. Now, nothing prevents small crystals from being joined together in an irregular manner through this adhesion, thereby forming seemingly non-crystalline bodies.

With what has been said regarding the conditions of crystallization and elasticity, there is an indication of what is ultimately true in the view of the <term>Dynamicists<gloss>Followers of "Dynamism," a 19th-century philosophical view (associated with Kant) that matter is not made of atoms but is a product of fundamental forces of attraction and repulsion.</gloss></term>: that bodies are formed by a conflict of attractive and repulsive forces that has reached equilibrium, and that through the former force alone they would merge into a single point, while through the latter alone they would scatter into infinity. They are truly correct if our principle is right, and one can then find a beautiful intuition from **Kant** within it—only with the essential reservation that, instead of a fulfillment of the dynamic view, it is rather **atomism** pushed to the extreme that provides the clear interpretation of Kant's intuition.

**Units of Measurement.** Until now, it has not been possible to discover an absolutely constant magnitude in nature to which one could always and everywhere return, and which could thus serve as the basis for an absolute measure. On Earth, we are inclined to borrow an absolute measure from the dimensions of the Earth or from the <term>pendulum length<gloss>The length of a pendulum that swings at a specific rate; historically used to define units of length like the meter.</gloss></term> determined under constant conditions; but the dimensions of the Earth are not absolutely fixed in view of its gradual cooling, and the pendulum length is accordingly not absolutely unchangeable either. Moreover, a measure useful only to Earth-dwellers would, in the best case, still not be an absolute measure. Our atomistic principle, however, provides the means to establish such a measure, which remains unchangeably the same for all time, on all celestial bodies, and through all their changes—though it will certainly require the finest investigations, which are not yet feasible at present,

# VII. Hypothesis on the General Law of Force in Nature.

...to determine the ratio of a known quantity to it; without, however, the hope for this needing to be abandoned altogether.

Fundamentally, an indefinite number of absolutely fixed quantities can be derived from our principle, which could serve as the basis for an absolute determination of measurement; such a possibility was already considered above. However, it would seem most practical to base the absolute unit of measurement on the following perspective, which—in connection with the absolute unit of length—grants absolute units for all other measures used in physics.

According to conditions of symmetry, one may assume that a cube made of 8 atoms—that is, whose 8 corners are respectively occupied by 8 atoms—can exist as a **molecule**. However, if the **attractive forces** are to be in equilibrium with the **repulsive forces**, it can only exist with certain dimensions, which will remain the same everywhere and always. This cube can therefore, with its side, side-surface, and cubic volume, simultaneously offer the unit for length, area, and volume. Its weight will serve as the unit of weight, and its density as the unit of density. The unit of time will be obtained through the duration of the **vibrations** <note>The original German term is *Schwingungen*, referring to the oscillating back-and-forth movement of particles.</note> into which the cube falls (alternating between expansion and contraction) if one imagines its particles shifted by an infinitely small amount toward the center of gravity; one must remember that the duration of infinitely small vibrations (as a <term>limit concept<gloss>In mathematics and physics, a value that a function or sequence approaches as the input approaches some value; here, the theoretical duration of a vibration as it becomes infinitesimally small.</gloss></term>) is nevertheless finite. Insofar as **heat**, **magnetism**, and **electricity**—as is perhaps not improbable—should differ only through differently arranged vibrations, one would also be able to obtain absolute units of measurement for these from the cube. For the spatial units, it is presupposed that the cube is absolutely cold, non-electric, and non-magnetic—that is, all its particles are in a state of complete rest, because the state of vibration produces changes in the mean position of the atoms. Perhaps the cube in question coincides with the molecule of the relatively simplest chemical substance, as will be considered in the following.

**Chemically simple substances.** According to the view already touched upon above, their difference rests on the fact that they are underpinned by molecules made of a different number of simple atoms.
