# VII. Hypothesis on the General Law of Force in Nature.

Since they cannot be decomposed by the forces at our disposal, the internal forces holding them together must be very strong in relation to the external forces that can act upon them; this can hardly be represented otherwise than as follows: The **molecules** <note>The author uses the term *Molecule* to describe a compound structure of atoms that we might today call a chemical element.</note> of so-called simple substances consist of such a high combination—that is, such a large number—of simple atoms that higher forces become active within them, which increase very strongly with proximity; and the molecules are so dense that these forces actually achieve a very powerful effect. As the attractive and repulsive forces reach an equilibrium according to the principles discussed under **elasticity** and **crystallization**, each molecule adopts a certain fundamental crystalline form.

If we imagine the atoms of the world initially in a very diverse arrangement, then such molecules of so-called simple substances could form identically in very different places, since it was only necessary that a sufficient number of atoms met in sufficient relative proximity to each other and at a sufficient distance from others so as not to be disturbed in the formation of the molecule by their influence. Once these molecules are formed, however, they cannot be so easily destroyed again; for when two or more such molecules approach each other from a great distance, an equilibrium of the attractive and repulsive forces between them will already occur under the influence of lower forces than those prevailing within themselves, and at greater distances than exist between their own particles. Thus, without a violent approach—for which we know no means—each molecule retains its composition without merging with the other or decomposing with it.

The most immediate important conclusion of the preceding conception of simple substances is this: If even the molecule of the relatively simplest substance—on our Earth, **hydrogen**—is still composite, then, taken in principle, it will not be expedient at all to regard the **atomic weight** of any of the so-called chemically simple substances as the basic unit of atomic weight, but rather the weight of the simple atom itself. The atomic weights—or more properly, the molecular weights—of all simple substances would then be designated by the number of atoms that respectively enter into the molecule of each. According to this, rational ratios must exist between the molecular weights of all so-called simple substances,

This is because the phenomena of chemical bonding and decomposition, into which the molecules of simple substances enter unchanged, would themselves require at least the noticeable interplay of the attractive force of the third level and the repulsive force of the fourth level. This would presuppose a proximity between the molecules corresponding to the proximity of the atoms within the 4-part molecules, such that their separate existence would not be possible. Since, moreover, even for the phenomena of ordinary **elasticity** the repulsive force of the fourth level is claimed as the minimum, the force of the fourth level cannot even suffice for chemical phenomena. Therefore, the formation of the molecules of simple substances—that is, those indecomposable by our chemical operations—must rest on even higher forces; that is, even more than 4 particles must enter into the simplest of them. For example, gravity follows an inverse-square law (second level).</note> on the other hand, in order to maintain a state of stable equilibrium for the particles of a molecule at levels higher than the fifth, the attractive forces of the sixth and seventh levels must be balanced by a repulsive force of the eighth level. But if we assume that the molecular weight of the simplest substance really is 8, its shape would indisputably be a cube, and—as considered above—one would thereby have simultaneously gained the <term>module</term> <note>A fundamental unit or standard of measurement.</note> for all units of measurement.

Since oxygen has 8 times the molecular weight of hydrogen, then—provided hydrogen were the simplest substance—the atomic weight of oxygen would be 64, which is the cube of 4, whereas 8 is the cube of 2. Oxygen could therefore represent a cube with twice the number of atoms along its side as hydrogen. Sulfur, with double the atomic weight of oxygen, could form a column made of two cubes placed one on top of the other. Carbon, with 6 times the weight of hydrogen, could be an <term>octahedron</term> <note>A three-dimensional shape with eight triangular faces.</note> made of 48 atoms; for if 6 atoms are sufficient to define the corners of the simplest octahedron, one could obtain a similar but larger octahedron with double the number of atoms along the edges, which would contain a total of 2³ · 6 = 48 atoms. However, it would be useless to lose oneself further in such possibilities without a more thorough investigation, which cannot take place here.

Furthermore, it is easy to see how the identical atomic weights of some simple substances now become possible. It is indisputably a different arrangement of the same number of atoms upon which they depend; and thus, this is the fundamental case of <term>isomerism</term>. <note>Isomerism is the phenomenon where two or more compounds have the same formula but a different arrangement of atoms, giving them different properties.</note>

# VII. Hypothesis on the General Law of Force in Nature.

<note>original: "das mechanische Aequivalent der Wärme erhalten hat"</note> ...the mechanical equivalent of heat <note>The "mechanical equivalent of heat" refers to the discovery that motion and heat are interchangeable, a foundational concept of thermodynamics developed in the 1840s.</note> has been obtained), without having to resort to special "heat atmospheres" <note>Early scientists often imagined heat as a physical fluid or "atmosphere" surrounding atoms. The author here rejects this in favor of a kinetic view where heat is simply the vibration of particles.</note> surrounding the particles, the following view regarding the relationship between the various states of matter <note>Original: "Aggregatzustände," referring to solid, liquid, and gas.</note> might be founded upon our principle.

If we start from a point of stable equilibrium, where all particles of a body are at rest—where repulsive forces gain the upper hand as they grow closer, and attractive forces as they move further apart—and we then set these particles into thermal vibration. So long as the thermal vibrations are small enough that the particles, in their mutual approach, do not cross the nearest point of unstable equilibrium <note>Original: "labiles Gleichgewicht." A point where the slightest nudge will push the particle away into a new state.</note> (from which point that relationship reverses), the body remains solid. However, as soon as this point is reached and crossed, the liquid state <note>Original: "tropfbarer Zustand," literally the "drippable state."</note> occurs. For it follows naturally that the next point of stable equilibrium is also immediately crossed; since the particle that has passed the point of unstable equilibrium is driven toward this second point of stable equilibrium by the resulting dominance of the attractive force with increasing speed. Due to its inertia <note>Original: "Beharrung."</note>, it overshoots this point until the repulsive force, which from then on becomes more and more dominant, finally halts further approach. Whereupon the particle, in its return, resumes all the speeds it had on its approach; thus, it is carried beyond its first position of equilibrium again and henceforth oscillates around two stable positions of equilibrium with an unstable position of equilibrium lying between them, rather than just one.

It is now easily understood from a general standpoint that this sudden enlargement of the vibration—with which new relationships of duration and speed are naturally connected—must bring an interruption of continuity to all phenomena that are linked to the magnitude, duration, and <term>kinetic energy</term> <note>Original: "lebendige Kraft," or *vis viva*. In 19th-century physics, this was the term for $mv^2$.</note> of the vibrations.

First, the suddenly increased mobility of all particles in the fluid can be seen as an expression of the fact that the vibrations themselves have now been suddenly enlarged and encompass a point of unstable equilibrium. The ratios of expansion must change suddenly, insofar as they are a function of the vibration conditions;

# VII. Hypothesis on the General Law of Force in Nature.

...not less must the sudden enlargement of the vibrations, which is simultaneously connected to a prolonged duration, bring about a sudden change in kinetic energy <note>original: "lebendige Kraft." This term, literally "living force," was the historical name for kinetic energy (vis viva) before the modern terminology was standardized.</note>, with which the latent heat <note>original: "Latentwerden der Wärme." The author is explaining "latent heat"—the energy absorbed during a phase change, like melting ice, that doesn't raise the temperature.</note> in the act of liquefaction is undeniably related.

The entry into the gaseous state then undeniably rests on the fact that the vibrations increase so far that the particles henceforth oscillate around three positions of stable equilibrium. Analogous phenomena must occur here as during the entry into the liquid <note>original: "tropfbaren," literally "drippable" or "capable of forming drops."</note> state, but they are not exactly the same.

If the view established here regarding the relationship of the states of matter should be confirmed—and the concept of the solid state were thus generally defined by the fact that the particles, in their oscillations, do not cross the nearest boundary of unstable equilibrium—then the common assumption that the ether <note>The "luminiferous ether" was a theoretical medium that 19th-century physicists believed permeated all space to allow light waves to travel.</note> is the most fluid thing in the world would have to give way to the view that it is the most solid thing there is; since the ether particles, even in their widest oscillations, undeniably always remain very far from crossing that boundary, and the ancients were to a certain extent right when they considered the heavens to be a crystal vault.

Nevertheless, the comparison of the ether with the solid bodies of the Earth remains, from another perspective, not very apt. Due to its thinness, the highest observable forces in the ether undeniably do not exceed the repulsive force of the fourth and at most fifth stage <note>The author uses a "hierarchy of forces" where different powers/stages of distance determine the strength of attraction and repulsion.</note>, and it is undeniably not grouped into specific molecules, whereas the solid bodies of the Earth consist of molecules in which high forces are active. This naturally results in very different behavior. The ether is simply a being *of its own kind* <note>original: "sui generis."</note>, and its state of matter is not perfectly comparable to any other.

***Imponderables.*** Few physicists today might still believe that the imponderables <note>A class of "weightless" substances—like light, heat, electricity, and magnetism—that early scientists thought were physical fluids before they were understood as energy or fields.</note> distinguished by different names are essentially different agents, even if the bridge between light and heat on one hand, and electricity and magnetism on the other, has not yet been found; and further, it is to be hoped that the completion of atomic theory <note>original: "Atomistik."</note> into the simplest and most unified fundamental concepts will ultimately also abolish the distinction between imponderables and ponderables <note>Substances with measurable weight (matter).</note> insofar as it makes the phenomena we count in the realm of imponderables dependent only on the relationships and states of motion of the same fundamental atoms which also underlie the phenomena of pon...

# VII. Hypothesis on the General Law of Force in Nature.

...imponderables <note>original: "Imponderabilien." In 19th-century physics, imponderables were substances or "fluids" that lacked measurable weight, such as light, heat, and electricity.</note> lie at the foundation; however, this reduction, like the previous one, remains reserved for the future; and our hypothesis of multiple forces leads no further in this or that respect than the previous assumptions; rather, as will be discussed in more detail shortly, it undeniably requires an expansion itself to be able to conform to such a reduction. Nevertheless, based on our previous assumptions, the general difference between the imponderables and the ponderables <note>original: "Ponderabilien." This refers to ordinary matter that has weight and can be measured on a scale.</note> might already be stated with some probability as follows:

All phenomena that we make dependent on imponderables are directly relatable only to the individual movement patterns of the ultimate particles; whereas those we attribute to ponderables relate to movement patterns of combinations of such particles—as molecules, bodies, or celestial bodies—as a whole. This is true even if, in the final instance, these latter phenomena must also be made dependent on the conditions of the ultimate particles, but in such a way that they represent resultants <note>A "resultant" is the single force or effect that represents the sum of multiple individual forces acting together.</note> or total effects for these combinations. Thus, light propagates through celestial space through vibrations of the particles of the ether, which, notably, should hardly be thought of as consisting of molecules, but rather more likely as being uniformly constituted directly from ultimate particles; thus, heat vibrations seem to be understood as vibrations of the ultimate particles of the ponderable bodies themselves; so too may electrical and magnetic phenomena rest on vibrations or other movements of ultimate particles, whether within the molecules or between the molecules. Meanwhile, the movements of celestial bodies, of the falling stone, of the pendulum, the wave movements of water, the sound vibrations of the air, and even chemical compounds and separations are relatable to larger or smaller combinations of ultimate particles, insofar as we are also prompted to assume molecules underlie even chemically simple substances (cf. p. 198 ff.).

Another essential difference is likely connected to this. It is evident that phenomena which affect the ultimate particles individually can only emerge from their interactions with other very close particles, whereas effects that are meant to manifest identically on molecules, celestial bodies, or the like as a whole can only be exerted from a greater distance. According to this, the phenomena of the imponderables...

# VII. Hypothesis on the General Law of Force in Nature.

...generally stand under the influence of stronger and higher forces, and are related to higher velocities of the particles than the phenomena of ponderable matter <note>original: "Ponderabilien." In 19th-century physics, this refers to ordinary matter that has measurable weight, distinguished from the weightless "ether."</note>. Thus, the light vibrations of the ether and heat vibrations of bodies are immensely faster than sound vibrations.

It is commonly assumed that the particles of weighable bodies are surrounded by special **ether atmospheres**, to which one attributes heat, light, electrical, and magnetic phenomena within weighable bodies; and indeed, just as air penetrates water, so too could ether—that is, atoms that are not grouped into special molecules—penetrate between the molecules of weighable bodies. However, just as the air content of water is not essential to the existence of water itself, an ether content might not be essential to the existence of weighable bodies. And if it seems that one can make the heat phenomena of bodies dependent on the vibrations of the final particles—not those contained *between* the molecules of weighable bodies, but those which *constitute* these molecules themselves—then the question arises as to how far one can succeed with this approach in the case of other <term>imponderables</term> <note>Weightless substances like electricity or magnetism.</note>.

Should the <term>galvanic current</term> <note>original: "galvanische Strömung." An early term for direct electric current.</note> be understood merely as a flow of ether particles between the molecules of the weighable closing wire, and the magnetic circular currents merely as circular movements of the ether around the ponderable iron particles, then this would certainly decide the case in favor of ether atmospheres. However, as long as the fundamental relationship of electricity and magnetism to the phenomena of ponderable matter remains unclear, and the final link has not been found, nothing can be decided; and the flow of electricity cannot be simply identified with the transport of material or ether particles.

Another important point for consideration presents itself. If the task is truly to be fulfilled—to make the phenomena of imponderables dependent on the same fundamental forces, and to relate them to the same or similar particles as those of ponderables—it does not seem that one can succeed with fundamental forces that depend only on distance, but not on the **state of motion** of the particles. Insofar as our principle of <term>multiple forces</term> <note>The author's theory that forces involve complex interactions between many particles at once.</note>—in its previous formulation and execution, and as has generally occurred with <term>binary forces</term> <note>Forces acting between only two points, like traditional gravity.</note> except in the case of **Weber** <note>Wilhelm Eduard Weber (1804–1891), a physicist who pioneered the idea that electromagnetic forces depend on the velocity and acceleration of charges, not just their distance.</note>—only takes distance into account, it seems therefore to still require a supplement.

<summary>The author debates whether heat and electricity are caused by a separate "ether" fluid surrounding atoms or by the motion of the atoms themselves. He concludes that current physical laws, which only account for the distance between particles, must be expanded to include the particles' velocity and movement.</summary>

<keywords>ether atmospheres, ponderables, imponderables, galvanic current, magnetism, molecular motion, Wilhelm Weber, binary forces, multiple forces</keywords>

# VII. Hypothesis on the General Law of Force in Nature.

Indeed, when one sees how forces of attraction and repulsion—which act at very noticeable distances—are developed through the rubbing or contact of dissimilar bodies, it is not clear how a principle that only takes the distance between particles into account could represent that development and the way these <term>molecular forces</term> <note>Forces acting between molecules, which the author is contrasting with large-scale gravitational forces.</note> become perceivable. Electromagnetic and induction phenomena, too, might elude any explanation based on a principle that regards the distance between particles as the sole determining factor for force.

Furthermore, <term>W. Weber</term> <note>Wilhelm Eduard Weber (1804–1891), a German physicist who sought to unify electromagnetic phenomena through a law dependent on the movement of charges.</note> has long since felt prompted by these latter phenomena to establish a dependence of the force of electrical particles on their relative velocity and acceleration. There should be all the less hindrance to thinking about a generalization of this dependence for material particles in general, as it emerges from new investigations by Weber (conducted in connection with <term>Kohlrausch</term> <note>Rudolf Kohlrausch (1809–1858), who worked with Weber to determine the ratio between electrostatic and electromagnetic units of charge.</note> and currently awaiting publication) that the velocity required for noticeable effects to emerge in the field of electricity is so immense that, if a similar dependence were assumed for planetary masses, their velocity would still be found too small to necessitate taking it into account in astronomical calculations.

It is evident, however, that the friction and contact of dissimilar bodies may very well be capable of producing changes in the relative velocity and acceleration ratios of the smallest particles; thus, a force that has a very small value according to its dependence on distance might possibly assume a very large value according to its simultaneous dependence on the relations of motion.

I find this subject, meanwhile, too difficult and the field of conjecture in this regard too large and vague to venture upon it with confidence; and if I nevertheless add a few remarks, it is with the awareness that I may perhaps entirely miss the truth in doing so.

It must be taken into consideration that <term>Weber’s formula</term> <note>Weber's Law of Electrodynamics, which was a precursor to Maxwell's equations.</note> for the dependence of the forces of electrical particles on their relative state of velocity and acceleration can provide no direct starting point if the question is whether and how the electrical state of the particles themselves—which is assumed as a given in this formula—might be made dependent on conditions of relative velocity and acceleration.

<summary>The author discusses the limitations of force laws based solely on distance, suggesting that relative velocity and acceleration (as explored by Wilhelm Weber) are necessary to explain electromagnetism and molecular interactions.</summary>
<keywords>Wilhelm Weber, Rudolf Kohlrausch, law of force, molecular forces, electromagnetism, induction, relative velocity, acceleration, electrodynamics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Wilhelm Weber’s electrodynamic theories, moving from a general justification for including motion in force laws to a specific mathematical proposal. The author is attempting to show that a unified law of force, which accounts for both distance and the movement of particles, could explain not just electricity but also fundamental thermodynamic properties like the expansion of matter when heated.</meta>

# VII. Hypothesis on the General Law of Force in Nature.

One can only conclude, from the necessity of introducing such a dependence within electrical phenomena, that if the electrical state can indeed be made dependent on general forces that apply equally to all particles of matter, then these forces must also depend upon velocity and acceleration.

The <term>Weber formula</term> <note>Proposed by Wilhelm Weber, this was a pioneering attempt to combine electrostatic and electrodynamic forces into a single equation.</note> for the force *K*, with which two electrical particles (whose masses are *e* and *e'*) repel or attract one another, is as follows*):

->K = (ee' / r^2) * (1 - Av^2 + 2 Arw)<-

In this formula, *r* represents the distance between the two particles, *v* their <term>relative velocity</term>, *w* their <term>relative acceleration</term>, and *A* is a positive constant. The charges *e* and *e'* are to be taken with the same or opposite signs, depending on whether the electricities are of the same or different types.

If I am not mistaken, the dependence of force on relative velocity could be assumed with some degree of probability as follows, and upon this, a simple and appealing explanation for the <term>thermal expansion</term> <note>The tendency of matter to change its shape, area, and volume in response to a change in temperature.</note> of bodies could be established.

If we designate the velocities possessed by two particles, *m* and *m'*, as *u* and *u'* when broken down in the direction of their connecting lines (conceived with positive or negative signs depending on whether the direction of movement is the same or opposite), then one could take either *u — u'* or *u' — u* for their relative velocity *v*. If one of these differences is positive (equal to +v), the other will be negative (equal to —v); consequently, the product of the two, or the square of the relative velocity, must necessarily be negative. Since there is no more reason to prefer a positive or negative value when determining the force's dependence on relative velocity than there is when determining its dependence on distance (compare p. 192), we may assume that a dependence on the product of both—or on the square of the relative velocity—exists (as is also the case in Weber’s formula), and that this square, much like the square of the distance, should be introduced with a negative sign.

For a combination of three particles, a product of three negative relative velocity squares would then come into consideration, just as with the product of three distance squares (p. 192); in general, the one product would always be positive and negative at the same time as the other. For the <term>ternary force</term> <note>A force involving the simultaneous interaction of three particles, rather than the usual two-body interactions found in Newtonian physics.</note> *K*, the shared dependence...

# VII. Hypothesis on the General Law of Force in Nature.

...dependence on the three distances *r*, *r₁*, *r₂* and the three relative velocities *v*, *v₁*, *v₂* for the three (assumed to be equal) masses *m*, *m₁*, *m₂* of the ultimate particles would be expressed by the formula:

-> *K* = − $\frac{m m_1 m_2}{r^2 r_1^2 r_2^2}$ (1 − *A v<sup>2</sup> v<sub>1</sub><sup>2</sup> v<sub>2</sub><sup>2</sup>*) = − $\frac{m m_1 m_2}{r^2 r_1^2 r_2^2}$ + $\frac{m m_1 m_2}{r^2 r_1^2 r_2^2}$ *A v<sup>2</sup> v<sub>1</sub><sup>2</sup> v<sub>2</sub><sup>2</sup>* <-

wherein *A* is a positive constant. Specifically, insofar as the attraction and repulsion—which depend on the relative velocity—also experience an influence from the distance and are weakened by it, the product of the distances must enter the term containing the product of the relative velocities as its reciprocal multiplier. However, since an attraction or repulsion based purely on distance persists even at a zero value for the relative velocities, the formula must also contain a term dependent only on distance. Both conditions are most simply satisfied by the previous form. In this way, both components of the <term>total force</term>, the one depending solely on distance and the one depending simultaneously on distance and relative velocity, receive opposite signs, as shown in the second of the preceding expressions.

This corresponds to all forces where the product of the distances is negative, such as with a <term>binary force</term> <note>A force acting between two points or particles; the author is likely distinguishing between simple two-body interactions and more complex multi-particle systems.</note>. Conversely, according to the same derivation, for all forces where the product of the distances is positive, both mentioned terms receive the same sign. In other words: attractive forces are diminished by an increase in relative velocity, while repulsive forces are increased. 

It is immediately evident how expansion through heating is connected to this, if one conceives of heating as an increase in, and thus an increased velocity of, the vibrations. Should the relative acceleration—as in <term>Weber's formula for electricity</term> <note>Wilhelm Weber’s law of electrodynamics (1846) which combined electrostatic and electrodynamic effects into one equation involving velocity and acceleration.</note>—enter the general force expression only in the first power, it would not be necessary to take it into account regarding heating, since relative acceleration and deceleration increase in the same proportion through the enlargement of <term>oscillation amplitudes</term> <note>The maximum displacement of a vibrating particle from its equilibrium position.</note>.

In the meantime, one might also conceive of the following way to explain repulsion through heating without regard for a dependence on velocity. If we start from the internal <term>state of equilibrium</term> of a body or molecule, the particles will alternately approach and recede from one another through their vibrations around their equilibrium positions...

<summary>The author proposes a mathematical formula for a universal force law where velocity influences attraction and repulsion. This model explains thermal expansion by suggesting that as particles vibrate faster (heat up), attractive forces weaken and repulsive forces strengthen.</summary>

<keywords>law of force, relative velocity, binary force, thermal expansion, Weber's formula, oscillation amplitudes, molecular equilibrium, particle physics</keywords>

# VII. Hypothesis on the Universal Law of Force in Nature.

distance themselves. However, through <term>oscillations</term> <note>back-and-forth vibrations of particles</note> that are of equal magnitude on both sides of the equilibrium position, the distance is decreased in a greater proportion during the approaching movement than it is increased during the receding movement. This is most easily seen in the following extreme case: Suppose two particles are vibrating directly toward and away from each other, and the magnitude of their oscillations—beginning at the same moment—is equal to their distance. During the approaching movement, this distance is reduced to zero (thereby decreased in an infinite proportion), whereas during the receding movement, it is only doubled. Thus, the repulsive force must gain an advantage over the force of attraction during the motion. The result of this is that the particle vibrates around a median position in which it is further from the other particle than it was at rest.

It is easy to see that this result is not exclusively tied to that extreme case, but must be the general result of randomly oriented oscillations of particles against one another.

One might object to this explanation by noting that sound vibrations do not carry any expansion with them, and that in <term>elasticity phenomena</term>, a body requires no noticeably greater force <note>original: "efodert"; likely a typo for "erfordert" (requires)</note> to undergo compression than to undergo expansion by a given small amount. However, perhaps it can be countered that acoustic and elasticity phenomena depend on the "lower" forces of <term>molecules</term> <note>here meaning larger clusters of matter</note> that are relatively far apart, while heat phenomena depend on the "higher" forces of particles that are relatively close, where a proportional approach and recession must then gain greater influence. For now, however, it will be difficult to decide with any certainty on the reality of one or the other mode of explanation.

Regarding the opposing types of electricity, one might perhaps consider that friction, like the contact of dissimilar bodies, has the effect of making the oscillations of the smallest particles of matter or <term>ether</term> <note>The "aether" was a hypothesized medium through which light and heat were thought to travel in 19th-century physics</note>—which as heat oscillations may be randomly oriented—respectively more parallel and more perpendicular to the surface of the bodies. Specifically, negative electricity might rest upon oscillations in the former sense. The different forms of positive and negative sparks and of <term>Lichtenberg figures</term> <note>Branching patterns formed by electrical discharges on the surface of insulating materials</note> would agree well with this; and it seems to me that if one wishes to think of the electrical state as depending on the oscillations of particles at all, one can hardly think of anything else. The <term>galvanic current</term> <note>A steady flow of electricity (direct current)</note> would then be an interference phenomenon of such oscillations.

In the meantime, I have not yet succeeded in using this concept to explain electrical attractions and repulsions through a suitable assumption regarding the nature of the fundamental—

In the announcement of this journal, the editor defined its task as follows:

> "To communicate as completely as possible from the ongoing research and discoveries in the fields of natural sciences and anthropology that which—considering the interconnection of all branches of these sciences—may appear worth knowing to anyone engaged in a particular branch, beyond their specific professional interest. By doing so, the journal aims to provide a supplementary aid and often-welcome convenience to specialized studies, while simultaneously satisfying the interest of the philosopher, the educator, and the physician in the progress of these fields.
> 
> Since the scientific needs in these respects go hand-in-hand with the *educational needs of every cultured person in general*, these requirements shall be met collectively. The scientific tendency and character of the Central Journal <note>original: "Centralblatt"</note> shall be combined with a serious, popular style through clarity, transparency, and precision of presentation."

What the editor promised the public with these words has, according to the unanimous judgment of readers both at home and abroad, been fulfilled. Within a limited space, a wealth of exceedingly valuable and interesting reports and notes on the latest research and discoveries across the entire broad field of natural sciences is recorded here, so that even in years to come, the Central Journal will be regarded as an inexhaustible source for studies in the relevant fields of knowledge.
